2.3.11
gives us the statement for
n=2
. Assume, by induction, that the statement is true for some
n-1\geq 2
. We will show the statement is also true for
n
. We first prove the case
k=1
.

\displaystyle\;\;\;D^{(2)}(D^{(n)}(Z^{n},\ldots,Z^{2},B^{2}),D(Z^{1},B^{1}))

\displaystyle=D(D(D^{(n-1)}(Z^{n},\ldots,Z^{2}),B^{2}),D(Z^{1},B^{1}))

\displaystyle=D(D(D^{(n-1)}(Z^{n},\ldots,Z^{2}),Z^{1}),B^{1})

\displaystyle=D(D^{(n)}(Z^{n},\ldots,Z^{1}),B^{1})

\displaystyle=D^{(n+1)}(Z^{n},\ldots,Z^{1},B^{1}).

The second equality above was a consequence of Lemma
2.3.11
.
Now, let
2\leq k\leq n-1
. Then, applying the definition of
D^{(k+1)}
followed by the induction assumption,

\displaystyle D^{(k+1)}(D^{(n-k+1)}(Z^{n},\ldots,Z^{k+1},B^{k+1}),D(Z^{k},B^{k}),\ldots,D(Z^{1},B^{1}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle D(D^{(k)}(D^{(n-k+1)}(Z^{n},\ldots,Z^{k+1},B^{k+1}),D(Z^{k},B^{k}),\ldots,D(Z^{2},B^{2})),D(Z^{1},B^{1}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle D(D^{(n)}(Z^{n},\ldots,Z^{2},B^{2}),D(Z^{1},B^{1}))=D^{(2)}(D^{(n)}(Z^{n},\ldots,Z^{2},B^{2}),D(Z^{1},B^{1})).

Hence, we have reduced this to the
k=1
case.
∎

We note that the case
k=n-1
of Theorem
2.3.12
gives us

D^{(n+1)}(Z^{n},\ldots,Z^{1},B^{1})=D^{(n)}(D(Z^{n},B^{n}),\ldots,D(Z^{1},B^{1})).

(2.28)

2.3.2
Multiclass Markov chains

We define here two discrete-time Markov chains on the state spaces
\mathcal{Y}_{n}^{A}
and
\mathcal{Y}_{n}^{A}
(
2.6
)–(
2.7
).

The multiline process is a discrete-time Markov chain on the state space
\mathcal{Y}_{n}^{(\sigma^{2}a,\infty)}
of (
2.6
) for some choice of
\sigma>0
and
a\in\mathbb{R}
. Analogous processes are defined in discrete settings in
[
63
]
and
[
60
]
.
The transition from the time
m-1
state
\mathbf{Z}_{m-1}=\mathbf{Z}=(Z^{1},Z^{2},\ldots,Z^{n})\in\mathcal{Y}_{n}^{(\sigma^{2}a,\infty)}
to the time
m
state

\mathbf{Z}_{m}=\overline{\mathbf{Z}}=(\overline{Z}^{1},\overline{Z}^{2},\ldots,\overline{Z}^{n})\in\mathcal{Y}_{n}^{(\sigma^{2}a,\infty)}

is defined as follows. The driving force is an auxiliary function
B\in C_{\text{pin}}(\mathbb{R})
that satisfies

\lim_{x\rightarrow-\infty}\frac{B(x)}{x}=\sigma^{2}a\in\mathbb{R}.

for some fixed choice of
a
.
First, set
B^{1}=B
, and
\overline{Z}^{1}=D(Z^{1},B^{1})
.
Then, iteratively for
i=2,3,\ldots,n
:

B^{i}=R(Z^{i-1},B^{i-1}),\qquad\text{and}\qquad\overline{Z}^{i}=D(Z^{i},B^{i}).

(2.29)

Lemma 2.3.13
.

The multiline process (
2.29
) is well-defined on the state space
\mathcal{Y}_{n}^{(\sigma^{2}a,\infty)}
.

Proof.

This follows from Lemma
2.3.6
: Inductively, each
B^{i}
satisfies

\lim_{x\rightarrow-\infty}\frac{B^{i}(x)}{x}=\sigma^{2}a

so since
\mathbf{Z}\in\mathcal{Y}_{n}^{(\sigma^{2}a,\infty)}
, we have that, for
1\leq i\leq n
,

\limsup_{x\rightarrow-\infty}Z^{i}(x)-B^{i}(x)=-\infty.\qed

Theorem 2.3.14
.

For each choice of
a\in\mathbb{R}
and
\bar{\xi}=(\xi_{1}<\cdots<\xi_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{>a}
, the measure
\nu_{\sigma}^{\bar{\xi}}
on
\mathcal{Y}_{n}^{(\sigma^{2}a,\infty)}
is invariant for the multiline process (
2.29
) if the driving function
B
at each step of the evolution is taken to be an independent two-sided Brownian motion with diffusivity
\sigma
and drift
\sigma^{2}a
.

Proof.

Assume that
\mathbf{Z}=(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{n})\in\mathcal{Y}_{n}^{(\sigma^{2}a,\infty)}
has distribution
\nu_{\sigma}^{\bar{\xi}}
. We will show that
\overline{\mathbf{Z}}
also has distribution
\nu_{\sigma}^{\bar{\xi}}
. The assumption on
\mathbf{Z}
means that
Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{n}
are independent two-sided Brownian motions with diffusivity
\sigma
and drift
\sigma^{2}\xi_{i}
. By Theorem
2.2.2
,

\overline{Z}^{1}=D(Z^{1},B^{1})

is a two-sided Brownian motion with diffusivity
\sigma
and drift
\sigma^{2}\xi_{1}
, independent of

B^{2}=R(Z^{1},B^{1}),

which is a two-sided Brownian motion with diffusivity
\sigma
and drift
\sigma^{2}a
. Hence, the random paths
\overline{Z}^{1},B^{2},Z^{2},\ldots,Z^{n}
are mutually independent. We iterate this process as follows: Assume, for some
2\leq k\leq n-1
, that the random paths
\overline{Z}^{1},\ldots,\overline{Z}^{k-1},B^{k},Z^{k},\ldots,Z^{n}
are mutually independent, where for
1\leq i\leq k-1
,
\overline{Z}^{i}
is a Brownian motion with diffusivity
\sigma
and drift
\sigma^{2}\xi_{i}
. Then, by another application of Theorem
2.2.2
,

\overline{Z}^{k}=D(Z^{k},B^{k})

is a two-sided Brownian motion with diffusivity
\sigma
and drift
\sigma^{2}\xi_{k}
, independent of

B^{k+1}=R(Z^{k},B^{k}),

which is a two-sided Brownian motion with diffusivity
\sigma
and zero drift.
Since
(\overline{Z}^{k},B^{k+1})
is a function of
(B^{k},Z^{k})
, we have that
\overline{Z}^{1},\ldots,\overline{Z}^{k},B^{k+1},Z^{k+1},\ldots,Z^{n}
are mutually independent, completing the proof.
∎

For a fixed diffusivity
\sigma>0
and a constant
a>0
, we now define a Markov chain

\eta:=\{\eta_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}=\{(\eta_{m}^{1},\ldots,\eta_{m}^{n})\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}

with state space
\mathcal{X}_{n}^{(\sigma^{2}a,\infty)}
. Henceforth,
\mathbf{F}=\{F_{m}\}_{m\geq 1}
denotes an i.i.d. sequence of two-sided Brownian motions with diffusivity
\sigma
and drift
\sigma^{2}a
, independent of the initial configuration
\eta_{0}\in\mathcal{X}_{n}^{(\sigma^{2}a,\infty)}
. At each discrete time step
m\geq 1
, set
F_{m}
to be the driving Brownian motion. Given the time
m-1
state
\eta_{m-1}
, define the time
m
state of the chain as

\eta_{m}=\bigl(D(\eta^{1}_{m-1},F_{m}),D(\eta^{2}_{m-1},F_{m}),\ldots,D(\eta^{n}_{m-1},F_{m})\bigr).

(2.30)

Lemmas
2.3.9
and
2.3.10
imply that if
\eta_{m-1}\in\mathcal{X}_{n}^{(\sigma^{2}a,\infty)}
, then
\eta_{m}\in\mathcal{X}_{n}^{(\sigma^{2}a,\infty)}
as well.

Theorem 2.3.15
.

For
a,\sigma>0
and
\bar{\xi}=(\xi_{1}<\cdots<\xi_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{>a}
, the measure
\mu_{\sigma}^{\bar{\xi}}
of (
2.9
) is invariant for the Markov chain (
2.30
).

Proof.

This follows by an intertwining argument developed by Ferrari and Martin for particle systems in
[
63
]
and carried out for exponential last-passage percolation in
[
60
]
. Let
\mathbf{Z}\sim\nu_{\sigma}^{\bar{\xi}}
. Assume that
\eta
has distribution
\mu_{\sigma}^{\bar{\xi}}
: the distribution of
\mathcal{D}^{(n)}(\mathbf{Z})
. Without loss of generality, we assume that
\eta=\mathcal{D}^{(n)}(\mathbf{Z})
. Then, for Brownian motion
B
, let
\mathcal{S}^{B}
denote the mapping of a single evolution step of
\mathbf{Z}
according to the multiline process (
2.29
) and
\mathcal{T}^{B}
denote the mapping of a single evolution step of
\eta
according to the Markov chain (
2.30
). Then, using the definition of
D^{(k)}
and Equation (
2.28
),

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{k}^{B}(\eta)=

\displaystyle D(\eta^{k},B)=D(D^{(k)}(Z^{k},\ldots,Z^{1}),B^{1})=D^{(k+1)}(Z^{k},\ldots,Z^{1},B^{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle D^{(k)}(D(Z^{k},B^{k}),D(Z^{k-1},B^{k-1}),\ldots,D(Z^{1},B^{1}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle D^{(k)}(\mathcal{S}_{k}^{B}(\mathbf{Z}),\mathcal{S}_{k-1}^{B}(\mathbf{Z}),\ldots,\mathcal{S}_{1}^{B}(\mathbf{Z}))=\mathcal{D}_{k}^{(n)}(\mathcal{S}^{B}(\mathbf{Z})).

Hence,
\mathcal{T}^{B}(\eta)=\mathcal{D}^{(n)}(\mathcal{S}^{B}(\mathbf{Z}))
. Since
\eta=\mathcal{D}^{(n)}(\mathbf{Z})
, we have that

\mathcal{T}^{B}(\mathcal{D}^{(n)}(\mathbf{Z}))=\mathcal{D}^{(n)}(\mathcal{S}^{B}(\mathbf{Z})).

By Theorem
2.3.14
,
\mathcal{S}^{B}(\mathbf{Z})\overset{d}{=}\mathbf{Z}\sim\nu_{\sigma}^{\bar{\xi}}
. Therefore,
\mathcal{T}^{B}(\eta)\overset{d}{=}\mathcal{D}^{(n)}(\mathbf{Z})\sim\mu_{\sigma}^{\bar{\xi}}
.
∎

2.3.3
A triangular array representation of the map
\mathcal{D}^{(n)}

Here, we give a triangular array construction of the mapping
\mathcal{D}^{(n)}
of (
2.8
). This construction has previously appeared for a discrete prelimiting analogue of the map in
[
60
]
and
[
31
]
. For
(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{n})\in\mathcal{Y}_{n}^{\mathbb{R}}
, define the following triangular array
\{\eta^{i,j},\zeta^{i,j}:1\leq j\leq i\leq n\}
inductively as follows: set
\eta^{1,1}=\zeta^{1,1}=Z^{1}
, and assuming that
\eta^{i-1,j}
and
\zeta^{i-1,j}
have been defined for
j\in\{1,\ldots,i-1\}
,

\displaystyle\eta^{i,1}

\displaystyle=Z^{i}

(2.31)

\displaystyle\eta^{i,j}

\displaystyle=D(\eta^{i,j-1},\zeta^{i-1,j-1}),\qquad j\in\{2,\ldots,i\}

\displaystyle\zeta^{i,j-1}

\displaystyle=R(\eta^{i,j-1},\zeta^{i-1,j-1}),\qquad j\in\{2,\ldots,i\}

\displaystyle\zeta^{i,i}

\displaystyle=\eta^{i,i}.

To show this construction is well-defined, we need to show that for
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, and
j\in\{2,\ldots,i\}
,

\lim_{x\to-\infty}[\eta^{i,j-1}(x)-\zeta^{i-1,j-1}(x)]=-\infty.

The following lemma ensures this is the case.

Lemma 2.3.16
.

Let
(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{n})\in\mathcal{Y}_{n}^{\mathbb{R}}
, and defined the triangular array as in (
2.31
). Let
\xi_{1}<\cdots<\xi_{n}
denote the asymptotic drifts:

\xi_{i}=\lim_{x\to-\infty}\frac{Z^{i}(x)}{x}.

Then, for each
1\leq j\leq i\leq n
,

\lim_{x\to-\infty}\frac{\eta^{i,j}(x)}{x}=\xi_{i},\qquad\text{and}\lim_{x\to-\infty}\frac{\zeta^{i,j}(x)}{x}=\xi_{j}.

If in addition, the following limits exist:

\lim_{x\to+\infty}\frac{Z^{i}(x)}{x}=\widetilde{\xi}_{i}

with
\widetilde{\xi}^{1}<\cdots<\widetilde{\xi}^{n}
, then for each
1\leq j\leq i\leq n
,

\lim_{x\to+\infty}\frac{\eta^{i,j}(x)}{x}=\widetilde{\xi}_{i},\qquad\text{and}\lim_{x\to+\infty}\frac{\zeta^{i,j}(x)}{x}=\widetilde{\xi}_{j}.

Proof.

This follows by Lemma
2.3.10
and induction.
∎

We prove the following facts about this triangular array. Given the preceding lemmas about the queuing mappings (which require different proofs than in the discrete case), the proofs of Lemmas
2.3.17
and
2.3.20
follow the same procedure as in
[
60
]
. We give full proofs here for completeness and to give clarity to which of the prior results are used. Lemma
2.3.18
does not have an analogue in
[
60
]
, although I expect one to hold in the discrete case. The basic building block of the proof is the bijection in Lemma
2.3.8
. The analogue of the mapping
(B,Z)\mapsto(R(Z,B),D(Z,B))
in the discrete case is also a bijection on the appropriate space, as shown to me in unpublished notes of Timo Seppäläinen. However, the proof of Lemma
2.3.8
requires different techniques than in the discrete case.

Lemma 2.3.17
.

Let
(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{n})\in\mathcal{Y}_{n}^{\mathbb{R}}
. Let
(\widetilde{\eta}^{1},\ldots,\widetilde{\eta}^{n})=\mathcal{D}^{(n)}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{n})
as defined in (
2.8
). Then, for
i=1,\ldots,n
,
\widetilde{\eta}^{i}=\eta^{i,i}
, where
\eta^{i,i}
is defined in (
2.31
).

Proof.

We first recall that
(\eta^{1,1},\eta^{2,1},\ldots,\eta^{n,1})=(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{n})
by definition. Thus, the
i=1
case is immediate. For
i=2
,

\eta^{2,2}=D(\eta^{2,1},\zeta^{1,1})=D(\eta^{2,1},\eta^{1,1})=D(Z^{2},Z^{1})=\widetilde{\eta}^{2}.

Now, assume that
i\geq 3
. For
m=1,2,3,\ldots,i-1
, it follows from the iterative definition that

\displaystyle(\eta^{i,m},\eta^{i-1,m},\ldots,\eta^{m+1,m},\eta^{m,m})

(2.32)

\displaystyle=\bigl(D(\eta^{i,m-1},\zeta^{i-1,m-1}),D(\eta^{i-1,m-1},\zeta^{i-2,m-1}),\ldots,D(\eta^{m,m-1},\zeta^{m-1,m-1})\bigr).

By definition, we also have
\zeta^{m-1,m-1}=\eta^{m-1,m-1}
and
\zeta^{j,m-1}=R(\eta^{j,m-1},\zeta^{j-1,m-1})
for
j=m,\ldots,i-1
. Substituting this into (
2.32
), when
m=1,\ldots,i-2
,

\displaystyle\quad\;D^{(i-m+1)}(\eta^{i,m},\eta^{i-1,m},\ldots,\eta^{m,m})

\displaystyle=D^{(i-m+1)}(D(\eta^{i,m-1},\zeta^{i-1,m-1}),D(\eta^{i-1,m-1},\zeta^{i-2,m-1}),\ldots,D(\eta^{m,m-1},\zeta^{m-1,m-1})\bigr)

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{intertwining}}{=}D^{(i-m+2)}(\eta^{i,m-1},\eta^{i-1,m-1},\ldots,\eta^{m-1,m-1}).

(2.33)

Then, using the definition of
D^{(i)}
(
2.5
) and applying (
2.33
) inductively for
m=1,\ldots,i-1
,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\eta}^{i}

\displaystyle=D^{(i)}(I^{1},\ldots,I^{1})=D^{(i)}(\eta^{i,1},\eta^{i-1,1}\ldots,\eta^{i,1})=D^{(i-1)}(\eta^{i,2},\eta^{i-1,2},\ldots,\eta^{2,2})

\displaystyle=\cdots=D^{(2)}(\eta^{i,i-1},\eta^{i-1,i-1})=D(\eta^{i,i-1},\zeta^{i-1,i-1})=\eta^{i,i},

where in the last two steps, we simply used the definitions (
2.31
).
∎

Lemma 2.3.18
.

Recall the mapping
\mathfrak{s}f(x)=f(-x)
. Let
(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{n})\in\mathcal{Y}_{n}^{\mathbb{R}}
, and assume also that
(\mathfrak{s}Z^{n},\mathfrak{s}Z^{n-1},\ldots,\mathfrak{s}Z^{1})\in\mathcal{Y}_{n}^{\mathbb{R}}
,. This means that for each
1\leq i\leq n
,
\lim_{x\to+\infty}x^{-1}Z^{i}(x)
exists, and

\lim_{x\to+\infty}\frac{Z^{i}(x)}{x}>\lim_{x\to+\infty}\frac{Z^{i-1}(x)}{x}.

Consider the triangular array
\{\eta^{i,j},\zeta^{i,j}:1\leq j\leq i\leq n\}
defined in (
2.31
). For
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
and
j\in\{1,\ldots,i\}
, define

Y^{i,j}=\mathfrak{s}\zeta^{i,i-j+1}.

(2.34)

Then, for
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
and
j\in\{1,\ldots,i\}
,

D^{(j)}(Y^{i,j},\ldots,Y^{i,1})=\mathfrak{s}D^{(i-j+1)}(Z^{i-j+1},\ldots,Z^{1}).

(2.35)

Remark 2.3.19
.

Lemma
2.3.18
shows that we can reverse the order of the queuing mappings. In the
i=n
case,
Y^{n,1}
is the reflected version of the last component of
\mathcal{D}^{(n)}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{n})
by Lemma
2.3.17
. This now becomes the first component of the input into the mapping
\mathcal{D}^{(n)}
. This is used later, along with the distributional invariance in Lemma
2.3.20
to show reflection invariance of the measures
\mu_{\sigma}^{\bar{\xi}}
(Lemma
2.3.21
(iv)
)

Proof.

The preservation of limits in Lemma
2.3.16
ensures that all these operations are well-defined. In this proof, we several times will use the
k=1
case of Lemma
2.3.12
, which for
r\geq 3
, we write as

D^{(r)}(Z^{r},\ldots,Z^{1})=D(D^{(r-1)}(Z^{r},\ldots,Z^{3},R(Z^{2},Z^{1})),D(Z^{2},Z^{1})).

(2.36)

We prove this by induction. For all
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, the
j=1
case follows from we the identity

D^{(1)}(Y^{i,1})=Y^{i,1}=\mathfrak{s}\zeta^{i,i}=\mathfrak{s}D^{(i)}(Z^{i},\ldots,Z^{1}),

where the last line holds by Lemma
2.3.17
, and the others are just the definitions. In particular, the
i=j=1
case of (
2.35
) holds. We turn to the
j=2
case. Recall the definitions (
2.31
) and (
2.34
):

\displaystyle Y^{i,1}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{s}\zeta^{i,i}=\mathfrak{s}D(\eta^{i,i-1},\zeta^{i-1,i-1}),

\displaystyle Y^{i,2}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{s}\zeta^{i,i-1}=\mathfrak{s}R(\eta^{i,i-1},\zeta^{i-1,i-1}).

Lemma
2.3.8
and the definition
\eta^{i-1,i-1}=\zeta^{i-1,i-1}
implies now that

D(Y^{i,2},Y^{i,1})=\mathfrak{s}\zeta^{i-1,i-1}=\mathfrak{s}\eta^{i-1,i-1},\qquad\text{and}\qquad R(Y^{i,2},Y^{i,1})=\mathfrak{s}\eta^{i,i-1}.

(2.37)

Since Lemma
2.3.17
states that
\eta^{i-1,i-1}=D^{(i-1)}(Z^{i-1},\ldots,Z^{1})
, the first equality of (
2.37
) shows the
j=2
case of (
2.35
).

Now, assume that for some
i\geq 2
that (
2.35
) holds for
i-1
and all
j\in\{1,\ldots,i-1\}
. We showed already that the
j=1,2
case holds for general
i
, so let
j\geq 3
. We show that the
i,j
case holds. By (
2.36
) and (
2.37
),

\displaystyle D^{(j)}(Y^{i,j},\ldots,Y^{i,1})

\displaystyle=D(D^{(j-1)}(Y^{i,j},\ldots,Y^{i,3},R(Y^{i,2},Y^{i,1})),D(Y^{i,2},Y^{i,1}))

(2.38)

\displaystyle=D(D^{(j-1)}(Y^{i,j},\ldots,Y^{i,3},\mathfrak{s}\eta^{i,i-1}),\mathfrak{s}\zeta^{i-1,i-1}).

Now, we prove, in a second layer of induction that, for
m=1,\ldots,j-2
,

\displaystyle D^{(j)}(Y^{i,j},\ldots,Y^{i,1})

(2.39)

\displaystyle=D(D(\cdots(D(D^{(j-m)}(Y^{i,j},\ldots,Y^{i,m+2},\mathfrak{s}\eta^{i,i-m}),\mathfrak{s}\zeta^{i-1,i-m}),\mathfrak{s}\zeta^{i-1,i-m+1}),\cdots),\mathfrak{s}\zeta^{i-1,i-1}).

Equation (
2.38
) proves the
m=1
case of (
2.39
). Now, assume that (
2.39
) holds for some
m\in 1,\ldots,j-3
. Then, using (
2.36
),

\displaystyle D^{(j-m)}(Y^{i,j},\ldots,Y^{i,m+2},\mathfrak{s}\eta^{i,i-m})

(2.40)

\displaystyle=D(D^{(j-(m+1))}(Y^{i,j},\ldots,Y^{i,m+3},R(Y^{i,m+2},\mathfrak{s}\eta^{i,i-m})),D(Y^{i,m+2},\mathfrak{s}\eta^{i,i-m})).

Again, recall the definitions (
2.31
) and (
2.34
):

\displaystyle\mathfrak{s}\eta^{i,i-m}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{s}D(\eta^{i,i-m-1},\zeta^{i-1,i-m-1}),

\displaystyle Y^{i,m+2}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{s}\zeta^{i,i-m-1}=\mathfrak{s}R(\eta^{i,i-m-1},\zeta^{i-1,i-m-1}).

Then, Lemma
2.3.8
gives us

D(Y^{i,m+2},\mathfrak{s}\eta^{i,i-m})=\mathfrak{s}\zeta^{i-1,i-m-1},\qquad\text{and}\qquad R(Y^{i,m+2},\mathfrak{s}\eta^{i,i-m})=\mathfrak{s}\eta^{i,i-m-1}.

(2.41)

Substituting (
2.41
) into (
2.40
) and then (
2.40
) into (
2.39
) completes the second layer of induction. We now return to the original layer of induction. We consider (
2.39
) in the extremal case
m=j-2
. Recalling
D^{(2)}=D
(
2.5
), equation (
2.41
) tells us that

D^{(2)}(Y^{i,j},\mathfrak{s}\eta^{i,i-(j-2)})=\mathfrak{s}\zeta^{i-1,i-(j-1)}

Substituting this into the
m=j-2
case of (
2.38
), and using the iterative definition of
D^{(j)}
(
2.5
), we obtain

\displaystyle\quad\;D^{(j)}(Y^{i,j},\ldots,Y^{i,1})

\displaystyle=D(D(\cdots(D(\mathfrak{s}\zeta^{i-1,i-(j-1)},\mathfrak{s}\zeta^{i-1,i-(j-2)}),\zeta^{i-1,i-(j-3)}),\cdots),\mathfrak{s}\zeta^{i-1,i-1})

\displaystyle=D^{(j-1)}(\mathfrak{s}\zeta^{i-1,i-(j-1)},\mathfrak{s}\zeta^{i-1,i-(j-2)},\ldots,\mathfrak{s}\zeta^{i-1,i-1})

\displaystyle=D^{(j-1)}(Y^{i-1,j-1},\ldots,Y^{i-1,1}).

By the induction assumption that (
2.35
) holds for
i-1
and
j-1
, this equals

\mathfrak{s}D^{(i-1-(j-1)+1)}(Z^{i-1-(j-1)+1},\ldots,Z^{1})=\mathfrak{s}D^{(i-j+1)}(Z^{i-j+1},\ldots,Z^{1}),

and this gives exactly (
2.35
), completing the induction.
∎

Lemma 2.3.20
.

For
\sigma>0
and
\xi_{1}<\cdots<\xi_{n}
, assume that
(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{n})
has distribution
\nu_{\sigma}^{(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{n})}
. Let the triangular array
\{\eta^{i,j},\zeta^{i,j}:1\leq j\leq i\leq n\}
be defined as in (
2.31
). Then, for each
1\leq i,j\leq n
, the random vector
(\eta^{j,j},\eta^{j+1,j},\ldots,\eta^{n,j})
has distribution
\nu_{\sigma}^{(\xi_{j},\ldots,\xi_{n})}
, and the random vector
(\zeta^{i,1},\ldots,\zeta^{i,i})
has distribution
\nu_{\sigma}^{(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{i})}

Proof.

By definition,
(\eta^{1,1},\ldots,\eta^{n,1})=(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{n})
, which has distribution
\sim\nu_{\sigma}^{(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{n})}
by assumption. Recall that this means that the
Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{n}
are mutually independent two-sided Brownian motions, each with diffusivity
\sigma
, and with drifts
\sigma^{2}\xi_{1},\ldots,\sigma^{2}\xi_{n}
. We assume, by way of induction, that, for some
j\in\{2,\ldots,n-1\}
,

(\eta^{j-1,j-1},\eta^{j,j-1},\ldots,\eta^{n,j-1})\sim\nu_{\sigma}^{(\xi_{j-1},\ldots,\xi_{n})}.

By definition (
2.31
), for
m\in\{0,\ldots,n-j\}
,

\eta^{j+m,j}=D(\eta^{j+m,j-1},\zeta^{j+m-1,j-1}),\qquad\text{and}\qquad\zeta^{j+m,j-1}=R(\eta^{j+m,j-1},\zeta^{j+m-1,j-1}).

From this, we see that we obtain the random vector
(\eta^{j,j},\ldots,\eta^{n,j})
from
(\eta^{j,j-1},\ldots,\eta^{n,j-1})
via a single iteration of the multiline process (
2.29
), where
\zeta^{j-1,j-1}=\eta^{j-1,j-1}
is the driving Brownian motion. Theorem
2.3.14
now implies that
(\eta^{j,j},\ldots,\eta^{n,j})\sim\nu_{\sigma}^{(\xi_{j},\ldots,\xi_{n})}
.

Next, we prove
(\zeta^{i,1},\ldots,\zeta^{i,i})\sim\nu_{\sigma}^{(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{i})}
. To start the induction, note that
\zeta^{1,1}=Z^{1}\sim\nu_{\sigma}^{(\xi_{1})}
. Now, assume that, for some
i\in\{2,\ldots,n-1\}
,

(\zeta^{i-1,1},\zeta^{i-1,2},\ldots,\zeta^{i-1,i-1})\sim\nu_{\sigma}^{(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{i})}.

We now show via a second layer of induction that, for
m\in\{1,\ldots,i\}
,

(\zeta^{i,1},\ldots,\zeta^{i,m-1},\zeta^{i-1,m},\ldots,\zeta^{i-1,i-1},\eta^{i,m})\sim\nu_{\sigma}^{(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{m-1},\xi_{m},\ldots,\xi_{i-1},\xi_{i})}.

(2.42)

In the
m=i
case of (
2.42
), since
\eta^{i,i}=\zeta^{i,i}
, this will complete the first layer of induction.

By construction of the triangular array (
2.31
),
(\zeta^{i-1,1},\zeta^{i-1,2},\ldots,\zeta^{i-1,i-1})
is a function of
(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{i-1})
and is therefore independent of
Z^{i}=\eta^{i,1}\sim\nu_{\sigma}^{(\xi_{i})}
. This gives the
m=1
case of (
2.42
). Now, assume that (
2.42
) holds for some
m\in\{1,\ldots,i-1\}
. By definition,

\eta^{i,m+1}=D(\eta^{i,m},\zeta^{i-1,m}),\qquad\text{and}\qquad\zeta^{i,m}=R(\eta^{i,m},\zeta^{i-1,m}).

By Theorem
2.2.2
,
\zeta^{i,m}
and
\eta^{i,m+1}
are independent two-sided Brownian motions with diffusivity
\sigma
and drifts
\sigma^{2}\xi_{m}
and
\sigma^{2}\xi_{i}
, respectively. Substituting the mapping of
(\zeta^{i-1,m},\eta^{i,m})\mapsto(\zeta^{i,m},\eta^{i,m+1})
into (
2.42
) completes the inductive step.
∎

2.3.4
Distributional invariances of the queuing measures

Lemma 2.3.21
.

Let
\sigma>0
and
\bar{\xi}=(\xi_{1}<\cdots<\xi_{n})
. Let
(\eta^{1},\ldots,\eta^{n})\sim\mu_{\sigma}^{\bar{\xi}}
. The following hold.

(i)

(Shift invariance) For
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

\{(\eta^{1}(x,x+y),\ldots,\eta^{n}(x,x+y)):y\in\mathbb{R}\}\sim\mu_{\sigma}^{\bar{\xi}}.

(ii)

(Scaling relations) Let and
b,c>0
, and
\nu\in\mathbb{R}
. Then,

\{(b\eta^{1}(c^{2}y)-(bc\sigma)^{2}\nu y,\ldots,b\eta^{n}(c^{2}y)-(bc\sigma)^{2}\nu y):y\in\mathbb{R}\}\sim\mu_{bc\sigma}^{(\xi_{1}/b-\nu,\ldots,\xi_{n}/b-\nu)}.

(iii)

(Consistency) Any subsequence
(\eta^{j_{1}},\ldots,\eta^{j_{k}})
has distribution
\mu_{\sigma}^{(\xi_{j_{1}},\ldots,\xi_{j_{k}})}
.

(iv)

(Reflection invariance)
(\mathfrak{s}\eta^{n},\ldots,\mathfrak{s}\eta^{1})\sim\mu_{\sigma}^{(-\xi_{n},\ldots,-\xi_{1})}
.

(v)

(Weak continuity)
Let
\bar{\xi}^{k}=(\xi_{1}^{k},\xi_{2}^{k},\ldots,\xi_{n}^{k})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a sequence converging to
\bar{\xi}
and let
\sigma^{k}
be a sequence converging to
\sigma>0
. Then,
\mu_{\sigma_{k}}^{\bar{\xi}^{k}}\rightarrow\mu_{\sigma}^{\bar{\xi}}
weakly, as probability measures on
\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\mathbb{R}}
.

Proof of Lemma
2.3.21
.

For all items of this proof, we set

\eta=(\eta^{1},\ldots,\eta^{n})=\mathcal{D}^{(n)}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{n}),

where
Z^{i}
are independent Brownian motions with diffusivity
\sigma
and drifts
\sigma^{2}\xi_{i}
. Then,

\eta\sim\mu_{\sigma}^{\bar{\xi}}
by definition (
2.9
). Recall the
j
th component of
\mathcal{D}^{(n)}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{n})

is
D^{(j)}(Z^{j},\ldots,Z^{1})
(
2.8
).

We also make heavy use of Lemma
2.3.4
, which states

\displaystyle D^{(j)}(Z^{j},\ldots,Z^{1})(y)

\displaystyle=Z^{1}(y)+\sup_{-\infty<x_{j-1}\leq x_{n-2}\leq\cdots\leq x_{1}\leq y}\Big\{\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}Z^{i+1}(x_{i})-Z^{i}(x_{i})\Big\}

(2.43)

\displaystyle-\sup_{-\infty<x_{j-1}\leq x_{j-2}\leq\cdots\leq x_{1}\leq 0}\Big\{\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}Z^{i+1}(x_{i})-Z^{i}(x_{i})\Big\}

Item
(i)
:

From (
2.43
), observe that

\displaystyle\quad\;D^{(j)}(Z^{j},\ldots,Z^{1})(x,x+y)

\displaystyle=Z^{1}(x,x+y)+\sup_{-\infty<x_{j-1}\leq x_{j-2}\leq\cdots\leq x_{1}\leq x+y}\Big\{\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}Z^{i+1}(x_{i})-Z^{i}(x_{i})\Big\}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\sup_{-\infty<x_{j-1}\leq x_{j-2}\leq\cdots\leq x_{1}\leq x}\Big\{\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}Z^{i+1}(x_{i})-Z^{i}(x_{i})\Big\}

\displaystyle=Z^{1}(x,x+y)+\sup_{-\infty<x_{j-1}\leq x_{j-2}\leq\cdots\leq x_{1}\leq y}\Big\{\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}Z^{i+1}(x_{i}+x)-Z^{i}(x_{i}+x)\Big\}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\sup_{-\infty<x_{j-1}\leq x_{j-2}\leq\cdots\leq x_{1}\leq 0}\Big\{\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}Z^{i+1}(x_{i}+x)-Z^{i}(x_{i}+x)\Big\}

\displaystyle=Z^{1}(x,x+y)+\sup_{-\infty<x_{j-1}\leq x_{j-2}\leq\cdots\leq x_{1}\leq y}\Big\{\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}Z^{i+1}(x,x_{i}+x)-Z^{i}(x,x_{i}+x)\Big\}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\sup_{-\infty<x_{j-1}\leq x_{j-2}\leq\cdots\leq x_{1}\leq 0}\Big\{\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}Z^{i+1}(x,x_{i}+x)-Z^{i}(x,x_{i}+x)\Big\}

\displaystyle=D^{(j)}(Z^{j}(x,x+{\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt}),\ldots,Z^{1}(x,x+{\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt}))(y)

where the penultimate step follows by adding and subtracting
\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}[Z^{i}(x)-Z^{i+1}(x)]
. The desired result follows because the law of
(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{n})
is preserved under shifts.

Item
(ii)

From (
2.43
),

\displaystyle\quad\;bD^{(j)}(Z^{j},\ldots,Z^{1})(c^{2}y)-(bc\sigma)^{2}\nu y

\displaystyle=bZ^{1}(c^{2}y)-(bc\sigma)^{2}\nu y+\sup_{-\infty<x_{j-1}\leq x_{j-2}\leq\cdots\leq x_{1}\leq c^{2}y}\Big\{\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}bZ^{i+1}(x_{i})-bZ^{i}(x_{i})\Big\}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\sup_{-\infty<x_{j-1}\leq x_{j-2}\leq\cdots\leq x_{1}\leq 0}\Big\{\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}bZ^{i+1}(x_{i})-bZ^{i}(x_{i})\Big\}

\displaystyle=bZ^{1}(c^{2}y)-(bc\sigma)^{2}\nu y+\sup_{-\infty<x_{j-1}\leq x_{j-2}\leq\cdots\leq x_{1}\leq y}\Big\{\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}bZ^{i+1}(c^{2}x_{i})-bZ^{i}(c^{2}x_{i})\Big\}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\sup_{-\infty<x_{j-1}\leq x_{j-2}\leq\cdots\leq x_{1}\leq 0}\Big\{\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}bZ^{i+1}(c^{2}x_{i})-bZ^{i}(c^{2}x_{i})\Big\}

\displaystyle=bZ^{1}(c^{2}y)-(bc\sigma)^{2}\nu y

\displaystyle+\sup_{-\infty<x_{j-1}\leq x_{j-2}\leq\cdots\leq x_{1}\leq y}\Big\{\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}bZ^{i+1}(c^{2}x_{i})-(bc\sigma)^{2}\nu x_{i}-(bZ^{i}(c^{2}x_{i})-(bc\sigma)^{2}\nu x_{i})\Big\}

\displaystyle-\sup_{-\infty<x_{j-1}\leq x_{j-2}\leq\cdots\leq x_{1}\leq 0}\Big\{\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}bZ^{i+1}(c^{2}x_{i})-(bc\sigma)^{2}\nu x_{i}-(bZ^{i}(c^{2}x_{i})-(bc\sigma)^{2}\nu x_{i})\Big\}

\displaystyle=D^{(j)}(\widetilde{Z}^{j},\ldots,\widetilde{Z}^{1}),

where
\widetilde{Z}^{i}(x)=bZ^{i}(c^{2}x)-(bc\sigma)^{2}\nu x
. Because the
\widetilde{Z}^{i}
are independent two-sided Brownian motions with diffusivity
bc\sigma
and drift
bc^{2}\sigma^{2}\xi_{i}-(bc\sigma)^{2}\nu=(bc\sigma)^{2}(\xi_{i}/b-\nu)
, we have

(\widetilde{Z}^{1},\ldots,\widetilde{Z}^{j})\sim\mu_{bc\sigma}^{\xi_{1}/b-\nu,\ldots,\xi_{n}/b-\nu}.

Item
(iii)
:
It suffices to show that, for
1\leq i\leq n
,

(\eta^{1},\ldots,\eta^{i-1},\eta^{i+1},\ldots,\eta^{n})\sim\mu_{\sigma}^{\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{i-1},\xi_{i+1},\ldots,\xi_{n}}.

For
i=n
, the statement is immediate from the definition of the map
\mathcal{D}^{(n)}
. Next, we show the case
i=1
. For
2\leq j\leq n
, we use (
2.28
) to write

D^{(j)}(Z^{j},\ldots,Z^{1})=D^{(j-1)}(D(Z^{j},\widetilde{Z}^{j-1}),\ldots,D(Z^{3},\widetilde{Z}^{2}),D(Z^{2},\widetilde{Z}^{1})),

where
\widetilde{Z}^{1}=Z^{1}
, and for
i>1
,
\widetilde{Z}^{i}=R(Z^{i},\widetilde{Z}^{i-1})
. Then,
(\eta^{2},\ldots,\eta^{n})=\mathcal{D}^{(n-1)}(\hat{Z}^{2},\ldots,\hat{Z}^{n})
, where
\hat{Z}^{i}=D(Z^{i},\widetilde{Z}^{i-1})
for
2\leq i\leq n
. By Theorem
2.3.14
,
\hat{Z}^{2},\ldots,\hat{Z}^{n}
are independent Brownian motions, each with diffusivity
\sigma
and with drifts
\sigma^{2}\xi_{2},\ldots,\sigma^{2}\xi_{n}
, so
this completes the proof of the
i=1
case. By definition of
D^{(j)}
(
2.5
), for
i<j\leq n
,

D^{(j)}(Z^{j},\ldots,Z^{1})=D(D(\cdots D(D^{(j-i+1)}(Z^{j},\ldots,Z^{i}),Z^{i-1}),\ldots,Z^{2}),Z^{1}).

(2.44)

Similarly as in the
i=1
case, we apply (
2.28
) to get that

\displaystyle\;\;\;D^{(j-i+1)}(Z^{j},\ldots,Z^{i})

\displaystyle=D^{(j-i)}(D(Z^{j},\widetilde{Z}^{j-1}),\ldots,D(Z^{i+1},\widetilde{Z}^{i}))=D^{(j-i)}(\hat{Z}^{j},\ldots,\hat{Z}^{i+1}),

(2.45)

where,
\widetilde{Z}^{i}=Z^{i}
, and for
j>i
,
\widetilde{Z}^{j}=R(Z^{j},\widetilde{Z}^{j-1})
and
\hat{Z}^{j}=D(Z^{j},\widetilde{Z}^{j-1})
.
Then, by (
2.44
) and (
2.3.4
), for
i<j\leq n
,

D^{(j)}(Z^{j},\ldots,Z^{1})=D^{(j-1)}(\hat{Z}^{j},\ldots,\hat{Z}^{i+1},Z^{i-1},\ldots,Z^{1}),

and so

(\eta^{1},\ldots,\eta^{i-1},\eta^{i+1},\ldots,\eta^{n})=\mathcal{D}^{(n-1)}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{i-1},\hat{Z}^{i+1},\ldots,\hat{Z}^{n}).

(2.46)

By Theorem
2.3.14
,
\hat{Z}^{i+1},\ldots,\hat{Z}^{n}
are independent Brownian motions with diffusivity
\sigma
and drifts
\sigma^{2}\xi_{i+1},\ldots,\sigma^{2}\xi_{n}
. Since these are functions of
Z^{i},\ldots,Z^{n}
, the functions
Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{i-1},\hat{Z}^{i+1},\ldots,\hat{Z}^{j}
are independent as well, and by (
2.46
),

(\eta^{1},\ldots,\eta^{i-1},\eta^{i+1},\ldots,\eta^{n})\sim\mu_{\sigma}^{(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{i-1},\xi_{i+1},\ldots,\xi_{n})}.

Item
(iv)
:
For
(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{n})
given, let the triangular array
\{\eta^{i,j},\zeta^{i,j}:1\leq j\leq i\leq n\}
be defined by (
2.31
). For
1\leq j\leq n
, set
Y^{j}=\mathfrak{s}\zeta^{n-j+1}
. Then, by Lemma
2.3.20
,

(Y^{1},\ldots,Y^{n})\sim\nu_{\sigma}^{(-\xi_{n},\ldots,-\xi_{1})}.

Note that the limits
\lim_{x\to\pm\infty}x^{-1}Z^{i}(x)
exist and equal
\sigma^{2}\xi_{i}
almost surely. Then, we may apply the
i=n
case of Lemma
2.3.18
, which states, for
1\leq j\leq n
,

D^{(j)}(Z^{j},\ldots,Z^{1})=\mathfrak{s}\eta^{(n-j+1)}.

Hence,

(\mathfrak{s}\eta^{n},\ldots,\mathfrak{s}\eta^{1})=\mathcal{D}^{(n)}(Y^{1},\ldots,Y^{n})\sim\mu_{\sigma}^{(-\xi_{n},\ldots,-\xi_{1})}.

Item
(v)

We show the existence of
\eta_{k}\sim\mu_{\sigma_{k}}^{\bar{\xi}^{k}}
such that, almost surely, for
1\leq i\leq n
,
\eta_{k}^{i}\rightarrow\eta^{i}
, uniformly on compact sets. For the sequence
\mathbf{Z}
defined at the beginning of the proof, define
Z_{k}^{i}(x)=\frac{\sigma_{k}}{\sigma}Z^{i}(x)+(\sigma_{k}^{2}\xi_{i}^{k}-\sigma\sigma_{k}\xi_{i})x
. Then,
(Z_{k}^{1},Z_{k}^{2},\ldots,Z_{k}^{n})\sim\nu_{\sigma_{k}}^{\xi^{k}}
. Set
\eta=\mathcal{D}^{(n)}(Z)\sim\mu_{\sigma}^{\bar{\xi}}
and
\eta_{k}=\mathcal{D}^{(n)}(Z_{k})
. By construction,
Z_{k}\rightarrow Z^{i}
, uniformly on compact sets. thus, the convergence
\eta_{k}^{1}=Z_{k}^{1}\rightarrow Z^{1}=\eta^{1}
is immediate. Further, since
Z^{i}
satisfies the almost sure asymptotic

\lim_{x\to-\infty}\frac{Z^{i}(x)}{x}=\sigma^{2}\xi_{i},

for
1\leq i\leq n
,
Z_{k}^{i}
satisfies

\limsup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\rightarrow-\infty\\
k\rightarrow\infty\end{subarray}}\Bigl|\frac{1}{x}Z_{k}^{i}(x)-\sigma^{2}\xi_{i}\Bigr|=0.

By Lemma
2.3.5
,
\eta_{k}^{2}=D(Z_{k}^{2},Z_{k}^{1})
converges to
\eta^{2}=D(Z^{2},Z^{1})
uniformly on compact sets, and

\limsup_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\rightarrow-\infty\\
k\rightarrow\infty\end{subarray}}\Bigl|\frac{1}{x}\eta_{k}^{2}(x)-\sigma^{2}\xi_{2}\Bigr|=0.

Now, assume by induction that for
i\geq 2
,
\eta_{k}^{i}=D^{(i)}(Z_{k}^{i},\ldots,Z_{k}^{1})
converges uniformly on compact sets to
\eta^{i}
and that

\limsup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\rightarrow-\infty\\
k\rightarrow\infty\end{subarray}}\Bigl|\frac{1}{x}\eta_{k}^{i}(x)-\sigma^{2}\xi_{i}\Bigr|=0.

Then, by shifting indices and setting
\widetilde{\eta}_{k}^{i}=D^{(i)}(Z_{k}^{i+1},\ldots,Z_{k}^{2})
and
\widetilde{\eta}^{i}=D^{(i)}(Z^{i+1},\ldots,Z^{2})
, it also holds by induction that
\widetilde{\eta}_{k}^{i}
converges uniformly on compact sets to
\widetilde{\eta}^{i}
, and

\limsup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\rightarrow-\infty\\
k\rightarrow\infty\end{subarray}}\Bigl|\frac{1}{x}\widetilde{\eta}_{k}^{i}(x)-\sigma^{2}\xi_{i}\Bigr|=0.

By definition of
D^{(i+1)}
(
2.5
) and the
i=2
case,

\eta_{k}^{i+1}=D^{(i+1)}(Z_{k}^{i+1},\ldots,Z_{k}^{1})=D(\widetilde{\eta}_{k}^{i},Z_{k}^{1})\to D(\widetilde{\eta}^{i},Z^{1})=D^{(i+1)}(Z^{i+1},\ldots,Z^{1})=\eta^{i+1},

where the convergence is almost sure, uniformly on compact sets. The
i=2
case also guarantees

\limsup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\rightarrow-\infty\\
k\rightarrow\infty\end{subarray}}\Bigl|\frac{1}{x}\eta_{k}^{i+1}(x)-\sigma^{2}\xi_{i+1}\Bigr|=0,

thereby completing the inductive step.
∎

2.3.5
Proof of Proposition
2.3.1
and Theorem
2.3.2

Proof.

Proof of Proposition
2.3.1

We remind the reader that this proposition shows the existence of the process
G^{\sigma}
, which we call the stationary horizon (SH), with a fixed diffusivity parameter
\sigma>0
.

Lemma
2.3.21
(iii)
establishes that the measures
\mu^{\bar{\xi}}_{\sigma}
are consistent. Hence, for
\bar{\xi}=(\xi_{1}<\cdots<\xi_{n})
, if
(\eta^{1},\ldots,\eta^{n})\sim\mu_{\sigma}^{\bar{\xi}}
, each
\eta^{i}
has distribution
\mu_{\sigma}^{\xi_{i}}
. By definition (
2.9
) (see also (
2.5
)), this is the distribution of
D^{(1)}(Z)=Z
which is a two-sided Brownian motion with diffusion coefficient
\sigma
and drift
\sigma^{2}\xi_{i}
. In particular,
\eta^{i}(0)=0
, almost surely. Kolmogorov’s extension theorem implies the existence of a unique measure
\mu^{\mathbb{Q}}_{\sigma}
on
C(\mathbb{R})^{\mathbb{Q}}=\prod_{\mathbb{Q}}C(\mathbb{R})
under which, for
\{\widetilde{G}_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\mathbb{Q}}\in C(\mathbb{R})^{\mathbb{Q}}

and any increasing finite vector
\bar{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n})\in\mathbb{Q}^{n}
,
(\widetilde{G}_{\alpha},\ldots,\widetilde{G}_{\alpha_{n}})\sim\mu^{\bar{\alpha}}_{\sigma}
. In particular, under
\mu_{\sigma}^{\mathbb{Q}}
, each
\widetilde{G}^{\alpha}
is a Brownian motion with diffusivity
\sigma
and drift
\sigma^{2}\alpha
. Furthermore, the measures
\mu_{\sigma}^{\bar{\xi}}
are supported on the set
\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\mathbb{R}}
(
2.7
), so

\mu^{\mathbb{Q}}_{\sigma}(\widetilde{G}_{\alpha_{1}}\;{\leq}_{\rm inc}\;\widetilde{G}_{\alpha_{2}}\;\;\forall\alpha_{1}<\alpha_{2}\in\mathbb{Q})=1.

(2.47)

Hence, under
\mu^{\mathbb{Q}}_{\sigma}
, on a single event of probability one, for each
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
, the limits

G^{\sigma}_{\xi}(x):=\lim_{\mathbb{Q}\ni\alpha\searrow\xi}\widetilde{G}^{\sigma}_{\alpha}(x)\qquad\text{and}\qquad G^{\sigma}_{\xi-}:=\lim_{\mathbb{Q}\ni\alpha\nearrow\xi}\widetilde{G}_{\alpha}^{\sigma}(x)

(2.48)

exist. Furthermore,

\mu^{\mathbb{Q}}_{\sigma}(G_{\xi}\;{\leq}_{\rm inc}\;\widetilde{G}_{\alpha}\;\;\forall\xi\in\mathbb{R},\alpha\in\mathbb{Q}\text{ with }\alpha>\xi)=1.

(2.49)

Then, on the event of (
2.49
), Lemma
A.1.2
implies that, for
A<a<b<B
,

0\leq\widetilde{G}_{\alpha}(a,b)-G_{\xi}(a,b)\leq\widetilde{G}_{\alpha}(A,B)-G_{\xi}(A,B),

so the convergence is uniform on compact sets. A symmetric argument holds for limits from the left. Monotonicity implies that
\mu^{\mathbb{Q}}_{\sigma}(G_{\xi-}\;{\leq}_{\rm inc}\;\widetilde{G}_{\xi}\;{\leq}_{\rm inc}\;G_{\xi}\;\;\forall\xi\in\mathbb{Q})=1
. Furthermore, by uniform convergence, for each
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
,
G_{\xi-}
and
G_{\xi}
are both Brownian motions with drift
\xi
. Hence,

\mu^{\mathbb{Q}}_{\sigma}(G_{\xi-}(x)=\widetilde{G}_{\xi}(x)=G_{\xi}(x)\;\;\forall x\in\mathbb{Q},\xi\in\mathbb{Q})=1,

but by continuity, we also have

\mu^{\mathbb{Q}}_{\sigma}(G_{\xi-}(x)=\widetilde{G}_{\xi}(x)=G_{\xi}(x)\;\;\forall x\in\mathbb{R},\xi\in\mathbb{Q})=1,

We have now defined a stochastic process
\{G_{\xi}\}_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}}
whose projection to the rationals agrees with the process
\{\widetilde{G}_{\xi}\}_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}}
originally constructed under the measure
\mu_{\sigma}^{\mathbb{Q}}
. Then,
\mu_{\sigma}^{\mathbb{Q}}
- almost surely, for
\xi<\xi_{2}<\xi_{3}
, and rational values
\alpha_{1}<\xi_{1}<\alpha_{2}<\xi_{2}<\xi_{3}<\alpha_{4}
.

G_{\alpha_{1}}\;{\leq}_{\rm inc}\;G_{\xi_{1}}\;{\leq}_{\rm inc}\;G_{\alpha_{2}}\;{\leq}_{\rm inc}\;G_{\xi_{2}}\;{\leq}_{\rm inc}\;G_{\alpha_{3}}\;{\leq}_{\rm inc}\;G_{\xi_{3}}\;{\leq}_{\rm inc}\;G_{\alpha_{4}}.

(2.50)

This implies that, simultaneously for every
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
, the following limits exist, in the sense of uniform convergence on compact sets, and they agree with the limits along rational directions.

G^{\sigma}_{\xi}=\lim_{\alpha\searrow\xi}G^{\sigma}_{\alpha}\qquad\text{and}\qquad G^{\sigma}_{\xi-}=\lim_{\alpha\nearrow\xi}G^{\sigma}_{\alpha}.

Hence, the random process
\{G_{\xi}\}_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}}
almost surely lies in the space
D(\mathbb{R},C(\mathbb{R}))
. Let
\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}
be the pushforward of the measure
\mu_{\sigma}^{\mathbb{Q}}
under the map to
D(\mathbb{R},C(\mathbb{R}))
defined by (
2.48
). Without reference to the measure, we use
\{G^{\sigma}_{\xi}\}_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}}
to denote the process.

We now verify that this process satisfies the items of the theorem. Item
(i)
follows because of the corresponding property for rational parameters and the uniform convergence. Item
(ii)
follows because the finite-dimensional measures for rational directions was defined to be
\mu_{\sigma}^{\bar{\xi}}
. The convergence in (
2.48
) and weak convergence (Lemma
2.3.21
(v)
) extend this property to all
n
-tuples of real directions. The uniqueness then follows because the
\sigma
-algebra on
D(\mathbb{R},C(\mathbb{R}))
is generated by the projection maps. The monotonicity of Item
(iii)
follows from (
2.50
). For Item
(iv)
, for any
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
, both
G_{\xi-}
and
G_{\xi+}
are Brownian motions with diffusivity
\sigma
and drift
\sigma^{2}\xi
, and they are ordered by Item
(iii)
. Thus, with
\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}
-probability one,
G_{\xi-}(x)=G_{\xi}(x)
for all
x\in\mathbb{Q}
. But both functions are continuous, so the equality extends to all
x\in\mathbb{R}
.

We finish by proving Item
(v)
. Since each
G_{\xi}
is a Brownian motion with drift
\sigma^{2}\xi
under
\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}
,

\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}\Bigl(\lim_{|x|\to\infty}\frac{G_{\xi}(x)}{x}=\sigma^{2}\xi\;\;\forall\xi\in\mathbb{Q}\Bigr)=1.

Then, by the monotonicity of Item
(iii)
, and since
G_{\xi}(0,x)=G_{\xi}(x)
, for
x>0
and
\varepsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\sigma^{2}(\xi-\varepsilon)=\lim_{x\to+\infty}\frac{G_{\xi-\varepsilon}(x)}{x}

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{x\to+\infty}\frac{G_{\xi-}(x)}{x}

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{x\to+\infty}\frac{G_{\xi+}(x)}{x}\leq\lim_{x\to+\infty}\frac{G_{\xi+\varepsilon}(x)}{x}=\sigma^{2}(\xi+\varepsilon).

Sending
\varepsilon\searrow 0
completes the proof. The limits as
x\to-\infty
are proved similarly.
∎

Proof of Theorem
2.3.2
.

Here, we verify the distributional invariances of the SH. Items
(i)
,
(ii)
, and
(iv)
follow because
(G^{\sigma}_{\xi_{1}},\ldots,G^{\sigma}_{\xi_{n}})\sim\mu_{\sigma}^{\bar{\xi}}
(Proposition
2.3.1
(ii)
) and from the corresponding invariances of the measures
\mu_{\sigma}^{\bar{\xi}}
(Lemma
2.3.21
(i)
,
(ii)
, and
(iv)
). The increment-stationarity of Item
(iii)
follows now from Item
(ii)
: Setting
c=b=1
,
we obtain

(G^{\sigma}_{\xi_{1}},\ldots,G^{\sigma}_{\xi_{n}})\overset{d}{=}(G^{\sigma}_{\xi_{1}+\xi^{\star}},\ldots,G^{\sigma}_{\xi_{n}+\xi^{\star}}),

and therefore,

(G^{\sigma}_{\xi_{2}}-G^{\sigma}_{\xi_{1}},\ldots,G^{\sigma}_{\xi_{n}}-G^{\sigma}_{\xi_{n-1}})\overset{d}{=}(G^{\sigma}_{\xi_{2}+\xi^{\star}}-G^{\sigma}_{\xi_{1}+\xi^{\star}},\ldots,G^{\sigma}_{\xi_{n}+\xi^{\star}}-G^{\sigma}_{\xi_{n-1}+\xi^{\star}}).\qed

2.4
Distributional calculations for the SH

We now state some explicit distributional calculations for the SH to be proved in Section
2.4.1
.

Theorem 2.4.1
.

Let
a,\xi_{0},\sigma_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
, and
z\geq 0
and
\sigma,d,\xi>0
. Then, the following probabilities are all equal to the quantity
F(z;\xi,\sigma,d)
, which is defined as

\Phi\Bigl(\frac{z-\xi\sigma^{2}d}{\sqrt{2\sigma^{2}d}}\Bigr)+e^{z\xi}\left((1+\xi z+\xi^{2}\sigma^{2}d)\Phi\Bigl(-\frac{z+\xi\sigma^{2}d}{\sqrt{2\sigma^{2}d}}\Bigr)-\xi\sqrt{\frac{\sigma^{2}d}{\pi}}\exp\Bigl(-\frac{(z+\xi\sigma^{2}d)^{2}}{4\sigma^{2}d}\Bigr)\right).

(i)

\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}(\sup_{x,y\in[a,a+d]}|G_{\xi_{0}+\xi}(x,y)-G_{\xi_{0}}(x,y)|\leq z)

(ii)

\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}(G_{\xi_{0}+\xi}(a,a+d)-G_{\xi_{0}}(a,a+d)\leq z)

(iii)

\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}(G_{\xi_{0}}(-d)-G_{\xi_{0}+\xi}(-d)\leq z)

(iv)

\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}(G_{\xi_{0}+\xi}(d)-G_{\xi_{0}}(d)\leq z)

(v)

\mathbb{P}^{1}\Bigl(G_{(\xi_{0}+\xi)\sqrt{\sigma^{2}d}}(1)-G_{\xi_{0}\sqrt{\sigma^{2}d}}(1)\leq\frac{z}{\sqrt{\sigma^{2}d}}\Bigr)
.

Furthermore,

F(0;\xi,\sigma,d)>0,\;\;\forall\xi,d,\sigma>0,\qquad\text{and}\qquad\lim_{\xi\to\infty}F(0;\xi.\sigma,d)=0.

(2.51)

Hence, for
d>0
, the distribution of
G_{\xi_{0}+\xi}(d)-G_{\xi_{0}}(d)
can be written as a mixture of probability measures

p\delta_{0}+(1-p)\pi

where
p=F(0,\xi,\sigma,d)
,
\delta_{0}
is the point mass at
z=0
, and
\pi
is a continuous probability measure on
(0,\infty)
with density

(1-p)^{-1}\Big[\dfrac{\partial}{\partial z}F(z;\xi,\sigma,d)\Big]\mathbf{1}(z>0).

Remark 2.4.2
.

We also compute the Laplace transform/moment generating function of this random variable in (
2.57
).

Corollary 2.4.3
.

The following hold.

(i)

For
\xi_{0},x,y\in\mathbb{R}
, as
|\xi|\to\infty
,
G^{\sigma}_{\xi_{0}+\xi}(x,x+y)-G^{\sigma}_{\xi_{0}}(x,x+y)-\sigma^{2}\xi y
converges in distribution to a Gaussian random variable with mean
0
and variance
2\sigma^{2}|y|
.

(ii)

Let
\xi_{0},x\in\mathbb{R}
. For
\xi>0
, define

\displaystyle S_{x}^{-}(\xi_{0},\xi_{0}+\xi)

\displaystyle=\inf\{y>0:G_{\xi_{0}+\xi}(x,x-y)<G_{\xi_{0}}(x,x-y)\},

(2.52)

\displaystyle S_{x}^{+}(\xi_{0},\xi_{0}+\xi)

\displaystyle=\inf\{y>0:G_{\xi_{0}+\xi}(x,x+y)>G_{\xi_{0}}(x,x+y)\}.

In words,
S_{x}^{-}
and
S_{x}^{+}
are the splitting times of the trajectories
y\mapsto G_{\xi_{0}}(x,x+y)
and
y\mapsto G_{\xi_{0}+\xi}(x,x+y)
to the left and the right of the origin, respectively.
For
y>0
, define

\displaystyle T_{\xi_{0}}^{-}(x,x+y)

\displaystyle=\inf\{\xi>0:G_{\xi_{0}-\xi}(x,x+y)<G_{\xi_{0}}(x,x+y)\},

\displaystyle T_{\xi_{0}}^{+}(x,x+y)

\displaystyle=\inf\{\xi>0:G_{\xi_{0}+\xi}(x,x+y)>G_{\xi_{0}}(x,x+y)\},

so that
T_{\xi_{0}}^{-}(x,x+y)
and
T_{\xi_{0}}^{+}(x,x+y)
are the first points of decrease and increase, respectively, of the function
\xi\mapsto G_{\xi_{0}+\xi}(x,x+y)
to the left and right of
\xi=0
. Then, for
y>0
and
\xi>0
,

\displaystyle\quad\;\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}(S_{x}^{-}(\xi_{0},\xi_{0}+\xi)\geq y)=\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}(S_{x}^{+}(\xi_{0},\xi_{0}+\xi)\geq y)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}(T_{\xi_{0}}^{-}(x,x+y)\geq\xi)=\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}(T_{\xi_{0}}^{+}(x,x+y)\geq\xi)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}(G_{\xi_{0}+\xi}(x,x+y)=G_{\xi_{0}}(x,x+y))

\displaystyle=(2+\xi^{2}\sigma^{2}y)\Phi\Bigl(-\xi\sqrt{\frac{\sigma^{2}y}{2}}\Bigr)-\xi\sqrt{\frac{\sigma^{2}y}{\pi}}e^{-\frac{\xi^{2}\sigma^{2}y}{4}}

(2.53)

(iii)

For
\sigma>0
,
x\in\mathbb{R}
,
y>0
, and
\xi_{1}<\xi_{2}
,
G^{\sigma}_{\xi_{2}}(x,x+y)-G^{\sigma}_{\xi_{1}}(x,x+y)
is not independent of
G^{\sigma}_{\xi_{1}}(x,x+y)
. The process
\xi\mapsto G^{\sigma}_{\xi}(x,x+y)
is not Markov and does not have independent increments. For
\xi_{1}<\xi_{2}
, the joint process
(G_{\xi_{1}},G_{\xi_{2}})
is not Markov.

Remark 2.4.4
.

Corollary 2 of
[
129
]
describes another coupling of two Brownian motions with drift
such that they agree for a finite amount of time. Their result is related to our work because it is used to show the stability of the Brownian queue (see, for example, page 289 in
[
119
]
).
In a similar vein, Section 5 of
[
58
]
explores maximum exit couplings–couplings of two Brownian motions such that the law of the separation point is stochastically maximal. The couplings of
[
129
,
58
]
are different from the SH, as can be seen from a calculation of the separation time. In
[
129
]
, the separation time is exponential, while in
[
58
]
, the separation time has the distribution of the absolute value of a Gaussian.

The fact that
(G_{\xi_{1}},G_{\xi_{2}})
follows because the splitting time of the trajectories is not exponential. Alternatively, this fact follows from Theorem 28 of
[
58
]
. Specifically, two elliptic diffusions with continuous coefficients cannot be coupled in a jointly Markovian manner with nonzero separation time. I thank Adam Jaffe for pointing me to the paper
[
58
]
.

2.4.1
Proofs

Proof of theorem
2.4.1
.

We first prove that each of the probabilities
(i)
–
(v)
is the same. We know from Proposition
2.3.1
(iii)
that for
\xi>0
,
G_{\xi_{0}}\;{\leq}_{\rm inc}\;G_{\xi_{0}+\xi}
. Lemma
A.1.2
implies that for
a\leq x\leq y\leq a+d
,
\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}
-almost surely,

0\leq G_{\xi_{0}+\xi}(x,y)-G_{\xi_{0}}(x,y)\leq G_{\xi_{0}+\xi}(a,a+d)-G_{\xi_{0}}(a,d).

Hence,
\ref{Pp1}=\ref{Pp2}
. The probabilities
(iii)
and
(iv)
are both equal to
(ii)
by translation invariance (Theorem
2.3.2
(i)
).
To see that
\ref{Pp4}=\ref{Pp5}
, we apply the scaling invariance of Theorem
2.3.2
(ii)
with
b=\sqrt{\sigma^{2}d}
and
c=d^{-1/2}
to obtain

\{\sqrt{\sigma^{2}d}G_{\xi\sqrt{\sigma^{2}d}}^{1}(1)\}_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}}=\{\sqrt{\sigma^{2}d}G_{\xi\sqrt{\sigma^{2}d}}^{1}(d/d)\}_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}}\overset{d}{=}\{G_{\xi}^{\sigma}(d)\}_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}},

and thus,
(iv)
=
(v)
.
Now, to compute this probability, we use
(v)
with
d=\sigma=1
. Then, the general case follows from replacing
z
with
z/\sqrt{\sigma^{2}d}
and
\xi
with
\xi\sqrt{\sigma^{2}d}
.

We know from the construction in Proposition
2.3.1
(ii)
that

(G_{\xi_{0}}^{1},G_{\xi_{0}+\xi}^{1})\overset{d}{=}(B,D(Z,B)),

where
B
and
Z

are independent two-sided Brownian motions with diffusivity
1
and drifts
\xi_{0},\xi_{0}+\xi
. Then, by Lemma
2.3.3
,

\displaystyle G_{\xi_{0}+\xi}^{1}(1)-G_{\xi_{0}}^{1}(1)

\displaystyle\overset{d}{=}\Bigl(\sup_{0\leq u\leq 1}\{Z(u)-B(u)\}-\sup_{-\infty<u\leq 0}\{Z(u)-B(u)\}\Bigr)^{+}.

\displaystyle\overset{d}{=}\Bigl(\sup_{0\leq u\leq 1}\{\sqrt{2}B(u)+\xi u\}-\sup_{-\infty<u\leq 0}\{\sqrt{2}B(u)+\xi u\}\Bigr)^{+}.

Notice that the two suprema on the right-hand side are independent, as the first depends on
B
for positive
u
and the second depends on
B
for negative
u
. For
x\geq 0
, Lemma
A.2.4
states

\displaystyle\quad\;\mathbb{P}\bigl(\sup_{0\leq u\leq 1}\{\sqrt{2}B(u)+\xi u\}\leq x\bigr)=\Phi\Bigl(\frac{x-\xi}{\sqrt{2}}\Bigr)-e^{\xi x}\Phi\Bigl(\frac{-x-\xi}{\sqrt{2}}\Bigr).

On the other hand, by Lemma
A.2.3
,

\sup_{-\infty<u\leq 0}\{\sqrt{2}B(u)+\xi u\}\sim\operatorname{Exp}(\xi).

The conclusion of the theorem follows by a simple, but tedious convolution.

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{1}(G_{\xi_{0}+\xi}(1)-G_{\xi_{0}}(1)\leq z)=\int_{-\infty}^{0}\left(\Phi\bigl(\frac{z-y-\xi}{\sqrt{2}}\bigr)-e^{\xi(z-y)}\Phi\bigl(\frac{-z+y-\xi}{\sqrt{2}}\bigr)\right)\xi e^{\xi y}\,dy

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{0}\int_{-\infty}^{\frac{z-y-\xi}{\sqrt{2}}}\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-x^{2}/2}e^{\xi y}\,dx\,dy-e^{\xi z}\int_{-\infty}^{0}\int_{-\infty}^{\frac{-z+y-\xi}{\sqrt{2}}}\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-x^{2}/2}\,dx\,dy.

We now use Fubini’s Theorem to switch the order of integration. This results in

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\frac{z-\xi}{\sqrt{2}}}\int_{-\infty}^{0}\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-x^{2}/2}e^{\xi y}\,dy\,dx+\int_{\frac{z-\xi}{\sqrt{2}}}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{z-\xi-\sqrt{2}x}\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-x^{2}/2}e^{\xi y}\,dy\,dx

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-e^{\xi z}\int_{-\infty}^{-\frac{z+\xi}{\sqrt{2}}}\int_{\sqrt{2}x+z+\xi}^{0}\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-x^{2}/2}\,dy\,dx

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\frac{z-\xi}{\sqrt{2}}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-x^{2}/2}\,dx+\int_{\frac{z-\xi}{\sqrt{2}}}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-x^{2}/2}e^{z\xi-\sqrt{2}\xi x-\xi^{2}}\,dx

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+e^{\xi z}\int_{-\infty}^{\frac{-z-\xi}{\sqrt{2}}}(\sqrt{2}x+z+\xi)\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-x^{2}/2}\,dx

\displaystyle=\Phi\bigl(\frac{z-\xi}{\sqrt{2}}\bigr)+e^{\xi z}\int_{\frac{z-\xi}{\sqrt{2}}}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{(x+\sqrt{2}\xi)^{2}}{2}}\,dx

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+e^{\xi z}\left(\int_{-\infty}^{-\frac{z+\xi}{\sqrt{2}}}\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{\pi}}xe^{-x^{2}/2}\,dx+(\xi z+\xi^{2})\Phi\bigl(-\frac{z+\xi}{\sqrt{2}}\bigr)\right)

\displaystyle=\Phi\bigl(\frac{z-\xi}{\sqrt{2}}\bigr)+e^{\xi z}\int_{\frac{z+\xi}{\sqrt{2}}}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-u^{2}/2}\,du+e^{\xi z}\left(-\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{\pi}}e^{-\frac{(z+\xi)^{2}}{4}}+(\xi z+\xi^{2})\Phi\bigl(-\frac{z+\xi}{\sqrt{2}}\bigr)\right)

\displaystyle=\Phi\bigl(\frac{z-\xi}{\sqrt{2}}\bigr)+e^{\xi z}\left((1+\xi z+\xi^{2})\Phi\bigl(-\frac{z+\xi}{\sqrt{2}}\bigr)-\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{\pi}}e^{-\frac{(z+\xi)^{2}}{4}}\right).

(2.54)

To prove (
2.51
), it again suffices to show the
d=\sigma=1
case.
Observe that

F(0;\xi,1,1)=(2+\xi^{2})\Phi\bigl(-\xi/\sqrt{2}\bigr)-\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{\pi}}e^{-\frac{\xi^{2}}{4}}.

(2.55)

from which it readily follows that
\lim_{\xi\to\infty}F(0;\xi,1,1)=0
.
Further, from (
2.55
), we can compute

\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\xi}F(0;\xi,1,1)=2\xi\Phi(-\xi/\sqrt{2})-\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}e^{-\frac{\xi^{2}}{4}}.

(2.56)

By
[
55
, Theorem 1.2.6]
, for all
y>0
,
\int_{-\infty}^{-y}e^{-x^{2}/2}\,dx<y^{-1}e^{-y^{2}/2}
. (The theorem is stated with a weak inequality, but the proof shows that the equality is strict.) Applying this to (
2.56
), we see that
\lambda\mapsto F(0;\xi,1,1)
is strictly decreasing.
Hence,
F(0;\xi,1,1)>0
for all
\xi>0
, and also
F(0;\xi,\sigma,d)>0
for all
\xi,\sigma,d>0
by the equality
\ref{Pp5}=\ref{Pp4}
.
∎

We now compute the Laplace transform/moment generating function of
G_{\xi_{0}+\xi}^{\sigma}(d)-G_{\xi_{0}}^{\sigma}(d)
. For
\alpha\neq\xi
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{\sigma}\Big[\exp\Big(-\alpha(G_{\xi_{0}+\xi}(a,a+d)-G_{\xi_{0}}(a,a+d))\Big)\Big]

(2.57)

\displaystyle=e^{\alpha^{2}\sigma^{2}d-\alpha\xi\sigma^{2}d}\Phi\Big((\xi-2\alpha)\sqrt{\frac{\sigma^{2}d}{2}}\Big)\Big(1-\frac{\alpha^{2}}{(\xi-\alpha)^{2}}\Big)

\displaystyle+\Phi\Big(-\xi\sqrt{\frac{\sigma^{2}d}{2}}\Big)\Big(1+\frac{\alpha\xi}{(\xi-\alpha)^{2}}-\frac{\alpha(1+\sigma^{2}\xi^{2}d)}{\xi-\alpha}\Big)

\displaystyle+\frac{\xi\alpha}{(\xi-\alpha)}\sqrt{\frac{\sigma^{2}d}{\pi}}e^{-\xi^{2}\sigma^{2}d/4}.

Again, we start with
\sigma=d=1
. By (
2.54
), we obtain

\mathbb{P}^{1}(G_{\xi_{0}+\xi}(1)-G_{\xi_{0}}(1)>z)=\Phi\Big(-\frac{z-\xi}{\sqrt{2}}\Big)-e^{\xi z}(1+\xi z+\xi^{2})\Phi\Big(-\frac{z+\xi}{\sqrt{2}}\Big)+\frac{\xi e^{\xi z}}{\sqrt{\pi}}e^{-\frac{(z+\xi)^{2}}{4}}.

(2.58)

we compute the Laplace transform via Lemma
A.2.1
: that is

\mathbb{E}^{1}\Bigl[-\alpha(G_{\xi_{0}+\xi}(a,b)-G_{\xi_{0}}(a,b))\Bigr]=1-\alpha\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\alpha z}\mathbb{P}^{1}(G_{\xi_{0}+\xi}(a,b)-G_{\xi_{0}}(a,b)>z)\,dz.

(2.59)

handling each of the three terms on in the sum on the right-hand side of (
2.58
). Using Fubini’s theorem, we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}-\alpha e^{-\alpha z}\Phi\Big(-\frac{z-\xi}{\sqrt{2}}\Big)\,dz=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{-\frac{z-\xi}{\sqrt{2}}}-\alpha e^{-\alpha z}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-x^{2}/2}\,dxdz

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}}\int_{0}^{\xi-\sqrt{2}x}-\alpha e^{-\alpha z}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-x^{2}/2}\,dz\,dx=\int_{-\infty}^{\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}}(e^{-\alpha(\xi-\sqrt{2}x)}-1)\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-x^{2}/2}\,dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}}\frac{e^{-\alpha(\xi-\sqrt{2}x)}e^{-x^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\,dx-\Phi\Big(\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}\Big)=e^{\alpha^{2}-\alpha\xi}\Phi\Big(\frac{\xi-2\alpha}{\sqrt{2}}\Big)-\Phi\Big(\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}\Big).

(2.60)

Next, we compute the second term, using integration by parts in the second step

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\alpha e^{(\xi-\alpha)z}(1+\xi z+\xi^{2})\Phi\Big(-\frac{z+\xi}{\sqrt{2}}\Big)\,dz

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{-\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}}\int_{0}^{-\sqrt{2}x-\xi}\alpha e^{(\xi-\alpha)z}\frac{(1+\xi z+\xi^{2})e^{-x^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\,dzdx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{-\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}}\Bigg((1+\xi(-\sqrt{2}x-\xi)+\xi^{2})\frac{\alpha}{\xi-\alpha}e^{(\alpha-\xi)(\sqrt{2}x+\xi)}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\frac{\alpha(1+\xi^{2})}{\xi-\alpha}-\int_{0}^{-\sqrt{2}x-\xi}\frac{\alpha\xi}{\xi-\alpha}e^{(\xi-\alpha)z}\,dz\Bigg)\frac{e^{-x^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\,dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{-\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}}\Bigg((1-\sqrt{2}x\xi)\frac{\alpha}{\xi-\alpha}e^{(\alpha-\xi)(\sqrt{2}x+\xi)}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\frac{\alpha(1+\xi^{2})}{\xi-\alpha}-\frac{\alpha\xi}{(\xi-\alpha)^{2}}e^{(\alpha-\xi)(\sqrt{2}x+\xi)}+\frac{\alpha\xi}{(\xi-\alpha)^{2}}\Bigg)\frac{e^{-x^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\,dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\alpha(\alpha\xi^{2}+\alpha-\xi^{3})}{(\xi-\alpha)^{2}}\Phi\Big(-\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\int_{-\infty}^{-\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}}\frac{\alpha(-\sqrt{2}x\xi^{2}-\alpha+\sqrt{2}x\alpha\xi)e^{(\alpha-\xi)(\sqrt{2}x+\xi)}}{(\xi-\alpha)^{2}}\frac{e^{-x^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\,dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\alpha(\alpha\xi^{2}+\alpha-\xi^{3})}{(\xi-\alpha)^{2}}\Phi\Big(-\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\int_{-\infty}^{-\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}}\frac{\alpha(-\sqrt{2}x\xi^{2}-\alpha+\sqrt{2}x\alpha\xi)e^{-(x-\sqrt{2}(\alpha-\xi))^{2}/2+\alpha(\alpha-\xi)}}{(\xi-\alpha)^{2}\sqrt{2\pi}}\,dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\alpha(\alpha\xi^{2}+\alpha-\xi^{3})}{(\xi-\alpha)^{2}}\Phi\Big(-\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+e^{\alpha^{2}-\alpha\xi}\Bigg(\frac{\xi\alpha}{\sqrt{\pi}(\xi-\alpha)}e^{-\xi^{2}/4+\alpha\xi-\alpha^{2}}+\Big(2\xi\alpha-\frac{\alpha^{2}}{(\xi-\alpha)^{2}}\Big)\Phi\Big(\frac{\xi-2\alpha}{\sqrt{2}}\Big)\Bigg)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\alpha(\alpha\xi^{2}+\alpha-\xi^{3})}{(\xi-\alpha)^{2}}\Phi\Big(-\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{\xi\alpha}{\sqrt{\pi}(\xi-\alpha)}e^{-\xi^{2}/4}+e^{\alpha^{2}-\alpha\xi}\Big(2\xi\alpha-\frac{\alpha^{2}}{(\xi-\alpha)^{2}}\Big)\Phi\Big(\frac{\xi-2\alpha}{\sqrt{2}}\Big).

(2.61)

Lastly, we handle the third term:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}-\alpha e^{(\xi-\alpha)z}\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{\pi}}e^{-(z+\xi)^{2}/4}\,dz=-2\alpha\xi e^{\alpha^{2}-\alpha\xi}\Phi\Big(\frac{\xi-2\alpha}{\sqrt{2}}\Big).

(2.62)

Adding (
2.60
),(
2.61
), and (
2.62
) to the number
1
, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\exp(-\alpha Z)]

\displaystyle=e^{\alpha^{2}-\alpha\xi}\Phi\Big(\frac{\xi-2\alpha}{\sqrt{2}}\Big)\Big(1-\frac{\alpha^{2}}{(\xi-\alpha)^{2}}\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\Phi\Big(-\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}\Big)\Big(1+\frac{\alpha\xi}{(\xi-\alpha)^{2}}-\frac{\alpha(1+\xi^{2})}{\xi-\alpha}\Big)+\frac{\xi\alpha}{\sqrt{\pi}(\xi-\alpha)}e^{-\xi^{2}/4},

and we see this matches (
2.57
) with
d=\sigma=1
. The general case follows by
(iv)
=
(v)
after replacing
\alpha
with
\alpha\sqrt{\sigma^{2}d}
and
\xi
with
\xi\sqrt{\sigma^{2}d}
.

Proof of Corollary
2.4.3

.

Item
(i)
(Convergence to Gaussian):
For
\xi,y>0
, Theorem
2.4.1
, tells us that

\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}(G_{\xi+\xi_{0}}(x,x+y)-G_{\xi_{0}}(x,x+y)-\sigma^{2}\xi d\leq z)\\
=\Phi\bigl(\frac{z}{\sqrt{2\sigma^{2}y}}\bigr)+e^{(z+\sigma^{2}\xi y)\xi}\Bigl((1+(z+\sigma^{2}\xi y)\xi+\xi^{2}\sigma^{2}y)\Phi\bigl(-\frac{z+2\sigma^{2}\xi y}{\sqrt{2\sigma^{2}y}}\bigr)\\
-\xi\sqrt{\frac{\sigma^{2}y}{\pi}}\exp\bigl(-\frac{(z+2\sigma^{2}\xi y)^{2}}{4\sigma^{2}y}\bigr)\Bigr)

In the limit as
\xi\to+\infty
, the only nonvanishing term is

\Phi\bigl(\frac{z}{\sqrt{2\sigma^{2}y}}\bigr)=\int_{-\infty}^{z/\sqrt{2\sigma^{2}y}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-x^{2}/2}\,dx=\int_{-\infty}^{z}\frac{1}{\sqrt{4\pi\sigma^{2}y}}e^{-x^{2}/(4\sigma^{2}y)}\,dx,

which we now recognize as the distribution function of a Gaussian random variable with
0
mean and variance
2\sigma^{2}y
. For
y<0
, the result follows by symmetry of the Gaussian.

For the limit as
\xi\to-\infty
, the stationarity of increments in Theorem
2.3.2
(iii)
implies

\displaystyle\quad\;G^{\sigma}_{\xi+\xi_{0}}(x,x+y)-G^{\sigma}_{\xi_{0}}(x,x+y)-\sigma^{2}\xi y

\displaystyle=-(G^{\sigma}_{\xi_{0}}(x,x+y)-G^{\sigma}_{\xi_{0}+\xi}(x,x+y)-\sigma^{2}|\xi|y)

\displaystyle\overset{d}{=}-(G_{-\xi}^{\sigma}(x,x+y)-G_{0}^{\sigma}(x,x+y)-\sigma^{2}|\xi|y).

The result now follows by the symmetry of the Gaussian distribution.

Item
(ii)
(Splitting times):
We first recall the definitions: for
\xi>0
,

\displaystyle S_{x}^{-}(\xi_{0},\xi_{0}+\xi)

\displaystyle=\inf\{y>0:G_{\xi_{0}+\xi}(x,x-y)<G_{\xi_{0}}(x,x-y)\},

\displaystyle S_{x}^{+}(\xi_{0},\xi_{0}+\xi)

\displaystyle=\inf\{y>0:G_{\xi_{0}+\xi}(x,x+y)>G_{\xi_{0}}(x,x+y)\},

and for
y>0
,

\displaystyle T_{\xi_{0}}^{-}(x,x+y)

\displaystyle=\inf\{\xi>0:G_{\xi_{0}-\xi}(x,x+y)<G_{\xi_{0}}(x,x+y)\},

\displaystyle T_{\xi_{0}}^{+}(x,x+y)

\displaystyle=\inf\{\xi>0:G_{\xi_{0}+\xi}(x,x+y)>G_{\xi}(x,x+y)\}.

Let
y,\xi>0
. We work on the
\mathbb{P}
-almost sure event (depending on a fixed
\xi_{0}\in\mathbb{R},\xi>0)
on which
G_{(\xi_{0}+\xi)-}(x)=G_{\xi_{0}+\xi}(x)
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
, and for arbitrary
\xi_{1}<\xi_{2}
,
G_{\xi_{1}}\;{\leq}_{\rm inc}\;G_{\xi_{2}}
(Proposition
2.3.1
(iii)
–
(iv)
). On this event, we first show that

(a)

S_{x}^{-}(\xi_{0},\xi_{0}+\xi)\geq y\iff G_{\xi_{0}+\xi}(x,x-y)=G_{\xi_{0}}(x,x-y)

(b)

T_{\xi_{0}}^{-}(x,x+y)\geq\xi\iff G_{\xi_{0}-\xi}(x,x+y)=G_{\xi_{0}}(x,x+y)

(c)

S_{x}^{+}(\xi_{0},\xi_{0}+\xi)\geq y\iff T_{\xi_{0}}^{+}(x,x+y)\geq\xi\iff G_{\xi_{0}+\xi}(x,x+y)=G_{\xi}(x,x+y)
.

We prove
(a)
and
(b)
, then
(c)
follows similarly. For
(a)
, if
S_{x}^{-}(\xi_{0},\xi_{0}+\xi)\geq y
, then since
G_{\xi_{0}+\xi}(x,x-z)\leq G_{\xi_{0}}(x,x-z)
holds for general
z>0
, we must have equality for
z<y
. Continuity implies the equality
G_{\xi_{0}+\xi}(x,x-y)=G_{\xi_{0}}(x,x-y)
. On the other hand, if
G_{\xi_{0}+\xi}(x,x-y)=G_{\xi_{0}}(x,x-y)
, then for all
z\in(0,y]
, by Lemma
A.1.2
,

0\geq G_{\xi_{0}+\xi}(x,x-z)-G_{\xi_{0}}(x,x-z)\geq G_{\xi_{0}+\xi}(x,x-y)-G_{\xi_{0}}(x,x-y)=0,

so
S_{x}^{-}(\xi_{0},\xi_{0}+\xi)\geq y
.

Now, we turn to
(b)
. If
T_{\xi_{0}}^{-}(x,x+y)\geq\xi>0
, then
G_{\xi_{0}-\eta}(x,x+y)=G_{\xi_{0}}(x,x+y)
for all
\eta\in(0,\xi)
. But the right-continuity of
G
implies that
G_{\xi_{0}-\xi}(x,x+y)=G_{\xi_{0}}(x,x+y)
as well. On the other hand, if
G_{\xi_{0}-\xi}(x,x+y)=G_{\xi_{0}}(x,x+y)
, then for
\eta\in(0,\xi]
,
G_{\xi_{0}-\eta}(x,x+y)\geq G_{\xi_{0}-\xi}(x,x+y)
, so

0\geq G_{\xi_{0}-\eta}(x,x+y)-G_{\xi_{0}}(x,x+y)\geq G_{\xi_{0}-\xi}(x,x+y)-G_{\xi_{0}}(x,x+y)=0.

Hence,
T_{\xi_{0}}^{-}(x,x+y)\geq\xi
. By stationarity of
G
in the
x
and
\xi
parameters (Theorem
2.3.2
(i)
,
(iii)
),

\displaystyle\quad\;\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}(G_{\xi_{0}+\xi}(x,x-y)=G_{\xi_{0}}(x,x-y))=\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}(G_{\xi_{0}+\xi}(x-y,x)=G_{\xi_{0}}(x-y,x)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}(G_{\xi_{0}+\xi}(x,x+y)=G_{\xi_{0}}(x,x+y)=\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}(G_{\xi_{0}}(x,x+y)=G_{\xi_{0}-\xi}(x,x+y),

where in the first equality we simply used the fact that
G_{\xi}(x,y)=-G_{\xi}(y,x)
. Hence, each of the events in Items
(a)
–
(b)
has the same probability, which is computed by setting
d=y
and
z=0
in the formula of Theorem
2.4.1
. We repeat this formula here:

(2+\xi^{2}\sigma^{2}y)\Phi\Bigl(-\xi\sqrt{\frac{\sigma^{2}y}{2}}\Bigr)-\xi\sqrt{\frac{\sigma^{2}y}{\pi}}e^{-\frac{\xi^{2}\sigma^{2}y}{4}}.

Item
(iii)
(Non-independence and non-Markovian structure):

Assume, by way of contradiction, that for
y>0
,
G^{\sigma}_{\xi_{2}}(x,y)-G^{\sigma}_{\xi_{1}}(x,y)
is independent of
G^{\sigma}_{\xi_{1}}(x,y)
. Then, since each
x\mapsto G^{\sigma}_{\xi}
is a Brownian motion with diffusivity
\sigma
,

\displaystyle\sigma^{2}y

\displaystyle=\mathbb{V}ar^{\sigma}(G_{\xi_{2}}(x,x+y))=\mathbb{V}ar^{\sigma}(G_{\xi_{2}}(x,x+y)-G_{\xi_{1}}(x,x+y))+\mathbb{V}ar^{\sigma}(G_{\xi_{1}}(x,x+y))

\displaystyle=\mathbb{V}ar^{\sigma}(G_{\xi_{2}}(x,x+y)-G_{\xi_{1}}(x,x+y))+\sigma^{2}y.

Hence,
\mathbb{V}ar^{\sigma}(G_{\xi_{2}}(x,x+y)-G_{\xi_{1}}(x,x+y))=0
. But this is not true, as Theorem
2.4.1
shows that
G_{\xi_{2}}^{\sigma}(x,x+y)-G_{\xi_{1}}^{\sigma}(x,x+y)
does not have a degenerate distribution.

We turn to showing the non-independence of increments. Assume, by way of contradiction,
\xi\mapsto G^{\sigma}_{\xi}(x,x+y)
has independent increments. Then for
0<\eta<\xi
,

\displaystyle\quad\;\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}(G_{\xi}(x,x+y)-G_{0}(x,x+y)=0)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}(G_{\eta}(x,x+y)-G_{0}(x,x+y)=0)\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}(G_{\xi}(x,x+y)-G_{\eta}(x,x+y)=0),

by monotonicity. Equivalently,

\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}(G_{\xi}(x,x+y)=G_{0}(x,x+y)|G_{\eta}(x,x+y)=G_{0}(x,x+y))\\
=\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}(G_{\xi}(x,x+y)=G_{\eta}(x,x+y)).

As we showed in the proof of Item
(ii)
, this is equivalent to

\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}(T_{0}^{+}(x,x+y)\geq\xi|T_{0}^{+}(x,x+y)\geq\eta)\\
=\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}(T_{\eta}^{+}(x,x+y)\geq\xi-\eta)=\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}(T_{0}^{+}(x,x+y)\geq\xi-\eta).

However, our computation in Item
(ii)
shows that
T_{0}^{+}(x,x+y)
does not have a memoryless distribution, a contradiction. The process
\xi\mapsto G^{\sigma}_{\xi}(x,x+y)
therefore is also not Markov because the time to its first point of increase is not memoryless.

Lastly, the joint process
(G_{\xi_{1}},G_{\xi_{2}})
is not Markov because the splitting time of
G_{\xi_{1}}(x)
and
G_{\xi_{2}}(x)
for
x\geq 0
is not memoryless, as seen in Item
(ii)
.
∎

2.5
The SH as a jump process and its path properties

The trajectories of
\xi\mapsto G^{\sigma}(x,y)
are step functions. This fact is the fundamental ingredient for the analysis of the infinite geodesics in the directed landscape studied in Chapter
4
. We state this theorem now.

Theorem 2.5.1
.

Fix
\sigma>0
and let
x\in\mathbb{R},y>0
. Then,
\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}
-almost surely, the paths of
\xi\mapsto G_{\xi}(x,x+y)
are nondecreasing step functions, whose jumps are a discrete subset of
\mathbb{R}
. The expected number of jumps in an interval
[\xi_{0},\xi_{0}+\xi]\subseteq\mathbb{R}
is

\mathbb{E}^{\sigma}[\#\{\eta\in[\xi_{0},\xi_{0}+\xi]:G_{\eta-}(x,x+y)<G_{\eta+}(x,x+y)\}]=2\xi\sqrt{\frac{\sigma^{2}y}{\pi}}.

Furthermore,
\lim_{\xi\to\pm\infty}G_{\xi}(x,x+y)=\pm\infty
almost surely; consequently, the set of jumps over all
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
is infinite and unbounded for both positive and negative
\xi
.

We now detail the path properties of the SH. Given
G\in D(\mathbb{R},C(\mathbb{R}))
and
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
, we define the random sets

\displaystyle\Xi_{G}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\{\xi\in\mathbb{R}:G_{\xi-}(x,y)\neq G_{\xi+}(x,y)\},\;\text{and}

(2.63)

\displaystyle\Xi_{G}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{x,y\in\mathbb{R}}\Xi_{G}(x,y)=\bigcup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\{\xi\in\mathbb{R}:G_{\xi-}(x)\neq G_{\xi+}(x)\}.

Observe that
\Xi_{G}(x,y)=\Xi_{G}(y,x)
. We define
\Xi_{G}(x)=\Xi_{G}(0,x)
. When we wish to refer to the set
\Xi_{G}
for a realization of the SH
G^{\sigma}
without referring to the measure
\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}
, we write
\Xi_{G^{\sigma}}
.

Theorem 2.5.2
.

Let
\sigma>0
. The following hold
\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}
-almost surely.

(i)

For
\xi_{1}<\xi_{2}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
,
y\mapsto G_{\xi_{2}}(x,x+y)-G_{\xi_{1}}(x,x+y)
is a nondecreasing function.

(ii)

For
a\leq x<y\leq b
,
\Xi_{G}(x,y)\subseteq\Xi_{G}(a,b)
.

(iii)

For every
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
and every compact set
K\subseteq\mathbb{R}
, there exists an
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(K,\xi)>0
so that when
x,y\in K
, and
\xi-\varepsilon<\alpha<\xi<\beta<\xi+\varepsilon
,

G_{\alpha}(x,y)=G_{\xi-}(x,y)
and
G_{\xi}(x,y)=G_{\beta}(x,y)
.

(iv)

For every
x<y
,
\xi\mapsto G_{\xi}(x,y)
is a step function converging to
\pm\infty
as
\xi\to\pm\infty
. The set
\Xi_{G}(x,y)
is countably infinite and contains only finitely many points in each compact interval.

(v)

Let
S_{x}^{-}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
and
S_{x}^{+}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
be defined as in (
2.52
). For every pair
\xi_{1}<\xi_{2}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
, there exists
\alpha,\beta\in[\xi_{1},\xi_{2}]\cap\Xi_{G}
so that

\displaystyle S_{x}^{-}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})

\displaystyle=\inf\{y>0:G_{\alpha}(x,x-y)<G_{\alpha_{-}}(x,x-y)\},\qquad\text{and}

\displaystyle S_{x}^{+}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})

\displaystyle=\inf\{y>0:G_{\beta}(x,x+y)>G_{\beta_{-}}(x,x+y)\}.

That is, when the trajectories
G_{\xi_{1}},G_{\xi_{2}}
split (to the left or right of the origin), there is an exceptional
\xi\in\Xi_{G}
so that
G_{\xi-}
and
G_{\xi}
also split at that same point.

(vi)

The set
\Xi_{G}
is dense in
\mathbb{R}
.

Figure
2.2
shows a simulation of the stationary horizon for
\sigma=1
and

\xi\in\{0,\pm 1,\pm 2,\pm 3,\pm 5,\pm 10\}
. The items of Theorem
2.5.2
can be seen from the graph. Item
(ii)
states that the discontinuities increase as we move away from the origin. This is manifest in the picture by the splitting of trajectories. Item
(v)
states that when the two trajectories split for positive
x
, there exists a value
\xi\in\Xi_{G}
so that
G_{\xi+}
follows the upper trajectory, and
G_{\xi-}
follows the lower trajectory. This results in the density of Item
(vi)
. The reverse happens for negative
x
. Once the trajectories split, the distance between the two never decreases, as stated in Item
(i)
. Items
(iii)
and
(iv)
reflect the fact that two trajectories
G_{\xi_{1}}
and
G_{\xi_{2}}
stick together in a neighborhood of the origin before splitting.

Figure 2.2
:
A simulation of the stationary horizon

The discreteness of the sets
\Xi_{G}(x,y)
in Theorem
2.5.2
(iv)
implies that, under
\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}
, the set
\Xi_{G}(x,y)
is a well-defined point processes. The set
\Xi_{G}
itself is dense, and it is not easy, a priori, to interpret as a random object. However, By Theorem
2.5.2
(ii)
,
\Xi_{G}
is the increasing union of the sets
\Xi_{G}(x,y)
as
x\to-\infty
and
y\to+\infty
(In fact, it suffices to fix either
x
or
y
and send the other to
\pm\infty
by Corollary
2.6.8
in Section
2.6
). We state some distributional invariances of these sets in the following corollary to Theorem
2.3.2
.

Corollary 2.5.3
.

For
\sigma>0
, let
G^{\sigma}
be the SH. For
c,b>0
and
\nu\in\mathbb{R}
, and
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}
, the sets
\Xi_{G}
satisfy the following distributional invariances:

\Xi_{G^{\sigma}}(x,y)\overset{d}{=}-\Xi_{G^{\sigma}}(-x,-y)\overset{d}{=}b\Xi_{G^{bc\sigma}}(c^{-2}x,c^{-2}y)+b\nu.

Proof.

The first equality follows by the reflection invariance of Theorem
2.3.2
(iv)
, and the second follows by the scaling relations in Theorem
2.3.2
(ii)
. More specifically, let
G^{\sigma}
be the SH, and let
\widetilde{G}^{bc\sigma}
be defined by
\widetilde{G}^{\sigma}_{\xi/b-\nu}(x)=bG_{\xi}^{\sigma}(c^{2}x)-(bc\sigma)^{2}\nu x
. Then,
\widetilde{G}^{bc\sigma}
is distributed as
G^{bc\sigma}
, and
\xi\in\Xi_{G^{\sigma}}(c^{2}x,c^{2}y)
if and only if
\xi\in b\Xi_{\widetilde{G}^{bc\sigma}}(x,y)+b\nu
.
∎

2.5.1
Proofs

The following more general theorem is the key to showing step function behavior of the SH. It gives a general condition for an increment-stationary process to be a jump process, and is of independent interest.

Theorem 2.5.4
.

On a probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
, let
Y=\{Y(t):t\geq 0\}
be a nondecreasing, increment-stationary process such that the following three conditions hold:

(i)

For
a<b
,
\mathbb{E}[Y(b)-Y(a)]<\infty
.

(ii)

\mathbb{P}[Y(t)=Y(0)]\in(0,1)
for all sufficiently small
t>0
.

(iii)

\liminf_{t\searrow 0}\mathbb{E}[Y(t)-Y(0)|Y(t)>Y(0)]>0
.

Then,
\mathbb{P}
-almost surely the paths of
t\mapsto Y(t)
are step functions with finitely many jumps in each bounded interval. For each
t\geq 0
, there is a jump at
t
with probability
0
. For
a<b\in I
, the expected number of jump points in the interval
[a,b]
equals

\frac{\mathbb{E}[Y(b)-Y(a)]}{\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}[Y(2^{-n})-Y(0)|Y(2^{-n})>Y(0)]}.

Remark 2.5.5
.

Heuristically, we can think of Condition
(iii)
in the following way: on average, the size of the jumps are bounded away from
0
, and therefore the jumps cannot accumulate because an increment of the process itself has finite expectation by Condition
(i)
.

Proof of Theorem
2.5.4
.

We first note that for
b>a
,

(b-a)\mathbb{E}[Y(1)-Y(0)]=\mathbb{E}[Y(b)-Y(a)].

(2.64)

Indeed, by increment-stationarity, it suffices to show that
\mathbb{E}[Y(t)-Y(0)]=t\mathbb{E}[Y(1)-Y(0)]
for all
t>0
. Since
Y
is nondecreasing,
t\mapsto\mathbb{E}[Y(t)-Y(0)]
is nondecreasing, so it further suffices to show that
\mathbb{E}[Y(t)-Y(0)]=t\mathbb{E}[Y(1)-Y(0)]
just for rational
t>0
. For any positive integer
k
,

\mathbb{E}[Y(k)-Y(0)]=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathbb{E}[Y(i)-Y(i-1)]=k\mathbb{E}[Y(1)-Y(0)]=k\mathbb{E}[Y(1)-Y(0)].

Then for positive integers
r
and
k
,

r\mathbb{E}[Y(1)-Y(0)]=\mathbb{E}[Y(r)-Y(0)]=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathbb{E}[Y(ri/k)-Y(r(i-1)/k)]=k\mathbb{E}[Y(r/k)-Y(0)],

and
\mathbb{E}[Y(r/k)-Y(0)]=\tfrac{r}{k}\mathbb{E}[Y(1)-Y(0)]
.

We now show that there are finitely many jumps in the interval
[0,1]
, and the general result holds by increment-stationarity and (
2.64
). Consider discrete versions of the process
Y
as follows. For
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, let
D_{n}=\{\frac{j}{2^{n}}:j\in\mathbb{Z},0\leq j\leq 2^{n}\}
, and consider the process
Y_{n}:=\{Y(t):t\in D_{n}\}
. Let
J_{n}
be the number of jumps of
Y_{n}
, i.e.,

J_{n}=\sum_{j=1}^{2^{n}}\mathbf{1}\Bigl(Y\bigl(\tfrac{j}{2^{n}}\bigr)>Y\bigl(\tfrac{j-1}{2^{n}}\bigr)\Bigr)

Then,
J_{n}
is nondecreasing in
n
, so it has a limit, denoted as the random variable
J_{\infty}
. Let
K\in\{0,1,2,\ldots\}\cup\{\infty\}
be the number of points of increase of
Y
on the interval
[0,1]
. Specifically, a point
t\in(0,1)
is a point of increase, if
Y(t+\varepsilon)>Y(t-\varepsilon)
for all
\varepsilon>0
. We say
0
is a point of increase if
Y(t)>Y(0)
for all
t>0
, and we likewise say that
1
is a point of increase if
Y(t)<Y(1)
for all
t<1
.

There are three steps we need to complete the proof. The first is to show
K\leq J_{\infty}
. Next, we show that
J_{\infty}
is finite almost surely by computing its mean. Lastly, we show that
J_{\infty}\leq K
.

We now show that
K\leq J_{\infty}
. If
K<\infty
, let
k=K
, and otherwise, let
k
be an arbitrary positive integer. It suffices to show that
J_{\infty}\geq k
. By definition of
k
, we may choose
k
points of increase
t_{1}<\dotsm<t_{k}
. First, we handle the case where
t_{i}\in(0,1)
for all
i
. Then, for all sufficiently large
n
, there exist
n
-dependent positive integers
0<j_{1}<\cdots<j_{k}<2^{n}
so that for each
i
,
j_{i+1}>j_{i}+2
, and

\frac{j_{i}-1}{2^{n}}<t_{i}<\frac{j_{i}+1}{2^{n}}.

(2.65)

Since
t_{i}
is a point of increase and
Y
is nondecreasing,
Y(\frac{j_{i}+1}{2^{n}})>Y(\frac{j_{i}-1}{2^{n}})
. Therefore,
Y(\frac{j_{i}+1}{2^{n}})>Y(\frac{j_{i}}{2^{n}})
or
Y(\frac{j_{i}}{2^{n}})>Y(\frac{j_{i}-1}{2^{n}})
. By assumption that
j_{i+1}>j_{i}+2
, the intervals
[\frac{j_{i}-1}{2^{n}},\frac{j_{i}+1}{2^{n}}]
are mutually disjoint, so
J_{n}\geq k
and therefore
J_{\infty}\geq k
. The case where
t_{1}=0
or
t_{k}=1
is handled similarly.

Next, we compute the finite mean of
J_{\infty}
.
Let

c_{n}=\mathbb{E}[Y(2^{-n})-Y(0)|Y(2^{-n})>Y(0)]

Then, using increment-stationarity,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[Y(1)-Y(0)]

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{2^{n}}\mathbb{E}\Big[Y\big(\tfrac{j}{2^{n}}\big)-Y\big(\tfrac{j-1}{2^{n}}\big)\Big]

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{2^{n}}\mathbb{E}\Big[Y\big(\tfrac{j}{2^{n}}\big)-Y\big(\tfrac{j-1}{2^{n}}\big)\Big|Y\big(\tfrac{j}{2^{n}}\big)>Y\big(\tfrac{j-1}{2^{n}}\big)\Big]\mathbb{P}\Big(Y\big(\tfrac{j}{2^{n}}\big)>Y\big(\tfrac{j-1}{2^{n}}\big)\Big)

\displaystyle=c_{n}\sum_{j=1}^{2^{n}}\mathbb{P}\Big(Y\big(\tfrac{j}{2^{n}}\big)>Y\big(\tfrac{j-1}{2^{n}}\big)\Big)=c_{n}\mathbb{E}[J_{n}].

By assumptions
(i)
and
(iii)
and the monotone convergence theorem,

\mathbb{E}[J_{\infty}]=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}[J_{n}]=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\mathbb{E}[Y(1)-Y(0)]}{c_{n}}<\infty.

(2.66)

Therefore,
\mathbb{P}(J_{\infty}<\infty)=1
. Since
K\leq J_{\infty}
, with probability one,
Y
has only finitely many points of increase on
[0,1]
. Therefore, with probability one,
Y:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
is locally constant except at the finitely many jump points. Hence, for each
t\in(0,1)
, the left and right limits of
Y
at
t,Y(t\pm)
exist. The limits
Y(0+)
and
Y(1-)
exist as well. Since
Y
is increasing, for each
t\in(0,1)
and
\varepsilon>0
, we can apply (
2.64
) and Assumption
(i)
to get

\mathbb{E}[Y(t+)-Y(t-)]\leq\mathbb{E}[Y(t+\varepsilon)-Y(t-\varepsilon)]=2\varepsilon\mathbb{E}[Y(1)-Y(0)]<\infty.

Sending
\varepsilon\searrow 0
, the left-hand side is
0
and therefore, a jump occurs at time
t
with probability
0
. Similar arguments apply to
t=0
and
t=1
. Therefore, there exists an event of probability one,
\Omega_{\mathbb{Q}_{2}}
on which
Y
has no jumps at points of the form
\frac{j}{2^{n}}
for positive integers
j
and
n
.

To compute the mean number of jumps, we show that
J_{\infty}=K
on the event
\Omega_{\mathbb{Q}_{2}}
. We already showed that
K\leq J_{\infty}
, so it remains to show
J_{\infty}\leq K
.

We start by showing that if
Y(b)>Y(a)
for some
a<b
, there must be some point of increase in the interval
[a,b]
. We prove this as follows: let
c
be the midpoint of
a
and
b
. Then, since
Y
is nondecreasing, either
Y(b)>Y(c)
or
Y(c)>Y(a)
. If, without loss of generality,
Y(b)>Y(c)
, then we can bisect the interval again with midpoint
d
and get that
Y(b)>Y(d)
or
Y(d)>Y(c)
, where
d
is the midpoint of
a
and
b
. Inductively, this constructs a sequence of nested intervals
[a_{n},b_{n}]\subseteq[a_{n-1},b_{n-1}]\subseteq[a,b]
, where
[a_{n},b_{n}]
is either the left or right half of the previous interval. Then,
a_{n}
is nondecreasing and
b_{n}
is nonincreasing and
b_{n}-a_{n}\rightarrow 0
. Then, set
t=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}a_{n}=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}b_{n}
, and we have that
t\in[a_{n},b_{n}]
for all
n
. If
t\in(0,1)
, then for all
\varepsilon>0
, we may choose
n
large enough so that, because
Y
is nondecreasing,

Y(t+\varepsilon)-Y(t-\varepsilon)\geq Y(b_{n})-Y(a_{n})>0.

Hence,
t
is a point of increase. The case where
t=0
or
1
is handled similarly.

Now, we show that on
\Omega_{\mathbb{Q}_{2}}
,
J_{n}\leq K
for all
n
. By definition,
J_{n}
is the number of integers
0<j\leq 2^{n}
such that
Y(j2^{-n})>Y((j-1)2^{-n})
. For each such
j
, we just showed that there must be a point of increase in
[(j-1)2^{-n},j2^{-n}]
, and on the event
\Omega_{\mathbb{Q}_{2}}
, that point of increase must lie in the interior of the interval. Thus,
J_{n}\leq K
, and
J_{\infty}\leq K
, so
J_{\infty}=K
on
\Omega_{\mathbb{Q}_{2}}
. Equation (
2.66
) computes the mean number of jump points.
∎

Proof of Theorem
2.5.1
.

We verify the conditions of Theorem
2.5.4
for the process

\{G_{\xi_{0}+\xi}^{\sigma}(x,x+y):\xi\geq 0\},

where
\xi_{0}
is arbitrary. This process is nondecreasing by Proposition
2.3.1
(iii)
, and has stationary increments by Theorem
2.3.2
(iii)
.

Condition
(i)
of Theorem
2.5.4
follows because each
G^{\sigma}_{\xi}
is a Brownian motion with diffusivity
\sigma
and drift
\sigma^{2}\xi
. In particular, for
\xi,y>0
,

\mathbb{E}^{\sigma}[G_{\xi_{0}+\xi}(x,x+y)-G_{\xi_{0}}(x,x+y)]=\sigma^{2}\xi y<\infty.

(2.67)

Theorem
2.4.1
states that
\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}(G_{\xi_{0}+\xi}(x,x+y)=G_{\xi_{0}}(x,x+y))\in(0,1)
for all
\xi,y>0
. Hence, Condition
(ii)
is satisfied.

Lastly, we verify Condition
(iii)
, which, combined with (
2.67
) allows us to compute the mean number of jumps in the interval
[\xi_{0},\xi_{0}+\xi]
. Observe that, because

\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}[G_{\xi_{0}+\xi}(x,x+y)-G_{\xi_{0}}(x,x+y)\geq 0]=1,

and using (
2.53
),

\displaystyle\quad\;\mathbb{E}^{\sigma}[G_{\xi_{0}+\xi}(x,x+y)-G_{\xi_{0}}(x,x+y)|G_{\xi_{0}+\xi}(x,x+y)>G_{\xi_{0}}(x,x+y)]

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{E}^{\sigma}[G_{\xi_{0}+\xi}(x,x+y)-G_{\xi_{0}}(x,x+y)]}{\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}[G_{\xi_{0}+\xi}(x,x+y)>G_{\xi_{0}}(x,x+y)]}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma^{2}\xi y}{1-(2+\xi^{2}\sigma^{2}y)\Phi\bigl(-\xi\sqrt{\frac{\sigma^{2}y}{2}}\bigr)+\xi\sqrt{\frac{\sigma^{2}y}{\pi}}\exp(-\xi^{2}\sigma^{2}y/4)}.

An application of L’Hôpital’s rule gives

\displaystyle\quad\;\lim_{\xi\searrow 0}\mathbb{E}^{\sigma}[G_{\xi_{0}+\xi}(x,x+y)-G_{\xi_{0}}(x,x+y)|G_{\xi_{0}+\xi}(x,x+y)>G_{\xi_{0}}(x,x+y)]

\displaystyle=\lim_{\xi\searrow 0}\frac{\sigma^{2}y}{-2\xi\sigma^{2}y\Phi\bigl(-\xi\sqrt{\frac{\sigma^{2}y}{2}}\bigr)+2\sqrt{\frac{\sigma^{2}y}{\pi}}}=\frac{\sqrt{\pi\sigma^{2}y}}{2}>0

Along with (
2.67
), Theorem
2.5.4
gives us

\mathbb{E}^{\sigma}[\#\{\eta\in[\xi_{0},\xi]:G_{\eta-}(x,x+y)<G_{\eta+}(x,x+y)\}]=2\xi\sqrt{\frac{\sigma^{2}y}{\pi}}.

The fact that there are infinitely many jumps over all
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
is as follows: We recall that
\xi\mapsto G^{\sigma}_{\xi}(x,x+y)
is nondecreasing. Thus, it has almost sure limits as
\xi\to\pm\infty
. Since
G^{\sigma}_{\xi}(x,x+y)
is Gaussian with mean
\sigma^{2}\xi y
and variance
\sigma^{2}y
, for all
z\in\mathbb{R}
, Markov’s inequality implies, for any
z\in\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle\lim_{\xi\to+\infty}\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}(G_{\xi}(x,x+y)\leq z)

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{\xi\to+\infty}\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}(|G_{\xi}(x,x+y)-\sigma^{2}\xi y|>\sigma^{2}\xi y-z)

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{\xi\to+\infty}\frac{\sigma^{2}y}{(\sigma^{2}\xi y-z)^{2}}=0.

so
\lim_{\xi\to\pm\infty}G^{\sigma}_{\xi}(x,x+y)=\pm\infty
, almost surely. Similarly,
\lim_{\xi\to-\infty}G^{\sigma}_{\xi}(x,x+y)=-\infty
. Since the set of jumps is discrete, there must be infinitely many of them on the real line, and they are unbounded for both positive and negative directions.
∎

Proof of Theorem
2.5.2
.

Item
(i)
:
The monotonicity of
y\mapsto f(y):=G_{\xi_{2}}(x,x+y)-G_{\xi_{1}}(x,x+y)
follows by
G_{\xi_{1}}\;{\leq}_{\rm inc}\;G_{\xi_{2}}
(Proposition
2.3.1
(iii)
): for
w<y
,

f(w,y)=G_{\xi_{2}}(x+w,x+y)-G_{\xi_{1}}(x+w,x+y)\geq 0.

Item
(ii)

Because
G_{\xi-}\;{\leq}_{\rm inc}\;G_{\xi+}
, Lemma
A.1.2
implies that for
a\leq x<y\leq b
,

0\leq G_{\xi+}(x,y)-G_{\xi-}(x,y)\leq G_{\xi+}(a,b)-G_{\xi-}(a,b),

giving the inclusion
\Xi_{G}(x,y)\subseteq\Xi_{G}(a,b)
.

Items
(iii)
–
(iv)
:
For a fixed
x\neq y
, Theorem
2.5.1
states that,
\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}
almost surely,
\Xi_{G}(a,b)
is a discrete and infinite set, and
\xi\mapsto G_{\xi}(a,b)
is a right-continuous step function. Thus,
\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}
-almost surely, for any
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
, there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that, if
\xi-\varepsilon<\alpha<\xi<\beta<\xi+\varepsilon
,

G_{\alpha}(a,b)=G_{\xi-}(a,b),\qquad\text{and}\qquad G_{\xi}(a,b)=G_{\beta}(a,b).

(2.68)

We consider the
\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}
almost sure event such a
\varepsilon>0
exists for all
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
and each rational pair
a\neq b
. To show Item
(iii)
, it suffices to take the compact set to be
[a,b]
for rational
a<b
. Then, for every
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
, there exists
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(\xi,a,b)
so that for
\xi-\varepsilon<\alpha<\xi<\beta<\xi+\varepsilon
(
2.68
) holds. Then, by
G_{\xi}\;{\leq}_{\rm inc}\;G_{\beta}
and Lemma
A.1.2
, for
a\leq x\leq y\leq b
, and all such
\alpha,\beta
,

0\leq G_{\beta}(x,y)-G_{\xi}(x,y)\leq G_{\beta}(a,b)-G_{\xi}(a,b)=0.

(2.69)

A symmetric argument shows that
G_{\alpha}(x,y)=G_{\xi-}(x,y)
.

The result of Item
(iv)
that
\Xi_{G}(x,y)
is countably infinite and discrete for all
x<y
is as follows. Without loss of generality, assume
x<y
. Choose rational values
a,b,c,d
with
a<x<c<d<y<b
. Then Item
(ii)
implies

\Xi_{G}(c,d)\subseteq\Xi_{G}(x,y)\subseteq\Xi_{G}(a,b),

and recall that
\Xi_{G}(b,c)
and
\Xi(a,d)
are both infinite and discrete. Furthermore, (
2.69
) implies that
\xi\mapsto G_{\xi}(x,y)
can only increase when
\xi\mapsto G_{\xi}(a,b)
increases, so
\xi\mapsto G_{\xi}(x,y)
is also a step function. The limits as
\xi\to\pm\infty
follow by a similar monotonicity argument.

Item
(v)
:
We handle the case for
S_{x}^{+}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
, and the other follows analogously. Recall the definition (
2.52
)

S_{x}^{+}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})=\inf\{y>0:G_{\xi_{2}}(x,x+y)>G_{\xi_{1}}(x,x+y)\}.

For shorthand, let
S=S_{x}^{+}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
. By definition of the nondecreasing property in Item
(i)
, the function
\xi\mapsto G_{\xi}(x,x+S)
is constant in the interval
[\xi_{1},\xi_{2}]
, but for every
\varepsilon>0
, the function
\xi\mapsto G_{\xi}(x,x+S+\varepsilon)
is not constant in the interval
[\xi_{1},\xi_{2}]
. Since
\xi\mapsto G_{\xi}(x,x+S+\varepsilon)
is a step function by Item
(iv)
, it follows that
[\xi_{1},\xi_{2}]\cap\Xi_{G}(x,x+S)=\varnothing
, but
[\xi_{1},\xi_{2}]\cap\Xi_{G}(x,x+S+\varepsilon)\neq\varnothing
for all
\varepsilon>0
. Since each
\Xi_{G}(x,x+S+\varepsilon)
contains only finitely many values in
[\xi_{1},\xi_{2}]
(Item
(iv)
), and since
\Xi_{G}(x,x+S+\varepsilon)
decreases as
\varepsilon
decreases (Item
(ii)
), there must be some
\xi^{\star}\in[\xi_{1},\xi_{2}]
so that, for every
\varepsilon>0

\xi^{\star}\in[\xi_{1},\xi_{2}]\cap\Xi_{G}(x,x+S+\varepsilon)
. Then,
G_{\xi^{\star}-}(x,x+S)=G_{\xi^{\star}+}(x,x+S)
, but
G_{\xi^{\star}-}(x,x+S+\varepsilon)<G_{\xi^{\star}+}(x,x+S+\varepsilon)
for all
\varepsilon>0
. Hence,

S=\inf\{y>0:G_{\xi^{\star}+}(x,x+y)>G_{\xi^{\star}-}(x,x+y)\}.

Item
(vi)
:
The density of
\Xi_{G}
follows directly from Item
(v)
.
∎

2.6
Random measures and Palm kernels

Figure 2.3
:
The SH difference profile
J_{\xi}(x)
. The function vanishes in a nondegenerate random neighborhood
of
x=0
and evolves as two independent Brownian local times to the left and right.

For
G\in D(\mathbb{R},C(\mathbb{R}))
, define the process of jumps

J:=\{J_{\xi}\}_{\xi\hskip 0.5pt\in\hskip 0.5pt\mathbb{R}}=\{G_{\xi}-G_{\xi-}\}_{\xi\hskip 0.5pt\in\hskip 0.5pt\mathbb{R}}

(2.70)

By Theorem
2.5.2
, for each
\sigma>0
,
\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}
-almost surely, either

J_{\xi}
vanishes identically (when
\xi\notin\Xi_{G}
or
J_{\xi}
is a nondecreasing continuous function that vanishes in a nondegenerate (random) neighborhood of the origin. We use
J^{\sigma}=\{J^{\sigma}_{\xi}\}_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}}
to denote the process when
G=G^{\sigma}
, without reference to the measure
\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}
. By Theorem
2.3.2
(i)
,
(iii)
,
