\{J^{\sigma}_{\xi+\eta}(y+x)-J^{\sigma}_{\xi+\eta}(y):x\in\mathbb{R}\}_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}}\;\overset{d}{=}\;\{J^{\sigma}_{\xi}(x):x\in\mathbb{R}\}_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}}\quad\forall\hskip 0.5pty,\eta\in\mathbb{R}.

(2.71)

The goal of this section is to prove the following.

Theorem 2.6.1
.

Let
\sigma>0
, and let
G^{\sigma}
be the SH. For
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
and
J^{\sigma}_{\xi}
defined above, let

\tau_{\xi}^{\sigma}=\inf\{x>0:J^{\sigma}_{\xi}(x)>0\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad{\overleftarrow{\tau^{\sigma}_{\xi}}}=\inf\{x>0:-J^{\sigma}_{\xi}(-x)>0\}

denote the points to the right and left of the origin beyond which
G_{\xi+}
and
G_{\xi-}
separate, if ever. Then, conditionally on
\xi\in\Xi_{G^{\sigma}}
in the appropriate Palm sense, the restarted functions

x\mapsto J^{\sigma}_{\xi}(x+\tau_{\xi}^{\sigma})-J^{\sigma}_{\xi}(\tau_{\xi}^{\sigma})\quad\text{ and }\quad x\mapsto-J_{\xi}^{\sigma}(-x-{\overleftarrow{\tau_{\xi}^{\sigma}}})+J^{\sigma}_{\xi}(-{\overleftarrow{\tau_{\xi}^{\sigma}}}),\quad x\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0},

are equal in distribution to two independent running maximums of Brownian motion with diffusivity
\sigma^{2}
and zero drift. In particular, they are equal in distribution to two independent appropriately normalized versions of Brownian local time. See Figure
2.3
.

Defining the appropriate sense of Palm conditioning requires some care. The set
\Xi_{G^{\sigma}}
is almost surely dense in
\mathbb{R}
, so we instead condition on pairs
(\xi,\tau_{\xi}^{\sigma})
, where
\xi\in\Xi_{G^{\sigma}}
. A more precise version of Theorem
2.6.1
is proved as Theorem
2.6.11
.

Theorem
2.6.1
has deep geometric significance for the study of semi-infinite geodesics in the directed landscape in Chapter
4
. For
\xi\in\Xi_{G}
, the function
x\mapsto J_{\xi}(x)
is a nondecreasing function and therefore defines a random Lebesgue-Stieltjes measure. The support of this measure, up to the removal of a countable set, is interpreted in Theorem
4.7.1
as the set of points with two disjoint semi-infinite geodesics in direction
\xi
.

2.6.1
The difference profile for positive
x

We first study the functions
J_{\xi}(x)
for
x\geq 0
, under the measure
\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}
. Approximate
J
by a process
J^{N}
defined on dyadic rational
\xi
. For
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
let

J^{N}_{\xi_{i}}=G_{\xi_{i}}-G_{\xi_{i-1}}\qquad\text{for }\ \xi_{i}=\xi^{N}_{i}=i2^{-N}\ \text{ and }\ i\in\mathbb{Z}.

(2.72)

For
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, let

\tau_{\xi_{i}}^{N}=\inf\{x>0:J^{N}_{\xi_{i}}(x)>0\}=S_{0}^{+}(\xi_{i-1},\xi_{i}).

(2.73)

Since the
G_{\xi_{i}}
have different drifts for different values of
i
,
\tau_{\xi_{i}}^{N}<\infty
almost surely. For
f\in C(\mathbb{R})
and
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
, let

[f]^{\tau}\in C(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
denote the restarted function

[f]^{\tau}(x)=f(\tau+x)-f(\tau)\ \text{ for }x\in[0,\infty).

(2.74)

Denote by
\mathcal{D}_{\alpha}^{\sigma}

the distribution on
C(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
of the running maximum of a Brownian motion with drift
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
and diffusivity
\sqrt{2\sigma^{2}}
. That is, if
X
denotes standard Brownian motion, then

\mathcal{D}_{\alpha}^{\sigma}(A)=\mathbb{P}\bigl\{\bigl[\sup_{0\leq u\leq s}\sqrt{2\sigma^{2}}X(u)+\alpha u\bigr]_{s\in[0,\infty)}\in A\bigr\}

for Borel sets
A\subset C(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
. When the drift vanishes (
\alpha=0
) we abbreviate
\mathcal{D}^{\sigma}=\mathcal{D}_{0}^{\sigma}
.

Lemma 2.6.2
.

On a probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},P)
,
Let
Y=\{Y(x):x\geq 0\}
be a Brownian motion with drift
\alpha
and diffusivity
\sqrt{2\sigma^{2}}
. Let
W
be an almost surely negative random variable independent of
B_{\alpha}^{\sigma}
. Let

\theta=\inf\{x>0:W+B_{\alpha}^{\sigma}(x)\geq 0\}.

Then, for all
x>0
,

P\Big(\Big[\sup_{0\leq s\leq\theta+u}W+Y(s)\Big]^{+}_{u\in[0,\infty)}\in{\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt}\,\Big|\,\theta=x\Big)=\mathcal{D}_{\alpha}^{\sigma}({\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt}).

(2.75)

In particular,

P\Big(\Big[\sup_{0\leq s\leq\theta+u}W+Y(s)\Big]^{+}_{u\in[0,\infty)}\in{\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt}\Big)=\mathcal{D}_{\alpha}^{\sigma}({\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt})

(2.76)

Proof.

Let
A\in\mathcal{B}(C(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}))
and
\theta>0
. Below, notice that
Y(\theta)=-W
. Then, noting that
\theta
is a stopping time with respect to the filtration
\mathcal{F}_{y}=\sigma\big(W,\{Y(x)\}_{x\in[0,y]}\big)
, we use the strong Markov property to restart at time
\theta
.

\displaystyle P\Big(\Big[\sup_{0\leq s\leq\theta+u}W+Y(s)\Big]^{+}_{u\in[0,\infty)}\in A\,\Big|\,\theta=x\Big)

(2.77)

\displaystyle=P\Big(\Bigl[\,\sup_{\theta\leq s\leq\theta+u}W+Y(s)\Bigr]_{u\in[0,\infty)}\in A\,\Big|\,\theta=x\Big)

\displaystyle=P\Big(\Bigl[\,\sup_{0\leq s\leq u}Y(\theta+s)-Y(\theta)\Bigr]_{u\in[0,\infty)}\in A\,\Big|\,\theta=x\Big)

\displaystyle=P\Big(\Bigl[\,\sup_{0\leq s\leq u}Y(s)\Bigr]_{u\in[0,\infty)}\in A\Big)=\mathcal{D}_{\alpha}^{\sigma}(A).

The first equality came from

\sup_{s\in[0,\theta]}\{W+Y(s)\}=0\leq\sup_{s\in[\theta\!,\,\theta+u]}\{W+Y(s)\}\quad\text{for all }u\geq 0.

The claim of (
2.75
) has now been verified. Equation (
2.76
) follows because

\begin{aligned} &\quad\;P\Big(\Big[\sup_{0\leq s\leq\theta+u}W+Y(s)\Big]^{+}_{u\in[0,\infty)}\in A\Big)\\
&=\int P\Big(\Big[\sup_{0\leq s\leq\theta+u}W+Y(s)\Big]^{+}_{u\in[0,\infty)}\in A\,\Big|\,\theta=x\Big)\,d\theta(x).\end{aligned}\qed

Corollary 2.6.3
.

Let
\sigma>0

\alpha_{N}=\sigma^{2}2^{-N}
. Then for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
and
x>0
,

\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}\big(\big[J^{N}_{\xi_{i}}\big]^{\tau^{N}_{\xi_{i}}}\in{\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt}\,\,\big|\,\tau^{N}_{\xi_{i}}=x\big)=\mathcal{D}^{\sigma}_{\alpha_{N}}({\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt}).

(2.78)

Proof.

From the definition of the finite-dimensional marginals of the stationary horizon (Proposition
2.3.1
(ii)
), we have

(G^{\sigma}_{\xi_{i-1}},G^{\sigma}_{\xi_{i}})\overset{d}{=}(Z^{1},D(Z^{2},Z^{1})),

(2.79)

where
Z^{1},Z^{2}
are independent two-sided Brownian motions, each with diffusivity
\sigma
and drifts
\sigma^{2}\xi_{i-1},\sigma^{2}\xi_{i}
, respectively. Then,
Y:=Z^{2}-Z^{1}
is a Brownian motion with diffusivity
\sqrt{2\sigma^{2}}
and drift
\sigma^{2}(\xi_{i}-\xi_{i-1})=\sigma^{2}2^{-N}
. Set
\widetilde{J}^{N}(y)=D(Z^{2},Z^{1})(y)-Z^{1}(y)
. By Lemma
2.3.3
,

\displaystyle\widetilde{J}^{N}(y)=\Bigl(\sup_{0\leq x\leq y}\{Y(x)\}-\sup_{-\infty<x\leq 0}\{Y(x)\}\Bigr)^{+}=\Bigl[\sup_{0\leq x\leq y}W+Y(x)\Bigr],

(2.80)

where
W=-\sup_{-\infty<x\leq 0}\{Y(x)\}
is an almost surely negative random variable, independent of
\{Y(x):x\geq 0\}
.
Define

\theta^{N}=\inf\{x>0:\widetilde{J}^{N}(x)>0\}=\inf\{x>0:Y(x)\geq 0\}.

(2.81)

Hence, now
(J^{N}_{\xi_{i}},\tau^{N}_{\xi_{i}})\overset{d}{=}(\widetilde{J}^{N},\theta^{N})
, and the corollary now follows from Lemma
2.6.2
.
∎

For
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
let

\tau_{\xi}=\inf\{x\geq 0:J_{\xi}(x)>0\}.

(2.82)

The connection with the discrete counterpart in (
2.73
) is

\tau^{N}_{\xi_{i}}=\min\{\tau_{\xi}:\xi\in(\xi_{i-1},\xi_{i}]\}.

(2.83)

On the space
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{R}
define the random point measure and its mean measure

\Gamma=\sum_{(\tau_{\xi},\xi):\tau_{\xi}<\infty}\delta_{(\tau_{\xi},\xi)}\quad\text{ and }\quad\lambda_{\Gamma}^{\sigma}({\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt}):=\mathbb{E}^{\sigma}[\hskip 0.9pt{\Gamma}({\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt})\hskip 0.9pt].

(2.84)

The point process
\Gamma
records the jump directions
\xi
and the points
\tau_{\xi}
where
G_{\xi}
and
G_{\xi-}
separate on
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
. Theorem
2.5.2
ensures that
\Gamma
and
\lambda_{\Gamma}
are locally finite,
\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}
-almost surely.
It will cause no confusion to use the same symbol
\Gamma
to denote the random set

\Gamma=\{(\tau_{\xi},\xi):\xi\in\mathbb{R},\tau_{\xi}<\infty\}.

Then also
\lambda^{\sigma}_{\Gamma}({\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt})=\mathbb{E}^{\sigma}(\hskip 0.9pt|\Gamma\cap{\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt}|\hskip 0.9pt)
where
|{\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt}|
denotes cardinality.
The counterparts for the approximating process are

\Gamma^{(N)}=\{(\tau^{N}_{\xi_{i}},\xi_{i}):i\in\mathbb{Z},\tau^{N}_{\xi_{i}}<\infty\}\quad\text{and}\quad\lambda_{\Gamma}^{(N)}({\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt}):=\mathbb{E}^{\sigma}(|\Gamma^{(N)}\cap{\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt}|),

(2.85)

where, at the risk of slightly abusing notation, we have dropped the dependence of
\sigma
in the notation for
\lambda_{\Gamma}^{(N)}
.

The dyadic partition in (
2.72
) imposes a certain monotonicity as
N
increases:
\tau_{\xi}
values can be added but not removed. The
\xi
-coordinates that are not dyadic rationals move as the partition refines. So we have

\{\tau^{N}_{\xi_{i}}:(\tau^{N}_{\xi_{i}},\xi_{i})\in\Gamma^{(N)}\}\subset\{\tau^{N+1}_{\xi_{i}}:(\tau^{N+1}_{\xi_{i}},\xi_{i})\in\Gamma^{(N+1)}\}\subset\{\tau_{\xi}:(\tau_{\xi},\xi)\in\Gamma\}.

(2.86)

Lemma 2.6.4
.

For
\sigma>0
, the measure
\lambda_{\Gamma}^{\sigma}
and Lebesgue measure
m
are mutually absolutely continuous on
\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times\mathbb{R}
.
The Radon-Nikodym derivative is given by

\frac{d\lambda_{\Gamma}^{\sigma}}{dm}(\tau,\xi)=\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{\pi\tau}}\qquad\text{for }\ (\tau,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times\mathbb{R}.

(2.87)

Proof.

Let
\xi\in\mathbb{R},y>0
, and
\varepsilon>0
. Theorem
2.5.1
states that

\mathbb{E}^{\sigma}[\#\{\eta\in(\xi,\xi+\varepsilon]:G_{\eta-}(y)<G_{\eta+}(y)\}]=2\varepsilon\sqrt{\frac{\sigma^{2}y}{\pi}}.

Further, observe that, for
\xi\in\mathbb{R},\tau>0
, and
\delta,\varepsilon>0
.

\;\lambda^{\sigma}_{\Gamma}\big((\tau,\tau+\delta]\times(\xi,\xi+\varepsilon]\big)\\
=\mathbb{E}^{\sigma}[\#\{\eta\in(\xi,\xi+\varepsilon]:G_{\eta-}(\tau+\delta)<G_{\eta+}(\tau+\delta)\}]\\
-\mathbb{E}^{\sigma}[\#\{\eta\in(\xi,\xi+\varepsilon]:G_{\eta-}(\tau)<G_{\eta+}(\tau)\}]

because
(\tau_{\eta},\eta)\in\Gamma\cap(\tau,\tau+\delta]\times(\xi,\xi+\varepsilon]
if and only if
\eta\in\cap(\xi,\xi+\varepsilon]
,
\tau_{\eta}<\infty
, and the splitting of
G_{\eta-}
and
G_{\eta}
occurred in
(0,\tau+\delta]
, but not in
[0,\tau]
.
Hence,

\lambda^{\sigma}_{\Gamma}\big((\tau,\tau+\delta]\times(\xi,\xi+\varepsilon]\big)=\frac{2\sigma\varepsilon}{\sqrt{\pi}}(\sqrt{\tau+\delta}-\sqrt{\tau})=\int_{\xi}^{\xi+\varepsilon}\int_{\tau}^{\tau+\delta}\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{\pi x}}\,dx\,d\alpha.\qed

By (
2.87
),
\lambda_{\Gamma}
does not have a finite marginal on the
\xi
-component, as expected since the set
\Xi_{G}
is dense,
\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}
almost surely. Below we do Palm conditioning on the pair
(\tau_{\xi},\xi)\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times\Xi_{G}
and not on the jump directions
\xi\in\Xi_{G}
alone.

Lemma 2.6.5
.

Let
A\subseteq C(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
be a Borel set. Then for any open rectangle
R=(a,b)\times(c,d)\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{R}
,

\mathbb{E^{\sigma}}\Bigl[\;\sum_{(\tau,\,\xi)\,\in\,\Gamma}\mathbf{1}_{A}([J_{\xi}]^{\tau})\hskip 0.9pt\mathbf{1}_{R}(\tau,\xi)\Bigr]=\lambda^{\sigma}_{\Gamma}(R)\hskip 0.9pt\mathcal{D}^{\sigma}(A).

(2.88)

Proof.

It suffices to prove (
2.88
) for continuity sets
A
of the distribution
\mathcal{D}^{\sigma}
of the type
A=\{f\in C(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}):f|_{[0,k]}\in A_{k}\}

for
k>0
and Borel
A_{k}\subseteq C[0,k]
.
Such sets form a
\pi
-system that generates the Borel
\sigma
-algebra of
C(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
.

We prove a discrete counterpart of (
2.88
) for
J^{N}
. Condition on
\tau^{N}_{\xi_{i}}
and use (
2.78
):

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{\sigma}\Big(\sum_{(\tau^{N}_{\xi_{i}}\!,\,\xi_{i})\in R\cap\Gamma^{(N)}}\mathbf{1}_{A}\big([J^{N}_{\xi_{i}}]^{\tau^{N}_{\xi_{i}}}\big)\Big)=\mathbb{E}^{\sigma}\Big(\sum_{\xi_{i}\in(c,d)}\mathbf{1}_{A}\big([J^{N}_{\xi_{i}}]^{\tau^{N}_{\xi_{i}}}\big)\mathbf{1}_{(a,b)}\big(\tau^{N}_{\xi_{i}}\big)\Big)

(2.89)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\xi_{i}\in(c,d)}\mathbb{E}^{\sigma}\bigg(\mathbf{1}_{(a,b)}\big(\tau^{N}_{\xi_{i}}\big)\,\mathbb{E}^{\sigma}\Big[\mathbf{1}_{A}\big([J^{N}_{\xi_{i}}]^{\tau^{N}_{\xi_{i}}}\big)\hskip 0.9pt\Big|\hskip 0.9pt\tau^{N}_{\xi_{i}}\Big]\bigg)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq4}}}{{=}}\sum_{\xi_{i}\in(c,d)}\mathbb{P}\big(\tau^{N}_{\xi_{i}}\in(a,b)\big)\mathcal{D}_{\alpha_{N}}^{\sigma}(A)=\mathcal{D}_{\alpha_{N}}^{\sigma}(A)\hskip 0.9pt\lambda_{\Gamma}^{(N)}(R).

To conclude the proof, we check that (
2.88
) arises as we let
N\to\infty
in the first and last member of the string of equalities above.
\mathcal{D}^{\sigma}_{\alpha_{N}}(A)\to\mathcal{D}^{\sigma}(A)
by the continuity of
\alpha\mapsto\mathcal{D}^{\sigma}_{\alpha}
in the weak topology of measures on
C(\mathbb{R})
with respect to uniform convergence on compact sets, and the assumption that
A
is a continuity set.

As an intermediate step, we verify that
\forall k>0
,

\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{U}_{N}^{k}}\to 1
almost surely
for the events

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{N}^{k}

\displaystyle=\bigl\{\,|\Gamma^{(N)}\cap R|=|\Gamma\cap R|\text{ \,and for every $(\tau,\xi)\in\Gamma\cap R$ there is a unique}

(2.90)

\displaystyle\qquad\text{ $(\tau^{N}_{\xi_{i}},\xi_{i})\in\Gamma^{(N)}\cap R$
such that $[J^{N}_{\xi_{i}}]^{\tau^{N}_{\xi_{i}}}\big|_{[0,k]}=[J_{\xi}]^{\tau_{\xi}}\big|_{[0,k]}$}\bigr\}.

\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}
almost surely,
\Gamma\cap R
is finite, and none of its points lie on the boundary of
R
(Proposition
2.3.1
(iv)
and Theorem
2.5.1
). For any such realization, the condition (
2.90
) holds when (i) all points
(\tau_{\xi},\xi)\in\Gamma\cap R
lie in distinct rectangles
(a,b)\times(\xi_{i-1},\xi_{i}]\subset(a,b)\times(c,d)
, (ii) when no point
(\tau^{N}_{\xi_{i}},\xi_{i})\in\Gamma^{(N)}\cap R
is generated by a point
(\tau_{\xi},\xi)\in\Gamma
outside
R
, and (iii) when
N
is large enough so that for the unique
i
with
\xi_{i}<\xi\leq\xi_{i+1}
,
G_{\xi-}(x)=G_{\xi_{i}}(x)
and
G_{\xi+}(x)=G_{\xi_{i+1}}(x)
for all
x\in[0,\tau_{\xi}+k]
. By Theorem
2.5.2
(iv)
, this happens for all the finitely many
(\tau,\xi)\in\Gamma\cap R
when the mesh
2^{-N}
is fine enough. Thus, for each
k>0
, almost every realization lies eventually in
\mathcal{U}_{N}^{k}
.

We prove that

\lambda_{\Gamma}^{(N)}(R)\to\lambda_{\Gamma}(R)
. The paragraph above gave
|\Gamma^{(N)}\cap R|\to|\Gamma\cap R|
almost surely. We also have
|\Gamma^{(N)}\cap R|\leq|\Gamma\cap((a,b)\times(c-1,d))|
because (
2.83
) shows that each point
(\tau^{N}_{\xi_{i}},\xi_{i})
that is not matched to a unique point
(\tau_{\xi},\xi)\in\Gamma\cap R
must be generated by some point
(\tau_{\xi},\xi)\in\Gamma\cap((a,b)\times(c-1,d))
. The limit
\lambda_{\Gamma}^{(N)}(R)\to\lambda^{\sigma}_{\Gamma}(R)
comes now from dominated convergence.

It remains to show that

\mathbb{E}^{\sigma}\Big(\sum_{(\tau^{N}_{\xi_{i}}\!,\,\xi_{i})\in R\cap\Gamma^{(N)}}\mathbf{1}_{A}([J^{N}_{\xi_{i}}]^{\tau^{N}_{\xi_{i}}})\Big)\underset{N\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}^{\sigma}\Big(\sum_{(\tau_{\xi},\xi)\in R\cap\Gamma}\mathbf{1}_{A}([J_{\xi}]^{\tau_{\xi}})\Big).

This follows by choosing
k>0
so that
A
depends only on the domain
[0,k]
. Then, the difference in absolute values in the display below vanishes on
\mathcal{U}_{N}^{k}
.

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}^{\sigma}\bigg[\,\Big|\sum_{(\tau^{N}_{\xi_{i}}\!,\,\xi_{i})\in R\cap\Gamma^{(N)}}\mathbf{1}_{A}\bigl([J^{N}_{\xi_{i}}]^{\tau^{N}_{\xi_{i}}}\bigr)-\sum_{(\tau_{\xi},\xi)\in R\cap\Gamma}\mathbf{1}_{A}\bigl([J_{\xi}]^{\tau_{\xi}}\bigr)\Big|\cdot(\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{U}_{N}^{k}}+\mathbf{1}_{(\mathcal{U}_{N}^{k})^{c}})\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{N\to\infty}2\hskip 0.9pt\mathbb{E}^{\sigma}\bigl[\hskip 0.9pt|\Gamma\cap((a,b)\times(c-1,d))|\cdot\mathbf{1}_{(\mathcal{U}^{k}_{N})^{c}}\bigr]=0,

and the last equality follows by dominated convergence.
∎

To describe the distribution of
[J_{\xi}]^{\tau_{\xi}}
, we augment the point measure
\Gamma
of (
2.84
) to a point measure on the space
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{R}\times C(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
:

\Lambda=\sum_{(\tau_{\xi},\,\xi)\,\in\,\Gamma}\delta_{(\tau_{\xi},\,\xi,\,[J_{\xi}]^{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\tau$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\tau$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\tau$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\tau$}}}_{\!\!\xi}})}.

(2.91)

The
Palm kernel
of
[J_{\xi}]^{\tau_{\xi}}
with respect to
\Gamma
is the stochastic kernel
Q^{\sigma}
from
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{R}
into
C(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
that satisfies the following identity: for every bounded Borel function
\Psi
on
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{R}\times C(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
that is supported on
B\times C(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
for some bounded Borel set
B\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{\sigma}\sum_{(\tau_{\xi},\,\xi)\,\in\,B\hskip 0.7pt\cap\hskip 0.7pt\Gamma}\Psi\bigl(\tau_{\xi},\,\xi,\,[J_{\xi}]^{\tau_{\xi}}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\;\mathbb{E}^{\sigma}\!\!\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{R}\times C(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})}\!\!\Psi(\tau,\xi,h)\,\Lambda(d\tau,d\xi,dh)

(2.92)

\displaystyle=\;\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{R}}\lambda^{\sigma}_{\Gamma}(d\tau,d\xi)\int\limits_{C(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})}Q^{\sigma}(\tau,\xi,dh)\,\Psi(\tau,\xi,h).

For more background on the general theory of Palm conditioning, we refer the reader to
[
95
]
. The first equality above is a restatement of the definition of
\Lambda
and included to give greater clarity to the next theorem.
The key result of this section is this characterization of
Q^{\sigma}
.

Theorem 2.6.6
.

For
Lebesgue-almost every
(\tau,\xi)
,
Q^{\sigma}(\tau,\xi,{\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt})=\mathcal{D^{\sigma}}({\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt})
, the distribution of the running maximum of a Brownian motion with diffusivity
\sqrt{2\sigma^{2}}
.

Proof.

This comes from Lemma
2.6.5
:
take
\Psi(\tau,\xi,h)=\mathbf{1}_{R}(\tau,\xi)\mathbf{1}_{A}(h)
in (
2.92
) and note that the left-hand side of (
2.88
) is exactly the left-hand side of (
2.92
). Lemma
2.6.4
turns
\lambda_{\Gamma}
-almost everywhere into Lebesgue-almost everywhere.
∎

Denote the set of directions
\xi
for which
G_{\xi}
and
G_{\xi-}
separate on
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
by

\displaystyle\Xi_{G}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\{\xi\in\mathbb{R}:\tau_{\xi}<\infty\}.

We show in Corollary
2.6.8
that
\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}(\Xi_{G}^{\prime}=\Xi_{G})=1
(recall the definition of
\Xi_{G}
in (
2.63
)), but it is not immediately obvious that these should be the same set. The key to proving equality is the following theorem.

Theorem 2.6.7
.

Let
A\subseteq C(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
be a Borel set such that
\mathcal{D}(A)=0
. Then

\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}\big(\exists\xi\in\Xi_{G}^{\prime}:[J_{\xi}]^{\tau_{\xi}}\in A\big)=0.

(2.93)

Proof.

Let
R_{N}=(0,N)\times(-N,N)
. Since
\xi\in\Xi_{G}^{\prime}
means that
\tau_{\xi}<\infty
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}\big(\exists\xi\in\Xi_{G}:[J_{\xi}]^{\tau_{\xi}}\in A\big)=\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}\big(\exists\xi\in\Xi_{G}:(\tau_{\xi},\xi)\in R_{N},[J_{\xi}]^{\tau_{\xi}}\in A\big)

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\leq\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E^{\sigma}}\sum_{(\tau,\,\xi)\in\Gamma}\mathbf{1}_{A}([J_{\xi}]^{\tau})\hskip 0.9pt\mathbf{1}_{R_{N}}(\tau,\xi)\overset{\eqref{Omr2}}{=}\lim_{N\to\infty}\lambda_{\Gamma}(R_{N})\hskip 0.9pt\mathcal{D}^{\sigma}(A)=0.\qquad\ \qed

Corollary 2.6.8
.

For all
\sigma>0
,
\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}(\Xi_{G}^{\prime}=\Xi_{G})=1
. Furthermore,

\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}(\forall\xi\in\Xi_{G}\;\;\lim_{x\to\pm\infty}J_{\xi}(x)=\pm\infty)=1.

Proof.

By Theorem
2.6.7
and the associated fact for the running max of a Brownian motion,

\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}(\forall\xi\in\Xi_{G}^{\prime},\lim_{x\to+\infty}J_{\xi}(x)=+\infty)=1.

(2.94)

Recall the notation
[J_{\xi}]^{m}(x)=J_{\xi}(m+x)-J_{\xi}(m)
(
2.74
). By definition,

\Xi_{G}^{\prime}=\{\xi\in\mathbb{R}:J_{\xi}(x)\neq 0\text{ for some }x>0\}\subseteq\{\xi\in\mathbb{R}:J_{\xi}(x)\neq 0\text{ for some }x\in\mathbb{R}\}=\Xi_{G}.

We show that,
\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}
-almost surely, if
J_{\xi}(x)\neq 0
for some
x<0
, then
J_{\xi}(x)\neq 0
for some
x>0
. If not, then there exist
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
and
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{<0}
such that
[J_{\xi}]^{m}|_{[0,\infty)}\neq 0
, but
[J_{\xi}]^{m}|_{[-m,\infty)}
is constant. In particular,
[J_{\xi}]^{m}|_{[0,\infty)}
is bounded. Let
\tau^{m}_{\xi}=\inf\{x>0:[J_{\xi}]^{m}(x)>0\}
. Then,
[J_{\xi}]^{m}|_{[0,\infty)}\neq 0
iff
\tau^{m}_{\xi}<\infty
, and we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}\bigl(\Xi_{G}\neq\Xi_{G}^{\prime}\bigr)\leq\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}_{<0}}\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}\bigl(\exists\xi\in\mathbb{R}:\;\tau^{m}_{\xi}<\infty\text{ but }[J_{\xi}]^{m}|_{[0,\infty)}\text{ is bounded}\bigr)=0.

(2.95)

The probability equals zero by (
2.94
) because by shift invariance (
2.71
),
[J]^{m}\overset{d}{=}J
.
To finish, (
2.94
) proves the limits for
x\to+\infty
. The limits as
x\to-\infty
then follow from (
2.94
) and the reflection invariance of Theorem
2.3.2
(iv)
.
∎

Let
\nu_{f}
denote the Lebesgue-Stieltjes measure of a non-decreasing function
f
on
\mathbb{R}
. Denote the support of
\nu_{f}
by
\text{supp}(\nu_{f})
. The Hausdorff dimension of a set
A
is denoted by
\dim_{H}(A)
(Recall Section
1.5
).

Corollary 2.6.9
.

Consider the Lebesgue-Stieltjes measure
\nu_{J_{\xi}}
for
\xi\in\Xi_{G}
on the entire real line. Then, we have

\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}\big(\forall\xi\in\Xi_{G}:\dim_{H}\big(\text{\rm supp}(\nu_{J_{\xi}})\big)=1/2\big)=1.

(2.96)

Proof.

First, note that

\big\{\exists\xi\in\Xi_{G}:\text{dim}_{H}\big(\text{supp}(\nu_{J_{\xi}})\big)\neq\tfrac{1}{2}\big\}\subseteq\!\bigcup_{m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}}\!\!\big\{\exists\xi\in\Xi_{G}:\text{dim}_{H}\big(\text{supp}(\nu_{J_{\xi}})\cap[m,\infty)\big)\neq\tfrac{1}{2}\big\}.

By shift invariance (
2.71
), it is enough to take
m=0
and show that

\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}\big(\exists\xi\in\Xi_{G}:\text{dim}_{H}\big(\text{supp}(\nu_{J_{\xi}})\cap[0,\infty)\big)\neq 1/2\big)=0.

This last claim follows from Theorem
2.6.7
because the event in question has zero probability for the running maximum of Brownian motion

[
110
, Theorem 4.24 and Exercise 4.12]
.
∎

Remark 2.6.10
.

Representation of the difference of Busemann functions as the running maximum of random walk goes back to
[
16
]
. It was used in
[
32
]
to capture the local universality of geodesics.
The representation of the difference profile as the running maximum of Brownian motion in the point-to-point setup emerges from the Pitman transform
[
68
,
44
]
. Theorem 1 and Corollary 2 in
[
68
]
are point-to-point analogues of our Theorem
2.6.6
and Corollary
2.6.9
. Their proof is different from ours. Although an analogue of the Pitman transform exists in the stationary case
[
31
, Section 3]
, comparing the running maximum of a Brownian motion to the profile requires different tools in the two settings.

2.6.2
Decoupling

By Corollary
2.6.8
, whenever
\xi
is a jump direction, the difference profile
x\mapsto J_{\xi}(x)
for both positive and negative
x
are nontrivial.
We extend Theorem
2.6.6
to show that these two difference profiles are independent and equal in distribution.
We spell out only the modifications needed in the arguments of the previous section. For the difference profile on the left, define for
x\geq 0

{\overleftarrow{J}}_{\xi}(x):=-J_{\xi}(-x)\quad\text{and}\quad{\overleftarrow{\tau_{\xi}}}:=\inf\{x>0:{\overleftarrow{J}}_{\xi}(x)>0\}.

For
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
and
\xi_{i}
as in (
2.72
), the discrete approximations are

{\overleftarrow{J}}^{N}_{\xi_{i}}(x):=-J^{N}_{\xi_{i}}(-x)\quad\text{and}\quad{\overleftarrow{\tau_{\xi_{i}}}}^{N}:=\inf\{x>0:{\overleftarrow{J}}^{N}_{\xi_{i}}(x)>0\}.

The measures
{\overleftarrow{\Gamma}}
,
\lambda_{{\overleftarrow{\Gamma}}}
,
{\overleftarrow{\Gamma}}^{(N)}
, and
\lambda_{{\overleftarrow{\Gamma}}}^{(N)}
are defined as in (
2.84
) and (
2.85
), but now with
({\overleftarrow{\tau_{\xi}}},\xi)
and
({\overleftarrow{\tau_{\xi_{i}}}},\xi_{i})
.
Extend the measure
\Lambda
of (
2.91
) with a component for the left profile:

\Lambda^{\prime}=\sum_{({\overleftarrow{\tau_{\xi}}},\,\xi)\,\in\,{\overleftarrow{\Gamma}}}\delta_{({\overleftarrow{\tau_{\xi}}},\,\xi,\;[J_{\xi}]^{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\tau$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\tau$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\tau$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\tau$}}}_{\!\!\xi}},\;[{\overleftarrow{J}}_{\xi}]^{{\overleftarrow{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\tau$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\tau$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\tau$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\tau$}}}}_{\!\!\xi}})}.

Since
\tau_{\xi}<\infty
if and only if
{\overleftarrow{\tau_{\xi}}}<\infty
(Corollary
2.6.8
), it is immaterial whether we sum over
({\tau}_{\xi},\xi)
or
({\overleftarrow{\tau_{\xi}}},\xi)
. The latter is more convenient for the next calculations.

The
Palm kernel
of
\big([J_{\xi}]^{\tau_{\xi}},[{\overleftarrow{J}}_{\xi}]^{{\overleftarrow{\tau_{\xi}}}}\big)
with respect to
{\overleftarrow{\Gamma}}
is the stochastic kernel
Q_{2}^{\sigma}
from
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{R}
into
C(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})\times C(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
that satisfies the following identity: for every bounded Borel function
\Psi
on
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{R}\times C(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})\times C(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
that is supported on
B\times C(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})\times C(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
for some bounded Borel set
B\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{\sigma}\Bigl[\;\sum_{({\overleftarrow{\tau_{\xi}}},\,\xi)\,\in\,B\hskip 0.7pt\cap\hskip 0.7pt{\overleftarrow{\Gamma}}}\Psi\bigl({\overleftarrow{\tau_{\xi}}},\,\xi,\,[J_{\xi}]^{\tau_{\xi}},[{\overleftarrow{J}}_{\xi}]^{{\overleftarrow{\tau_{\xi}}}}\bigr)\Bigr]

(2.97)

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{R}}\lambda_{{\overleftarrow{\Gamma}}}(d{\overleftarrow{\tau}},d\xi)\int\limits_{C(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})\times C(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})}Q_{2}^{\sigma}({\overleftarrow{\tau}},\xi,dh^{1},dh^{2})\,\Psi({\overleftarrow{\tau}},\xi,h^{1},h^{2}).

Theorem 2.6.11
.

For
Lebesgue-almost every
(\tau,\xi)
,
Q_{2}^{\sigma}(\tau,\xi,{\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt})=(\mathcal{D}^{\sigma}\otimes\mathcal{D}^{\sigma})({\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt})
, the product of the distribution of the running maximum of a Brownian motion with diffusivity
\sqrt{2\sigma^{2}}
. In particular, for any Borel set
A\subseteq C(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})\times C(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
such that
(\mathcal{D}^{\sigma}\otimes\mathcal{D}^{\sigma})(A)=0
,

\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}\bigl(\exists\xi\in\Xi_{G}:\bigl([J_{\xi}]^{\tau_{\xi}},[{\overleftarrow{J}}_{\xi}]^{{\overleftarrow{\tau_{\xi}}}}\bigr)\in A\bigr)=0.

Proof.

As in (
2.79
) and (
2.80
), as functions in
C(\mathbb{R})
,

J_{\xi_{i}}^{N}(y)\overset{d}{=}\widetilde{J}_{\xi_{i}}^{N}(y):=\sup_{-\infty<x\leq y}\{Y(x)\}-\sup_{-\infty<x\leq 0}\{Y(x)\},

(2.98)

where
Y
is a two-sided Brownian motion with drift
\alpha_{N}=\sigma^{2}2^{-N}
and diffusivity
\sqrt{2\sigma^{2}}
. Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},P)
be a probability space on which
Y
is defined.
Define two independent sub
\sigma
-algebras of
\mathcal{F}
:

\mathcal{F}_{-}=\sigma(Y(x):x\leq 0),\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathcal{F}_{+}=\sigma(Y(x):x\geq 0).

When
y>0
, (
2.80
) states that

\widetilde{J}_{\xi_{i}}^{N}(y)=\Bigl[W+\sup_{0\leq x\leq y}Y(x)\Bigr]^{+},

(2.99)

where
W=-\sup_{-\infty<x\leq 0}\{Y(x)\}\in\mathcal{F}_{-}
, and
\sup_{0\leq x\leq y}Y(x)\in\mathcal{F}_{+}
. Then, conditional on
\mathcal{F}_{-}
,
W
is constant while the law of
Y(x)
for
x\geq 0
is unchanged. Let

\widetilde{\tau}^{N}_{\xi_{i}}=\inf\{x>0:\widetilde{J}^{N}_{\xi_{i}}(x)>0\},\qquad\text{and}\qquad{\overleftarrow{\widetilde{\tau}_{\xi_{i}}^{N}}}=\inf\{x>0:-\widetilde{J}^{N}_{\xi_{i}}(-x)>0\}

Then, by (
2.99
) and Equation (
2.76
) of Lemma
2.6.2
in the special case where
W
is constant (using the exact same reasoning as in the proof of Corollary
2.6.3
),

P(\big[\widetilde{J}_{\xi_{i}}^{N}\big]^{\widetilde{\tau}_{\xi_{i}}^{N}}\in{\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{-})=P(\big[\widetilde{J}_{\xi_{i}}^{N}\big]^{\widetilde{\tau}_{\xi_{i}}^{N}}\in{\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt}\,|\,W)=\mathcal{D}^{\sigma}_{\alpha_{N}}({\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt}).

(2.100)

For a fixed
i
,
{\overleftarrow{J}}^{N}_{\xi_{i}}
and
J^{N}_{\xi_{i}}
have the same distribution as functions on
\mathbb{R}
. This comes by first applying the reflection invariance in Theorem
2.3.2
(iv)
and then the shift invariance in (
2.71
), shifting the directions by
\xi_{i-1}+\xi_{i}
:

\displaystyle{\overleftarrow{J}}^{N}_{\xi_{i}}(x)

\displaystyle=-{J}^{N}_{\xi_{i}}(-x)=-G^{\sigma}_{\xi_{i}}(-x)+G^{\sigma}_{\xi_{i-1}}(-x)\overset{d}{=}-G^{\sigma}_{-\xi_{i}}(x)+G^{\sigma}_{-\xi_{i-1}}(x)

\displaystyle\overset{d}{=}-G^{\sigma}_{\xi_{i-1}}(x)+G^{\sigma}_{\xi_{i}}(x)={J}^{N}_{\xi_{i}}(x).

By (
2.98
),
({\overleftarrow{\widetilde{J}^{N}}},{\overleftarrow{\widetilde{\tau}^{N}}})\in\mathcal{F}_{-}
. We mimic the calculation in (
2.89
), for two Borel sets
A_{1},A_{2}\subseteq C(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
and an open rectangle
R=(a,b)\times(c,d)\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{R}
:

\displaystyle\quad\;\mathbb{E}^{\sigma}\Big(\sum_{({\overleftarrow{\tau}}^{N}_{\!\!\xi_{i}},\;\xi_{i})\in R\cap{\overleftarrow{\Gamma}}^{(N)}}\mathbf{1}_{A_{1}}\big([J^{N}_{\xi_{i}}]^{\tau^{N}_{\xi_{i}}}\big)\mathbf{1}_{A_{2}}\big([{\overleftarrow{J}}^{N}_{\xi_{i}}]^{{\overleftarrow{\tau}}^{N}_{\xi_{i}}}\big)\Big)

(2.101)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\xi_{i}\in(c,d)}E\bigg(\mathbf{1}_{A_{2}}\big([{\overleftarrow{\widetilde{J}}}^{N}_{\xi_{i}}]^{{\overleftarrow{\widetilde{\tau}}}^{N}_{\xi_{i}}}\big)\;\mathbf{1}_{(a,b)}\big({\overleftarrow{\widetilde{\tau}^{N}_{\xi_{i}}}}\big)E\Big[\big(\mathbf{1}_{A_{1}}\big([\widetilde{J}^{N}_{\xi_{i}}]^{\widetilde{\tau}^{N}_{\xi_{i}}}\big)\hskip 0.9pt\Big|\mathcal{F}_{-}\Big]\bigg)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{F-cond}}}{{=}}\sum_{\xi_{i}\in(c,d)}E\bigg(E\Big[\mathbf{1}_{A_{2}}\big([{\overleftarrow{\widetilde{J}}}^{N}_{\xi_{i}}]^{{\overleftarrow{\widetilde{\tau}}}^{N}_{\xi_{i}}}\big)\;\mathbf{1}_{(a,b)}\big({\overleftarrow{\widetilde{\tau}}}^{N}_{\xi_{i}}\big)\hskip 0.9pt\Big|{\overleftarrow{\widetilde{\tau}}}_{\xi_{i}}^{N}\Big]\bigg)\mathcal{D}^{\sigma}_{\alpha_{N}}(A_{1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\xi_{i}\in(c,d)}E\bigg(\mathbf{1}_{(a,b)}\big({\overleftarrow{\widetilde{\tau}}}^{N}_{\xi_{i}}\big)E\Big[\mathbf{1}_{A_{2}}\big([{\overleftarrow{\widetilde{J}}}^{N}_{\xi_{i}}]^{{\overleftarrow{\widetilde{\tau}}}^{N}_{\xi_{i}}}\big)\;\hskip 0.9pt\Big|{\overleftarrow{\widetilde{\tau}}}_{\xi_{i}}^{N}\Big]\bigg)\mathcal{D}^{\sigma}_{\alpha_{N}}(A_{1})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq4}}}{{=}}\sum_{\xi_{i}\in(c,d)}P\big({\overleftarrow{\widetilde{\tau}}}^{N}_{\xi_{i}}\in(a,b)\big)\mathcal{D}^{\sigma}_{\alpha_{N}}(A_{1})\mathcal{D}^{\sigma}_{\alpha_{N}}(A_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\xi_{i}\in(c,d)}\mathbb{P}^{\sigma}\big({\overleftarrow{\tau}}^{N}_{\xi_{i}}\in(a,b)\big)\mathcal{D}^{\sigma}_{\alpha_{N}}(A_{1})\mathcal{D}^{\sigma}_{\alpha_{N}}(A_{2})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{D}^{\sigma}_{\alpha_{N}}(A_{1})\mathcal{D}^{\sigma}_{\alpha_{N}}(A_{2})\hskip 0.9pt\lambda_{{\overleftarrow{\Gamma}}}^{(N)}(R).

As in the proof of Lemma
2.6.5
, we derive from the above that

\mathbb{E}^{\sigma}\Big(\sum_{({\overleftarrow{\tau}}_{\xi},\xi)\in R\cap{\overleftarrow{\Gamma}}}\mathbf{1}_{A_{1}}\big([J_{\xi}]^{\tau_{\xi}}\big)\mathbf{1}_{A_{2}}\big([{\overleftarrow{J}}_{\xi}]^{{\overleftarrow{\tau}}_{\xi}}\big)\Big)=\mathcal{D}^{\sigma}(A_{1})\mathcal{D}^{\sigma}(A_{2})\hskip 0.9pt\lambda_{{\overleftarrow{\Gamma}}}(R),

(2.102)

through the convergence of line (
2.101
) to the left-hand side of (
2.102
). Instead of the events
\mathcal{U}_{N}^{k}
in (
2.90
), consider

\displaystyle\mathcal{\widetilde{U}}_{N}^{k}

\displaystyle=\bigl\{\,|{\overleftarrow{\Gamma}}^{(N)}\cap R|=|{\overleftarrow{\Gamma}}\cap R|,\text{ \,and $\forall\hskip 0.9pt({\overleftarrow{\tau}},\xi)\in{\overleftarrow{\Gamma}}\cap R$, \ $\exists$ unique $(\tau^{N}_{\xi_{i}},\xi_{i})\in{\overleftarrow{\Gamma}}^{(N)}\cap R$
}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\text{ such that $[J^{N}_{\xi_{i}}]^{\tau^{N}_{\xi_{i}}}\big|_{[0,k]}=[J_{\xi}]^{\tau_{\xi}}\big|_{[0,k]}$ and $[{\overleftarrow{J}}^{N}_{\xi_{i}}]^{{\overleftarrow{\tau}}^{N}_{\xi_{i}}}\big|_{[0,k]}=[{\overleftarrow{J}}_{\xi}]^{{\overleftarrow{\tau}}_{\xi}}\big|_{[0,k]}$}\bigr\}.

For each
k>0
,
\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{\widetilde{U}}_{N}^{k}}\to 1
almost surely, as it did for (
2.90
). Indeed, there are finitely many pairs
({\overleftarrow{\tau}},\xi)\in{\overleftarrow{\Gamma}}\cap R
, and each has a finite forward splitting time
\tau
. All these can be confined in a common compact rectangle.
From here, the proof
continues as for Lemma
2.6.5
and Theorem
2.6.7
.
∎

Chapter 3
The stationary horizon as the unique coupled invariant measure for the KPZ fixed point

3.1
Introduction

In this chapter, we prove that the stationary horizon is the unique invariant coupled distribution and an attractor for the KPZ fixed point (Theorem
3.2.2
). This theorem was originally proved in
[
35
]
using a limit transition from exponential last-passage percolation. In this chapter, we present an alternate proof using a model called Brownian last-passage percolation (BLPP). There are three main ingredients: invariance of the SH for BLPP (proved in Section
3.4.2
), the convergence of BLPP to the directed landscape (DL) from
[
47
,
49
]
(recorded here as Theorem
3.2.1
), and exit point bounds for BLPP from its stationary initial condition. For this, we adapt techniques from
[
57
]
to prove what we call the EJS-Rains identity (short for Emrah-Jenjigian-Seppäiäinen-Rains) for BLPP (Theorem
3.4.8
). A detailed discussion of the hisotry of the EJS-Rains technique and related results comes in Section
3.4
. The proof of Theorem
3.2.2
comes in Section
3.6
.

3.2
Brownian last-passage percolation, the directed landscape, and the KPZ fixed point

3.2.1
Brownian last-passage percolation

On an appropriate probability space, let
\mathbf{B}=\{B_{r}\}_{r\in\mathbb{Z}}
be a field of independent, two-sided standard Brownian motions. For
x\leq y
and
m\leq n
, define

L(x,m;y,n)=\sup\Bigl\{\sum_{r=m}^{n}B_{r}(x_{r-1},x_{r}):x=x_{m-1}\leq x_{m}\leq\cdots\leq x_{n-1}\leq x_{n}=y\Bigr\}

(3.1)

We call this model
Brownian last-passage percolation
(BLPP). Observe that for
n=m
,
L(x,m;y,m)=B_{m}(x,y)
.

x
x_{0}
x_{1}
x_{2}
x_{3}
y
0
1
2
3
4

Figure 3.1
:
The Brownian increments
B_{r}(x_{r-1},x_{r})
for
r=0,\dotsc,4
in (
3.1
) that make up the weight of the path depicted in Figure
3.2
.

Figure
3.1
depicts the Brownian motions on each horizontal level. Maximizing sequences
x_{m-1}\leq x_{m}\leq\cdots\leq x_{n-1}\leq x_{n}
exist in (
3.1
) by continuity of the Brownian motions. For such a sequence, we associate an up-right path consisting of horizontal segments
[x_{r-1},x_{r}]\times\{r\}
and vertical segments
\{x_{r}\}\times[r,r+1]
, as depicted in Figure
3.2
.
Despite the fact that this is a maximal instead of minimal path, by convention, we call this path a
geodesic
between the points.

(x,0)
(y,4)
x_{0}
x_{1}
x_{2}
x_{3}
0
1
2
3
4

Figure 3.2
:
Example of a planar path from
(x,0)
to
(y,4)
, represented by the sequence
(x=x_{-1}\leq x_{0}\leq x_{1}\leq x_{2}\leq x_{3}\leq x_{4}=y)
.

We often consider BLPP with a boundary condition. Let
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be a function satisfying,

\liminf_{x\to-\infty}\frac{f(x)}{x}>0.

For
m<n
and
y\in\mathbb{R}
, define

H_{L}(n,y;m,f)=\sup_{-\infty<x\leq y}\{f(x)+L(x,m+1;y,n)\}.

(3.2)

When
m=0
, we simply write
H_{L}(n,y;f)
.

BLPP was first introduced in
[
73
]
. We call it a semi-discrete model because there is one discrete and one continuous coordinate. As such, it is often used as an intermediate object for studying the directed landscape. A remarkable fact about BLPP (while we do not use this in the present dissertation) is that, for all
n\geq 1
,
L(1,0;n,1)
has the same distribution as the largest eigenvalue of an
n\times n
GUE matrix
[
19
,
75
,
118
]
.

3.2.2
The directed landscape

The directed landscape (DL), originally constructed in
[
47
]
, is a random continuous function
\mathcal{L}:\mathbb{R}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\uparrow$}}}}^{4}\to\mathbb{R}
that arises as the scaling limit of a large class of models in the KPZ universality class, and is expected to be a universal limit of such models. We summarize some key points from
[
47
]
here. The directed landscape satisfies the metric composition law: for
(x,s;y,u)\in\mathbb{R}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\uparrow$}}}}^{4}
and
t\in(s,u)
,

\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,u)=\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\{\mathcal{L}(x,s;z,t)+\mathcal{L}(z,t;y,u)\}.

(3.3)

This implies the reverse triangle inequality: for
s<t<u
and
(x,y,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
\mathcal{L}(x,s;z,t)+\mathcal{L}(z,t;y,u)\leq\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,u)
.
Furthermore, over disjoint time intervals
(s_{i},t_{i})
,
1\leq i\leq n
, the processes
(x,y)\mapsto\mathcal{L}(x,s_{i};y,t_{i})
are independent.

Under the directed landscape, the length of a continuous path
g:[s,t]\to\mathbb{R}
is

\mathcal{L}(g)=\inf_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}}\;\inf_{s=t_{0}<t_{1}<\cdots<t_{k}=t}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}(g(t_{i-1}),t_{i-1};g(t_{i}),t_{i}),

where the second infimum is over all partitions
s=t_{0}<t_{1}<\cdots<t_{k}<t
.
By the reverse triangle inequality,
\mathcal{L}(g)\leq\mathcal{L}(g(s),s;g(t),t)
. We call
g
a
geodesic
if equality holds. When this occurs, every partition
s=t_{0}<t_{1}<\cdots<t_{k}=t
satisfies

\mathcal{L}(g(s),s;g(t),t)=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}(g(t_{i-1}),t_{i-1};g(t_{i}),t_{i}).

For fixed
(x,s;y,t)\in\mathbb{R}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\uparrow$}}}}^{4}
, there exists a unique geodesic between
(x,s)
and
(y,t)
, almost surely
[
47
, Sect. 12–13]
. Across all points, there exist leftmost and rightmost geodesics. The leftmost geodesic
g
is such that, for each
u\in(t,s)
,
g(u)
is the leftmost maximizer of
\mathcal{L}(x,s;z,u)+\mathcal{L}(z,u;y,t)
over
z\in\mathbb{R}
. The analogous fact holds for the rightmost geodesic. Geodesics in the directed landscape have Hölder regularity
\frac{2}{3}-\varepsilon
but not
\frac{2}{3}

[
47
,
48
]
.

Perhaps the most straightforward way to understand the DL is through the limit of last-passage models. BLPP is the first model for which convergence to the DL was shown. While this convergence was first shown in
[
47
]
, the convergence for an arbitrary direction
\rho>0
was later recorded in
[
49
]
.

Theorem 3.2.1
.

[
47
, Theorems 11.1,13.1]

[
49
, Theorems 1.7-1.8]

Fix
\rho\in(0,\infty)
, and let
\chi,\alpha,\beta,\tau
be defined by the relations

\chi^{2}=\rho,\;\;\alpha=2\sqrt{\rho},\;\;\beta=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\rho}},\;\;\chi/\tau^{2}=\frac{1}{4\rho^{3/2}}.

(3.4)

Then, there exists a coupling
(\{L^{N}\}_{N\geq 1},\mathcal{L})
of copies
L^{N}
of
L
(
3.1
) and
\mathcal{L}
so that

\displaystyle\chi N^{-1/3}\Bigl(L^{N}(N\rho s+N^{2/3}\tau x,\lfloor Ns\rfloor;N\rho t+N^{2/3}\tau y,\lfloor Nt\rfloor)-\alpha N(t-s)-\beta\tau N^{2/3}(y-x)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{L}+o_{N})(x,s;y,t),

where the random function
o_{\sigma}
is small in the sense that, for any compact set
K\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\uparrow$}}}}^{4}
,
\sup_{K}|o_{N}|\to 0
almost surely, and there is
c>0
such that

\mathbb{E}\exp\bigl(c\sup_{K}[(o_{N}^{-})^{3/4}+(o_{N}^{+})^{3/2}]\bigr)\to 1.

Furthermore, under this coupling, let
\pi_{N}
be the image of an arbitrary geodesic for
L^{N}
under the linear map satisfying
(N\rho,N)\mapsto(0,1)
and
N^{2/3}(\tau,0)\to(1,0)
. Then, as
N\to\infty
, if the endpoints of
\pi_{N}
converge to points
p,q
with
(p;q)\in\mathbb{R}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\uparrow$}}}}^{4}
, then
\pi_{N}
is precompact in the Hausdorff topology. All subsequential limits are geodesics from
p
to
q
for
\mathcal{L}
.

We remark here that our formulation of BLPP differs from that in
[
47
,
49
]
. there, the index of the discrete level increases as we move downward. To compensate for that, the version of Theorem
3.2.1
we cite has been modified so that the discrete coordinates are
\lfloor Ns\rfloor
and
\lfloor Nt\rfloor
instead of
-\lfloor Ns\rfloor
and
-\lfloor Nt\rfloor
.

3.2.3
The KPZ fixed point

The KPZ fixed point
h(t,{\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt};\mathfrak{h})
started from initial state
\mathfrak{h}
is a Markov process on the space of upper semi-continuous functions. More precisely, its state space is defined as

\displaystyle\operatorname{UC}

\displaystyle=\{\text{ upper semi-continuous functions }\mathfrak{h}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty\}:

(3.5)

\displaystyle\text{ there exist }a,b>0\text{ such that }\quad\mathfrak{h}(x)\leq a+b|x|\text{ for all }x\in\mathbb{R},

\displaystyle\text{ and }\mathfrak{h}(x)>-\infty\text{ for some }x\in\mathbb{R}\}.

The topology on this space is that of local Hausdorff convergence of hypographs. When restricted to continuous functions, this convergence is equivalent to uniform convergence on compact sets. See Section 3.1 in
[
106
]
for more on the topology on the space
\operatorname{UC}
. This subspace of continuous functions is preserved under the KPZ fixed point (see
[
106
]
, or alternatively, Lemma
3.5.3
). The process
\{h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,{\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt};\mathfrak{h})\}_{t>s}
, started from initial data
\mathfrak{h}
at time
s
can be represented as
[
115
]

h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,y;s,\mathfrak{h})=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\{\mathfrak{h}(x)+\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t)\},\quad t>s,y\in\mathbb{R},

(3.6)

where
\mathcal{L}
is the directed landscape. Note here the analogy with (
3.2
). Here, we add the subscript
\mathcal{L}
to emphasize that a realization of
\mathcal{L}
drives the growth of
h
. When
s=0
, we simply write
h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,y;\mathfrak{h})
. This formulation allows us to couple the KPZ fixed point from different initial data, but with the same random noise.
If
\mathfrak{h}
is a two-sided Brownian motion with diffusivity
\sqrt{2}
and arbitrary drift, then
h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,{\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt};\mathfrak{h})-h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,0;\mathfrak{h})\overset{d}{=}\mathfrak{h}({\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt})
for each
t>0

[
106
,
122
,
120
]
.

3.2.4
Main result

The main result of this chapter is the unique invariance and attractiveness of SH under the KPZ fixed point. This generalizes the invariance of a single Brownian motion with drift and provides a new uniqueness statement (Remark
3.2.5
below). Attractiveness is proved under these assumptions on the asymptotic drift
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
of the initial function
\mathfrak{h}\in\operatorname{UC}
:

\displaystyle\text{If }\xi=0,\quad

\displaystyle\limsup_{x\to+\infty}\frac{\mathfrak{h}(x)}{x}\in[-\infty,0]

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad

\displaystyle\liminf_{x\to-\infty}\frac{\mathfrak{h}(x)}{x}\in[0,+\infty],

(3.7)

\displaystyle\text{if }\xi>0,\quad

\displaystyle\lim_{x\to+\infty}\frac{\mathfrak{h}(x)}{x}=2\xi

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad

\displaystyle\liminf_{x\to-\infty}\frac{\mathfrak{h}(x)}{x}\in(-2\xi,+\infty],

\displaystyle\text{and if }\xi<0,\quad

\displaystyle\lim_{x\to-\infty}\frac{\mathfrak{h}(x)}{x}=2\xi

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad

\displaystyle\limsup_{x\to+\infty}\frac{\mathfrak{h}(x)}{x}\in[-\infty,-2\xi).

As spelled out in the theorem below, these conditions describe the basins of attraction for the KPZ fixed point. When
\xi>0
and
x>0
is large, this condition forces
\mathfrak{h}(x)
to be approximated by
2\xi x
. The directed landscape
\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t)
can be approximated by
-\frac{(x-y)^{2}}{t-s}
(Lemma
B.1.2
), so that
\mathfrak{h}(x)+\mathcal{L}(x,0;y,t)\approx 2\xi x-\frac{(y-x)^{2}}{t}
, which has its maximum at
x=y+\xi t
. Once we can control the maximizers, Lemma
B.1.5
allows us to compare the KPZ fixed point from different initial conditions. This, of course, must be made precise. In the
\xi>0
case of the proof of Lemma
3.5.1
, the
\liminf
condition as
x\to-\infty
forces the maximizer to be positive, and an analogous statement holds for
\xi<0
. These drift conditions are analogous to the conditions on the drift studied in
[
11
,
13
,
9
,
8
,
10
]
for stationary solutions of the Burgers’ equation with random Poisson and kick forcing.

We recall that
G^{\sqrt{2}}
is the parameterization of the SH where
G^{\sqrt{2}}_{\xi}
is a Brownian motion with diffusivity
\sqrt{2}
and drift
2\xi
.

Theorem 3.2.2
.

Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
be a probability space on which the stationary horizon
G^{\sqrt{2}}=\{G^{\sqrt{2}}_{\xi}\}_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}}
and directed landscape
\mathcal{L}
are defined, and such that the processes

\{\mathcal{L}(x,0;y,t):x,y\in\mathbb{R},t>0\}
and
G^{\sqrt{2}}
are independent. For each
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
, let
G^{\sqrt{2}}_{\xi}
evolve under the KPZ fixed point in the same environment
\mathcal{L}
, i.e., for each
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
,

h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,y;G^{\sqrt{2}}_{\xi})=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\{G^{\sqrt{2}}_{\xi}(x)+\mathcal{L}(x,0;y,t)\},\qquad\text{for all }y\in\mathbb{R}\text{ and }t>0.

(3.8)

(Invariance)

For each
t>0
, the equality in distribution

\{h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,{\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt};G^{\sqrt{2}}_{\xi})-h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,0;G^{\sqrt{2}}_{\xi})\}_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}}\overset{d}{=}G^{\sqrt{2}}

holds between random elements of
D(\mathbb{R},C(\mathbb{R}))
.

(Attractiveness)

Let
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
and
\xi_{1}<\cdots<\xi_{k}
in
\mathbb{R}
. Let
(\mathfrak{h}_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak{h}_{k})
be a
k
-tuple of functions in
\operatorname{UC}
, coupled with
(G^{\sqrt{2}},\mathcal{L})

arbitrarily
, and that almost surely satisfy (
3.7
) for
(\mathfrak{h},\xi)=(\mathfrak{h}_{i},\xi_{i})
for each
i\in\{1,\dotsc,k\}
. Then, for any
a>0
,

\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\bigl\{h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,x;\mathfrak{h}_{i})-h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,0;\mathfrak{h}_{i})=h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,x;G^{\sqrt{2}}_{\xi_{i}})-h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,0;G^{\sqrt{2}}_{\xi_{i}})\\
\forall x\in[-a,a],1\leq i\leq k\bigr\}=1.

Consequently, as
t\to\infty
, the distributional limit

\bigl(h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,{\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt};\mathfrak{h}_{1})-h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,0;\mathfrak{h}_{1}),\ldots,h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,{\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt};\mathfrak{h}_{k})-h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,0;\mathfrak{h}_{k})\bigr)\Longrightarrow\bigl(G^{\sqrt{2}}_{\xi_{1}}({\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt}),\ldots,G^{\sqrt{2}}_{\xi_{k}}({\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt})\bigr)

holds in
\operatorname{UC}^{k}
(or in
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R})^{k}
if the
\mathfrak{h}_{i}
are continuous).

(Uniqueness)

In particular, on the space
\operatorname{UC}^{k}
,
\bigl(G^{\sqrt{2}}_{\xi_{1}},\dotsc,G^{\sqrt{2}}_{\xi_{k}})
is the unique invariant distribution of the KPZ fixed point such that for each
i\in\{1,\dotsc,k\}
the condition (
3.7
) holds for
(\mathfrak{h},\xi)=(\mathfrak{h}_{i},\xi_{i})
.

Remark 3.2.3
.

Theorem
4.4.1
(viii)
in Section
4.4
states that the Busemann process is a global attractor of the backward KPZ fixed point. Namely, start the KPZ fixed point at time
t
with initial data
\mathfrak{h}
satisfying (
3.7
) and run it backward in time to a fixed final time
s
. Then, in a given a compact set, for large enough
t
the increments of the backwards KPZ fixed point at time
s
, started from initial data
\mathfrak{h}
at time
t
, match those of the Busemann function in direction
\xi
.

Remark 3.2.4
.

The process
t\mapsto\{h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,{\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt};\mathfrak{h}_{\xi})-h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,0;\mathfrak{h}_{\xi})\}_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}}
is a well-defined Markov process on a state space which is a Borel subset of
\{\mathfrak{h}_{\xi}\}_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}}=D(\mathbb{R},C(\mathbb{R}))
(Lemma
3.6.1
). By the uniqueness result for finite-dimensional distributions,
G^{\sqrt{2}}
is the unique invariant distribution on this space of
C(\mathbb{R})
-valued cadlag paths.

Remark 3.2.5
.

In the above strength, the attractiveness result was previously unknown even in the case
k=1
(a single initial function).
[
122
,
120
]
proved attractiveness for
k=1
and
\xi=0
under the following condition on the initial data
\mathfrak{h}
: there exist
\gamma_{0}>0
and
\psi(r)
such that for all
\gamma>\gamma_{0}
and
r\geq 1
,

\mathbb{P}(\gamma^{-1}\mathfrak{h}(\gamma^{2}x)\leq r|x|\,\forall x\geq 1)\geq 1-\psi(r),\ \text{ where }\lim_{r\to\infty}\psi(r)=0.

(3.9)

3.3
Invariance of the SH for BLPP

Lemma 3.3.1
.

Recall the map
D
(
2.3
). Let
f\in C_{\text{pin}}(\mathbb{R})
be a function satisfying

\liminf_{x\to-\infty}\frac{f(x)}{x}>0.

Fix
m\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then, almost surely, for all
y\in\mathbb{R}
and
n\geq m
,

H_{L}(n+1,y;m,f)=\sup_{-\infty<w\leq y}\{H_{L}(n,w;m,f)+B_{n+1}(w,y)\}<\infty,

(3.10)

where we define
H_{L}(m,w;m,f)=f
.
Furthermore, for
n\geq m
, define

f_{n}(y)=H_{L}(n,y;m,f)-H_{L}(n;0,m,f).

Then, for
n\geq m
,

f_{n+1}=D(f_{n},B_{n+1}).

Proof.

BLPP satisfies the dynamic programming principle: for
m<r\leq n
,

L(x,m+1;y,n+1)=\sup_{x\leq w\leq y}\{L(x,m+1;w,r)+L(w,r+1;y,n+1)\}.

In the case
r=n
, this becomes

L(x,m+1;y,n+1)=\sup_{x\leq w\leq y}\{L(x,m;w,n)+B_{n+1}(w,y)\}.

Below, if
m=n
, we define
L(x,m+1;w,n)=0
. Applying dynamic programming,

\displaystyle\qquad\;H_{L}(n+1,y;m,f)

\displaystyle=\sup_{-\infty<x\leq y}\{f(x)+L(x,m+1;y,n+1)\}

\displaystyle=\sup_{-\infty<x\leq y}\sup_{x\leq w\leq y}\{f(x)+L(x,m+1;w,n)+B_{n+1}(w,y)\}

\displaystyle=\sup_{-\infty<w\leq y}\{H_{L}(n,w;m,f)+B_{n+1}(w,y)\},

this proving the equality (
3.10
) (we prove finiteness a the end). Next, we have

\displaystyle\quad\;f_{n+1}(y)=H_{L}(n+1,y;m,f)-H_{L}(n+1,0;m,f)

\displaystyle=\sup_{-\infty<w\leq y}\{H_{L}(n,w;m,f)+B_{n+1}(w,y)\}-\sup_{-\infty<w\leq 0}\{H_{L}(n,w;m,f)+B_{n+1}(w,0)\}

\displaystyle=B_{n+1}(y)+\sup_{-\infty<w\leq y}\{H_{L}(n,w;m,f)-H_{L}(n,0;m,f)-B_{n+1}(w)\}

\displaystyle\qquad-\sup_{-\infty<w\leq 0}\{H_{L}(n,w;m,f)-H_{L}(n,0;m,f)-B_{n+1}(w)\}=D(f_{n},B_{n+1})(y),

as desired. To show finiteness in (
3.10
), we use the almost sure limits
\lim_{x\to-\infty}\frac{B_{n}(x)}{x}=0
. By inductively applying Lemma
2.3.7
, for each
n\geq m
,
\liminf_{x\to-\infty}\frac{f_{n}(x)}{x}>0
. Hence,

\limsup_{x\to-\infty}[f_{n}(x)-B_{n+1}(x)]-\infty,

so
f_{n+1}(y)=D(f_{n},B_{n+1})(y)
is finite for all
y\in\mathbb{R}
.
∎

Lemma 3.3.2
.

Let
\{B_{r}\}_{r\geq\in\mathbb{Z}}
be a sequence of i.i.d. two-sided Brownian motions with diffusivity
1
and zero drift that define BLPP times
L
(
3.1
). Assume
\{B_{r}\}_{r\geq\in\mathbb{Z}}
is coupled with the SH
G^{1}=\{G^{1}_{\xi}\}_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}}
so that
G^{1}
and
\{B_{r}\}_{r>m}
are independent. Recall the notation
H_{L}
(
3.2
). Let
0<\xi_{1}<\cdots<\xi_{k}
. Then, for each
n\geq m
, as processes on
C(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R}^{k})
,

\{H_{L}(n,{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}};m,G^{1}_{\xi_{i}})-H_{L}(n,0;m,G^{1}_{\xi_{i}})\}_{1\leq i\leq k}\overset{d}{=}\{G^{1}_{\xi_{i}}\}_{1\leq i\leq k}.

Proof.

For
n\geq m
and
1\leq i\leq k
, set

f_{n}^{i}(y)=H_{L}(n,{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}};m,G^{1}_{\xi_{i}})-H_{L}(n,0;m,G^{1}_{\xi_{i}}).

By Lemma
3.3.1
,
f_{n}^{i}=D(f_{n-1}^{i},B_{n})
, so as a process in
n\geq m
with state space
C(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R}^{k})
,
(f_{n}^{1},\ldots,f_{n}^{k})
evolves as the Markov chain (
2.30
) started from initial data
(G^{1}_{\xi_{1}},\ldots,G^{1}_{\xi_{k}})
. From the construction in Proposition
2.3.1
(ii)
,
(G^{1}_{\xi_{1}},\ldots,G^{1}_{\xi_{k}})\sim\mu_{1}^{(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k})}
, and Theorem
2.3.15
states that this measure is invariant for the Markov chain.
∎

The SH is in fact the unique coupled invariant measure in the sense of Lemma
3.3.2
. To see this, we first cite the following result.

Theorem 3.3.3
.

[
39
, Theorem 3]

Let
B_{\xi}
be a two-sided Brownian motion with diffusivity
1
and drift
\xi\in(0,\infty)
, independent of the field of Brownian motions
\{B_{r}\}_{r\in\mathbb{Z}}
defining
L
. Let
f
be a random continuous function
\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
, independent of the environment defining
L
, so that
(f,B_{\xi})
has jointly stationary and ergodic increments and such that, almost surely,

\liminf_{x\to-\infty}\frac{f(x)}{x}\geq\xi,\qquad\text{and}\qquad\limsup_{x\to+\infty}\frac{f(x)}{x}\leq\xi.

(3.11)

Let
m\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then, for all compact sets
K\subseteq\mathbb{R}
and
\varepsilon>0
,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(\sup_{y\in K}\bigl|\bigl(H_{L}(n,y;m,f)-H_{L}(n,0;m,f))\\
-(H_{L}(n,y;m,B_{\xi})-H_{L}(n,0;m,B_{\xi}))\bigr|>\varepsilon)=0.

Since each
G^{1}_{\xi_{i}}
is a two-sided Brownian motion with diffusivity
1
and drift
\xi_{i}
, we get the following extension to coupled initial data from a simple union bound.

Corollary 3.3.4
.

Let
G^{1}
be the SH, independent of
\{B_{r}\}_{r\geq m}
. Let
0<\xi_{1}<\cdots<\xi_{k}
, and assume that
(f_{1},\ldots,f_{k})
is a random function
\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
independent of
\{B_{r}\}_{r\geq m}
so that each
(f_{i},G^{1}_{\xi_{i}})
has jointly stationary and ergodic increments and satisfies (
3.11
) with
\xi=\xi_{i}
. Then,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in K\\
1\leq i\leq k\end{subarray}}\bigl|\bigl(H_{L}(n,y;m,f_{i})-H_{L}(n,0;m,f_{i}))\\
-(H_{L}(n,y;m,G^{1}_{\xi_{i}})-H_{L}(n,0;m,G^{1}_{\xi_{i}}))\bigr|>\varepsilon)=0.

In particular,
(G^{1}_{\xi_{1}},\ldots,G^{1}_{\xi_{k}})
is the unique stationary distribution in the sense of Lemma
3.3.2
such that, for each
1\leq i\leq k
,
G^{1}_{\xi_{i}}
has stationary and ergodic increments and satisfies (
3.11
) with
\xi=\xi_{i}
.

3.4
Exit point bounds for stationary BLPP and the EJS-Rains identity

3.4.1
Main theorem for exit point bounds

We have seen in Lemma
3.3.2
that the SH is a stationary initial condition for BLPP with coupled boundary conditions. In this section, we focus on a single stationary initial condition.
For a continuous initial condition
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
with
\liminf_{x\to-\infty}\frac{f(x)}{x}>0
, we set

Z_{L}^{f}(n,y)=\sup\{x\leq y:f(x)+L(x,1;y,n)=H_{L}(n,y;f)\}.

(3.12)

That is,
Z_{L}^{f}
is the largest maximizer of
x\mapsto f(x)+L(x,1;y,n)
over
x\leq y
.

The goal of Section
3.4
is to prove the following.

Theorem 3.4.1
.

Let
t,\rho>0
, and
y,\xi\in\mathbb{R}
. For
N\geq 1
, let
\lambda_{N}=\rho^{-1/2}+\xi N^{-1/3}
. For each
N
, let
B_{\lambda_{N}}
be a two-sided Brownian motion with diffusivity
1
and zero drift, independent of the Brownian motions
\{B_{r}\}_{r\geq 0}
defining
L
. Let
(\Omega^{N},\mathcal{F}^{N},\mathbb{P}^{N})
be probability spaces on which these are defined. Then, there exists a constant
C=C(y,\xi,\rho,t)
so that for all sufficiently large
M\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\limsup_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}^{N}(Z_{L}^{B_{\lambda_{N}}}(\lfloor Nt\rfloor,\rho tN+yN^{2/3})>MN^{2/3})\leq e^{-CM^{3}}\qquad\text{and}

(3.13)

\displaystyle\limsup_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}^{N}(Z_{L}^{B_{\lambda_{N}}}(\lfloor Nt\rfloor,\rho tN+yN^{2/3})<-MN^{2/3})\leq e^{-CM^{3}}.

(3.14)

Theorem
3.4.1
states that the stationary BLPP model obeys the KPZ wandering exponent
\frac{2}{3}
. In analogy with the results of
[
57
,
22
,
27
]
for exponential LPP, the bound of
e^{-CM^{3}}
should be of optimal order. However, the optimality of this bound is not needed in the present dissertation, so we do not prove it here. To my knowledge, such bounds have not appeared in the literature for BLPP. Exit point bounds have, however, appeared for a positive-temperature variant of this model known as the O’Connell-Yor polymer
[
136
,
116
,
100
]
.

The study of exit point bounds has a rich history and has been done using both integrable and non-integrable techniques. Using what is known as the
coupling technique
developed in
[
37
]
, exit point bounds with
CM^{-3}
instead of
e^{-CM^{3}}
were proved for Poisson last-passage percolation in
[
38
]
and then in exponential last-passage percolation in
[
15
]
. Since then, there has been a large amount of research centered around the stationary exponential LPP model. Refinements to the results of
[
15
]
have come in
[
123
,
122
,
132
]
. Closely related to the study of exit points is that of the coalescence times of semi-infinite geodesics, as shown by the duality in
[
121
]
, and studied further in
[
133
]
.

On the integrable side of things, improvements to the exit point upper bounds came in
[
65
,
62
]
, which gave exit point bounds of order
e^{-CM^{2}}
. Fluctuation bounds for geodesics in the bulk (as opposed to the exit point) appeared in
[
65
,
22
,
27
,
105
]
using integrable methods. These methods allow one to derive the optimal-order exit point bounds with the appropriate inputs (see for example
[
27
, Theorem 2.5]
and
[
65
, Lemma 2.5]
).

A major breakthrough came using the coupling technique in the work of Emrah, Janigian, and Seppäläinen
[
57
]
. Adapting a moment generating function identity of Rains
[
127
]
(now called the EJS-Rains identity), they obtained the optimal
e^{-CM^{3}}
order bounds for exit points in exponential LPP from an arbitrary down-right path. Shortly after
[
27
]
proved these same bounds using integrable methods for exit points from the axes. Since then, Busani and Ferrari
[
32
]
used the results of
[
57
]
to develop optimal-order bounds for fluctuations of geodesics in the bulk. The EJS-Rains identity has been adapted for interacting diffusions and polymers in
[
99
,
101
,
100
]
.

In the present chapter, we prove Theorem
3.4.1
using the coupling technique. We first develop some facts about stationary BLPP from
[
119
]
in Section
3.4.2
. In Section
3.4.3
, we derive the analogue of the EJS-Rains identity for BLPP (Theorem
3.4.8
) and use it to prove the bounds of Theorem
3.4.1
.

Before moving to the next section, we make the following observation, which follows from a standard paths-crossing argument (See, for example,
[
1
,
20
,
68
,
120
]
)

Lemma 3.4.2
.

Let
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be continuous with
\liminf_{x\to-\infty}\frac{f(x)}{x}>0
. Then, if
x<y
,

Z_{L}^{f}(n,x)\leq Z_{L}^{f}(n,y).

Proof.

Suppose, to the contrary, that
z_{x}:=Z_{L}^{f}(n,x)>Z_{L}^{f}(n,y)=:z_{y}
. By planarity, the BLPP geodesic from
(z_{x},1)
to
(x,n)
must cross the BLPP geodesic from
(z_{y},1)
to
(y,n)
at some point
(w,r)
. Since
z_{x}
is maximal for
f(x)+L(x,1;y,n)
and
(w,r)
lies on the geodesic from
(z_{x},1)
to
(x,n)
,

\displaystyle\quad\;f(z_{x})+L(z_{x},1;w,r)+L(w,r;x,n)

\displaystyle=f(z_{x})+L(z_{x},1;x,n)

\displaystyle\geq f(z_{y})+L(z_{y},1;w,r)+L(w,r;x,n),

which implies
f(z_{x})+L(z_{x},1;w,r)\geq f(z_{y})+L(z_{y},1;w,r)
.
Next, since
(w,r)
lies on the geodesic from
(z_{y},1)
to
(y,n)
,

\displaystyle\quad\;f(z_{x})+L(z_{x},1;y,n)

\displaystyle\geq f(z_{x})+L(z_{x},1;w,r)+L(w,r;y,n)

\displaystyle\geq f(z_{y})+L(z_{y},1;w,r)+L(w,r;y,n)

\displaystyle=f(z_{y})+L(z_{y},1;y,n)=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\{f(x)+L(x,1;y,n)\}.

Hence,
z_{x}
is a maximizer for
f(x)+L(x,1;y,n)
. This contradicts the definition of
z_{y}
as the largest such maximizer.
∎

3.4.2
Stationary and near-stationary BLPP

In this section, we study a coupling of initial conditions that is
different
from the SH. This coupling is not jointly stationary under the BLPP dynamics, but will allow us to derive valuable information about a single initial condition. Let
B
be a two-sided Brownian motion with diffusivity
1
and zero drift, independent of the field of Brownian motions
\{B_{r}\}_{r\geq 0}
defining
L
(
3.1
). We let
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
be the probability space on which these are defined. Henceforth, we consider BLPP from an initial boundary condition on level
0
. For
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
,
y\geq 0
, and
\rho,\lambda>0
, set

\displaystyle L^{\rho,\lambda}(n,y)

\displaystyle=\sup_{-\infty<x\leq 0}\{B(x)+\rho x+L(x,1;y,n)\}\vee\sup_{0\leq x\leq y}\{B(x)+\lambda x+L(x,1;y,n)\}

\displaystyle=\sup_{-\infty<x\leq y}\{f^{\rho,\lambda}(x)+L(x,1;y,n)\}

where
f^{\rho,\lambda}(x)=B(x)+\rho(x)
for
x<0
and
B(x)+\lambda x
for
x>0
.
When
\lambda=\rho
, we use the shorthand notation
L^{\lambda}
.
We adopt the notation

Z^{\rho,\lambda}(n,y)=Z_{L}^{f^{\rho,\lambda}}(n,y)=\sup\{x\leq y:f^{\rho,\lambda}(x)+L(x,1;y,n)=L^{\rho,\lambda}(n,y)\},

where we denote
Z^{\lambda}(n,y)=Z^{\lambda,\lambda}(n,y)
. We record the following facts.

Lemma 3.4.3
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
and
y\geq 0
. If
\rho^{\prime}\geq\rho>0
and
\lambda^{\prime}\geq\lambda>0
, then
Z^{\rho^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime}}(n,y)\geq Z^{\rho,\lambda}(n,y)
.

Proof.

By Lemma
A.1.1
, it suffices to prove that
f^{\rho,\lambda}\;{\leq}_{\rm inc}\;f^{\rho^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime}}
, meaning
f^{\rho,\lambda}(x,y)\leq f^{\rho^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime}}(x,y)
for all
x<y
. If
x<y\leq 0
or
0\leq x\leq y
, the result is immediate. If
x<0<y
, then

f^{\rho,\lambda}(x,y)=B(x,y)+\lambda y-\rho x\leq B(x,y)+\lambda^{\prime}y-\rho^{\prime}x=f^{\rho^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime}}(x,y).\qed

Lemma 3.4.4
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
,
y\geq 0
, and
\rho,\alpha,\lambda>0
. If
Z^{\rho,\alpha}(n,y)\leq 0
and
Z^{\rho,\lambda}(n,y)\leq 0
, then
L^{\rho,\alpha}(n,y)=L^{\rho,\lambda}(n,y)
. If
Z^{\alpha,\lambda}(n,y)\geq 0
and
Z^{\rho,\lambda}(n,y)\geq 0
, then
L^{\alpha,\lambda}(n,y)=L^{\rho,\lambda}(n,y)
.

Proof.

If
Z^{\rho,\alpha}(n,y)\leq 0
and
Z^{\rho,\lambda}(n,y)\leq 0
, then

L^{\rho,\alpha}(n,y)=\sup_{-\infty\leq x\leq 0}\{B(x)+\rho x+L(x,1;y,n)\}=L^{\rho,\lambda}(n,y).

The other statement is proved analogously.
∎

Lemma 3.4.5
.

BLPP satisfies the following shift invariance: for
r\in\mathbb{Z}
and
z\in\mathbb{R}
,

\{L(x,m;y,n):x\leq y,m\leq n\}\overset{d}{=}\{L(x+z,m+r;y+z,n+r):x\leq y,m\leq n\}

Proof.

The invariance under
r
-shifts follows because
\{B_{r}\}_{r\in\mathbb{Z}}
is i.i.d. For
z\in\mathbb{R}
, the following distributional equality holds on the process level:

\displaystyle\quad\;L(x,m;y,n)

\displaystyle=\sup\Bigl\{\sum_{r=m}^{n}B_{r}(x_{r-1},x_{r}):x=x_{m-1}\leq x_{m}\leq\cdots\leq x_{n-1}\leq x_{n}=y\Bigr\}

\displaystyle\overset{d}{=}\sup\Bigl\{\sum_{r=m}^{n}B_{r}(x_{r-1}+z,x_{r}+z):x=x_{m-1}\leq x_{m}\leq\cdots\leq x_{n-1}\leq x_{n}=y\Bigr\}

\displaystyle=\sup\Bigl\{\sum_{r=m}^{n}B_{r}(x_{r-1},x_{r}):x+z=x_{m-1}\leq x_{m}\leq\cdots\leq x_{n-1}\leq x_{n}=y+z\Bigr\}

\displaystyle=L(x+z,m;y+z,n).\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qed

For
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
and
y\geq 0
, define

\mathcal{E}^{\rho,\lambda}(n,y)=L^{\rho,\lambda}(n,y)-B(y)-\lambda y.

(3.15)

Again, when
\rho=\lambda
, we write a single superscript.
We now cite the following result.

Lemma 3.4.6
.

[
119
, Theorem 2 and Equation (24)]

The random variable

\sup_{-\infty<x\leq 0}\{B(x)+\rho x+L(x,1;0,n)\}

has the distribution of the sum of
n
i.i.d. exponential random variables with rate
\rho
.

Corollary 3.4.7
.

For
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
and
y\geq 0
, the random variable
\mathcal{E}^{\rho}(n,y)
has the distribution of a sum of
n
i.i.d. exponential random variables with rate
\rho
.

Proof.

Using shift invariance of
B
as well as the shift invariance of
L
from Lemma
3.4.5
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\rho}(n,y)

\displaystyle=\sup_{-\infty<x\leq y}\{B(x)+\rho x+L(x,1;y,n)\}-By-\lambda y

\displaystyle=\sup_{-\infty<x\leq y}\{B(y,x)+\rho(x-y)+L(x,1;y,n)\}

\displaystyle\overset{d}{=}\sup_{-\infty<x\leq y}\{B(x-y)+\rho(x-y)+L(x-y,1;0,n)\}

\displaystyle=\sup_{-\infty<x\leq 0}\{B(x)+\rho x+L(x,1;0,n)\},

and the result now follows from Lemma
3.4.6
.
∎

Corollary
3.4.7
illustrates one way in which Brownian motion with drift is a stationary initial condition for BLPP. Much richer information can be derived about the stationarity in this model, to which we refer the reader to
[
119
,
1
]
, and Appendix C of
[
134
]
.

3.4.3
The EJS-Rains identity for BLPP and Proof of Theorem
3.4.1

Recalling the definition of
\mathcal{E}^{\rho}(n,y)
(
3.15
), Corollary
3.4.7
implies that

\mathbb{E}[L^{\rho}(n,y)]=\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{E}^{\rho}(n,y)]+\mathbb{E}[B(y)+\rho y]=\frac{n}{\rho}+\rho y.

In light of this calculation, we make the following definitions.
For
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
and
y\geq 0
,

\displaystyle M^{\rho}(n,y)

\displaystyle=\frac{n}{\rho}+\rho y\qquad R^{\rho,\lambda}(n,y)=\int_{\lambda}^{\rho}M^{\alpha}(n,y)\,d\alpha=\frac{\rho^{2}-\lambda^{2}}{2}y+n\log\rho-n\log\lambda,

\displaystyle\zeta(n,y)

\displaystyle=\arg\inf_{\rho>0}M^{\rho}(n,y)=\sqrt{\frac{n}{y}},\qquad\gamma(n,y)=\inf_{\rho>0}M^{\rho}(n,y)=\frac{n}{\zeta}+\zeta y=2\sqrt{ny}

We make a simple observation: For
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, there exists a constant
c=c(\varepsilon)>0
so that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
n\varepsilon<y<n\varepsilon^{-1}
,

c(n+y)\leq\gamma(n,y)=2\sqrt{ny}\leq n+y.

(3.16)

The upper bound holds by comparing geometric and arithmetic means, and the lower bound holds because
\gamma(n,y)\geq n\sqrt{\varepsilon}
, and
n+y\leq n(1+\varepsilon^{-1})
.

Theorem 3.4.8

(The
EJS-Rains identity for BLPP)
.

Let
y\geq 0
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
. For
\rho,\lambda>0
, we have

\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\exp\Bigl((\rho-\lambda)L^{\rho,\lambda}(n,y)\Bigr)\Bigr]=\exp\Bigl(R^{\rho,\lambda}(n,y)\Bigr).

Proof.

Recall

L^{\rho,\lambda}(n,y)=\sup_{-\infty<x\leq 0}\{B(x)+\rho x+L(x,1;y,n)\}\vee\sup_{0\leq x\leq y}\{B(x)+\lambda x+L(x,1;y,n)\},

and this is a function of three independent processes, namely

\{B(x)+\rho x:x\leq 0\},\qquad\{B(x)+\lambda x:x\in[0,y]\},\qquad\text{and}\qquad L.

Now, we observe that

\displaystyle\quad\;\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\exp\Bigl((\rho-\lambda)L^{\rho,\lambda}(n,y)\Bigr)\Bigr]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Bigl[e^{(\rho-\lambda)(B(y)+\lambda y)}\exp\Bigl((\rho-\lambda)(L^{\rho,\lambda}(n,y)-B(y)-\lambda y)\Bigr)\Bigr]

\displaystyle=\exp\Bigl(\frac{\rho^{2}-\lambda^{2}}{2}y\Bigr)\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\exp\Bigl((\rho-\lambda)(L^{\rho}(n,y)-B(y)-\rho y)\Bigr)\Bigr]

Here, the last step came from the Cameron-Martin-Girsanov Theorem (Theorem
A.2.2
): we changed the process
\{B(x)+\lambda x:[0,y]\}
to a Brownian motion with drift
\rho
, thus producing
L^{\rho}
. Now, we recall (
3.15
) that
\mathcal{E}^{\rho}(n,y)=L^{\rho}(n,y)-B(y)-\rho y
, which has the distribution of the sum of
n
i.i.d. exponential random variables with rate
\rho
(Corollary
3.4.7
). Hence, we can compute the expectation above:

\displaystyle\quad\;\exp\Bigl(\frac{\rho^{2}-\lambda^{2}}{2}y\Bigr)\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\exp\Bigl((\rho-\lambda)\mathcal{E}^{\rho}(n,y)\Bigr)\Bigr]

\displaystyle=\exp\Bigl(\frac{\rho^{2}-\lambda^{2}}{2}y\Bigr)\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{(\rho-\lambda)x}\frac{\rho^{n}}{\Gamma(n)}x^{n-1}e^{-\rho x}\,dx

\displaystyle=\exp\Bigl(\frac{\rho^{2}-\lambda^{2}}{2}y+n\log(\rho)-n\log(\lambda)\Bigr),

and this is
\exp(R^{\rho,\lambda}(n,y))
, as desired.
∎

Before proving Theorem
3.4.1
, we first prove the following technical lemmas.

Lemma 3.4.9
.

For all
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
,
y\geq 0
and
\rho>0
,

M^{\rho}(n,y)-\gamma(n,y)-\frac{\gamma(n,y)}{2\zeta(n,y)^{2}}(\rho-\zeta)^{2}=-\frac{\gamma(n,y)}{2\rho\zeta^{2}}(\rho-\zeta)^{3}.

Consequently, for each
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, there exists a constant
C=C(\varepsilon)
so that whenever
\varepsilon<\frac{n}{y}<\varepsilon^{-1}
and
\varepsilon<\rho<\varepsilon^{-1}
, for
\zeta=\zeta(n,y)
and
\gamma=\gamma(n,y)
,

\Bigl|M^{\rho}(n,y)-\gamma-\frac{\gamma}{2\zeta^{2}}(\rho-\zeta)^{2}\Bigr|\leq C(n+y)|\rho-\zeta|^{3}.

Proof.

The “consequently” part comes because of the assumption that
\rho
and
\zeta
are bounded away from
0
and
\infty
and (
3.16
). Now, observe that

\displaystyle M^{\rho}(n,y)-\gamma

\displaystyle=\frac{n}{\rho}+\rho y-\frac{n}{\zeta}-\zeta y=(\rho-\zeta)y+\frac{n(\zeta-\rho)}{\rho\zeta}=(\rho-\zeta)\frac{\rho\zeta y-n}{\rho\zeta}

\displaystyle=(\rho-\zeta)\Bigl(\frac{\rho\sqrt{\frac{n}{y}}y-n}{\rho\zeta}\Bigr)=\frac{\sqrt{ny}}{\rho\zeta}(\rho-\zeta)^{2}=\frac{\gamma}{2\rho\zeta}(\rho-\zeta)^{2}.

Then,

M^{\rho}(n,y)-\gamma-\frac{\gamma}{2\zeta^{2}}(\rho-\zeta)^{2}=(\rho-\zeta)^{2}\Bigl(\frac{\gamma}{2\rho\zeta}-\frac{\gamma}{2\zeta^{2}}\Bigr)=-\frac{\gamma}{2\rho\zeta^{2}}(\rho-\zeta)^{3}.\qed

Lemma 3.4.10
.

For
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, there exists a constant
C=C(\varepsilon)
so that for
\rho,\lambda,\frac{n}{y}\in(\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{-1})
and
\zeta=\zeta(n,y)=\sqrt{\frac{n}{y}}
,

\Bigl|R^{\rho,\lambda}(n,y)-\gamma(\rho-\lambda)-\frac{\gamma}{6\zeta^{2}}((\rho-\zeta)^{3}-(\lambda-\zeta)^{3})\Bigr|\leq C(n+y)((\rho-\zeta)^{4}+(\lambda-\zeta)^{4}).

Proof.

Since
R^{\rho,\lambda}(n,y)=\int_{\lambda}^{\rho}M^{\alpha}(n,y)\,d\alpha
, the proof follows from integrating inside the absolute value on the left in Lemma
3.4.9
.
∎

Lemma 3.4.11
.

For each
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, there exists a constant
C=C(\varepsilon)>0
so that for
\zeta=\zeta(n,y)=\sqrt{\frac{n}{y}}
, whenever
\varepsilon<\rho<\zeta<\varepsilon^{-1}
,

\mathbb{P}(Z^{\rho}(n,y)\geq 0)\leq\exp(-C(n+y)(\zeta-\rho)^{3}),

(3.17)

and whenever
\varepsilon<\zeta<\rho<\varepsilon^{-1}
,

\mathbb{P}(Z^{\rho}(n,y)\leq 0)\leq\exp(-C(n+y)(\rho-\zeta)^{3}).

(3.18)

Proof.

We prove (
3.17
), and (
3.18
) follows by a symmetric argument. Let
\varepsilon<\rho<\zeta<\varepsilon^{-1}
, and let
\lambda=(\zeta-\rho)/4
so that
\rho=\zeta-4\lambda
. Lemma
3.4.3
implies that, on the event
\{Z^{\rho}\geq 0\}
(dropping the
(n,y)
argument for simplicity), we also have

Z^{\zeta,\zeta-2\lambda}=Z^{\rho+4\lambda,\rho+2\lambda}\geq 0,\qquad\text{and}\qquad Z^{\zeta-4\lambda,\zeta-2\lambda}=Z^{\rho,\rho+2\lambda}\geq 0.

Hence, by Lemma
3.4.4
,
L^{\zeta,\zeta-2\lambda}=L^{\zeta-4\lambda,\zeta-2\lambda}
on the event
\{Z^{\rho}\geq 0\}
.
Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(Z^{\rho}\geq 0)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\exp(\lambda L^{\zeta,\zeta-2\lambda}-\lambda L^{\zeta-4\lambda,\zeta-2\lambda})\mathbf{1}(Z^{\rho}\geq 0)]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}[\exp(\lambda L^{\zeta,\zeta-2\lambda}-\lambda L^{\zeta-4\lambda,\zeta-2\lambda})]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}[\exp(2\lambda L^{\zeta,\zeta-2\lambda})]^{1/2}\mathbb{E}[\exp(-2\lambda L^{\zeta-4\lambda,\zeta-2\lambda})]^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\exp\Bigl(\frac{1}{2}R^{\zeta,\zeta-2\lambda}+\frac{1}{2}R^{\zeta-4\lambda,\zeta-2\lambda}\Bigr)=\exp\Bigl(\frac{1}{2}R^{\zeta,\zeta-2\lambda}-\frac{1}{2}R^{\zeta-2\lambda,\zeta-4\lambda}\Bigr).

In the second inequality, we used Hölder’s inequality, and in the last equality, we used
R^{\rho,\lambda}=-R^{\lambda,\rho}
. Then, using the bounds of Lemma
3.4.10
, we obtain (changing the constant
C
from line to line),

\displaystyle\quad\;R^{\zeta,\zeta-2\lambda}-R^{\zeta-2\lambda,\zeta-4\lambda}

\displaystyle\leq\gamma(2\lambda)+\frac{\gamma}{6\zeta^{2}}(-(-2\lambda)^{3})+C(n+y)((4\lambda)^{4})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-\Bigl(\gamma(2\lambda)+\frac{\gamma}{6\zeta^{2}}((-2\lambda)^{3}-(-4\lambda)^{3})-C(n+y)((2\lambda^{4})+(4\lambda)^{4}\Bigr))

\displaystyle=-\frac{8\gamma}{\zeta^{2}}\lambda^{3}+C(n+y)\lambda^{4}\leq-C(n+y)\lambda^{3},

thus proving (
3.17
).
Here, we used (
3.16
) and the assumption that
\zeta^{2}=\frac{n}{y}\in(\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{-1})
.
∎

We now complete this section by proving Theorem
3.4.1
.

Proof of Theorem
3.4.1
.

We prove this via the coupling introduced in Section
3.4.2
. Here, the probability space is
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
. Let
\lambda_{N}=\rho^{-1/2}+\xi N^{-1/3}
. From shift-invariance of BLPP (Lemma
3.4.5
),

\mathbb{P}(Z^{\lambda_{N}}(\lfloor Nt\rfloor,\rho tN+yN^{2/3})>MN^{2/3})=\mathbb{P}(Z^{\lambda_{N}}(\lfloor Nt\rfloor,\rho tN+(y-M)N^{2/3})>0).

Note that

\displaystyle\zeta_{N}

\displaystyle:=\zeta(Nt,\rho tN+(y-M)N^{2/3})=\sqrt{\frac{Nt}{\rho tN+(y-M)N^{2/3}}}-\rho^{-1/2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\rho+(y-M)N^{-1/3}/t}}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\rho}}=\frac{(M-y)N^{-1/3}/t}{\sqrt{\rho}\sqrt{\rho+(y-M)N^{-1/3}/t}(\sqrt{\rho}+\sqrt{\rho+(y-M)N^{-1/3}/t})}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{(M-y)N^{-1/3}/t}{\sqrt{\rho}\sqrt{\rho+yN^{-1/3}/t}(\sqrt{\rho}+\sqrt{\rho+yN^{-1/3}/t})}\geq C(M-y)N^{-1/3}.

Comparing to
\lambda_{N}
, we have
\zeta_{N}>\lambda_{N}
for sufficiently large
M
(independent of
N
). Choose such an
M
and let
ve\in(0,1)
. Let
N
be sufficiently large (depending on the choice of
M
) so that
\varepsilon<\lambda_{N}<\zeta_{N}<\varepsilon^{-1}
.
By a Taylor approximation,

\displaystyle\quad\;\zeta_{N}

\displaystyle=\rho^{-1/2}\sqrt{\frac{1}{1+\rho^{-1}(y-M)N^{-1/3}}}=\rho^{-1/2}-\frac{1}{2}(y-M)\rho^{-3/2}N^{-1/3}+O(N^{-2/3}),

where the
O(N^{-2/3})
term may depend on
M
. However, this presents no substantive issue because the term will vanish in the limit with
N
. Indeed, by Lemma
3.4.11
, there exists a constant
C=C(t,\xi,\rho,y)>0
so that

\displaystyle\quad\;\limsup_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(Z^{\lambda_{N}}(Nt,\rho tN+(y-M)N^{2/3})>0)

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{N\to\infty}\exp\Biggl[-C\Bigl(Nt+\rho tN+(y-M)N^{2/3}\Bigr)((M-y)N^{-1/3}+O(N^{-2/3}))^{3}\Biggr]

\displaystyle=\exp(-C(t+\rho t)(M-y)^{3}).

This proves (
3.13
) for
M
large enough. The proof of (
3.14
) is analogous.
∎

3.5
Maximizers and bounds for the KPZ fixed point

We now prove the following technical lemmas for the DL and the KPZ fixed point.

Lemma 3.5.1
.

Fix
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
and
a>0
. Consider the KPZ fixed point starting at time
s
from a function
\mathfrak{h}\in\operatorname{UC}
.
For
t>s
, let
Z_{\mathfrak{h}}^{a,s,t}\in\mathbb{R}
denote the set of exit points from the time horizon
\mathcal{H}_{s}
of the geodesics associated with
\mathfrak{h}
and that terminate in
\{t\}\times[-a,a]
. That is,

Z_{\mathfrak{h}}^{a,s,t}=\bigcup_{y\in[-a,a]}\argmax_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\{\mathfrak{h}(x)+\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t)\}.

(3.19)

Then, on the full probability event of Lemma
B.1.2
, whenever
\mathfrak{h}\in\operatorname{UC}
satisfies condition (
3.7
), and when
\varepsilon>0
,
a>0
, and
s\in\mathbb{R}
, there exists a random
t_{0}=t_{0}(\varepsilon,a,s)>s\vee 0
such that for any
t>t_{0}
,

Z_{\mathfrak{h}}^{a,s,t}\subset\big[(\xi-\varepsilon)t,(\xi+\varepsilon)t\big].

(3.20)

In particular, if
\mathfrak{h}
is a random function almost surely satisfying condition (
3.7
), then this random
t_{0}
exists almost surely, and

\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\Big(Z_{\mathfrak{h}}^{a,s,t}\subset\big[(\xi-\varepsilon)t,(\xi+\varepsilon)t\big]\Big)=1.

Furthermore, an analogous statement holds on the same full-probability event if
t
is held fixed and
s\to-\infty
. That is, there exists a random
s_{0}=s_{0}(\varepsilon,a,t)<t\wedge 0
such that for any
s<s_{0}
,

Z_{\mathfrak{h}}^{a,s,t}\subset\big[-(\xi-\varepsilon)s,-(\xi+\varepsilon)s\big]

(3.21)

Proof.

We show (
3.20
), and (
3.21
) follows by an analogous proof. The idea of the proof is that
\mathfrak{h}(x)+\mathcal{L}(x,0;y,t)
is a noisy version of
2\xi x-\frac{x^{2}}{t}
and
\argmax_{x\in\mathbb{R}}(2\xi x-\frac{x^{2}}{t})=\xi t
, but the noise cannot change the exit point by much when
t
is large. The drift conditions (
3.7
) are used in the proof to ensure that for
t
large enough, all maximizers are positive for
\xi>0
and negative for
\xi<0
. Below, we prove the result for
\xi>0
, and the proof for
\xi<0
follows by symmetry. The case
\xi=0
will be proven separately. Fix
\varepsilon>0
. Suppressing the dependence on
a,s
, set

F(x;t)=C_{\text{DL}}(t-s)^{1/3}\log^{2}\big(2\sqrt{a^{2}+x^{2}+s^{2}+t^{2}}+4\big),

(3.22)

where
C_{\text{DL}}
is the random positive constant from Lemma
B.1.2
.

Lemma 3.5.2
.

For
\varepsilon>0,a>0,
and
s<t\in\mathbb{R}
, there exists
t_{1}=t_{1}(\varepsilon,a,s)>s
such that for all
t>t_{0}
,
|F^{\prime}(x;t)|<\varepsilon
uniformly for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
. In addition, for
t>t_{0}
,
F^{\prime}(x;t)>0
for
x>0
while
F^{\prime}(x;t)<0
for
x<0
.

Proof.

A quick computation shows that

F^{\prime}(x;t)=\frac{2C_{\text{DL}}(t-s)^{1/3}x\log\big(2\sqrt{a^{2}+x^{2}+t^{2}+s^{2}}+4\big)}{(\sqrt{a^{2}+x^{2}+t^{2}+s^{2}}+2)\sqrt{a^{2}+x^{2}+t^{2}+s^{2}}}.

We note that

|x|(a^{2}+x^{2}+t^{2}+s^{2})^{1/2}\leq 1,

and that
\log(x)/x
is decreasing for
x>e
.
Hence, for all
a>0,s<t,\varepsilon>0
, and
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

|F^{\prime}(x;t)|\leq 2C(t-s)^{1/3}\frac{\log(2|t|+4)}{|t|+2}\overset{t\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}0.\qed

Back to the main proof, from the drift assumption (
3.7
), for each
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
R_{\varepsilon}>0
so that for all
x\geq R_{\varepsilon}
,
|\frac{\mathfrak{h}(x)}{x}-\xi|\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
. Let
C_{\varepsilon}=\sup_{0\leq R_{\varepsilon}}\mathfrak{h}(x)
. By the global bound
\mathfrak{h}(x)\leq a+b|x|
(assumed in the definition of
\operatorname{UC}
),
C_{\varepsilon}<\infty
. Further, observe that
-\frac{(x-y)^{2}}{t-s}=-\frac{x^{2}}{t-s}+\frac{2xy}{t-s}-\frac{y^{2}}{t-s}
, and
|\frac{2xy}{t-s}-\frac{y^{2}}{t-s}|\leq\varepsilon+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}|x|
for large
t
, uniformly for
y\in[-a,a]
. Using this and the bounds of Lemma
B.1.2
, for
t>s+1
sufficiently large (depending on
\varepsilon,a
), for all
y\in[-a,a]
and
x\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}(x)+\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t)\leq M_{U}(x;t):=C_{\varepsilon}+2\xi x+\varepsilon x-\frac{x^{2}}{t-s}+\varepsilon+F(x;t),

(3.23)

Further, for all
x\geq R_{\varepsilon}
, and sufficiently large
t>s+1
,

\mathfrak{h}(x)+\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t)\geq M_{L}(x;t):=2\xi x-\varepsilon x-\frac{x^{2}}{t-s}-\varepsilon-F(x;t).

(3.24)

By the assumption (
3.7
), we may choose
\gamma
so that

-2\xi<2\gamma<\liminf_{x\to-\infty}\frac{\mathfrak{h}(x)}{x}\leq+\infty
. Then, applying a similar procedure as before and adjusting the constant
C_{\varepsilon}
if needed, for all
y\in[-a,a]
and
x\leq 0
,

\mathfrak{h}(x)+\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t)\leq M_{U}^{-}(x;t):=C_{\varepsilon}+2\gamma x-\frac{x^{2}}{t-s}+F(x;t).

(3.25)

We start by using these bounds to show that when
t>s
is sufficiently large,

\sup_{x\geq 0}\{\mathfrak{h}(x)+\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t)\}>\sup_{x\leq 0}\{\mathfrak{h}(x)+\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t)\},\qquad\forall y\in[-a,a].

(3.26)

so that all maximizers of
\mathfrak{h}(x)+\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t)
over
x\in\mathbb{R}
are nonnegative. First, we observe that for
t
large enough so that
\xi(t-s)\geq R_{\varepsilon}
, for all
y\in[-a,a]
,

\displaystyle\quad\;\sup_{x\geq 0}\{\mathfrak{h}(x)+\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t)\}\geq M_{L}(\xi(t-s),t)=(\xi^{2}-\xi\varepsilon)(t-s)+o(t).

(3.27)

Next, using (
3.25
), we obtain

\displaystyle\quad\;\sup_{x\leq 0}\{\mathfrak{h}(x)+\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t)\}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{x\leq 0}\{2\gamma x-\frac{x^{2}}{t-s}+C_{\varepsilon}+F(x;t)\}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{x\leq 0}\{2\gamma x-2\varepsilon x-\frac{x^{2}}{t-s}\}+\sup_{x\leq 0}\{2\varepsilon x+F(x;t)\}+C_{\varepsilon}.=(\gamma-\varepsilon)^{2}(t-s)+o(t).

To justify the last equality, we note that the first supremum on the RHS above is equal to
(\gamma-\varepsilon)^{2}(t-s)
, while for large enough
t
, Lemma
3.5.2
implies that the function inside the second supremum is increasing, so the maximum is achieved at
x=0
. Note that
F(0;t)=o(t)
. Since
\gamma>-\xi
, by choosing
\varepsilon>0
small enough, a comparison with (
3.27
) verifies (
3.26
) for sufficiently large
t
.
