Next, we find (approximately) where the maximizers of
M_{U}
on
x\geq 0
are. The function
M_{U}(x;t)
has leading order
-(x-y)^{2}/(t-s)
, so maximizers exist on
x\geq 0
. A quick computation shows that
M_{U}(0;t)=o(t)
, so by (
3.27
), all maximizers are strictly positive for sufficiently large
t
. Hence, for any maximizer
x
,
M_{U}^{\prime}(x;t)=0
.
First, note that

M^{\prime}_{U}(x;t)=2(\xi+\varepsilon)-\frac{2x}{t-s}+F^{\prime}(x;t).

(3.28)

By Lemma
3.5.2
, for sufficiently large
t>s
,
0<F^{\prime}(x;t)<\varepsilon
for all
x>0
. For such
t
, and
y\in[-a,a]
,

\{x\geq 0:M^{\prime}_{U}(x;t)=0\}\subseteq\big((\xi+\varepsilon)(t-s),(\xi+2\varepsilon)(t-s)\big).

(3.29)

and that

\displaystyle M^{\prime}_{U}(x;t)<0\quad\forall x\geq(\xi+2\varepsilon)(t-s)

(3.30)

\displaystyle M^{\prime}_{U}(x;t)>0\quad\forall x\leq(\xi+\varepsilon)(t-s).

Next we consider the supremum of
x\mapsto M_{U}
outside the interval

I_{\varepsilon}:=[(\xi-2\sqrt{\xi\varepsilon})(t-s),(\xi+2\sqrt{\xi\varepsilon})(t-s)].

By choosing
\varepsilon>0
small enough, then for
t
large enough (depending on
\varepsilon,a
),

\big((\xi-\varepsilon)(t-s),(\xi+2\varepsilon)(t-s)\big)\subseteq I_{\varepsilon}.

(3.31)

From (
3.30
) and (
3.31
), we see that to determine the supremum of
M_{U}
outside
I_{\varepsilon}
it is enough to take the maximum of
M_{U}
at the endpoints of
I_{\varepsilon}
. Plugging the end points of the interval on the right-hand side of (
3.31
) in
M_{U}
, for small enough
\varepsilon
,

\displaystyle M_{U}((\xi-2\sqrt{\xi\varepsilon})(t-s);t)=\big[\xi^{2}-3\xi\varepsilon-2\xi^{1/2}\varepsilon^{3/2}\big](t-s)+o(t),\qquad\text{and}

(3.32)

\displaystyle M_{U}((\xi+2\sqrt{\xi}\varepsilon^{1/2})(t-s);t)=\big[\xi^{2}-3\xi\varepsilon+2\xi^{1/2}\varepsilon^{3/2}\Big](t-s)+o(t).

It follows that for
\varepsilon
small enough and
t>t_{0}(\varepsilon,a,s,C_{\text{DL}},C_{\varepsilon})
,

\sup_{x\notin I_{\varepsilon}}M_{U}(x;t)\leq\max\{M_{U}((\xi-2\sqrt{\xi\varepsilon})(t-s)),M_{U}((\xi+2\sqrt{\xi\varepsilon})(t-s))\}\leq(\xi^{2}-2\xi\varepsilon)(t-s).

(3.33)

From (
3.23
), (
3.26
), and (
3.31
), for sufficiently large
t
,

\displaystyle\big\{\sup_{x\notin I_{\varepsilon}}M_{U}(x;t)<\sup_{x\in I_{\varepsilon}}M_{L}(x;t)\big\}

(3.34)

\displaystyle\subseteq\big\{\sup_{x\notin I_{\varepsilon}}\mathfrak{h}(x)+\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t)<\sup_{x\in I_{\varepsilon}}\mathfrak{h}(x)+\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t)\quad\forall y\in[-a,a]\big\}\subseteq\{Z_{\mathfrak{h}}^{a,s,t}\subset I_{\varepsilon}\}.

(
3.27
) and (
3.33
), imply that, almost surely, there is a random
t_{0}=t_{0}(\varepsilon,a,s)>0
so that for
t>t_{0}
,

\sup_{x\notin I_{\varepsilon}}M_{U}(x;t)<\sup_{x\in I_{\varepsilon}}M_{L}(x;t),

(3.35)

so by replacing
\varepsilon
with
\varepsilon^{2}/(4\xi)
, the inclusion (
3.34
) completes the proof in the case
\xi>0
.

Now we prove the separate
\xi=0
case. This time, we set
I_{\varepsilon}=[2\sqrt{\varepsilon}\hskip 0.9pt(t-s),2\sqrt{\varepsilon}\hskip 0.9pt(t-s)]
. Fix a point
x^{\star}\in\mathbb{R}
so that
\mathfrak{h}(x^{\star})>-\infty
. Then, for
t
large enough
x^{\star}\in I_{\varepsilon}
, and

\sup_{x\in I_{\varepsilon}}\mathfrak{h}(x)+\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t)\geq\mathfrak{h}(x^{\star})+\mathcal{L}(x^{\star},s;y,t)\geq\mathfrak{h}(x^{\star})-\frac{(x^{\star}-y)^{2}}{t-s}+F(x^{\star},t)=o(t).

(3.36)

By the assumption (
3.7
) and upper semi-continuity, following a similar argument as in the previous case, for all
y\in[-a,a]
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}(x)+\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t)\leq M_{U}(x;t)

\displaystyle:=-\frac{x^{2}}{t-s}+\varepsilon x+C_{\varepsilon}+F(x;t)

A similar proof as before shows that

\sup_{x\notin I_{\varepsilon}}M_{U}(x;t)\leq\max\{M_{U}(-2\sqrt{\varepsilon}(t-s)),M_{U}(2\sqrt{\varepsilon}(t-s))\}=-3\varepsilon(t-s)+o(t),

and comparison with (
3.36
) completes the proof.
∎

We believe the following Lemma is well-known, but we do not have a precise reference. In particular,
[
106
]
states that the KPZ fixed point preserves the space of linearly bounded continuous functions
and gives regularity estimates for the KPZ fixed point.

Lemma 3.5.3
.

Let
\mathfrak{h}\in\operatorname{UC}
be initial data for the KPZ fixed point sampled at time
s\in\mathbb{R}
. Let
h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,y;s,\mathfrak{h})
be defined as in (
B.4
).
Then, on the full-probability event of Lemma
B.1.2
, the following hold

(i)

If
\mathfrak{h}
is continuous, then
(t,y)\mapsto h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,y;s,\mathfrak{h})
is continuous on
(s,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
.

(ii)

For each compact set
K\subseteq(s,\infty)
, there exist constants
A=A(a,b,K)
and
B=B(a,b,K)
such that for all
t\in K
and all
y\in\mathbb{R}
,
h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,y;s,\mathfrak{h})\leq A+B|y|
. If we assume that
\mathfrak{h}(x)\geq-a-b|x|
for some constants
a,b>0
, then we also obtain the bound
h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,y;s,\mathfrak{h})\geq-A-B|y|
for all
t\in K
and
y\in\mathbb{R}

(the upper bound
\mathfrak{h}(x)\leq a+b|x|
is assumed in the definition of
\operatorname{UC}
).

(iii)

If there exists
a,b>0
so that
|\mathfrak{h}(x)|\leq a+b|x|
for all
x
, then for any
t>s
,
\delta>0
, there exists
Y=Y(t,\delta)>0
so that when
|y|\geq Y
, all maximizers of
\mathfrak{h}(x)+\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t)
over
x\in\mathbb{R}
lie in the interval
(y-|y|^{1/2+\delta},y+|y|^{1/2+\delta})

Proof.

Item
(i)

By definition of
\operatorname{UC}
, there exists constants
a,b>0
so that
\mathfrak{h}(x)\leq a+b|x|
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
. Combined with the bounds on the directed landscape in Lemma
B.1.2
, this implies that when
(y,t)
varies over a compact set of
(s,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
, the supremum in (
B.4
) can be taken uniformly over a common compact set. Then, continuity of
\mathfrak{h}
and
\mathcal{L}
gives the continuity of
h
.

Item
(ii)
:

By Lemma
B.1.2
, for each
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

\mathfrak{h}(x)+\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t)\leq a+b|x|-\hskip 1.1pt\frac{(x-y)^{2}}{t-s}+C(t-s)^{1/3}\log^{2}\Bigl(\frac{2\sqrt{x^{2}+y^{2}+s^{2}+t^{2}}+4}{(t-s)\wedge 1}\Bigr).

(3.37)

The
\log
term in (
3.37
). can be bounded by an affine function uniformly for
t\in K
and
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
. Then, for constants
a_{1}=a_{1}(a,b,K),b_{1}=b_{1}(a,b,K)
, and
b_{2}=b_{2}(a,b,K)
,

\displaystyle h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,y;s,\mathfrak{h})

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\Bigl\{-\frac{(x-y)^{2}}{t-s}+a_{1}+b_{1}|x|+b_{2}|y|\Bigr\}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\Bigl\{-\frac{(x-y)^{2}}{t-s}+a_{1}+b_{1}x+b_{2}|y|\Bigr\}\vee\Bigl\{-\frac{(x-y)^{2}}{t-s}+a_{1}-b_{1}x+b_{2}|y|\Bigr\}

\displaystyle\leq a_{1}+b_{2}|y|+\Bigl(b_{1}y+\frac{b_{1}^{2}t}{4}\Bigr)\vee\Bigl(-b_{1}y+\frac{b_{1}^{2}t}{4}\Bigr),

giving a linear bound, uniformly for
t\in K
. The lower bound is simpler: By Lemma
B.1.2
and the assumption
\mathfrak{h}(x)\geq-a-b|x|
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,y;s,\mathfrak{h})

\displaystyle=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\{\mathfrak{h}(x)+\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t)\}

(3.38)

\displaystyle\geq\mathfrak{h}(y)+\mathcal{L}(y,s;y,t)\geq-a-b|y|-C(t-s)^{1/3}\log^{2}\Bigl(\frac{2\sqrt{2y^{2}+t^{2}+s^{2}}+4}{(t-s)\wedge 1}\Bigr),

and again, the
\log
term can be bounded by an affine function, uniformly for
t\in K
and
y\in\mathbb{R}
.

Item
(iii)
:
By comparing (
3.37
) to (
3.38
), when
|y|
is sufficiently large, for
x\notin(y-|y|^{1/2+\delta},y+|y|^{1/2+\delta})
, (
3.37
) is strictly less than
h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,y;s,\mathfrak{h})
, so maximizers cannot lie outside the interval
(y-|y|^{1/2+\delta},y+|y|^{1/2+\delta})
.
∎

Lemma 3.5.4
.

The following holds simultaneously for all initial data and all
t>s
on the event of probability one from Lemma
B.1.2
. Let
\mathfrak{h}\in\operatorname{UC}
be initial data for the KPZ fixed point, sampled at time
s
. For
t>s
, let
h_{\mathcal{L}}
be defined as in (
B.4
).
Then, simultaneously for all
t>s
,

\liminf_{x\to+\infty}\frac{h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,x;s,\mathfrak{h})}{x}\geq\liminf_{x\to+\infty}\frac{\mathfrak{h}(x)}{x},\qquad\text{and}\qquad\limsup_{x\to-\infty}\frac{h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,x;s,\mathfrak{h})}{x}\leq\limsup_{x\to-\infty}\frac{\mathfrak{h}(x)}{x}.

(3.39)

Furthermore, assuming that
\mathfrak{h}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is
continuous
and satisfies

\liminf_{x\to\pm\infty}\frac{\mathfrak{h}(x)}{x}>-\infty\qquad\text{and}\qquad\limsup_{x\to\pm\infty}\frac{\mathfrak{h}(x)}{x}<+\infty,

(3.40)

then also

\limsup_{x\to+\infty}\frac{h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,x;s,\mathfrak{h})}{x}\leq\limsup_{x\to+\infty}\frac{\mathfrak{h}(x)}{x},\qquad\text{and}\qquad\liminf_{x\to-\infty}\frac{h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,x;s,\mathfrak{h})}{x}\geq\liminf_{x\to-\infty}\frac{\mathfrak{h}(x)}{x}.

(3.41)

In particular, for
continuous
initial data
\mathfrak{h}
satisfying (
3.40
), if either
(or both) of the limits

\lim_{x\to\pm\infty}\frac{\mathfrak{h}(x)}{x}

exist (potentially with different limits on each side), then for
t>s
,

\lim_{x\to\pm\infty}\frac{h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,x;s,\mathfrak{h})}{x}=\lim_{x\to\pm\infty}\frac{\mathfrak{h}(x)}{x}.

Proof.

We start with (
3.39
) by proving the first inequality, and the other is analogous.
If
\liminf_{x\to+\infty}\frac{\mathfrak{h}(x)}{x}=-\infty
, there is nothing to show. Otherwise, let
\xi_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
be an arbitrary number smaller than
\liminf_{x\to+\infty}\frac{\mathfrak{h}(x)}{x}
. Let
y
be sufficiently large and positive so that
\mathfrak{h}(y)\geq\xi_{1}y
. Then, using Lemma
B.1.2
, for such sufficiently large positive
y
,

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\{\mathfrak{h}(x)+\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t)\}

(3.42)

\displaystyle\geq\mathfrak{h}(y)+\mathcal{L}(y,s;y,t)\geq\xi_{1}y-C(t-s)^{1/3}\log^{2}\Bigl(\frac{2\sqrt{2y^{2}+t^{2}+s^{2}}+4}{(t-s)\wedge 1}\Bigr),

where
C
is a constant. Therefore,

\liminf_{y\to\infty}\frac{h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,y;s,\mathfrak{h})}{y}\geq\xi_{1},

but this is true for all
\xi_{1}<\liminf_{x\to+\infty}\frac{\mathfrak{h}(x)}{x}
, so

\liminf_{y\to\infty}\frac{h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,y;s,\mathfrak{h})}{y}\geq\liminf_{x\to+\infty}\frac{\mathfrak{h}(x)}{x}.

Next, we turn to proving (
3.41
). Again, we prove the first inequality and the second follows analogously. Set
\xi_{2}=\limsup_{x\to+\infty}\frac{\mathfrak{h}(x)}{x}
and let
\varepsilon>0
. By continuity, the assumption (
3.40
) on the asymptotics of
\mathfrak{h}
implies there exist constants
a,b>0
so that
|\mathfrak{h}(x)|\leq a+b|x|
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
. Lemma
3.5.3
(iii)
implies that for
\varepsilon>0
and sufficiently large
y>0
,

\displaystyle\quad\;\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\{\mathfrak{h}(x)+\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t)\}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{x\in(y-y^{2/3},y+y^{2/3})}\Big\{(\xi_{2}+\varepsilon)x-\frac{(x-y)^{2}}{t-s}+C(t-s)^{1/3}\log^{2}\Bigl(\frac{2\sqrt{x^{2}+y^{2}+t^{2}+s^{2}}+4}{(t-s)\wedge 1}\Bigr)\Big\}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{x\in(y-y^{2/3},y+y^{2/3})}\Big\{(\xi_{2}+\varepsilon)x-\frac{(x-y)^{2}}{t-s}+\varepsilon(x+y)\Big\}

\displaystyle=(\xi_{2}+3\varepsilon)y+C(\varepsilon,s,t,\xi_{2}),

and so

\limsup_{y\to\infty}\frac{h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,y;s,\mathfrak{h})}{y}\leq\xi_{2}+3\varepsilon.\qed

3.6
Proof of invariance and attractiveness (Theorem
3.2.2
)

On a probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
, let
G^{\sqrt{2}}=\{G^{\sqrt{2}}_{\xi}\}_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}}
be the stationary horizon with
\sigma=\sqrt{2}
, independent of
\{\mathcal{L}(x,0;y,t):x,y\in\mathbb{R},t>0\}
. For
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
, define
h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,y;G_{\xi}^{\sqrt{2}})
as in (
3.8
):

h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,y;G_{\xi})=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\{G_{\xi}^{\sqrt{2}}(x)+\mathcal{L}(x,0;y,t)\},\qquad\text{for all }y\in\mathbb{R}\text{ and }t>0.

Define the following state space:

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}

\displaystyle:=\bigl\{\{\mathfrak{h}_{\xi}\}_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}}\in D(\mathbb{R},C(\mathbb{R})):\mathfrak{h}_{\xi_{1}}\;{\leq}_{\rm inc}\;\mathfrak{h}_{\xi_{2}}\text{ for }\xi_{1}<\xi_{2},

(3.43)

\displaystyle\text{ and for all }\xi\in\mathbb{R},\;\mathfrak{h}_{\xi}(0)=0\text{ and }\mathfrak{h}_{\xi}\text{ satisfies condition \eqref{eqn:drift_assumptions}}

\displaystyle\text{ with all $\limsup$ and $\liminf$ terms finite}\bigr\}.

Lemma 3.6.1
.

The space
\mathcal{Y}
defined in (
3.43
) is a measurable subset of
D(\mathbb{R},C(\mathbb{R}))
. Let
\mathcal{L}
be the directed landscape,
\{\mathfrak{h}_{\xi}\}_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}}\in\mathcal{Y}
,
h_{0}({\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt};\mathfrak{h}_{\xi})=\mathfrak{h}_{\xi}
, and

h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,y;\mathfrak{h}_{\xi})=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\{\mathfrak{h}_{\xi}(x)+\mathcal{L}(x,0,y;t)\}\qquad\text{for }\ t>0,\ y\in\mathbb{R}\text{ and }\xi\in\mathbb{R}.

Then

t\mapsto\{h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,{\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt};\mathfrak{h}_{\xi})-h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,0;\mathfrak{h}_{\xi})\}_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}}
is a Markov process on
\mathcal{Y}
. Specifically, on the event of full probability from Lemma
B.1.2
,
\{h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,{\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt};\mathfrak{h}_{\xi})-h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,0;\mathfrak{h}_{\xi})\}_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}}\in\mathcal{Y}
for each
t>0
.

Proof.

Recall that we defined the
\sigma
-algebra on
D(\mathbb{R},C(\mathbb{R}))
be the smallest
\sigma
-algebra such that
h_{\xi}(x)
is measurable for each
x,\xi\in\mathbb{R}
. We claim that

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}

\displaystyle=\bigcap_{\alpha_{1}<\alpha_{2}\in\mathbb{Q}}\Bigl\{\{\mathfrak{h}_{\xi}\}_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}}\in D(\mathbb{R},C(\mathbb{R})):\mathfrak{h}_{\alpha_{1}}(q_{1},q_{2})\leq\mathfrak{h}_{\alpha_{2}}(q_{1},q_{2})\text{ for $q_{1}<q_{2}\in\mathbb{Q}$, }

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\mathfrak{h}_{\alpha_{1}}(0)=0,\text{ and }\mathfrak{h}_{\alpha_{1}}\text{ satisfies condition \eqref{eqn:drift_assumptions} with }\xi=\alpha_{1}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\text{and}\limsup_{|x|\to\infty}\frac{|h_{\alpha_{1}}(x)|}{|x|}<\infty\Bigr\}.

In particular, for
\{h_{\xi}\}_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}}
lying in the set on the right,
\mathfrak{h}_{\alpha_{1}}\;{\leq}_{\rm inc}\;\mathfrak{h}_{\alpha_{2}}
for
\alpha_{1}\leq\alpha_{2}\in\mathbb{Q}
by continuity of each function. Then,
\mathfrak{h}_{\xi_{1}}\;{\leq}_{\rm inc}\;\mathfrak{h}_{\xi_{2}}
extends to all
\xi_{1}<\xi_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
by taking limits of rational numbers
\alpha_{1}^{n}\searrow\xi_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}^{n}\searrow\xi_{2}
. Since
\mathfrak{h}_{\xi}(0)=0
and
\mathfrak{h}_{\xi_{1}}\leq\mathfrak{h}_{\xi_{2}}
for
\xi_{1}\leq\xi_{2}
,
\mathfrak{h}_{\xi_{1}}(x)\leq\mathfrak{h}_{\xi_{2}}(x)
for
x>0
, and the inequality flips for
x<0
. Hence, if Condition (
3.7
) holds
\alpha\in\mathbb{Q}
and all
\limsup,\liminf
terms are finite, this extends to all
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
. To finish the proof of measurability of
\mathcal{Y}
, it remains to show that the four quantities
\liminf_{x\to\pm\infty}\frac{\mathfrak{h}_{\xi}(x)}{x}
and
\limsup_{x\to\pm\infty}\frac{\mathfrak{h}_{\xi}(x)}{x}
are measurable. This was demonstrated previously in Section
2.2
.

We now show that
\{h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,{\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt};\mathfrak{h}_{\xi})-h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,0;\mathfrak{h}_{\xi})\}_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}}\in\mathcal{Y}
for all
t>0
. Lemma
B.1.5
(iii)
shows the preservation of the ordering of functions, Lemma
3.5.4
shows the preservation of limits, and Lemma
3.5.3
(i)
shows that
h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}};\mathfrak{h}_{\xi})\in C(\mathbb{R})
for all
\xi
. It remains to show that
\{h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,{\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt};\mathfrak{h})\}_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}}\in D(\mathbb{R},C(\mathbb{R}))
for each
t>0
. Since
\mathfrak{h}_{\xi_{1}}\;{\leq}_{\rm inc}\;\mathfrak{h}_{\xi_{2}}
, Lemma
A.1.1
and the global bounds of Lemma
B.1.2
imply that, for each compact
K\subseteq\mathbb{R}
and
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
, there exists a random
M=M(\xi,t,K)>0
such that for all
y\in K
,
\alpha\in(\xi-1,\xi+1)
,

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\{\mathfrak{h}_{\alpha}(x)+\mathcal{L}(x,0;y,t)\}=\sup_{x\in[-M,M]}\{\mathfrak{h}_{\alpha}(x)+\mathcal{L}(x,0;y,t)\}.

Then, it follows that
\{h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,{\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt};\mathfrak{h}_{\xi})\}_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}}
, as an
\mathbb{R}\to C(\mathbb{R})
function of
\xi
, is right-continuous with left limits because this is true of
\{\mathfrak{h}_{\xi}\}_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}}
.
By the metric composition (
3.3
) of the directed landscape
\mathcal{L}
, for
0<s<t
,

\displaystyle\quad\;h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,y;\mathfrak{h}_{\xi})-h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,0;\mathfrak{h}_{\xi})

\displaystyle=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R},z\in\mathbb{R}}\{\mathfrak{h}_{\xi}(x)+\mathcal{L}(x,0,z,s)+\mathcal{L}(z,s;y,t)\}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R},z\in\mathbb{R}}\{\mathfrak{h}_{\xi}(x)+\mathcal{L}(x,0,z,s)+\mathcal{L}(z,s;0,t)\}

\displaystyle=\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\{h_{\mathcal{L}}(s,z;\mathfrak{h}_{\xi})+\mathcal{L}(z,s;y,t)\}-\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\{h_{\mathcal{L}}(s,z;\mathfrak{h}_{\xi})+\mathcal{L}(z,s;0,t)\}

\displaystyle=\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\{h_{\mathcal{L}}(s,z;\mathfrak{h}_{\xi})-h_{\mathcal{L}}(s,0;\mathfrak{h}_{\xi})+\mathcal{L}(z,s;y,t)\}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\{h_{\mathcal{L}}(s,z;\mathfrak{h}_{\xi})-h_{\mathcal{L}}(s,0;\mathfrak{h}_{\xi})+\mathcal{L}(z,s;0,t)\}.

Then,
t\mapsto\{h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,{\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt};\mathfrak{h}_{\xi})-h_{\mathcal{L}}(s,0;\mathfrak{h}_{\xi})\}_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}}
is Markov because
\mathcal{L}
has i.i.d. time increments.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.2.2
.

Invariance:
Since the
\sigma
-algebra on
D(\mathbb{R},C(\mathbb{R}))
is generated by the projection maps and since the subspace
\mathcal{Y}\subseteq D(\mathbb{R},C(\mathbb{R}))
is preserved under
\mathcal{L}
(Lemma
3.6.1
), it suffices to show that for
-\infty<\xi_{1}<\cdots<\xi_{k}<\infty
and
t>0
,

\bigl(h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,{\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt};G^{\sqrt{2}}_{\xi_{1}})-h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,0;G^{\sqrt{2}}_{\xi_{1}}),\ldots,h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,{\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt};G^{\sqrt{2}}_{\xi_{k}})-h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,0;G^{\sqrt{2}}_{\xi_{k}})\bigr)\overset{d}{=}(G^{\sqrt{2}}_{\xi_{1}},\ldots,G^{\sqrt{2}}_{\xi_{k}}).

(3.44)

We prove this by using the invariance of
G^{1}
for BLPP (Lemma
3.3.2
), the convergence of BLPP to
\mathcal{L}
(Theorem
3.2.1
), and the exit point bounds for BLPP from its stationary initial condition proved in Theorem
3.4.1
. For this, choose
\rho>0
, and let
\alpha,\beta,\tau
be defined as in (
3.4
):

\chi^{2}=\rho,\;\;\alpha=2\sqrt{\rho},\;\;\beta=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\rho}},\;\;\chi/\tau^{2}=\frac{1}{4\rho^{3/2}}.

We observe here that

\frac{\tau}{\chi^{2}}=\frac{\tau}{\rho}=\frac{\sqrt{\chi 4\rho^{3/2}}}{p}=\frac{2\rho}{\rho}=2

(3.45)

We will assume that our probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
contains the coupling of
\{L^{N}\}_{N\geq 1}
and
\mathcal{L}
from Theorem
3.2.1
and an independent realization of the SH
G^{1}
with
\sigma=1
.
By convention, we say that
L^{N}(x,m;y,n)=-\infty
if
m>n
or
x>y
. For
1\leq i\leq k,N\geq 1,x,y\in\mathbb{R}
, and
t>s
, define

\displaystyle\lambda_{i}^{N}

\displaystyle=\beta+\frac{\xi_{i}}{\chi N^{1/3}},

\displaystyle H_{i}^{N}(t,y)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\chi N^{1/3}}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\Bigl(\Bigl\{G_{\lambda_{i}^{N}}^{1}(x)+L^{N}(x,1;N\rho t+N^{2/3}\tau y,\lfloor Nt\rfloor)\Bigr\}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-\alpha Nt-\beta\tau N^{2/3}y\Bigr),

\displaystyle Z_{i}^{N}(t,y)

\displaystyle=Z_{L^{N}}^{G_{\lambda_{i}^{N}}}(\lfloor Nt\rfloor,\rho tN+yN^{2/3}y)\qquad\qquad\text{(Recall \eqref{BLPPZgen})},

\displaystyle G_{i}^{N}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\chi N^{1/3}}\Bigl(G^{1}_{\lambda_{i}^{N}}(N^{2/3}\tau x)-\beta\tau N^{2/3}x\Bigr),\qquad\text{and}

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{N}(x,s;y,t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\chi N^{1/3}}\Bigl(L^{N}(N\rho s+N^{2/3}\tau x,\lfloor Ns\rfloor;N\rho t+N^{2/3}\tau y,\lfloor Nt\rfloor)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-\alpha N-\beta\tau N^{2/3}(y-x)\Bigr).

By the invariance of
G^{1}
for
L^{N}
(Lemma
3.3.2
) followed by shift invariance of the SH (Theorem
2.3.2
(i)
), the scaling relations of the SH (Theorem
2.3.2
(ii)
with

b=\frac{1}{\chi N^{1/3}}
,
c=N^{1/3}\sqrt{\tau}
, and
\nu=\frac{\beta\chi}{\tau}N^{1/3}
), and lastly (
3.45
), for
t>0
and
Nt\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\{H_{i}^{N}(t,{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}})-H_{i}^{N}(t,0)\}_{1\leq i\leq k}

(3.46)

\displaystyle\overset{d}{=}\Bigl\{\frac{1}{\chi N^{1/3}}\bigl(G_{\lambda_{i}^{N}}^{1}(N\rho t+N^{2/3}\tau{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}})-G_{\xi_{i}}^{1}(N\rho t)-\beta\tau N^{2/3}{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}}\bigr)\Bigr\}_{1\leq i\leq k}

\displaystyle\overset{d}{=}\Bigl\{\frac{1}{\chi N^{1/3}}(G_{\lambda_{i}^{N}}^{1}(N^{2/3}\tau{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}})-\beta\tau N^{2/3}{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}})\Bigr\}_{1\leq i\leq k}

\displaystyle=\Bigl\{\frac{1}{\chi N^{1/3}}G_{\lambda_{i}^{N}}^{1}(N^{2/3}\tau{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}})-\frac{\tau}{\chi^{2}}\beta\chi N^{1/3}{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}})\Bigr\}_{1\leq i\leq k}

\displaystyle\overset{d}{=}\bigl\{G^{\sqrt{\tau}/\chi}_{(\lambda_{i}^{N}-\beta)\chi N^{1/3}}\bigr\}_{1\leq i\leq k}=\{G^{\sqrt{2}}_{\xi_{i}}\}_{1\leq i\leq k}.

Therefore, to show

\{h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,{\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt};G^{\sqrt{2}}_{\xi})-h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,0;G^{\sqrt{2}}_{\xi})\}_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}}\overset{d}{=}G^{\sqrt{2}}

it suffices to show that, for each
t>0
, the following distributional convergence holds as processes on
C(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R}^{k})
:

\{H_{i}^{N}(t,{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}})\}_{1\leq i\leq k}\overset{N\to\infty}{\Longrightarrow}\{h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}};G_{\xi_{i}}^{\sqrt{2}})\}_{1\leq i\leq k}.

(3.47)

From definition of
H_{i}^{N}
, we observe the following:

\displaystyle H_{i}^{N}(t,y)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\chi N^{1/3}}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\Bigl(\Bigl\{G_{\lambda_{i}^{N}}^{1}(x)+L^{N}(x,1;N\rho t+N^{2/3}\tau y,\lfloor Nt\rfloor)\Bigr\}

(3.48)

\displaystyle-\alpha Nt-\beta\tau N^{2/3}y\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\chi N^{1/3}}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\Bigl\{G_{\lambda_{i}^{N}}^{1}(N^{2/3}\tau x)-\beta\tau N^{1/3}x

\displaystyle+L^{N}(N^{2/3}\tau x,1;N\rho t+N^{2/3}\tau y,\lfloor Nt\rfloor)-\alpha Nt-\beta\tau N^{2/3}(y-x)\Bigr\}

\displaystyle=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\{G_{i}^{N}(x)+\mathcal{L}_{N}(x-\rho\tau^{-1}N^{-2/3},N^{-1};y,t)\}-\frac{\alpha-\beta\rho}{\chi N^{1/3}},

where the
\frac{\alpha-\beta\rho}{\chi N^{-1/3}}
is a correction term that appears simply because we start
L^{N}
along level
1
instead of level
0
. We observed in (
3.46
) that the distribution of
G_{i}^{N}
does not depend on
N
; specifically,
\{G_{i}^{N}\}_{1\leq i\leq k}\overset{d}{=}\{G^{\sqrt{2}}_{\xi_{i}}\}_{1\leq i\leq k}
. We define the process
\{G^{\sqrt{2}}_{\xi_{i}}\}_{1\leq i\leq k}
on our probability space by setting
G^{\sqrt{2}}_{\xi}(x)=G^{1}_{\xi}(2x)
(Theorem
2.3.2
(ii)
). For
1\leq i\leq k
, define

\widetilde{H}_{i}^{N}(t,y)=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\{G^{\sqrt{2}}_{\xi_{i}}(x)+\mathcal{L}_{N}(x-\rho\tau^{-1}N^{-2/3};N^{-1};y,t)\},

and let
\widetilde{Z}_{i}^{N}(t,y)
be the largest maximizer of the above. Then, by (
3.48
),

(\{\widetilde{H}_{i}^{N}-\frac{\alpha-\beta\rho}{\chi N^{1/3}}\}_{1\leq i\leq k},\{N^{2/3}\tau\widetilde{Z}_{i}^{N}\}_{1\leq i\leq k})\overset{d}{=}(\{H_{i}^{N}\}_{1\leq i\leq k},\{Z_{i}^{N}\}_{1\leq i\leq k}),

(3.49)

noting that we made the change of variable
x\mapsto N^{2/3}\tau x
in (
3.48
).
To show (
3.47
), it therefore suffices to show that, for any
t,a,\varepsilon>0
,

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq k\\
y\in[-a,a]\end{subarray}}\Bigl|\widetilde{H}_{i}^{N}(t,y)-h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,y;G_{\xi_{i}})\Bigr|>\varepsilon\Bigr)=0.

Before estimating this probability, we make one last shorthand definition:

Z_{\mathcal{L}}^{i}(t,y)=\max\argmax\{G_{\xi_{i}}(x)+\mathcal{L}(x,0;y,t)\}.

Observe that
Z_{i}^{N}(t,x)\leq Z_{i}^{N}(t,y)
for
x<y
(Lemma
3.4.2
). By a similar argument,
Z_{\mathcal{L}}^{i}(t,x)\leq Z_{\mathcal{L}}^{i}(t,y)
for
x<y
. Then, we observe that for any choice of
M>0
,

\displaystyle\quad\;\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq k\\
y\in[-a,a]\end{subarray}}\Bigl|\widetilde{H}_{i}^{N}(t,y)-h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,y;G_{\xi_{i}})\Bigr|>\varepsilon\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq k\\
y\in[-a,a]\end{subarray}}\Bigl|\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\{G^{\sqrt{2}}_{\xi_{i}}(x)+\mathcal{L}_{N}(x-\rho\tau^{-1}N^{-2/3};N^{-1};y,t)\}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\{G^{\sqrt{2}}_{\xi_{i}}(x)+\mathcal{L}(x,0;y,t)\}\Bigr|>\varepsilon\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\sup_{y\in[-a,a]}\Bigl|\sup_{x\in[-M,M]}\{G^{\sqrt{2}}_{\xi_{i}}(x)+\mathcal{L}_{N}(x-\rho\tau^{-1}N^{-2/3};N^{-1};y,t)\}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-\sup_{x\in[-M,M]}\{G^{\sqrt{2}}_{\xi_{i}}(x)+\mathcal{L}(x,0;y,t)\}\Bigr|>\varepsilon\Bigr)

(3.50)

\displaystyle+\mathbb{P}(Z_{\mathcal{L}}^{i}(t,-a)<-M)+\mathbb{P}(Z_{\mathcal{L}}^{i}(t,a)>M)

(3.51)

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\bigl[\mathbb{P}(\widetilde{Z}_{i}^{N}(t,-a)<-M)+\mathbb{P}(\widetilde{Z}_{i}^{N}(t,a)>M)\bigr].

(3.52)

The term (
3.50
) goes to
0
as
N\to\infty
by the uniform convergence on compact sets
\mathcal{L}_{N}\to\mathcal{L}
(Theorem
3.2.1
) and continuity of
\mathcal{L}
, the term (
3.51
) goes to
0
as
M\to\infty
because, by Lemma
B.1.2
,
\mathcal{L}(x,0;y,t)\sim-\frac{(x-y)^{2}}{t}
and
G_{\xi_{i}}^{\sqrt{2}}
is a Brownian motion with drift, so
x\mapsto G_{\xi_{i}}^{\sqrt{2}}(x)+\mathcal{L}(x,0;y,t)
almost surely has a finite maximizer. Then, using (
3.49
),(
3.52
) and the exit point bounds of Theorem
3.4.1
,

\displaystyle\quad\;\limsup_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq k\\
y\in[-a,a]\end{subarray}}\Bigl|\widetilde{H}_{i}^{N}(t,y)-h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,y;G_{\xi_{i}})\Bigr|>\varepsilon\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{k}\limsup_{M\to\infty}\limsup_{N\to\infty}[\mathbb{P}(\widetilde{Z}_{i}^{N}(t,-a)<-M)+\mathbb{P}(\widetilde{Z}_{i}^{N}(t,a)>M)\bigr]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\limsup_{M\to\infty}\limsup_{N\to\infty}[\mathbb{P}(Z_{i}^{N}(t,-a)<-M\tau N^{2/3})+\mathbb{P}(Z_{i}^{N}(t,a)>M\tau N^{2/3})\bigr]=0.

This concludes the proof of invariance.

Attractiveness and uniqueness:
The proof idea is similar to that of Theorem 3.3 in
[
11
]
.
Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
and let
\bar{\xi}=(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k})\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
be a strictly increasing vector. Let
\bar{\mathfrak{h}}=(\mathfrak{h}_{1},...,\mathfrak{h}_{k})\in\operatorname{UC}^{k}
satisfy (
3.7
) with
\mathfrak{h}=\mathfrak{h}_{i}
and
\xi=\xi_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq k
.
Let
\varepsilon>0
. By the
\sigma=\sqrt{2}
case of Theorem
2.5.2
(iii)
, almost surely, there exists
\delta>0
such that
G^{\sqrt{2}}_{\xi_{i}\pm\delta}(x)=G^{\sqrt{2}}_{\xi_{i}}(x)\ \text{ for all }x\in[-a,a],\,1\leq i\leq k
. Hence, we may choose
\delta>0
small so that

\mathbb{P}\bigl\{G^{\sqrt{2}}_{\xi_{i}\pm\delta}(x)=G^{\sqrt{2}}_{\xi_{i}}(x)\ \,\forall x\in[-a,a],\,1\leq i\leq k\bigr\}\geq 1-{\varepsilon}/{2}.

Then, by invariance of
G^{\sqrt{2}}
under
h_{\mathcal{L}}
, for all
t>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl\{h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,x;G^{\sqrt{2}}_{\xi_{i}\pm\delta})-h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,0;G^{\sqrt{2}}_{\xi_{i}\pm\delta})

\displaystyle=h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,x;G^{\sqrt{2}}_{\xi_{i}})-h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,0;G^{\sqrt{2}}_{\xi_{i}})

(3.53)

\displaystyle\forall x\in[-a,a],\,1\leq i\leq k\bigr\}\geq 1-{\varepsilon}/{2}.

Recall the sets
Z^{a,0,t}_{f}
of exit points from (
3.19
). Because
G^{\sqrt{2}}_{\xi_{i}\pm\delta}
is a Brownian motion with diffusivity
\sqrt{2}
and drift
2(\xi_{i}\pm\delta)
(Proposition
2.3.1
(i)
), it satisfies (
3.7
) for
\xi_{i}\pm\delta
. By
the temporal reflection symmetry of Lemma
B.1.1
, Lemma
3.5.1
implies that, almost surely for all
t
sufficiently large,

\displaystyle Z^{a,0,t}_{G^{\sqrt{2}}_{\xi_{i}-\delta}}

\displaystyle\subseteq[(\xi_{i}-5\delta/4)t,(\xi_{i}-3\delta/4)t],\;\;Z^{a,0,t}_{\mathfrak{h}_{i}}\subseteq[(\xi_{i}-\delta/4)t,(\xi+\delta/4)t],

\displaystyle\;\;\text{and}\;\;Z^{a,0,t}_{G^{\sqrt{2}}_{\xi_{i}+\delta}}

\displaystyle\subseteq[(\xi_{i}+3\delta/4)t,(\xi_{i}+5\delta/4)t].

Hence, for sufficiently large
t
,

\mathbb{P}\bigl(Z^{a,0,t}_{G^{\sqrt{2}}_{\xi_{i}-\delta}}\leq Z^{a,0,t}_{\mathfrak{h}_{i}}\leq Z^{a,0,t}_{G^{\sqrt{2}}_{\xi_{i}+\delta}}\ \,\forall\hskip 0.7pt1\leq i\leq k\bigr)>1-{\varepsilon}/{2},

(3.54)

where for
A,B\subseteq\mathbb{R}
we say
A\leq B
if
\sup A\leq\inf B
. By Lemma
B.1.5
(iii)
, on the event in (
3.54
) the following holds for all
x\in[0,a]
and
1\leq i\leq k
:

\displaystyle h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,x;G^{\sqrt{2}}_{\xi_{i}-\delta})-h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,0;G^{\sqrt{2}}_{\xi_{i}-\delta})

(3.55)

\displaystyle\leq h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,x;\mathfrak{h}_{i})-h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,0;\mathfrak{h}_{i})

\displaystyle\leq h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,x;G^{\sqrt{2}}_{\xi_{i}+\delta})-h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,0;G^{\sqrt{2}}_{\xi_{i}+\delta}).

The reverse inequalities hold for
x\in[-a,0]
. Combining (
3.53
)–(
3.55
), we have that for sufficiently large
t
,

\mathbb{P}\bigl\{h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,x;G^{\sqrt{2}}_{\xi_{i}})-h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,0;G^{\sqrt{2}}_{\xi_{i}})=h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,x;\mathfrak{h}_{i})-h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,0;\mathfrak{h}_{i})\ \,\forall x\in[-a,a],1\leq i\leq k\bigr\}\geq 1-\varepsilon.

The proof of Theorem
3.2.2
is complete.
∎

Chapter 4
Busemann process and the global structure of semi-infinite geodesics in the directed landscape

4.1
Introduction

4.1.1
Semi-infinite geodesics in the DL

Recall the notion of geodesics for the directed landscape (DL) discussed in Section
3.2.2
. A
semi-infinite geodesic
starting from
(x,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
is a continuous path
g:[s,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
such that
g(s)=x
and the restriction of
g
to each compact interval
[s,t]\subseteq[s,\infty)
is a geodesic between
(x,s)
and
(g(t),t)
. Such an infinite path
g
has
direction

\xi\in\mathbb{R}
if
\lim_{t\to\infty}g(t)/t=\xi
. Two semi-infinite geodesics
g_{1}
and
g_{2}

coalesce
if there exists
t
such that
g_{1}(u)=g_{2}(u)
for all
u\geq t
. If
t
is the minimal such time, then
(g_{1}(t),t)
is the
coalescence point
.
Two semi-infinite geodesics
g_{1},g_{2}:[s,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
are
distinct
if
g_{1}(t)\neq g_{2}(t)
for at least some
t\in(s,\infty)
and
disjoint
if
g_{1}(t)\neq g_{2}(t)
for all
t\in(s,\infty)
.

In this chapter, we give a detailed study of semi-infinite geodesics in the DL, as has appeared in the author’s joint work with Busani and Seppäläinen
[
35
]
. A significant consequence of Theorem
3.2.2
is that the stationary horizon characterizes the distribution of the Busemann process of the directed landscape (Theorem
4.4.3
). The Busemann process is a key tool that allows us to construct semi-infinite geodesics in every direction and from every initial point. Many of the tools necessary for this study were developed in the BLPP context in the author’s work with Seppäläinen
[
134
,
135
]
, to which we refer the reader for a detailed study of infinite geodesics in BLPP.

It is not immediately obvious that semi-infinite geodesics should exist, nor is it obvious that they should have directions. The first to study semi-infinite geodesics in the DL was Rahman and Virág
[
126
]
, and we give a summary of their results in Section
4.3
. For a fixed direction, they established the existence of semi-infinite geodesics from all points and showed the coalescence of these geodesics. Closely tied to the study of geodesics is that of
Busemann functions
, a tool originating from differential geometry
[
36
]
. The work of
[
126
]
showed the existence of Busemann functions for a fixed direction. They also showed that, for a fixed point, there exists semi-infinite geodesics in each direction, with an at most countable set of directions for which the geodesic is not unique.

Starting from the definition in
[
126
]
, we construct the full Busemann process across all directions. Through the properties of this process, we establish a classification of uniqueness and coalescence of semi-infinite geodesics in the directed landscape. Similar constructions of the Busemann process and classifications for discrete and semi-discrete models have previously been achieved
[
132
,
90
,
93
,
135
]
, but the procedure in the directed landscape is more delicate. One reason is that the space is fully continuous. Another difficulty is that Busemann functions in DL possess monotonicity only in horizontal directions, while discrete and semi-discrete models exhibit monotonicity in both horizontal and vertical directions. A new perspective is needed to construct the Busemann process for arbitrary initial points.

The full Busemann process is necessary for a complete understanding of the geometry of semi-infinite geodesics. In particular, countable dense sets of initial points or directions cannot capture non-uniqueness of geodesics or the singularities of the Busemann process.

After the first version of
[
35
]
was posted, Ganguly and Zhang
[
70
]
gave an independent construction of a Busemann function and semi-infinite geodesics, again for a fixed direction. They defined a notion of “geodesic local time” which was key to understanding the global fractal geometry of geodesics in DL. Later in
[
69
]
, the same authors showed that the discrete analogue of geodesic local time in exponential LPP converges to geodesic local time for the DL.

Matching the conjectures emanating from the nonexistence of bi-infinite geodesics in discrete models, it was recently shown by Bhatia
[
26
]
that, with probability one, bi-infinite geodesics do not exist for the DL.

4.1.2
Non-uniqueness of geodesics and random fractals

Among the key questions is the uniqueness of semi-infinite geodesics in the directed landscape. We show the existence of a countably infinite, dense random set
\Xi
of directions
\xi
such that, from each initial point in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, two semi-infinite geodesics in direction
\xi
emanate, separate immediately or after some time, and never return back together. It is interesting to relate this result and its proof to earlier work on disjoint finite geodesics.

The set of exceptional pairs of points between which there is a non-unique geodesic in DL was studied in
[
23
]
. Their approach relied on
[
20
]
which studied the random nondecreasing function
z\mapsto\mathcal{L}(y,s;z,t)-\mathcal{L}(x,s;z,t)
for fixed
x<y
and
s<t
. This process is locally constant except on an exceptional set of Hausdorff dimension
\frac{1}{2}
. From here
[
23
]
showed that for fixed
s<t
and
x<y
, the set of
z\in\mathbb{R}
such that there exist disjoint geodesics from
(x,s)
to
(z,t)
and from
(y,s)
to
(z,t)
is exactly the set of local variation of the function
z\mapsto\mathcal{L}(x,s;z,t)-\mathcal{L}(y,s;z,t)
, and therefore has Hausdorff dimension
\frac{1}{2}
. Going further, they showed that for fixed
s<t
, the set of pairs
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that there exist two disjoint geodesics from
(x,s)
to
(y,t)
also has Hausdorff dimension
\frac{1}{2}
, almost surely. Later, this exceptional set in the time direction was studied in
[
70
]
, and was shown to have Hausdorff dimension
2/3
. Across the entire plane, this set has Hausdorff dimension
\frac{5}{3}
.
In a similar spirit, Dauvergne
[
45
]
recently posted a paper detailing all the possible configurations of non-unique point-to-point geodesics, along with the Hausdorff dimensions–with respect to a particular metric–of the sets of points with those configurations.

Our focus is on the limit of the measure studied in
[
20
]
, namely, the nondecreasing function
\xi\mapsto W_{\xi}(y,s;x,s)=\lim_{t\to\infty}[\mathcal{L}(y,s;t\xi,t)-\mathcal{L}(x,s;t\xi,t)]
, which is exactly the Busemann function in direction
\xi
.
The support of its Lebesgue-Stieltjes measure corresponds to the existence of disjoint geodesics (Theorem
4.6.9
), but in contrast to
[
23
]
, the measure is supported on a countable discrete set instead of on a set of Hausdorff dimension
\frac{1}{2}
(Theorem
4.4.5
(iv)
and Remark
4.4.6
).

We encounter a Hausdorff dimension
\frac{1}{2}
set if we look along a fixed time level
s
for those space-time points
(x,s)
out of which there are disjoint semi-infinite geodesics in a
random, exceptional
direction (Theorem
4.2.6
(iii)
). Up to the removal of an at-most countable set, this Hausdorff dimension
\frac{1}{2}
set is the support of the random measure defined by the function

x\mapsto f_{s,\xi}(x)=W_{\xi+}(x,s;0,s)-W_{\xi-}(x,s;0,s),

where
W_{\xi\pm}

are the right and left-continuous Busemann processes (Theorem
4.7.1
). This is a semi-infinite analogue of the result in
[
23
]
.

The distribution of
f_{s,\xi}
is delicate. The set of directions
\xi
such that
W_{\xi-}\neq W_{\xi+}
, or equivalently such that

\tau_{\xi}=\inf\{x>0:f_{s,\xi}(x)>0\}<\infty
,
is the set
\Xi
mentioned above. A fixed direction
\xi
lies in
\Xi
with probability
0
. Because the SH describes the Busemann process for the DL, Theorem
2.6.1
shows that the law of
f_{s,\xi}(\tau_{\xi}+{\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt})
on
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, conditioned on
\xi\in\Xi
in the appropriate Palm sense, is exactly that of the running maximum of a Brownian motion, or equivalently, that of Brownian local time. This complements the fact that the function
z\mapsto\mathcal{L}(y,s;z,t)-\mathcal{L}(x,s;z,t)
is locally absolutely continuous with respect to Brownian local time
[
68
]
.

Since the first version of
[
35
]
appeared, Bhatia
[
25
,
26
]
posted two papers that use the results as inputs. The first,
[
25
]
studies the Hausdorff dimension of the set of splitting points of geodesics along a geodesic itself. The second,
[
26
]
answers an open problem presented in
[
35
]
. Namely, for all points in the set
\operatorname{NU}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}
defined in (
4.40
), the geodesics split immediately from the initial point, and for a fixed direction
\xi
the set
\operatorname{NU}^{\xi}
almost surely has Hausdorff dimension
\frac{4}{3}
.

4.2
Main results

In this chapter, the Busemann process is used to construct a special class of semi-infinite geodesics called
Busemann geodesics
simultaneously from all initial points and in all directions (Theorem
4.4.9
). The definition of Busemann geodesics, along with a detailed study, comes in Section
4.4
.

The first theorem states our conclusions for general semi-infinite geodesics. The random countably infinite dense set
\Xi
of directions is later characterized in (
4.4
) as the discontinuity set of the Busemann process, and its properties stated in Theorem
4.4.5
.

We assume the probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
of the directed landscape
\mathcal{L}
complete. All statements about semi-infinite geodesics are with respect to
\mathcal{L}
.

Theorem 4.2.1
.

The following statements hold on a single event of full probability. There exists a random countably infinite dense subset
\Xi
, of
\mathbb{R}
, such that parts
(ii)
–
(iii)
below hold.

(i)

Every semi-infinite geodesic has a direction
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
. From each initial point
p\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and in each direction
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
, there exists at least one semi-infinite geodesic from
p
in direction
\xi
.

(ii)

When
\xi\notin\Xi
, all semi-infinite geodesics in direction
\xi
coalesce. There exists a random set of initial points, of zero planar Lebesgue measure, outside of which the semi-infinite geodesic in each direction
\xi\notin\Xi
is unique.

(iii)

When
\xi\in\Xi
, there exist at least two families of semi-infinite geodesics in direction
\xi
, called the
\xi-
and
\xi+
geodesics. From every initial point
p\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
there exists both a
\xi-
geodesic and a
\xi+
geodesic which eventually separate and never come back together. All
\xi-
geodesics coalesce, and all
\xi+
geodesics coalesce.

Remark 4.2.2

(Busemann geodesics and general geodesics)
.

Theorem
4.2.1
is proved by controlling all semi-infinite geodesics with Busemann geodesics. Namely, from each initial point
p
and in each direction
\xi
, all semi-infinite geodesics lie between the leftmost and rightmost Busemann geodesics (Theorem
4.5.7
(i)
). Furthermore, for all
p
outside a random set of Lebesgue measure zero and all
\xi\notin\Xi
, the two extreme Busemann geodesics coincide and thereby imply the uniqueness of the semi-infinite geodesic from
p
in direction
\xi
(Theorem
4.2.1
(ii)
).
Even more generally, whenever
\xi\notin\Xi
, all semi-infinite geodesics in direction
\xi
are Busemann geodesics (Theorem
4.6.3
(viii)
). This is presently unknown for
\xi\in\Xi
, but may be expected by virtue of what is known about exponential LPP
[
93
]
.

This work therefore gives a nearly complete description of the global behavior of semi-infinite geodesics in the directed landscape. The conjecture that all semi-infinite geodesics are Busemann geodesics is equivalent to the following statement: In Item
(iii)
, for
\xi\in\Xi
, there are
exactly
two families of coalescing semi-infinite geodesics in direction
\xi
. That is, each
\xi
-directed semi-infinite geodesic coalesces either with the
\xi-
geodesics or the
\xi+
geodesics.

Remark 4.2.3

(Non-uniqueness of geodesics)
.

The non-uniqueness of geodesics from initial points in a Lebesgue null set in Theorem
4.2.1
(ii)
is temporary in the sense that these geodesics eventually coalesce. This forms a “bubble.” The first point of intersection after the split is the coalescence point (Theorem
4.6.1
(ii)
). Hence, these particular geodesics form at most one bubble. This contrasts with the non-uniqueness of Theorem
4.2.1
(iii)
, where geodesics do not return together (Figure
4.1
). Non-uniqueness is discussed in detail in Section
4.5
.

Remark 4.2.4
.

The authors of
[
126
]
alluded to non-uniqueness of geodesics. They showed that for a fixed initial point, with probability one, there are at most countably many directions with a non-unique geodesic. On page 23 of
[
126
]
, they note that the set of directions with a non-unique geodesic “should be dense over the real line.” Our result is that this set is dense and, furthermore, it is the set
\Xi
of discontinuities of the Busemann process.

Figure 4.1
:
On the left, a depiction of the non-uniqueness in Theorem
4.2.1
(ii)
: geodesics separate and coalesce back together, forming a bubble. Due to work of Bhatia
[
26
]
and Dauvergne
[
45
]
, this is the only possible configuration for this type of non-uniqueness–that is, geodesics which split and later coalesce can only split at the initial point. On the right,
\xi\in\Xi
. The blue/thin paths depict the
\xi-
geodesics, while the red/thick paths depict the
\xi+
geodesics. From each point, the
\xi-
and
\xi+
geodesics separate at points of
\mathfrak{S}
. The
\xi-
and
\xi+
families each have a coalescing structure.

The second theorem of this section describes the set of initial points with disjoint geodesics in the same direction. By disjoint, we mean that the geodesics only common point in the plane is the initial point. Let
\Xi
be the random set from Theorem
4.2.1
(precisely characterized in (
4.4
)). Define the following random sets of splitting points.

\displaystyle\mathfrak{S}_{s,\xi}

\displaystyle:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}:\exists\text{ {disjoint}}\text{ }\text{semi-infinite geodesics from }(x,s)\text{ in direction }\xi\}

(4.1)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{S}

\displaystyle:=\bigcup_{s\hskip 0.9pt\in\hskip 0.9pt\mathbb{R},\,\xi\hskip 0.9pt\in\hskip 0.9pt\Xi}\mathfrak{S}_{s,\xi}\times\{s\}.

(4.2)

Remark 4.2.5
.

From Theorem
4.2.1
(ii)
,
\mathfrak{S}_{s,\xi}=\varnothing
whenever
\xi\notin\Xi
.

Theorem 4.2.6
.

The following hold.

(i)

On a single event of full probability, the set
\mathfrak{S}
is dense in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

(ii)

For each fixed
p\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
\mathbb{P}(p\in\mathfrak{S})=0
.

(iii)

For each
s\in\mathbb{R}
, on an
s
-dependent full-probability event, for every
\xi\in\Xi
, the set
\mathfrak{S}_{s,\xi}
has Hausdorff dimension
\frac{1}{2}
.

(iv)

On a single event of full probability, simultaneously for every
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
\xi\in\Xi
, the set
\mathfrak{S}_{s,\xi}
is nonempty and unbounded in both directions.

Remark 4.2.7
.

For each
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
\xi\in\Xi
, the set

\mathfrak{S}_{s,\xi}

has an interpretation as the support of a random measure, up to the removal of a countable set. Thus, since
\Xi
is countable, for each
s\in\mathbb{R}
, the set
\{x\in\mathbb{R}:(x,s)\in\mathfrak{S}\}
is the countable union of supports of random measures, up to the removal of an at most countable set. By Item
(iii)
, this set also has Hausdorff dimension
\frac{1}{2}
.
Conditioning in the appropriate Palm sense on
\xi\in\Xi
, the random measure whose support is “almost”
\mathfrak{S}_{s,\xi}
is equal to the local time of a Brownian motion (Theorems
4.7.1
,
2.6.1
,and
2.6.11
).
We expect that, simultaneously for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
, the set
\mathfrak{S}_{s,\xi}
has Hausdorff dimension
\frac{1}{2}
, but currently lack a global result stronger than Item
(iv)
.

4.2.1
Organization of the chapter

In the following section, we cite results from
[
126
]
. The remainder of the chapter covers finer results on the Busemann process and semi-infinite geodesics. Sections
4.4
–
4.6
each start with several theorems that are then proved later in the section. The theorems can be read independently of the proofs. Each section depends on the sections that came before. Section
4.4
describes the construction of the Busemann process and infinite geodesics in all directions. Section
4.5
gives a detailed discussion of non-uniqueness of geodesics. Section
4.6
is concerned with coalescence and connects the regularity of the Busemann process to the geometry of geodesics. The proofs of Theorems
4.2.1
and
4.2.6
come in Section
4.7
.

4.3
Summary of the Rahman–Virág results

The paper
[
126
]
shows existence of the Busemann function for a fixed direction.
Below is a summary of their results that we use.

Theorem 4.3.1

(
[
126
]
)
.

The following hold.

(i)

For fixed initial point
p
, there exist almost surely leftmost and rightmost semi-infinite geodesics
g_{p}^{\xi,\ell}
and
g_{p}^{\xi,r}
from
p
in every direction
\xi
simultaneously. There are at most countably many directions
\xi
such that
g_{p}^{\xi,\ell}\neq g_{p}^{\xi,r}

(ii)

For fixed direction
\xi
, there exist almost surely leftmost and rightmost geodesics
g_{p}^{\xi,\ell}
and
g_{p}^{\xi,r}
in direction
\xi
from every initial point
p
.

(iii)

For fixed
p=(x,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
,
g:=g_{p}^{\xi,\ell}=g_{p}^{\xi,r}
with probability one.

(iv)

Given
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
, all semi-infinite geodesics in direction
\xi
coalesce with probability one.

Remark 4.3.2
.

Article
[
126
]
used
-
and
+
in place of the superscripts
\ell
and
r
used above. We replaced
-/+
with
\ell/r
to avoid confusion with our
\pm
notation that links with the left- and right-continuous Busemann processes. As demonstrated in Section
4.5
, non-uniqueness of geodesics is properly characterized by two parameters
{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.9}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}\in\{-,+\}
and
S\in\{L,R\}
.

For fixed direction
\xi
,
[
126
]
defines
\kappa^{\xi}(p,q)
as the coalescence point of the rightmost geodesics in direction
\xi
from initial points
p
and
q
. Then, they define the Busemann function

W_{\xi}(p;q)=\mathcal{L}(p;\kappa^{\xi}(p,q))-\mathcal{L}(q;\kappa^{\xi}(p,q)).

(4.3)

Theorem 4.3.3

(
[
126
]
, Corollary 3.3, Theorem 3.5, Remark 3.1)
.

(i)

For each
t\in\mathbb{R}
, the process
x\mapsto W_{\xi}(x,t;0,t)
is a two-sided Brownian motion with diffusivity
\sqrt{2}
and drift
2\xi
.

Given a direction
\xi
, the following hold on a
\xi
-dependent event of probability one.

(ii)

Additivity:
W_{\xi}(p;q)+W_{\xi}(q;r)=W_{\xi}(p;r)
for all
p,q,r\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

(iii)

For all
s<t
and
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
,

W_{\xi}(x,s;y,t)=\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\{\mathcal{L}(x,s;z,t)+W_{\xi}(z,t;y,t)\}.

The supremum is attained exactly at those
z
such that
(z,t)
lies on a semi-infinite geodesic from
(x,s)
in direction
\xi
.

(iv)

The function
W_{\xi}:\mathbb{R}^{4}\to\mathbb{R}
is continuous.

Moreover:

(v)

For a pair of fixed directions
\xi_{1}<\xi_{2}
, with probability one, for every
t\in\mathbb{R}
and

x<y
,
W_{\xi_{1}}(y,t;x,t)\leq W_{\xi_{2}}(y,t;x,t)
.

4.4
Busemann process and Busemann geodesics

In this section, we first present a list of theorems regarding the Busemann process in Section
4.4.1
. Section
4.4.2
defines Busemann geodesics and states their main properties. The proofs are found in Section
4.4.3
, except for the proofs of Theorem
4.4.1
(vi)
-
(viii)
and the mixing in Theorem
4.4.3
(ii)
, which are proved in Section
4.7.1
, and Theorem
4.4.5
(ii)
, which is proved in Section
4.7.2
.

4.4.1
The Busemann process

The Busemann process
\{W_{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}(p;q)\}
is indexed by points
p,q\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, a direction
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
, and a sign
{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.9}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}\in\{-,+\}
.
The following theorems describe this process. The parameter
{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.9}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}\in\{-,+\}
denotes the left- and right-continuous versions of this process as a function of
\xi
.

Theorem 4.4.1
.

On
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
, there exists a process

\{W_{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}(p;q):\xi\in\mathbb{R},\,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.9}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}\in\{-,+\},\,p,q\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\}

satisfying the following properties. All the properties below hold on a single event of probability one, simultaneously for all directions
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
, signs
{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.9}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}\in\{-,+\}
, and points
p,q\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, unless otherwise specified. Below, for
p,q\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we define the sets

\Xi(p;q)=\{\xi\in\mathbb{R}:W_{\xi-}(p;q)\neq W_{\xi+}(p;q)\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\Xi=\textstyle\bigcup_{p,q\,\in\,\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Xi(p;q).

(4.4)

(i)

(Continuity)
As an
\mathbb{R}^{4}\to\mathbb{R}
function,
(x,s;y,t)\mapsto W_{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}(x,s;y,t)
is continuous.

(ii)

(Additivity)
For all
p,q,r\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,

W_{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}(p;q)+W_{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}(q;r)=W_{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}(p;r)
. In particular,
W_{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}(p;q)=-W_{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}(q;p)
and
W_{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}(p;p)=0
.

(iii)

(Monotonicity along a horizontal line)

Whenever
\xi_{1}<\xi_{2}
,
x<y
, and
t\in\mathbb{R}
,

W_{\xi_{1}-}(y,t;x,t)\leq W_{\xi_{1}+}(y,t;x,t)\leq W_{\xi_{2}-}(y,t;x,t)\leq W_{\xi_{2}+}(y,t;x,t).

(iv)

(Backwards evolution as the KPZ fixed point)
For
all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
and
s<t
,

W_{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}(x,s;y,t)=\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\{\mathcal{L}(x,s;z,t)+W_{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}(z,t;y,t)\}.

(4.5)

(v)

(Regularity in the direction parameter)

The process
\xi\mapsto W_{\xi+}
is right-continuous in the sense of uniform convergence on compact sets of functions
\mathbb{R}^{4}\to\mathbb{R}
, and
\xi\mapsto W_{\xi-}
is left-continuous in the same sense. The restrictions to compact sets are locally constant in the parameter
\xi
: for each
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
and compact set
K\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{4}
there exists a random
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(\xi,K)>0
such that, whenever
\xi-\varepsilon<\alpha<\xi<\beta<\xi+\varepsilon
and
{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.9}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}\in\{-,+\}
, we have these equalities for all
(x,s;y,t)\in K
:

W_{\alpha{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}(x,s;y,t)=W_{\xi-}(x,s;y,t)\qquad\text{and}\qquad W_{\beta{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}(x,s;y,t)=W_{\xi+}(x,s;y,t).

(4.6)

(vi)

(Busemann limits I) If
\xi\notin\Xi
, then, for any compact set
K\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
and any net
r_{t}=(z_{t},u_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}
with
u_{t}\to\infty
and
z_{t}/u_{t}\to\xi
as
t\to\infty
, there exists
R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that, for all
p,q\in K
and
t\geq R
,

W_{\xi}(p;q)=\mathcal{L}(p;r_{t})-\mathcal{L}(q;r_{t}).

(vii)

(Busemann limits II) For all
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
,
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
x<y\in\mathbb{R}
, and any net
(z_{t},u_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that
u_{t}\to\infty
and
z_{t}/u_{t}\to\xi
as
t\to\infty
,

\displaystyle W_{\xi-}(y,s;x,s)

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{t\to\infty}\mathcal{L}(y,s;z_{t},u_{t})-\mathcal{L}(x,s;z_{t},u_{t})

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{t\to\infty}\mathcal{L}(y,s;z_{t},u_{t})-\mathcal{L}(x,s;z_{t},u_{t})\leq W_{\xi+}(y,s;x,s).

(viii)

(Global attractiveness) Assume that
\xi\notin\Xi
, and let
\mathfrak{h}\in\operatorname{UC}
satisfy condition (
3.7
) for the parameter
\xi
. For
s<t
, let

h_{s,t}(x;\mathfrak{h})=\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\{\mathcal{L}(x,s;z,t)+\mathfrak{h}(z)\}.

Then, for any
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
a>0
, there exists a random
t_{0}=t_{0}(a,\xi,s)<\infty
such that for all
t>t_{0}
and
x\in[-a,a]
,
h_{s,t}(x;\mathfrak{h})-h_{s,t}(0;\mathfrak{h})=W_{\xi}(x,s;0,s)
.

Remark 4.4.2
.

Item
(vi)
is novel in that it shows the limits simultaneously for all
\xi\notin\Xi
, uniformly over compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. The existence of Busemann limits in fixed directions
is shown in
[
126
]
and
[
70
]
.
Item
(viii)
is analogous to Theorem 3.3 in
[
11
]
and Theorem 3.3 in
[
13
]
on the global solutions of the Burgers’ equation with random forcing. When comparing with
[
11
,
13
]
, note that our geodesics travel north while theirs head south.

We describe the distribution of this process. The key to Item
(iii)
is Theorem
3.2.2
.

Theorem 4.4.3
.

The following hold.

(i)

(Independence)
For each
T\in\mathbb{R}
, these processes are independent:

\displaystyle\{W_{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}(x,s;y,t):\xi\in\mathbb{R},\,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.9}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}\in\{-,+\},\,x,y\in\mathbb{R},\,s,t\geq T\}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\text{and }\ \ \{\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t):x,y\in\mathbb{R},\,s<t\leq T\}.

(ii)

(Stationarity and mixing)
The process

\{\mathcal{L}(v),W_{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}(p;q):v\in\mathbb{R}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\uparrow$}}}}^{4},\,p,q\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\,\xi\in\mathbb{R},\,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.9}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}\in\{-,+\}\}

(4.7)

is stationary and mixing under shifts in any space-time direction. More precisely, let
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
, not both
0
, and
z>0
. Set
r_{z}=(az,bz)
. Then, the process (
4.7
) is stationary and mixing
(
for fixed
a,b
as
z\to+\infty
)
under the transformation

\displaystyle\bigl\{\mathcal{L}(v),W_{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}(p;q)\bigr\}\mapsto T_{z;a,b}\{\mathcal{L},W\}:=\{\mathcal{L}(v+(r_{z};r_{z})),W_{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}(p+r_{z};q+r_{z})\},

where the process on each side is a function of
(v,(p,q))\in\mathbb{R}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\uparrow$}}}}^{4}\times\mathbb{R}^{4}
. Mixing means that, for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
,
\xi_{1},\dotsc,\xi_{k}\in\mathbb{R}
, and Borel subsets
A,B\subseteq C(\mathbb{R}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\uparrow$}}}}^{4},\mathbb{R})\times C(\mathbb{R}^{4},\mathbb{R})^{k}
, if we denote
W_{\xi_{1:k}}=(W_{\xi_{1}},\dotsc,W_{\xi_{k}})\in C(\mathbb{R}^{4},\mathbb{R})^{k}
, then

\displaystyle\lim_{z\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\{\mathcal{L},W_{\xi_{1:k}}\}\in A,\{T_{z;a,b}\mathcal{L},T_{z;a,b}W_{\xi_{1:k}}\}\in B\Bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad=\mathbb{P}\bigl(\{\mathcal{L},W_{\xi_{1:k}}\}\in A\bigr)\mathbb{P}\bigl(\{\mathcal{L},W_{\xi_{1:k}}\}\in B\bigr).

(iii)

(Distribution along a time level)
For each
t\in\mathbb{R}
, the following equality in distribution holds between random elements of the Skorokhod space
D(\mathbb{R},C(\mathbb{R}))
:

\{W_{\xi+}({\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt},t;0,t)\}_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}}\overset{d}{=}\bigl\{G^{\sqrt{2}}_{\xi}({\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt})\bigr\}_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}},

where
G^{\sqrt{2}}
is the stationary horizon with diffusivity
\sqrt{2}
and drifts
2\xi
.

Remark 4.4.4
.

Combining Items
(i)
and
(iii)
with Theorem
4.4.1
(iv)
gives a description of the Busemann process on the full plane
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

We describe the random sets of Busemann discontinuities defined in (
4.4
). Item
(ii)
below states that the discontinuities of the Busemann process are present along each horizontal line. Since the Busemann process along each line is described by the SH (Theorem
4.4.3
(iii)
), the distributional invariances for
\Xi_{G}
proved in Theorem
2.5.2
also hold for
\Xi({\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}},t;{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}},t)
.

Theorem 4.4.5
.

The following hold on a single event of probability one.

(i)

For each
t\in\mathbb{R}
, the set
\Xi(x,t;-x,t)
is nondecreasing as a function of
x\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
.

(ii)

For
s,\xi\in\mathbb{R}
, define the function

x\mapsto f_{s,\xi}(x):=W_{\xi+}(x,s;0,s)-W_{\xi-}(x,s;0,s).

(4.8)

Then,
\xi\in\Xi
if and only if, for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
,

\lim_{x\to\pm\infty}f_{s,\xi}(x)=\pm\infty.

(4.9)

In particular, simultaneously for all
s,x\in\mathbb{R}
and all sequences
|x_{k}|\to\infty
,

\Xi=\bigcup_{k}\hskip 0.9pt\Xi(x_{k},s;x,s).

(4.10)

(iii)

The set
\Xi
is countably infinite and dense in
\mathbb{R}
, while for each fixed
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
,

\mathbb{P}(\xi\in\Xi)=0
. In particular, the full-probability event of the theorem can be chosen so that
\Xi
contains no directions
\xi\in\mathbb{Q}
.

(iv)

For each
p\neq q
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, the set
\Xi(p;q)
is discrete, that is, has no limit points in
\mathbb{R}
. The function
\xi\mapsto W_{\xi-}(p;q)=W_{\xi+}(p;q)
is constant on each open interval
I\subseteq(\mathbb{R}\setminus\Xi(p;q))
. For
t\in\mathbb{R}
, on a
t
-dependent full-probability event, for all
x<y
,
\Xi(y,t;x,t)
is infinite and unbounded, for both positive and negative
\xi
.

Remark 4.4.6
.

Item
(ii)
states that all discontinuities of the Busemann process are present on each horizontal ray. By Item
(iv)

\xi\mapsto W_{\xi\pm}(p;q)
are the left- and right-continuous versions of a jump process. This function defines a random signed measure supported on a discrete set. When
p
and
q
lie on the same horizontal line, this function is monotone (Theorem
4.4.1
(iii)
) and the support of the measure is exactly the set of directions at which a properly chosen coalescence point of semi-infinite geodesics jumps (see Definition
4.6.7
and Theorems
4.6.8
–
4.6.9
).

4.4.2
Busemann geodesics

The study of Busemann geodesics starts with this definition.

Definition 4.4.7
.

For
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
,
{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.9}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}\in\{-,+\}
,
(x,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
t\in[s,\infty)
, let
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},L}(t)
and
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},R}(t)
denote, respectively, the leftmost and rightmost maximizer of
\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t)+W_{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}(y,t;0,t)
over
y\in\mathbb{R}
.

Remark 4.4.8
.

The modulus of continuity bounds of the directed landscape recorded in Lemma
B.1.2
, along with continuity of
W_{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}
, imply that
\lim_{t\searrow s}g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},L/R}(t)=x
, so we define
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},L/R}(s)=x
.

As noted earlier, Rahman and Virág
[
126
]
showed the existence of semi-infinite geodesics, almost surely for a fixed initial point across all directions and almost surely for a fixed direction across all initial points. We extend this simultaneously across both all initial points and directions.
Theorem
4.3.3
(iii)
, quoted from
[
126
]
, states that for a
fixed
direction
\xi
, with probability one at times
t>s
,
the maximizers
z
of the function
\mathcal{L}(x,s;z,t)+W_{\xi}(z,t;0,t)
are exactly the points on semi-infinite
\xi
-directed geodesics from
(x,s)
.
Theorem
4.4.9
clarifies this on a global scale: across all directions, initial points and signs, one can construct semi-infinite geodesics from the Busemann process.
Furthermore,
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},L}
and
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},R}
both define semi-infinite geodesics in direction
\xi

and give the leftmost (or rightmost) geodesic between any two of their points. We use this fact heavily in the present chapter.

Theorem 4.4.9
.

The following hold on a single event of probability one across all initial points
(x,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, times
t>s
, directions
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
, and signs
{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.9}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}\in\{-,+\}
.

(i)

All maximizers of
z\mapsto\mathcal{L}(x,s;z,t)+W_{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}(z,t;0,t)
are finite. Furthermore, as
x,s,t
vary over a compact set
K\subseteq\mathbb{R}
with
s\leq t
, the set of all maximizers is bounded.

(ii)

Let
s=t_{0}<t_{1}<t_{2}<\cdots
be an arbitrary increasing sequence with
t_{n}\to\infty
. Set
g(t_{0})=x
, and for each
i\geq 1
, let
g(t_{i})
be any maximizer of
\mathcal{L}(g(t_{i-1}),t_{i-1};z,t_{i})+W_{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}(z,t_{i};0,t_{i})
over
z\in\mathbb{R}
. Then, pick any geodesic of
\mathcal{L}
from
(g(t_{i-1}),t_{i-1})
to
(g(t_{i}),t_{i})
, and for
t_{i-1}<t<t_{i}
, let
g(t)
be the location of this geodesic at time
t
. Then, regardless of the choices made at each step, the following hold.

(a)

The path
g:[s,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
is a semi-infinite geodesic.

(b)

For all
t<u
in
[s,\infty)
,

\mathcal{L}(g(t),t;g(u),u)=W_{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}(g(t),t;g(u),u).

(4.11)

(c)

For all
t<u
in
[s,\infty)
,
g(u)
maximizes
\mathcal{L}(g(t),t;z,u)+W_{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}(z,u;0,u)
over
z\in\mathbb{R}
.

(d)

The geodesic
g
has direction
\xi
, i.e.,
g(t)/t\to\xi
as
t\to\infty
.

(iii)

For

S\in\{L,R\}
,
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},S}:[s,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
is a semi-infinite geodesic from
(x,s)
in direction
\xi
. Moreover, for any
s\leq t<u
, we have that

\mathcal{L}\bigl(g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},S}(t),t;g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},S}(u),u\bigr)=W_{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}\bigl(g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},S}(t),t;g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},S}(u),u\bigr),

and
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},S}(u)
is the leftmost/rightmost
(
depending on
S
)
maximizer of

\mathcal{L}(g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},S}(t),t;z,u)+W_{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}(z,u;0,u)
over
z\in\mathbb{R}
.

(iv)

The path
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},L}
is the leftmost geodesic between any two of its points, and
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},R}
is the rightmost geodesic between any two of its points.

Definition 4.4.10
.

We refer to the geodesics constructed in Theorem
4.4.9
(ii)
as
\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}

Busemann geodesics
, or simply
\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}
geodesics
.

Remark 4.4.11
.

The geodesics
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},L}
and
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},R}
are special Busemann geodesics. By Theorem
4.4.9
(iii)
–
(iv)
, for any sequence
s=t_{0}<t_{1}<t_{2}<\cdots
with
t_{n}\to\infty
, the path
g=g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},L}
can be constructed by choosing
g(t_{i})
as the leftmost maximizer of
\mathcal{L}(g(t_{i-1}),t_{i-1};z,t_{i})+W_{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}(z,t_{i};0,t_{i})
over
z\in\mathbb{R}
, and for
t\in(t_{i-1},t_{i})
, taking
g(t)
to be the leftmost geodesic from
(g(t_{i-1}),t_{i-1})
to
(g(t_{i}),t_{i})
. The analogous statement holds for
L
replaced with
R
and “leftmost” replaced with “rightmost”.

4.4.3
Construction and proofs for the Busemann process and Busemann geodesics

This section proves the results of Sections
4.4.1
and
4.4.2
. The order in which the items are proved is somewhat delicate, so we outline that here. After proving some lemmas, we prove Theorem
4.4.1
(i)
–
(iv)
and Theorem
4.4.3
. We then skip ahead to constructing the semi-infinite geodesics, culminating in the proof of Theorem
4.4.9
. Afterward, we turn to the proof of the regularity in Theorem
4.4.1
(v)
, then prove Theorem
4.4.5
, except for Item
(ii)
, which is proved in Section
4.7.2
.

We construct a full-probability event
\Omega_{1}
. Later in (
4.28
) and (
4.76
) follow full-probability events
\Omega_{3}\subseteq\Omega_{2}\subseteq\Omega_{1}
. For the rest of the proofs, we work almost exclusively on these events. Once the events are constructed and shown to have full probability, the remaining proofs are deterministic statements that hold on those events.

We define
\Omega_{1}\subseteq\Omega
to be the event of probability one on which the following hold.

(4.12)

(i)

Simultaneously for all
(x,s;y,t)\in\mathbb{R}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\uparrow$}}}}^{4}
there exist leftmost and rightmost geodesics (possibly in agreement) between
(x,s)
and
(y,t)
(see Section
3.2.2
).

(ii)

For each rational direction
\xi\in\mathbb{Q}
and each point
p\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, there exist leftmost and rightmost semi-infinite geodesics (possibly in agreement) from
p
in direction
\xi
, and all semi-infinite geodesics in direction
\xi
coalesce (see Theorem
4.3.1
, Items
(ii)
and
(iv)
).

(iii)

For each rational direction
\xi\in\mathbb{Q}
and each rational point
p\in\mathbb{Q}^{2}
, there is a unique semi-infinite geodesic from
p
in direction
\xi
(see Theorem
4.3.1
(iii)
).

(iv)

For each rational direction
\xi\in\mathbb{Q}
, the Busemann process defined by (
4.3
) satisfies conditions
(ii)
–
(iv)
of Theorem
4.3.3
. For any pair
\xi_{1}<\xi_{2}
or rational directions, Item
(v)
of Theorem
4.3.3
holds.

(v)

For each
(x,t,y,\xi)\in\mathbb{Q}^{4}
,

\lim_{\mathbb{Q}\ni\alpha\to\xi}W_{\alpha}(y,t;x,t)=W_{\xi}(y,t;x,t).

(vi)

For every rational time
t\in\mathbb{Q}
and rational direction
\xi\in\mathbb{Q}
,

\lim_{x\to\pm\infty}x^{-1}{W_{\xi}(x,t;0,t)}=2\xi.

(4.13)

This holds with probability one by properties of Brownian motion and Theorem
4.3.3
(i)
.

(vii)

The conclusions of Lemmas
B.1.2
,
B.2.1
, and
B.2.5
hold for
\mathcal{L}
. Note that then Lemma
B.1.2
holds also for the reflected version

\{\mathcal{L}(y;-t,x;-s):(x,s;y,t)\in\mathbb{R}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\uparrow$}}}}^{4}\}.

To justify
\mathbb{P}(\Omega_{1})=1
, it remains to check Item
(v)
. By Theorem
4.3.3
(v)
, for
y\geq x
,

\lim_{\mathbb{Q}\ni\alpha\nearrow\xi}W_{\alpha}(y,t;x,t)\leq W_{\xi}(y,t;x,t)\leq\lim_{\mathbb{Q}\ni\alpha\searrow\xi}W_{\alpha}(y,t;x,t).

(4.14)

By Theorem
4.3.3
(i)
,
W_{\alpha}(y,t;x,t)\sim\mathcal{N}(2\alpha(y-x),2(y-x))
. Hence,
all terms in (
4.14
) have the same distribution and are almost surely equal.

Now, on the full-probability event
\Omega_{1}
, we have defined the process

\{W_{\alpha}(p;q):p,q\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\alpha\in\mathbb{Q}\}.

(4.15)

On this event, for an arbitrary direction
\xi
, and
t,x,y\in\mathbb{R}
, define

W_{\xi-}(y,t;x,t)=\lim_{\mathbb{Q}\ni\alpha\nearrow\xi}W_{\alpha}(y,t;x,t)\;\;\text{and}\;\;W_{\xi+}(y,t;x,t)=\lim_{\mathbb{Q}\ni\alpha\searrow\xi}W_{\alpha}(y,t;x,t).

(4.16)

By Theorem
4.3.3
(v)
, these limits exist for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Complete the definition by setting,

\displaystyle\text{ for $s<t$, }\ W_{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}(x,s;y,t)

\displaystyle=\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\{\mathcal{L}(x,s;z,t)+W_{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}(z,t;y,t)\},

(4.17)

\displaystyle\text{ and finally for $s>t$, }\ W_{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}(x,s;y,t)

\displaystyle=-W_{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}(y,t;x,s).

With this construction in place, we prove an intermediate lemma.

Lemma 4.4.12
.

The following hold on the event
\Omega_{1}
, across all points, directions and signs.

(i)

For all
x,y,t\in\mathbb{R}
, and
\xi\in\mathbb{Q}
,
W_{\xi-}(y,t;x,t)=W_{\xi+}(y,t;x,t)=W_{\xi}(y,t;x,t)
, where
W_{\xi}
is the originally defined Busemann function from (
4.15
).

(ii)

Horizontal Busemann functions are additive:
\forall\,x,y,z,t\in\mathbb{R}
,
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
, and
{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.9}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}\in\{-,+\}
,

W_{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}(x,t;y,t)+W_{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}(y,t;z,t)=W_{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}(x,t;z,t).

(iii)

For every
t,\xi\in\mathbb{R}
, the limits (
4.16
) hold uniformly over
(x,y)
on compact sets. Further, for each
t,\xi\in\mathbb{R}
and
{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.9}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}\in\{-,+\}
,
these limits hold in the same sense:

\lim_{\alpha\nearrow\xi}W_{\alpha{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}(y,t;x,t)=W_{\xi-}(y,t;x,t)\ \text{ and }\ \lim_{\alpha\searrow\xi}W_{\alpha{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}(y,t;x,t)=W_{\xi+}(y,t;x,t).

(4.18)

(iv)

For every
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
,
{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.9}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}\in\{-,+\}
,
(p,q)\mapsto W_{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}(p;q)
is continuous, and for each
t\in\mathbb{R}
,
