\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}
geodesic
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}
, and therefore this geodesic is both the leftmost and rightmost
\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}
geodesic from
(x,s)
. Since leftmost (resp. rightmost) Busemann geodesics are leftmost (rightmost) geodesics between any two of their points (Theorem
4.4.9
(iv)
), it follows that
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}
, restricted to times
t\in[s,s+2]
, is the unique geodesic from
(x,s)
to
(g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}(s+2),s+2)
. By Lemma
4.4.13
, for each compact set
K
, the set

\{g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}(s+1):x\in\mathbb{Q}\cap K\}

is contained in some compact set
K^{\prime}
.
Then, we have the following inclusion of sets:

\displaystyle\quad\;\{g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}(s+1):x\in\mathbb{Q}\cap K\}\subseteq\bigcup_{g\in\mathcal{A}_{K,K^{\prime}}}\{g(s+1)\}

(4.52)

where

\mathcal{A}_{K,K^{\prime}}=\{g:\text{$g$ is the unique geodesic from }(x,s)\text{ to }(y,s+2)\text{ for some }x\in K,y\in K^{\prime}\}.

By Lemma
B.2.5
, the set in the RHS of (
4.52
) is finite, so the set on the LHS is finite as well. Therefore, the set

\{g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}(s+1):x\in\mathbb{Q}\}=\bigcap_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}}\{g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}(s+1):x\in\mathbb{Q}\cap[-k,k]\}

(4.53)

is a union of finite nested sets. Further, by the ordering of geodesics from Theorem
4.5.5
(iv)
, for each
k
, the difference

\{g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}(s+1):x\in\mathbb{Q}\cap[-(k+1),k+1]\}\setminus\{g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}(s+1):x\in\mathbb{Q}\cap[-k,k]\}

lies entirely in the union of intervals

\Bigl(-\infty,\inf\bigl\{g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}(s+1):x\in\mathbb{Q}\cap[-k,k]\bigr\}\Bigr]\cup\Bigl[\sup\bigl\{g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}(s+1):x\in\mathbb{Q}\cap[-k,k]\bigr\},\infty\Bigr).

Therefore, the set (
4.53
) has no limit points.
Further, by Equation (
4.45
) of Theorem
4.5.5
(v)
, the set (
4.53
) is unbounded in both directions. These two facts imply that there exist infinitely many disjoint nonempty intervals whose intersection with the set (
4.53
) is empty, and the set of endpoints of such intervals is unbounded. By Lemma
4.5.9
, for each
k>0
, there exists
(x,s)\in\operatorname{NU}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}
such that
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},R}(s+1)\geq k
, and there exists
(x,s)\in\operatorname{NU}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}
such that
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},L}(s+1)\leq-k
. Next, assume, by way of contradiction, that the set
\{x\in\mathbb{R}:(x,0)\in\operatorname{NU}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}\}
has an upper bound
b
. Then, by the monotonicity of Theorem
4.5.5
(iv)
, for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
with
(x,s)\in\operatorname{NU}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}
,
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},R}(s+1)\leq g_{(b,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},R}(s+1)
. But this contradicts the fact we showed that
\{g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},R}(s+1):x\in\mathbb{R}\}
is not bounded above. Hence, there exists a sequence
y_{n}\to\infty
such that
(y_{n},s)\in\operatorname{NU}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}
for all
n
. By a similar argument, there exists a sequence
x_{n}\to-\infty
such that
(x_{n},s)\in\operatorname{NU}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}
for all
n
.

Now, for arbitrary
s\in\mathbb{R}
, pick a rational number
T>s
. Pick
(z,T)\in\operatorname{NU}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}
, and let

\displaystyle x_{1}=\sup\{x\in\mathbb{R}:g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},L}(T)\leq z\},\qquad\text{and}\qquad x_{2}=\inf\{x\in\mathbb{R}:g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},R}(T)\geq z\}.

By the limits in Equation (
4.45
) of Theorem
4.5.5
(v)
,

x_{1}
and
x_{2}
lie in
\mathbb{R}
.

We first show that
x_{2}\leq x_{1}
. If not, then choose
x\in(x_{1},x_{2})
. Then,
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},R}(T)<z<g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},L}(T)
, contradicting the meaning of L and R. Hence
x_{2}\leq x_{1}
. For any
x>x_{2}
,
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},R}(T)\geq z
, and by the limit in Equation (
4.43
) of Theorem
4.5.5
(v)
,
g_{(x_{2},s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},R}(T)\geq z
as well. By an analogous argument, for
x<x_{1}
,
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},L}(T)\leq z
, and the inequality
g_{(x_{1},s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},L}(T)\leq z
holds by the same argument. Hence, for
x\in[x_{2},x_{1}]
,

g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},L}(T)\leq z,\qquad\text{and}\qquad g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},R}(T)\geq z.

Then, by the monotonicity of Theorem
4.5.5
(iv)
, for
t\geq T
,

g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},L}(t)\leq g_{(z,T)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},L}(t)\leq g_{(z,T)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},R}(t)\leq g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},R}(t).

(4.54)

By assumption that
(z,T)\in\operatorname{NU}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}
, there exists
t>T
such that the middle inequality in (
4.54
) is strict, so
(x,s)\in\operatorname{NU}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}
. Furthermore, by assumption, the set
\{z\in\mathbb{R}:(z,T)\in\operatorname{NU}\}
has neither an upper or lower bound. Then, by the
t=T
case of (
4.54
) and a similar argument as for the
s=0
case, the set
\{x\in\mathbb{R}:(x,s)\in\operatorname{NU}\}
also has neither an upper nor lower bound.

We lastly show countability of the sets. By (
4.42
), it suffices to show that for each
\xi\in\mathbb{Q}
and
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
\operatorname{NU}^{\xi}\cap\,\mathcal{H}_{s}
is countable. The proof is that of Theorem 3.4, Item 3 in
[
126
]
, adapted to all horizontal lines simultaneously. For each
(x,s)\in\operatorname{NU}^{\xi}
, there exists
t>s
such that
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi,L}(t)<g_{(x,s)}^{\xi,R}(t)
. By continuity of geodesics, the space between the two geodesics contains an open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. By the monotonicity of Theorem
4.5.5
(iv)
, for
x<y
,
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi,R}(t)\leq g_{(y,s)}^{\xi,L}(t)
for all
t\geq s
. Hence, for
x<y
, with
(x,s),(y,s)\in\operatorname{NU}^{\xi}
, the associated open sets in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
are disjoint, and
\operatorname{NU}^{\xi}\cap\,\mathcal{H}_{s}
is at most countably infinite.
∎

4.6
Coalescence and the global geometry of geodesics

We can now describe the global structure of the semi-infinite geodesics, beginning with coalescence.

Theorem 4.6.1
.

On a single event of full probability, the following hold across all directions
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
and signs
{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.9}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}\in\{-,+\}
.

(i)

For all

p,q\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, if
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
are
\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}
Busemann geodesics from
p
and
q
, respectively, then
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
coalesce. If the first point of intersection of the two geodesics is not
p
or
q
, then the first point of intersection is the coalescence point of the two geodesics.

(ii)

Let
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
be two distinct
\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}
Busemann geodesics from an initial point
(x,s)\in\operatorname{NU}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}
. Then, the set
\{t>s:g_{1}(t)\neq g_{2}(t)\}
is a bounded open interval. That is, after the geodesics split, they coalesce exactly when they meet again.

(iii)

For each
compact set
K\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
, there exists a random
T=T(K,\xi,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.9}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}})<\infty
such that for any two
\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}
geodesics
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
whose starting points lie in
K
,
g_{1}(t)=g_{2}(t)
for all
t\geq T
. That is, there is a time level
T
after which all semi-infinite geodesics started from points in
K
have coalesced into a single path.

Remark 4.6.2
.

Theorem 1 of
[
26
]
implies the following refinements of the results in this section. In Theorem
4.6.1
(ii)
,
\{t>s:g_{1}(t)\neq g_{2}(t)\}=(s,r)
for some
r\in(s,\infty)
. Under Condition
(i)
of Theorem
4.6.3
below, the entire collection of semi-infinite geodesics in direction
\xi
is a tree.

The following gives a full classification of the directions in which geodesics coalesce. We refer the reader to Theorems
4.6.8
and
4.6.9
below for the connection between coalescence and the regularity of the Busemann process.

Theorem 4.6.3
.

On a single event of probability one, the following are equivalent.

(i)

\xi\notin\Xi
.

(ii)

g_{p}^{\xi-,S}=g_{p}^{\xi+,S}
for all
p\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
S\in\{L,R\}
.

(iii)

All semi-infinite geodesics in direction
\xi
coalesce
(
whether Busemann geodesics or not
)
.

(iv)

For all
p\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\operatorname{NU}
, there is a unique geodesic starting from
p
with direction
\xi
.

(v)

There is a unique
\xi
-directed semi-infinite geodesic from some
p\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

(vi)

There exists
p\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that
g_{p}^{\xi-,L}=g_{p}^{\xi+,L}
.

(vii)

There exists
p\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that
g_{p}^{\xi-,R}=g_{p}^{\xi+,R}

Under these equivalent conditions, the following also holds.

(viii)

From any
p\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, all semi-infinite geodesics in direction
\xi
are Busemann geodesics.

Remark 4.6.4
.

The equivalence
(i)
\Leftrightarrow
(vi)
implies that
\forall\xi\in\Xi
and
p\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, geodesics
g_{p}^{\xi-,L}
and
g_{p}^{\xi+,L}
are distinct. The same is true when
L
is replaced with
R
. Since
g_{p}^{\xi-,L}
and
g_{p}^{\xi+,L}
are both leftmost geodesics between any two of their points (Theorem
4.4.9
(iv)
)
then if
\xi\in\Xi
,
these two geodesics must separate at some time
t\geq s
, and they cannot ever come back together. For each
\xi\in\Xi
, there are two coalescing families of geodesics, namely the
\xi-
and
\xi+
geodesics. (See again Figure
4.1
.) In particular, whenever
\xi\in\Xi
,
s\in\mathbb{R}
, and
x<y
,
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi+,L}(t)>g_{(y,s)}^{\xi-,R}(t)
for sufficiently large
t
, as alluded to in Remark
4.5.6
.

4.6.1
Proofs

In each of these theorems, the full-probability event is
\Omega_{2}
(
4.28
). We start by proving some lemmas that allow us to prove Theorem
4.6.1
. The proof of Theorem
4.6.3
comes at the very end of this subsection. Section
4.7.1
proves Theorem
4.2.1
as well as lingering results from Section
4.4
.

Lemma 4.6.5
.

Let
\omega\in\Omega_{1}
,
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
x<y\in\mathbb{R}
. Assume, for some
\alpha<\xi
and
{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.9}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}_{1},{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.9}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}_{2}\in\{-,+\}
, that
W_{\alpha{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}_{1}}(y,s;x,s)=W_{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}_{2}}(y,s;x,s)
. We also allow
\alpha=\xi
if
{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.9}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}_{1}=-
and
{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.9}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}_{2}=+
. If
t>s
and
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}_{2},R}(t)\leq g_{(y,s)}^{\alpha{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}_{1},L}(t)
, then for all
u\in[s,t]
,

g_{(x,s)}^{\alpha{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}_{1},R}(u)=g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}_{2},R}(u)\qquad\text{and}\qquad g_{(y,s)}^{\alpha{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}_{1},L}(u)=g_{(y,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}_{2},L}(u).

(4.55)

Proof.

By assumption, whenever
w<z
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
, Theorem
4.4.1
(iii)
gives

W_{\alpha{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}_{1}}(z,t;w,t)\leq W_{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}_{2}}(z,t;w,t).

(4.56)

For the rest of the proof, we suppress the
{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.9}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}_{1},{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.9}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}_{2}
notation.
By Theorem
4.4.1
(ii)
,
(iv)
,

\displaystyle W_{\xi}(y,s;x,s)

\displaystyle=W_{\xi}(y,s;0,t)-W_{\xi}(x,s;0,t)

\displaystyle=\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\{\mathcal{L}(y,s;z,t)+W_{\xi}(z,t;0,t)\}-\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\{\mathcal{L}(x,s;z,t)+W_{\xi}(z,t;0,t)\},

(4.57)

and the same with
\xi
replaced by
\alpha
. Recall that
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},L}(t)
and
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},R}(t)
are, respectively, the leftmost and rightmost maximizers of
\mathcal{L}(x,s;z,t)+W_{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}(z,t;0,t)
over
z\in\mathbb{R}
.
Understanding that these quantities depend on
s
and
t
, we use the shorthand notation
g_{x}^{\xi,R}=g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}_{1},R}(t)
, and similarly with the other quantities. Then, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(x,s;g_{x}^{\xi,R},t)+W_{\xi}(g_{x}^{\xi,R},t;0,t)

\displaystyle-(\mathcal{L}(x,s;g_{x}^{\xi,R},t)+W_{\alpha}(g_{x}^{\xi,R},t;0,t))

\displaystyle\geq\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\{\mathcal{L}(x,s;z,t)+W_{\xi}(z,t;0,t)\}

\displaystyle-\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\{\mathcal{L}(x,s;z,t)+W_{\alpha}(z,t;0,t)\}

(4.58)

\displaystyle=\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\{\mathcal{L}(y,s;z,t)+W_{\xi}(z,t;0,t)\}

\displaystyle-\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\{\mathcal{L}(y,s;z,t)+W_{\alpha}(z,t;0,t)\}

\displaystyle\geq\mathcal{L}(y,s;g_{y}^{\alpha,L},t)+W_{\xi}(g_{y}^{\alpha,L},t;0,t)

\displaystyle-(\mathcal{L}(y,s;g_{y}^{\alpha,L},t)+W_{\alpha}(g_{y}^{\alpha,L},t;0,t)),

(4.59)

where the middle equality came from the assumption that
W_{\xi}(y,s;x,s)=W_{\alpha}(y,s;x,s)
and Equation (
4.57
) applied to both
\xi
and
\alpha
.
Rearranging the first and last lines yields

W_{\xi}(g_{y}^{\alpha,L},t;g_{x}^{\xi,R},t)\leq W_{\alpha}(g_{y}^{\alpha,L},t;g_{x}^{\xi,R},t).

However, the assumption
g_{x}^{\xi,R}\leq g_{y}^{\alpha,L}

combined with (
4.56
) implies that this inequality is an equality. Hence, inequalities (
4.58
) and (
4.59
) are also equalities. From the equality (
4.58
),

\mathcal{L}(x,s;g_{x}^{\xi,R},t)+W_{\alpha}(g_{x}^{\xi,R},t;0,t)=\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\{\mathcal{L}(x,s;z,t)+W_{\alpha}(z,t;0,t)\},

so
z=g_{x}^{\xi,R}
is a maximizer of
\mathcal{L}(x,s;z,t)+W_{\alpha}(z,t;0,t)
. By definition,
g_{x}^{\alpha,R}
is the rightmost maximizer, and by geodesic ordering (Theorem
4.5.5
(i)
),
g_{x}^{\xi,R}\geq g_{x}^{\alpha,R}
, so
g_{x}^{\xi,R}=g_{x}^{\alpha,R}
. An analogous argument applied to (
4.59
) implies
g_{y}^{\alpha,L}=g_{y}^{\xi,L}
. We have shown that

g_{(x,s)}^{\alpha{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}_{1},R}(t)=g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}_{2},R}(t),\qquad\text{and}\qquad g_{(y,s)}^{\alpha{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}_{1},L}(t)=g_{(y,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}_{2},L}(t).

Since
g_{(x,s)}^{\alpha{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}_{1},R}
and
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}_{2},R}
are both the rightmost geodesics between any two of their points and similarly with the leftmost geodesics from
(y,s)
(Theorem
4.4.9
(iv)
), Equation (
4.55
) holds for all
u\in[s,t]
, as desired.
∎

Lemma 4.6.6
.

Let
\omega\in\Omega_{2}
,
s\in\mathbb{R}
, and
x<y
. If, for some
\alpha<\xi
and
{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.9}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}_{1},{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.9}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}_{2}\in\{-,+\}
we have that
W_{\alpha{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}_{1}}(y,s;x,s)=W_{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}_{2}}(y,s;x,s)
, then
g_{(x,s)}^{\alpha{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}_{1},R}
coalesces with
g_{(y,s)}^{\alpha{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}_{1},L}
,
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}_{2},R}
coalesces with
g_{(y,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}_{2},L}
, and the coalescence points of the two pairs of geodesics are the same.

Proof.

By Theorem
4.4.9
(iii)
,
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}_{2},R}(t)/t\to\xi
while
g_{(y,s)}^{\alpha{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}_{1},L}(t)/t\to\alpha
as
t\to\infty
. By this and continuity of geodesics, there exists a minimal time
t>s
such that

z:=g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}_{2},R}(t)=g_{(y,s)}^{\alpha{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}_{1},L}(t).

By Lemma
4.6.5
,

g_{(x,s)}^{\alpha{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}_{1},R}(u)=g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}_{2},R}(u)\qquad\text{and}\qquad g_{(y,s)}^{\alpha{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}_{1},L}(u)=g_{(y,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}_{2},L}(u)\qquad\text{for all }u\in[s,t].

Since
t
was chosen to be minimal, Theorem
4.5.5
(iv)
implies that the pair
g_{(x,s)}^{\alpha{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}_{1},R}
,
g_{(y,s)}^{\alpha{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}_{1},L}
and the pair
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}_{2},R}
,
g_{(y,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}_{2},L}
both coalesce at
(z,t)
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.6.1
.

Item
(i)
(Coalescence):
Let
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
be
\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.9}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}
Busemann

geodesics from
(x,s)
and
(y,t)
, respectively, and take
s\leq t
without loss of generality. Let
a=(g_{1}(t)\wedge y)-1
and
b=(g_{1}(t)\vee y)+1
. By Theorem
4.5.5
(iv)
, for all
u\geq t
,

g_{(a,t)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},R}(u)\leq g_{1}(u)\wedge g_{2}(u)\leq g_{1}(u)\vee g_{2}(u)\leq g_{(b,t)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},L}(u).

(4.60)

By Theorem
4.4.1
(v)
, there exists
\alpha
, sufficiently close to
\xi
, (from the left for
{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.9}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}=-
and from the right for
{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.9}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}=+
) such that
W_{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}(b,t;a,t)=W_{\alpha{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}(b,t;a,t)
. By Lemma
4.6.6
,
g_{(a,t)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},R}
coalesces with
g_{(b,t)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},L}
. Then, for
u
large enough, all inequalities in (
4.60
) are equalities, and
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
coalesce.

If the first point of intersection is not
(y,t)
, then
g_{1}(t)\neq y
, and the coalescence point of
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
is the first point of intersection by Theorem
4.5.5
(iv)
.

Item
(ii)
(Geodesics coalesce when they meet):
Let
(x,s)\in\operatorname{NU}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}
, and let
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
be two distinct
\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}
Busemann geodesics from
(x,s)
. The set
\text{GNEQ}:=\{t>s:g_{1}(t)\neq g_{2}(t)\}
is therefore nonempty and infinite by continuity of
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
. Assume, by way of contradiction, that
GNEQ
is not an open interval. By continuity of geodesics,
GNEQ
cannot be a closed or half-closed interval, so
GNEQ
is not path connected. Thus, there exists
t_{1}<t_{2}<t_{3}
so that

g_{1}(t_{1})\neq g_{2}(t_{1}),\quad g_{1}(t_{2})=g_{2}(t_{2}),\quad\text{ and}\quad g_{1}(t_{3})\neq g_{2}(t_{3}).

The geodesics
g_{1}|_{[t_{1},\infty)}
and
g_{2}|_{[t_{1},\infty)}
started from
(g_{1}(t_{1}),t_{1})
and
(g_{2}(t_{1}),t_{1})
, respectively, are both Busemann geodesics by their construction in Theorem
4.4.9
. Since the geodesics
g_{1}|_{[t_{1},\infty)}
and
g_{2}|_{[t_{1},\infty)}
start at different spatial locations (namely
g_{1}(t_{1})
and
g_{2}(t_{1})
) along the same time level
t_{1}
, they cannot intersect at either of their starting points. By Item
(i)
, the two geodesics
g_{1}|_{[t_{1},\infty)}
and
g_{2}|_{[t_{1},\infty)}
must coalesce, and the first point of intersection is the coalescence point. Since
g_{1}(t_{2})=g_{2}(t_{2})
, this implies that
g_{1}(t)=g_{2}(t)
for all
t>t_{2}
, a contradiction to the existence of
t_{3}
.

Item
(iii)
(Uniformity of coalescence):
Let
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
,
{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.9}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}\in\{-,+\}
, and let the compact set
K
be given. Let
S
be the smallest integer greater than
\max\{s:(x,s)\in K\}
. Set

A:=\inf\{g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},L}(S):(x,s)\in K\},\qquad\text{and}\qquad B:=\sup\{g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},R}(S):(x,s)\in K\}.

By Lemma
4.4.13
,
-\infty<A\leq B<\infty
. Then, by Theorem
4.5.5
(iv)
, whenever
g
is a
\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}
geodesic starting from
(x,s)\in K
,

g_{(A,S)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},L}(t)\leq g(t)\leq g_{(B,S)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},R}(t)\qquad\text{for all }t\geq S.

To complete the proof, let
T
be the time at which
g_{(A,S)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},L}
and
g_{(B,S)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},R}
coalesce, which is guaranteed to be finite by Item
(i)
.
∎

For two initial points on a horizontal level, as
\xi
varies, a constant Busemann process corresponds to a constant coalescence point of the geodesics.
The non-uniqueness of geodesics
requires us to be careful about the choice of left and right geodesic.

Definition 4.6.7
.

For
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
x<y
, let
\mathbf{z}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}(y,s;x,s)
be the coalescence point of
g_{(y,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},L}
and
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},R}
.

Theorem 4.6.8
.

On a single event of probability one, for all reals
\alpha<\beta
,
s
, and
x<y
, the following are equivalent.

(i)

W_{\alpha+}(y,s;x,s)=W_{\beta-}(y,s;x,s)
.

(ii)

\mathbf{z}^{\alpha+}(y,s;x,s)=\mathbf{z}^{\beta-}(y,s;x,s)
.

(iii)

There exist
t>s
and
z\in\mathbb{R}
such that there are paths
g_{1}:[s,t]\to\mathbb{R}

(
connecting
(x,s)
and
(z,t)
)
and
g_{2}:[s,t]\to\mathbb{R}

(
connecting
(y,s)
to
(z,t)
)
such that for all
\xi\in(\alpha,\beta)
,
{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.9}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}\in\{-,+\}
, and
u\in[s,t)
,

\displaystyle g_{1}(u)

\displaystyle=g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},R}(u)=g_{(x,s)}^{\alpha+,R}(u)=g_{(x,s)}^{\beta-,R}(u)

(4.61)

\displaystyle<g_{2}(u)=g_{(y,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},L}(u)=g_{(y,s)}^{\alpha+,L}(u)=g_{(y,s)}^{\beta-,L}(u).

Proof.

(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii)
follows from Lemma
4.6.6
.

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(i)
: Assume
(z,t):=\mathbf{z}^{\alpha+}(y,s;x,s)=\mathbf{z}^{\beta-}(y,s;x,s)
.

By additivity (Theorem
4.4.1
(ii)
) and Theorem
4.4.9
(iii)
,

\displaystyle W_{\alpha+}(y,s;x,s)

\displaystyle=W_{\alpha+}(y,s;z,t)-W_{\alpha+}(x,s;z,t)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}(y,s;z,t)-\mathcal{L}(x,s;z,t)

\displaystyle=W_{\beta-}(y,s;z,t)-W_{\beta-}(x,s;z,t)=W_{\beta-}(y,s;x,s).

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(iii)
: Let
(z,t)
be as in the proof of
(ii)
\Rightarrow
(i)
. By Theorem
4.4.9
(iv)
, the restriction of
g_{(x,s)}^{\alpha+,R}
and
g_{(x,s)}^{\beta-,R}
to the domain
[s,t]
are both rightmost geodesics between
(x,s)
and
(z,t)
, and therefore they agree on this restricted domain. Similarly,
g_{(y,s)}^{\alpha+,L}
and
g_{(y,s)}^{\beta-,L}
agree on the domain
[s,t]
. By the monotonicity of Theorem
4.5.5
(i)
, and since
(z,t)
is the common coalescence point, (
4.61
) holds for
u\in[s,t)
, as desired.

(iii)
\Rightarrow
(ii)
is immediate.
∎

Theorem 4.6.9
.

On a single event of probability one, for all reals
s,\xi\in\mathbb{R}
, and
x<y
, the following are equivalent.

(i)

W_{\xi-}(y,s;x,s)=W_{\xi+}(y,s;x,s).

(ii)

\mathbf{z}^{\xi-}(y,s;x,s)=\mathbf{z}^{\xi+}(y,s;x,s)
.

(iii)

g_{(x,s)}^{\xi-,R}(t)=g_{(y,s)}^{\xi+,L}(t)
for some
t>s
, i.e., the paths
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi-,R}
and
g_{(y,s)}^{\xi+,L}
intersect.

Remark 4.6.10
.

In Item
(iii)
, if
\xi\in\Xi
, then despite intersecting, the geodesics
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi-,R}
and
g_{(y,s)}^{\xi+,L}
cannot coalesce. This follows from Theorem
4.6.3
, which gives a full classification of the directions in which all semi-infinite geodesics coalesce.

Proof of Theorem
4.6.9
.

(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii)
: If
W_{\xi-}(y,s;x,s)=W_{\xi+}(y,s;x,s)
, then

Theorem
4.4.1
(v)
implies that for some
\alpha<\xi<\beta
,
W_{\alpha+}(y,s;x,s)=W_{\beta-}(y,s;x,s)
. Then, we apply
(i)
\Rightarrow
(iii)
of Theorem
4.6.8
to conclude that for some
t>s
and
z\in\mathbb{R}
,

g_{(x,s)}^{\xi-,R}(u)=g_{(x,s)}^{\xi+,R}(u)<g_{(y,s)}^{\xi-,L}(u)=g_{(y,s)}^{\xi+,L}(u),\qquad\text{for }u\in[s,t),

whereas for
u=t
, all terms above equal some common value
z
. Therefore,
(z,t)=\mathbf{z}^{\xi-}(y,s;x,s)=\mathbf{z}^{\xi+}(y,s;x,s)
.

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(i)
:
Similarly as in the proof of
(ii)
\Rightarrow
(i)
of Theorem
4.6.8
, if
(z,t)=\mathbf{z}^{\xi-}(y,s;x,s)=\mathbf{z}^{\xi+}(y,s;x,s)
, then

W_{\xi-}(y,s;x,s)=\mathcal{L}(y,s;z,t)-\mathcal{L}(x,s;z,t)=W_{\xi+}(y,s;x,s).

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(iii)
: Assume
(z,t)=\mathbf{z}^{\xi-}(y,s;x,s)=\mathbf{z}^{\xi+}(y,s;x,s)
. Then,
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi-,R}(t)=z=g_{(y,s)}^{\xi+,L}(t)
.

(iii)
\Rightarrow
(ii)
:
Assume that
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi-,R}(t)=g_{(y,s)}^{\xi+,L}(t)
for some
t>s
. Let
t
be the minimal such time, and let
(z,t)
be the point where the geodesics first intersect. By Theorem
4.5.5
, Items
(i)
and
(iv)
, for
u>s
,

g_{(x,s)}^{\xi-,R}(u)\leq g_{(x,s)}^{\xi+,R}(u)\wedge g_{(y,s)}^{\xi-,L}(u)\leq g_{(x,s)}^{\xi+,R}(u)\vee g_{(y,s)}^{\xi-,L}(u)\leq g_{(y,s)}^{\xi+,L}(u).

(4.62)

In particular, when
u=t
, all inequalities in (
4.62
) are equalities. Further, since
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi-,R}
,
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi+,R}
are rightmost geodesics between
(x,s)
and
(z,t)
(Theorem
4.4.9
(iv)
),
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi-,R}(u)=g_{(x,s)}^{\xi+,R}(u)
for
u\in[s,t]
. Similarly,
g_{(y,s)}^{\xi-,L}(u)=g_{(y,s)}^{\xi+,L}(u)
for
u\in[s,t]
. Since
t
was chosen minimally for
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi-,R}(t)=g_{(y,s)}^{\xi+,L}(t)
, we have
(z,t)=\mathbf{z}^{\xi-}(y,s;x,s)=\mathbf{z}^{\xi+}(y,s;x,s)
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.6.3
(Classification of directions).

(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii)
: If
\xi\notin\Xi
, then
W_{\xi-}=W_{\xi+}
, so
(ii)
follows by the construction of the Busemann geodesics from the Busemann functions.

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(iii)
: Since a geodesic in direction
\xi
from
(x,s)
must pass through each horizontal level
t>s
, it is sufficient to show that, for
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
x<y
, whenever
g_{1}
is a semi-infinite geodesic from
(x,s)
in direction
\xi
and
g_{2}
is a semi-infinite geodesic from
(y,s)
in direction
\xi
,
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
coalesce. Assuming
(ii)
and using Theorem
4.5.7
(i)
, for all
t>s
,

g_{(x,s)}^{\xi+,L}(t)=g_{(x,s)}^{\xi-,L}(t)\leq g_{1}(t)\wedge g_{2}(t)\leq g_{1}(t)\vee g_{2}(t)\leq g_{(y,s)}^{\xi+,R}(t).

By Theorem
4.6.1
(i)
,
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi+,L}
and
g_{(y,s)}^{\xi+,R}
coalesce, so all inequalities above are equalities for large
t
, and
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
coalesce.

(iii)
\Rightarrow
(i)
: We prove the contrapositive. If
\xi\in\Xi
, then by Theorem
4.4.1
(iii)
-
(iv)
,

W_{\xi-}(y,s;x,s)<W_{\xi+}(y,s;x,s)
for some
x<y
and
s\in\mathbb{R}
. By
(i)
\Leftrightarrow
(iii)
of Theorem
4.6.9
,
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi-,R}(t)<g_{(y,s)}^{\xi+,L}(t)
for all
t>s
. In particular,
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi-,R}
and
g_{(y,s)}^{\xi+,L}
do not coalesce.

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(iv)
: By definition of
\operatorname{NU}
, whenever
p\notin\operatorname{NU}
,
g_{p}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},L}=g_{p}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},R}
for
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
and
{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.9}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}\in\{-,+\}
. Hence, assuming
p\notin\operatorname{NU}
and
g_{p}^{\xi-,R}=g_{p}^{\xi+,R}
, we also have
g_{p}^{\xi-,L}=g_{p}^{\xi+,R}
, so there is a unique geodesic from
p
in direction
\xi
by Theorem
4.5.7
(i)
.

(iv)
\Rightarrow
(v)
: By Theorem
4.5.2
(i)
, on the event
\Omega_{2}
,
\operatorname{NU}
contains no points of
\mathbb{Q}^{2}
, and therefore,
\operatorname{NU}
is not all of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

(v)
\Rightarrow
(vi)
and
(v)
\Rightarrow
(vii)
are direct consequences of Theorem
4.5.7
(i)
: If there is a unique semi-infinite geodesic in direction
\xi
from a point
p\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, then
g_{p}^{\xi-,L}=g_{p}^{\xi+,L}=g_{p}^{\xi-,R}=g_{p}^{\xi+,R}
.

(vi)
\Rightarrow
(ii)
: Let
p
be a point from which
g_{p}^{\xi-,L}=g_{p}^{\xi+,L}
, and call this common geodesic
g
. Let
q
be an arbitrary point in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. By Theorem
4.6.1
(i)
,
g_{q}^{\xi-,L},g_{q}^{\xi+,L},g_{q}^{\xi-,R}
, and
g_{q}^{\xi+,R}
each coalesce with
g
, so
g_{q}^{\xi-,L}
and
g_{q}^{\xi+,L}
coalesce. Since both geodesics are the leftmost geodesics between their points by Theorem
4.4.9
(iv)
, they must be the same. Similarly,
g_{q}^{\xi-,R}=g_{q}^{\xi+,R}
.

(vii)
\Rightarrow
(ii)
: follows by the same proof.

Item
(viii)
:
Let
\xi\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\Xi
, and let
g
be a semi-infinite geodesic in direction
\xi
, starting from a point
(x,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. By Lemma
4.4.14
and Theorem
4.4.9
(ii)
, it is sufficient to show that for sufficiently large
t
,

\mathcal{L}(x,s;g(t),t)=W_{\xi}(x,s;g(t),t).

(4.63)

(we dropped the
\pm
distinction since
W_{\xi-}=W_{\xi+}
). By Item
(iii)
,
g
coalesces with
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi,R}
. Then, for sufficiently large
t
,
g(t)=g_{(x,s)}^{\xi,R}(t)
and by Theorem
4.4.9
(iii)
, (
4.63
) holds.
∎

4.7
Remaining proofs from Section
4.4
and the proofs of the main theorems

In this section, we complete the unfinished business of the chapter. In Section
4.7.1
, we start by proving Items
(vi)
–
(viii)
of Theorem
4.4.1
and mixing in Theorem
4.4.3
(ii)
. Then, we prove the first main theorem of the chapter, namely Theorem
4.2.1
. In Section
4.7.2
, we prove Theorems
4.4.5
(ii)
and
4.2.6
, along with some necessary lemmas.

4.7.1
Proof of Theorem
4.2.1
and related results

Proof of Items
(vi)
–
(viii)
of Theorem
4.4.1
and mixing in Theorem
4.4.3
(ii)
.

We

continue to work on
\Omega_{2}
.

Item
(vi)
of Theorem
4.4.1
(Busemann limits I):
By Theorem
4.6.3
(viii)
, if
\xi\notin\Xi
, all
\xi
-directed semi-infinite geodesics are Busemann geodesics, and they all coalesce. By Theorem
4.6.1
(iii)
, there exists a level
T
such that all geodesics from points starting in the compact set
K
have coalesced by time
T
. Let
(Z,T)
denote the location of the point of the common geodesics at time
T
. Let
r_{t}=(z_{t},u_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}
be any net with
u_{t}\to\infty
and
z_{t}/u_{t}\to\xi
. By Theorem
4.5.7
(ii)
, for all sufficiently large
t
and
p\in K
, all geodesics from
p
to
r_{t}
pass through
(Z,T)
. Then, for
p,q\in K
,

\mathcal{L}(p;r_{t})-\mathcal{L}(q;r_{t})=\mathcal{L}(p;Z,T)+\mathcal{L}(Z,T;r_{t})-(\mathcal{L}(q;Z,T)+\mathcal{L}(Z,T;r_{t})).

By Theorems
4.4.9
(ii)
(ii)(b)
and
4.4.1
(ii)
, the right-hand side is equal to

W_{\xi}(p;Z,T)-W_{\xi}(q;Z,T)=W_{\xi}(p;q).

Item
(vii)
of Theorem
4.4.1
(Busemann limits II):
By Theorem
4.4.5
(iii)
, on the event
\Omega_{2}
,
\Xi
contains no rational directions. Then, for arbitrary
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
,
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
x<y\in\mathbb{R}
,
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{Q}
with
\alpha<\xi<\beta
, and a net
(z_{r},u_{r})
with
u_{r}\to\infty
and
z_{r}/u_{r}\to\xi
, for sufficiently large
r
,
\alpha u_{r}<z_{r}<\beta u_{r}
. Theorem
4.4.1
(vi)
gives the existence of the limits in the first and last lines below, while the monotonicity of Lemma
B.1.5
(i)
justifies the first and last inequalities:

\displaystyle W_{\alpha}(y,s;x,s)

\displaystyle=\lim_{r\to\infty}\mathcal{L}(y,s;\alpha u_{r},u_{r})-\mathcal{L}(x,s;\alpha u_{r},u_{r})

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{r\to\infty}\mathcal{L}(y,s;z_{r},u_{r})-\mathcal{L}(x,s;z_{r},u_{r})

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{r\to\infty}\mathcal{L}(y,s;z_{r},u_{r})-\mathcal{L}(x,s;z_{r},u_{r})

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{r\to\infty}\mathcal{L}(y,s;\beta u_{r},u_{r})-\mathcal{L}(x,s;\beta u_{r},u_{r})=W_{\beta}(y,s;x,s).

Sending
\mathbb{Q}\ni\alpha\nearrow\xi
and
\mathbb{Q}\ni\beta\searrow\xi
and using Item
(v)
completes the proof.

Item
(viii)
of Theorem
4.4.1
(Global attractiveness):
We follow a similar proof to the attractiveness in Theorem
3.2.2
. Let
\xi\notin\Xi
and assume
\mathfrak{h}\in\operatorname{UC}
is a function satisfying the drift condition (
3.7
). Recall that we define

h_{s,t}(x)=\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\{\mathcal{L}(x,s;z,t)+\mathfrak{h}(z)\}.

(4.64)

For
a>0
and
s<t
, Theorems
4.4.1
(v)
and
4.4.5
(iii)
allows us to choose
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(\xi)>0
small enough so that
\xi\pm 2\varepsilon\in\mathbb{Q}
(and thus
\xi\pm 2\varepsilon\notin\Xi
), and so for all
x\in[-a,a]
,

W_{\xi\pm 2\varepsilon}(x,s;0,s)=W_{\xi}(x,s;0,s).

(4.65)

By Theorem
4.6.1
(iii)
, there exists a random
T=T(a,\xi\pm\varepsilon)
such that all
\xi-2\varepsilon
Busemann geodesics have coalesced by time
T
and all
\xi+2\varepsilon
Busemann geodesics have coalesced by time
T
. For
t>T
, let
g^{\xi\pm 2\varepsilon}(t)
be locations of these two common geodesics at time
t
. By Theorem
4.4.9
(ii)
(ii)(d)
,
g^{\xi\pm 2\varepsilon}(t)/t\to\xi\pm 2\varepsilon
. By Equation (
3.21
) in Lemma
3.5.1
applied to the temporally reflected version of
\mathcal{L}
, there exists
t_{0}(a,\varepsilon(\xi),s)
so that for
t>t_{0}
, whenever
x\in[-a,a]
and
z
is a maximizer in (
4.64
),

g^{\xi-2\varepsilon}(t)<z<g^{\xi+2\varepsilon}(t).

Then, by Lemma
B.1.5
(iii)
, for such large
t
,

W_{\xi-2\varepsilon}(x,s;0,s)\leq h_{s,t}(x)-h_{s,t}(0)\leq W_{\xi+2\varepsilon}(x,s;0,s),

while for
-a\leq x\leq 0
, the equalities reverse. Combined with (
4.65
), this completes the proof.

Item
(ii)
of Theorem
4.4.3
(Mixing):

This proof follows a similar idea as that in Lemma 7.5 of
[
11
]
, and the key is that, within a compact set, the Busemann functions are equal to differences of the directed landscape for large enough
t
. Then, we use Lemma
B.1.4
, which states that, as a projection of
\{\mathcal{L},W\}
, the directed landscape
\mathcal{L}
is mixing under the transformation
T_{z;a,b}
. Set
r_{z}=(az,bz)
. By a standard
\pi-\lambda
argument, it suffices to show that for
\xi_{1},\ldots\xi_{k}\in\mathbb{R}
(ignoring the sign

\textstyle\boxempty

since
\xi_{i}\notin\Xi
a.s.), all compact sets
K:=K_{1}\times K_{2}^{k}\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\uparrow$}}}}^{4}\times(\mathbb{R}^{4})^{k}
, and all Borel sets
A,B\in C(K,\mathbb{R})
,

\displaystyle\lim_{z\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\{\mathcal{L},W_{\xi_{1:k}}\}\big|_{K}\in A,\{T_{z;a,b}\mathcal{L},T_{z;a,b}W_{\xi_{1:k}}\}\big|_{K}\in B\Bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=\mathbb{P}\bigl(\{\mathcal{L},W_{\xi_{1:k}}\}\big|_{K}\in A\bigr)\mathbb{P}\bigl(\{\mathcal{L},W_{\xi_{1:k}}\}\big|_{K}\in B\bigr),

where we use the shorthand notation

\{\mathcal{L},W_{\xi_{1:k}}\}\big|_{K}:=\{\mathcal{L}(v),W_{\xi_{i}}(p;q):1\leq i\leq k,(v,p,q)\in K\},

and
T_{z;a,b}
acts on
\mathcal{L}
and
W
as projections of
\{\mathcal{L},W\}
.
By Theorem
4.4.1
(vi)
, we may choose
t>0
sufficiently large so that

\mathbb{P}(W_{\xi_{i}}(p;q)=\mathcal{L}(p;(t\xi,t))-\mathcal{L}(q;(t\xi,t))\;\forall(p,q)\in K_{2},1\leq i\leq k)\geq 1-\varepsilon.

(4.66)

By stationarity of the process under space-time shifts, we also have that for such large
t
and all
z\in\mathbb{R}
,

\mathbb{P}(T_{z;a,b}W_{\xi_{i}}(p;q)=T_{z;a,b}[\mathcal{L}(p;(t\xi,t))-\mathcal{L}(q;(t\xi,t))]\;\forall(p,q)\in K_{2},1\leq i\leq k)\geq 1-\varepsilon

(4.67)

Let
C_{z,t}
be the intersection of the events in (
4.66
) over
1\leq i\leq k
with the event (
4.67
). Then for large enough
t
,
\mathbb{P}(C_{z,t})\geq 1-2\varepsilon
, and

\displaystyle\Bigl|\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\{\mathcal{L},W_{\xi_{1:k}}\}|_{K}\in A,\{T_{z;a,b}\mathcal{L},T_{z;a,b}W_{\xi_{1:k}}\}|_{K}\in B\Bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad-\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\{\mathcal{L},W_{\xi_{1:k}}\}|_{K}\in A\Bigr)\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\{\mathcal{L},W_{\xi_{1:k}}\}|_{K}\in B\Bigr)\Bigr|

\displaystyle\leq\Bigl|\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\{\mathcal{L},W_{\xi_{1:k}}\}|_{K}\in A,\{T_{z;a,b}\mathcal{L},T_{z;a,b}W_{\xi_{1:k}}\}|_{K}\in B,C_{z,t}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad-\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\{\mathcal{L},W_{\xi_{1:k}}\}|_{K}\in A,C_{z,t}\Bigr)\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\{\mathcal{L},W_{\xi_{1:k}}\}|_{K}\in B,C_{z,t}\Bigr)\Bigr|+C\varepsilon

\displaystyle=\Bigl|\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\{\mathcal{L}(v),\mathcal{L}(p;(t\xi_{1:k},t))-\mathcal{L}(q;(t\xi_{1:k},t))\}|_{K}\in A,

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\{T_{z;a,b}\mathcal{L}(v),T_{z;a,b}[\mathcal{L}(p;(t\xi_{1:k},t))-\mathcal{L}(q;(t\xi_{1:k},t))]\}|_{K}\in B,C_{z,t}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad-\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\{\mathcal{L}(v),\mathcal{L}(p;(t\xi_{1:k},t))-\mathcal{L}(q;(t\xi_{1:k},t))\}|_{K}\in A,C_{z,t}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\{\mathcal{L}(v),\mathcal{L}(p;(t\xi_{1:k},t))-\mathcal{L}(q;(t\xi_{1:k},t))\}|_{K}\in B,C_{z,t}\Bigr)\Bigr|+C\varepsilon

\displaystyle\leq\Bigl|\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\{\mathcal{L}(v),\mathcal{L}(p;(t\xi_{1:k},t))-\mathcal{L}(q;(t\xi_{1:k},t))\}|_{K}\in A,

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\{T_{z;a,b}\mathcal{L}(v),T_{z;a,b}[\mathcal{L}(p;(t\xi_{1:k},t))-\mathcal{L}(q;(t\xi_{1:k},t))]\}|_{K}\in B\Bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad-\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\{\mathcal{L}(v),\mathcal{L}(p;(t\xi_{1:k},t))-\mathcal{L}(q;(t\xi_{1:k},t))\}|_{K}\in A\Bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\{\mathcal{L}(v),\mathcal{L}(p;(t\xi_{1:k},t))-\mathcal{L}(q;(t\xi_{1:k},t))\}|_{K}\in B\Bigr)\Bigr|+C^{\prime}\varepsilon,

where the constants
C,C^{\prime}
came as the cost of adding and removing the high probability event
C_{z,t}
. The proof is complete by sending
z\to\infty
and using the mixing of
\mathcal{L}
under the shift
T_{z;a,b}
(Lemma
B.1.4
).
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.2.1
.

Item
(i)
(All geodesics have a direction):
First, we show that, on
\Omega_{2}
, if
g
is a semi-infinite geodesic starting from
(x,s)
, then

-\infty<\liminf_{t\to\infty}t^{-1}{g(t)}\leq\limsup_{t\to\infty}t^{-1}{g(t)}<\infty.

(4.68)

We show the rightmost inequality, the leftmost being analogous. Assume, by way of contradiction, that
\limsup_{t\to\infty}g(t)/t=\infty
. By the directedness of Theorem
4.4.9
(iii)
,
\forall\xi\in\mathbb{R}
there exists an infinite sequence
t_{i}\to\infty
such that
g(t_{i})>g_{(x,s)}^{\xi+,L}(t_{i})
for all
i
. Since
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi+,L}
is the leftmost geodesic between any two of its points (Theorem
4.4.9
(iv)
), we must have
g(t)\geq g_{(x,s)}^{\xi+,L}(t)

\forall\xi\in\mathbb{R}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
. By Theorem
4.5.5
(iii)
,
g(t)=\infty

\forall t>s
, a contradiction.

Having established (
4.68
), assume by way of contradiction that

\liminf_{t\to\infty}t^{-1}{g(t)}<\limsup_{t\to\infty}t^{-1}{g(t)}.

Choose some
\xi
strictly between the two values above. By the directedness of Theorem
4.4.9
(iii)
, there exists a sequence
t_{i}\to\infty
such that
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi+,R}(t_{i})<g(t_{i})
for
i
even and
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi+,R}(t_{i})>g(t_{i})
for
i
odd. This cannot occur since
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi+,R}
is the rightmost geodesic between any two of its points.

By Theorem
4.4.9
(iii)
, for each
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
and
(x,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi+,R}
, for example, is a semi-infinite geodesic from
(x,s)
in direction
\xi
, justifying the claim that there is at least one semi-infinite geodesic from each point and in every direction.

Item
(ii)
(Coalescence):
The first statement follows from the equivalences

(i)
\Leftrightarrow
(iii)
\Leftrightarrow
(iv)
of Theorem
4.6.3
.
By Theorem
4.5.2
(i)
,
\mathbb{P}(p\in\operatorname{NU})=0

\forall p\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. This and Tonelli’s theorem imply that the expected Lebesgue measure of the set
\operatorname{NU}
is

\mathbb{E}\int_{(x,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathbf{1}(p\in\operatorname{NU})\,dx\,ds=\int_{(x,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathbb{P}(p\in\operatorname{NU})\,dx\,ds=0,

so
\operatorname{NU}
almost surely has planar Lebesgue measure zero.

Item
(iii)
(Non-uniqueness in exceptional directions):
This follows from Remark
4.6.4
.
∎

4.7.2
Proof of Theorem
4.2.6
and related results.

Recall the functions
f_{s,\xi}(x)=W_{\xi+}(x,s;0,s)-W_{\xi-}(x,s;0,s)
defined in (
4.8
) and the sets
\mathfrak{S}_{s,\xi}
from (
4.1
):

\displaystyle\mathfrak{S}_{s,\xi}

\displaystyle:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}:\exists\text{ {disjoint}}\text{ }\text{semi-infinite geodesics from }(x,s)\text{ in direction }\xi\}

(4.69)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{S}

\displaystyle:=\bigcup_{s\hskip 0.9pt\in\hskip 0.9pt\mathbb{R},\,\xi\hskip 0.9pt\in\hskip 0.9pt\Xi}\mathfrak{S}_{s,\xi}\times\{s\}.

Theorem 4.7.1
.

On a single event of full probability, the function
f_{s,\xi}
is nondecreasing simultaneously for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
\xi\in\Xi
. Denote the set of local variation of
f_{s,\xi}
by

\mathcal{D}_{s,\xi}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}:f_{s,\xi}(x-\varepsilon)<f_{s,\xi}(x+\varepsilon)\;\forall\varepsilon>0\}.

(4.70)

Then, on a single event of full probability, simultaneously for each
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
\xi\in\Xi
,

\mathcal{D}_{s,\xi}=\mathfrak{S}_{s,\xi}^{L}\cup\mathfrak{S}_{s,\xi}^{R}\;\subseteq\;\mathfrak{S}_{s,\xi},

(4.71)

where for
S\in\{L,R\}
,

\mathfrak{S}_{s,\xi}^{S}:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}:g_{(x,s)}^{\xi-,S}\text{ and }g_{(x,s)}^{\xi+,S}\text{ are disjoint}\}.

(4.72)

Furthermore,
(\mathfrak{S}_{s,\xi}\setminus\mathcal{D}_{s,\xi})\times\{s\}
is contained in the countable set
\operatorname{NU}^{\xi-}\cap\hskip 0.9pt\operatorname{NU}^{\xi+}\cap\,\mathcal{H}_{s}
.

Remark 4.7.2
.

Presently, we do not know if
\mathcal{D}_{s,\xi}
equals
\mathfrak{S}_{s,\xi}
. Since
\operatorname{NU}^{\xi-}\cap\operatorname{NU}^{\xi+}\subseteq\operatorname{NU}
and
\operatorname{NU}\cap\,\mathcal{H}_{s}
is at most countable (Theorem
4.5.2
(ii)
),
\mathfrak{S}_{s,\xi}
and
\mathcal{D}_{s,\xi}
have the same Hausdorff dimension for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
\xi\in\Xi
.

Remark 4.7.3
.

In contrast with
\mathfrak{S}
in (
4.69
), the sets
\mathfrak{S}^{S}
are concerned only with leftmost (
S=L
) and rightmost (
S=R
) Busemann geodesics.
In BLPP, the analogues of
\mathfrak{S}^{L}
and
\mathfrak{S}^{R}
are both equal to the set of initial points from which some geodesic travels initially vertically (Theorems 2.10 and 4.30 in
[
135
]
). Furthermore, in BLPP, the analogue of this set contains
\operatorname{NU}
. We do not presently know whether either is true in DL.

Proof of Theorem
4.7.1
.

The full-probability event is
\Omega_{2}
in (
4.28
). The monotonicity of the function
f_{s,\xi}
follows from (
4.37
). We now prove that
\mathcal{D}_{s,\xi}=\mathfrak{S}_{s,\xi}^{L}\cup\mathfrak{S}_{s,\xi}^{R}
. Assume that
y\notin\mathcal{D}_{s,\xi}
. Then, there exist
a<y<b
such that
f_{s,\xi}
is constant on
[a,b]
. Hence, for
a\leq x<y
,

W_{\xi+}(x,s;0,s)-W_{\xi-}(x,s;0,s)=W_{\xi+}(y,s;0,s)-W_{\xi-}(y,s;0,s),

and by additivity (Theorem
4.4.1
(ii)
),
W_{\xi-}(y,s;x,s)=W_{\xi+}(y,s;x,s)
. Choose
t>s
sufficiently small so that
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi+,R}(t)<g_{(y,s)}^{\xi-,L}(t)
. By Lemma
4.6.5
,
g_{(y,s)}^{\xi-,L}(u)=g_{(y,s)}^{\xi+,L}(u)
for
u\in[s,t]
. By a symmetric argument, instead choosing a point
x>y
,
g_{(y,s)}^{\xi-,R}
and
g_{(y,s)}^{\xi+,R}
agree near the starting point
(y,s)
. Hence,
y\notin\mathfrak{S}_{s,\xi}^{L}\cup\mathfrak{S}_{s,\xi}^{R}
.

Next, assume that
y\in\mathcal{D}_{s,\xi}
. Then, for all
x<y<z
,

W_{\xi+}(x,s;0,s)-W_{\xi-}(x,s;0,s)<W_{\xi+}(z,s;0,s)-W_{\xi-}(z,s;0,s),

Since the difference is a monotone function,
either (i)
W_{\xi-}(y,s;x,s)<W_{\xi+}(y,s;x,s)
for all
x<y
or (ii)
W_{\xi-}(z,s;y,s)<W_{\xi+}(z,s;y,s)
for all
z>y.

We show that
g_{(y,s)}^{\xi-,L}
and
g_{(y,s)}^{\xi+,L}
are disjoint in the first case. A symmetric proof shows that
g_{(y,s)}^{\xi-,R}
and
g_{(y,s)}^{\xi+,R}
are disjoint in the second case. So
assume
W_{\xi-}(y,s;x,s)<W_{\xi+}(y,s;x,s)
for all
x<y
. Sending
x\nearrow y
,
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi-,R}
converges to
g_{(y,s)}^{\xi-,L}
by Theorem
4.5.5
(v)
. Assume, by way of contradiction, that
g_{(y,s)}^{\xi-,L}(u)=g_{(y,s)}^{\xi+,L}(u)
for some
u>s
. This implies then
g_{(y,s)}^{\xi-,L}(t)=g_{(y,s)}^{\xi+,L}(t)
for all
t\in[s,u]
since both paths are the leftmost geodesic between any two of their points (Theorem
4.4.9
(iv)
). For
t\geq s
, the convergence
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi-,R}(t)\to g_{(y,s)}^{\xi-,L}(t)
is monotone by Theorem
4.5.5
(iv)
. Since geodesics are continuous paths, Dini’s theorem implies that, as
x\nearrow y
,
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi-,R}(t)
converges to
g_{(y,s)}^{\xi-,L}(t)=g_{(y,s)}^{\xi+,L}(t)
uniformly in
t\in[s,u]
. Lemma
B.2.1
implies that, for sufficiently close
x<y
,
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi-,R}
and
g_{(y,s)}^{\xi+,L}
are not disjoint. This contradicts
(i)
\Leftrightarrow
(iii)
of Theorem
4.6.9
since we assumed
W_{\xi-}(y,s;x,s)<W_{\xi+}(y,s;x,s)
for all
x<y
.

Lastly, we show that
(\mathfrak{S}_{s,\xi}\setminus\mathcal{D}_{s,\xi})\times\{s\}\subseteq\operatorname{NU}^{\xi-}\cap\operatorname{NU}^{\xi+}\cap\,\mathcal{H}_{s}
. Let
x\in\mathfrak{S}_{s,\xi}\setminus\mathcal{D}_{s,\xi}
. By Theorem
4.5.7
(i)
,
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi-,L}
is the leftmost
\xi
-directed geodesic from
(x,s)
, and
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi+,R}
is the rightmost. Since
x\in\mathfrak{S}_{s,\xi}
, these two geodesics must be disjoint. Since
x\notin\mathcal{D}_{s,\xi}
,
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi-,L}
and
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi+,L}
are not disjoint, and
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi-,R}
and
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi+,R}
are not disjoint. Since the leftmost/rightmost semi-infinite geodesics are leftmost/rightmost geodesics between their points (Theorem
4.4.9
(iv)
), there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that for
t\in(s,s+\varepsilon)
,

g_{(x,s)}^{\xi-,L}(t)=g_{(x,s)}^{\xi+,L}(t)<g_{(x,s)}^{\xi-,R}(t)=g_{(x,s)}^{\xi+,R}(t),

and therefore,
(x,s)\in\operatorname{NU}^{\xi-}\cap\operatorname{NU}^{\xi+}\cap\,\mathcal{H}_{s}
. Recall this set is at most countable by Theorem
4.5.2
(ii)
.
∎

Lemma 4.7.4
.

Given
\omega\in\Omega_{2}
and
(x,s;y,u)\in\mathbb{R}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\uparrow$}}}}^{4}
, let
g:[s,u]\to\mathbb{R}
be the leftmost
(
resp. rightmost
)
geodesic between
(x,s)
and
(y,u)
. Then,
(g(t),t)\in\mathfrak{S}^{L}

{\rm(}\text{resp.\ }\mathfrak{S}^{R}{\rm)}
for some
t\in[s,u)
. Furthermore, among the directions
\xi
for which
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi-,L}
and
g_{(x,s)}^{\xi+,L}
separate at some
t\in[s,u)
, there is a unique direction
\widehat{\xi}
such that

g_{(x,s)}^{\widehat{\xi}-,L}(u)\leq y<g_{(x,s)}^{\widehat{\xi}+,L}(u).

The same holds with
L
replaced by
R
and the strict and weak inequalities swapped.

Proof.

We prove the statement for leftmost geodesics. The proof for rightmost geodesics is analogous. Set

\widehat{\xi}:=\sup\{\xi\in\mathbb{R}:g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},L}(u)\leq y\}=\inf\{\xi\in\mathbb{R}:g_{(x,s)}^{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},L}(u)>y\}.

(4.73)

The monotonicity of Theorem
4.5.5
(i)
guarantees that the second equality holds, and that the definition is independent of the choice of
{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.9}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}\in\{-,+\}
. Theorem
4.5.5
(iii)
guarantees that
\widehat{\xi}\in\mathbb{R}
. By definition of
\widehat{\xi}
and the monotonicity of Theorem
4.5.5
(i)
,
g_{(x,s)}^{\alpha{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},L}(u)\leq y=g(u)<g_{(x,s)}^{\beta{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}},L}(u)
whenever
\alpha<\widehat{\xi}<\beta
and
{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.9}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}\in\{-,+\}
.
But by Theorem
4.5.5
(ii)
, the
\beta{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.9}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}
and
\widehat{\xi}+
geodesics agree locally when
\beta
is close enough to
\widehat{\xi}
. We can conclude that

g_{(x,s)}^{\widehat{\xi}-,L}(u)\leq y=g(u)<g_{(x,s)}^{\widehat{\xi}+,L}(u).

(4.74)

Since all three are leftmost geodesics (recall Theorem
4.4.9
(iv)
),

g_{(x,s)}^{\widehat{\xi}-,L}(t)\leq g(t)\leq g_{(x,s)}^{\widehat{\xi}+,L}(t)\qquad\text{for }t\in[s,u].

(4.75)

By (
4.74
) the paths
g_{(x,s)}^{\widehat{\xi}-,L}
and
g_{(x,s)}^{\widehat{\xi}+,L}
must separate at some time
t\in[s,u)
. Furthermore, once
g_{(x,s)}^{\widehat{\xi}-,L}
splits from
g_{(x,s)}^{\widehat{\xi}+,L}
at a point
(z_{1},t_{1})
, the geodesics must stay apart. Otherwise, they would meet again at a point
(z_{2},t_{2})
, and Theorem
4.4.9
(iv)
implies that both paths are the leftmost geodesic between
(z_{1},t_{1})
and
(z_{2},t_{2})
. See Figure
4.6
. Set
\hat{t}=\inf\{t>s:g_{(x,s)}^{\widehat{\xi}-,L}(t)<g_{(x,s)}^{\widehat{\xi}+,L}(t)\}
. Then,
g_{(x,s)}^{\widehat{\xi}-,L}(t)<g_{(x,s)}^{\widehat{\xi}+,L}(t)
for all
t>\hat{t}
. By (
4.75
) and continuity of geodesics,
g_{(x,s)}^{\widehat{\xi}-,L}(t)=g(t)=g_{(x,s)}^{\widehat{\xi}+,L}(t)
for
t\in[s,\hat{t}\hskip 0.7pt]
, and so
(g(\hat{t}),\hat{t}\hskip 0.7pt)\in\mathfrak{S}^{L}
.
∎

Figure 4.6
:
The black/thin path is the path
g
. The red/thick paths are the semi-infinite geodesics
g_{(x,s)}^{\widehat{\xi}-,L}
and
g_{(x,s)}^{\widehat{\xi}+,L}
after they split from
g
. Once the red paths split, they cannot return, or else there would be two leftmost geodesics from
(g(\hat{t}),\hat{t})
to the point where they come back together.

It remains to prove Theorems
4.4.5
(ii)
and
4.2.6
. Recall the definition of the function from (
4.8
):

f_{s,\xi}(x)=W_{\xi+}(x,s;0,s)-W_{\xi-}(x,s;0,s).

\displaystyle\text{Let $\Omega_{3}$ be the subset of $\Omega_{2}$ on which the following holds: for each $T\in\mathbb{Z}$,}

(4.76)

\displaystyle\text{whenever $\xi\in\mathbb{R}$ is such that $f_{T,\,\xi}\neq 0$, then}\lim_{x\to\pm\infty}f_{T,\,\xi}(x)=\pm\infty.

By Theorem
4.4.3
(iii)
and Corollary
2.6.8
,
\mathbb{P}(\Omega_{3})=1
.

Proof of Theorem
4.4.5
(ii)
.

We work on the full-probability event
\Omega_{3}
. The statement (
4.9
) to be proved is
\xi\in\Xi\iff\forall s\in\mathbb{R}:\lim_{x\to\pm\infty}f_{s,\xi}(x)=\pm\infty
.
If, for
any

s
,
f_{s,\xi}\to\pm\infty
as
x\to\pm\infty
, then
W_{\xi-}(x,s;0,s)\neq W_{\xi+}(x,s;0,s)
for
|x|
sufficiently large, and
\xi\in\Xi
. It remains to prove the converse statement.
From (
4.39
),

\Xi=\bigcup_{T\in\mathbb{Z}}\{\xi\in\mathbb{R}:W_{\xi-}(x,T;0,T)\neq W_{\xi+}(x,T;0,T)\text{ for some }x\in\mathbb{R}\}.

To finish the proof of (
4.9
), by definition of
\Omega_{3}
, it suffices to show these two statements:

(i)

If
f_{s,\xi}\neq 0
for some
s,\xi\in\mathbb{R}
then
f_{T,\,\xi}\neq 0
for all
T>s
.

(ii)

For
T\in\mathbb{Z},\xi\in\mathbb{R}
, if
f_{T,\,\xi}\neq 0
, then for all
s<T
,
\lim_{x\to\pm\infty}f_{s,\xi}(x)=\pm\infty
.

Part (i) follows from the equality below. By (
4.38
), for
s<T
,

\displaystyle W_{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}(x,s;0,s)

\displaystyle=\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\{\mathcal{L}(x,s;z,T)+W_{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}(z,T;0,T)\}

(4.77)

\displaystyle-\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\{\mathcal{L}(0,s;z,T)+W_{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}(z,T;0,T)\}.

To prove (ii), we show the limits as
x\to+\infty
, and the limits as
x\to-\infty
follow analogously. Let
T\in\mathbb{Z},\xi\in\mathbb{R}
be such that
f_{T,\,\xi}\neq 0
, and let
R>0
. By definition of the event
\Omega_{3}
, we may choose
Z>0
sufficiently large so that
\inf_{z\geq Z}\{f_{T,\,\xi}(z)\}\geq R
. Then, by Equation (
4.45
) of Theorem
4.5.5
(v)
, for all sufficiently large
x
and
{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.9}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}\in\{-,+\}
,

\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\{\mathcal{L}(x,s;z,T)+W_{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}(z,T;0,T)\}=\sup_{z\geq Z}\{\mathcal{L}(x,s;z,T)+W_{\xi{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\boxempty$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}}}(z,T;0,T)\}.

Let

A:=\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\{\mathcal{L}(x,s;z,T)+W_{\xi+}(z,T;0,T)\}-\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\{\mathcal{L}(x,s;z,T)+W_{\xi-}(z,T;0,T)\},

and note that this does not depend on
x
. Then,
by (
4.77
),

\displaystyle-f_{s,\xi}(x)

\displaystyle=\sup_{z\geq Z}\{\mathcal{L}(x,s;z,T)+W_{\xi-}(z,T;0,T)\}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\sup_{z\geq Z}\{\mathcal{L}(x,s;z,T)+W_{\xi+}(z,T;0,T)\}+A

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{z\geq Z}\{W_{\xi-}(z,T;0,T)-W_{\xi+}(z,T;0,T)\}+A

\displaystyle=-\inf_{z\geq Z}\{f_{T,\,\xi}(z)\}+A\leq-R+A,

so that
f_{s,\xi}(x)\geq R-A
. Since
A
is constant in
x
and
R
is arbitrary, the desired result follows.

Note that (
4.9
) immediately proves (
4.10
) in the case
x=0
. The general case follows from additivity of the Busemann functions (Theorem
4.4.1
(ii)
) and (
4.9
).
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.2.6
.

Item
(i)
(
\mathfrak{S}
is dense):
Work on the full-probability event
\Omega_{2}
. Since
\mathfrak{S}\supseteq\mathfrak{S}^{L}\cup\mathfrak{S}^{R}
, it suffices to show that for
(x,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
there is a sequence
(y_{n},t_{n})\in\mathfrak{S}^{L}
converging to
(x,s)
. Let
g
be the leftmost geodesic from
(x,s)
to
(x,s+1)
. Then
\forall n\geq 1
,
g|_{[s,s+n^{-1}]}
is the leftmost geodesic from
(x,s)
to
(x,s+n^{-1})
. By Lemma
4.7.4
,
\forall n\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}

\exists(x_{n},t_{n})\in\mathfrak{S}^{L}
such that
x_{n}=g(t_{n})
and
s\leq t_{n}\leq s+n^{-1}
. The proof is complete by continuity of geodesics.

Item
(ii)
(
\mathbb{P}(p\in\mathfrak{S})=0
for all
p\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
):
If there exist disjoint semi-infinite geodesics from
(x,s)
, then for each level
t>s
, there exist disjoint geodesics from
(x,s)
to some points
(y_{1},t),(y_{2},t)
. For each fixed
(x,s)
, with probability one, this occurs for no such points by
[
23
, Remark 1.12]
.

Item
(iii)
(Hausdorff dimension of
\mathfrak{S}_{s,\xi})
:
Since
s
is fixed, it suffices to take
s=0
. By Theorem
4.4.3
(iii)
,
\{W_{\xi+}({\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}},0;0,0)\}\overset{d}{=}G^{\sqrt{2}}
, and by Theorem
4.4.5
(ii)
,
\xi\in\Xi
if and only if
f_{0,\xi}\neq 0
. Therefore, Corollary
2.6.9
implies that, with probability one,
\dim_{H}(\mathcal{D}_{0,\xi})=\frac{1}{2}
for all
\xi\in\Xi
. Then, as noted in Remark
4.7.2
,
\mathbb{P}(\dim_{H}(\mathfrak{S}_{0,\xi})=\frac{1}{2}\;\forall\xi\in\Xi)=1
.

Item
(iv)
(
\mathfrak{S}_{s,\xi}
is nonempty and unbounded for all
s
):

By Theorem
4.4.5
(ii)
, on the event
\Omega_{3}
, whenever
\xi\in\Xi
, for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
f_{s,\xi}(x)\to\pm\infty
as
x\to\pm\infty
. Since
f_{s,\xi}
is continuous (Theorem
4.4.1
(i)
), the set
\mathcal{D}_{s,\xi}
is unbounded in both directions. The proof is complete since
\mathcal{D}_{s,\xi}\subseteq\mathfrak{S}_{s,\xi}
by definition.
∎

Chapter 5
Scaling limit of the TASEP speed process

5.1
Introduction

5.1.1
The totally asymmetric simple exclusion process

In this final chapter, we show how the SH appears as a scaling limit for coupled initial data for a particle system know as the totally asymmetric simple exclusion process (TASEP). In the simplest TASEP dynamics each site of
\mathbb{Z}
contains either a particle or a hole. Each site has an independent rate 1 Poisson clock. If at time
t
the clock rings at site
x\in\mathbb{Z}
the following happens. If there is a particle at site
x
and no particle at site
x+1
then the particle at site
x
jumps to site
x+1
, while the other sites remain unchanged. If there is no particle at site
x
or there is a particle at site
x+1
then the jump is suppressed. In other words, a particle can jump to the right only if the target site has no particle at the time of the jump attempt. This is the
exclusion rule
. TASEP is a Markov process on the compact state space
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{Z}}
. Generic elements of
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{Z}}
, or
particle configurations
, are denoted by
\eta=\{\eta(x)\}_{x\in\mathbb{Z}}
, where
\eta(x)=1
means that site
x
is occupied by a particle and
\eta(x)=0
that site
x
is occupied by a hole, in other words, is empty.

For each density
\rho\in[0,1]
, the i.i.d. Bernoulli distribution
\nu^{\rho}
on
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{Z}}
with density
\rho
is the unique translation-invariant extremal stationary distribution of particle density
\rho
under the TASEP dynamics.

There is a natural way to couple multiple TASEPs from different initial conditions but with the same driving dynamics. Let
\{\mathcal{N}_{x}:x\in\mathbb{Z}\}
be a
\mathbb{Z}
-indexed collection of independent rate
1
Poisson processes on
\mathbb{R}
. The clock at location
x
rings at the times that correspond to points in
\mathcal{N}_{x}
. One can then take two densities
0<\rho^{1}<\rho^{2}<1
and ask whether there exists a coupling measure
\pi^{\rho_{1},\rho_{2}}
on
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{Z}}\times\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{Z}}
with Bernoulli marginals
\nu^{\rho_{1}}
and
\nu^{\rho_{2}}
that is stationary under the joint TASEP dynamics
and ordered. In other words, the twin requirements are that if initially
(\eta^{1},\eta^{2})\sim\pi^{\rho_{1},\rho_{2}}
, then
(\eta^{1}_{t},\eta^{2}_{t})\sim\pi^{\rho_{1},\rho_{2}}
at all subsequent times
t\geq 0
, and
\eta^{1}(x)\leq\eta^{2}(x)
for all
x\in\mathbb{Z}
with
\pi^{\rho_{1},\rho_{2}}
-probability one. Such a two-component stationary distribution exists and is unique
[
103
]
.

One reason for the interest in stationary measures of more than one density comes from the connection between the TASEP dynamics on
k
coupled profiles in the state space
(\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{Z}})^{k}
and the TASEP dynamics on particles with classes in
\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket=\{1,\ldots,k\}
, called multiclass or multitype dynamics. In the
k
-type dynamics, each particle has a class in
\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
that remains the same for all time. A particle jumps to the right, upon the ring of a Poisson clock, only if there is either a hole or a particle of lower class (higher label) to the right. If this happens, the lower class particle moves left. The state space of
k
-type dynamics is
\{1,\ldots,k,\infty\}^{\mathbb{Z}}
, with generic configurations denoted again by
\eta=\{\eta(x)\}_{x\in\mathbb{Z}}
. A value
\eta(x)=i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
means that site
x
is occupied by a particle of class
i
, and
\eta(x)=\infty
means that site
x
is empty, equivalently, occupied by a hole. Denoting a hole by
\infty
is convenient now because holes can be equivalently viewed as particles of the absolute lowest class.
For
k=1
the multitype dynamics is the same as basic TASEP.

The next question is whether we can couple all the invariant multiclass distributions so that the resulting construction is still invariant under TASEP dynamics. This was achieved by
[
2
]
: such couplings can be realized by applying projections to an object they constructed and named the
TASEP speed process
. We describe briefly the construction. To start, each site
i\in\mathbb{Z}
is occupied by a particle of class
i
. This creates the initial profile
\eta_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{Z}}
such that
\eta_{0}(i)=i
. Let
\eta_{t}
evolve under TASEP dynamics, now interpreted so that a particle switches places with the particle to its right only if the particle to the right is of lower class, that is, has a higher label. Note that now each site is always occupied by a particle of some integer label. The limit from
[
111
]

implies that each particle has a well-defined limiting speed: if
X_{t}(i)
denotes the time-
t
position of the particle initially at site
i
, then the following random limit exists almost surely:

U_{i}=\lim_{t\to\infty}t^{-1}X_{t}(i).

(5.1)

The process
\{U_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
is the TASEP speed process. It is a random element of the space
[-1,1]^{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Theorem 5.1.1

(
[
2
, Theorem 1.5]
)
.

The TASEP speed process
\{U_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
is the unique invariant distribution of TASEP that is ergodic under translations of the lattice
\mathbb{Z}
and such that each
U_{i}
is uniformly distributed on
[-1,1]
.

In the context of the theorem above, the TASEP state
\eta=\{\eta(i)\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a real-valued sequence, but the meaning of the dynamics is the same as before. Namely, at each pair
\{i,i+1\}
of nearest-neighbor sites, at the rings of a rate one exponential clock, the variables
\eta(i)
and
\eta(i+1)
are swapped if
\eta(i)<\eta(i+1)
, otherwise left unchanged.

A key point is that the
TASEP speed process projects to multitype stationary distributions.

Theorem 5.1.2

(
[
63
, Theorem 2.1]
,
[
2
, Theorem 2.1]
)
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
be the number of

classes. Let
\bar{\rho}=(\rho_{1},\ldots,\rho_{k})\in(0,1)^{k}
be a parameter vector such that
\sum_{i=1}^{k}\rho_{i}\leq 1
. Then there is a translation-invariant stationary distribution
\widecheck{\pi}^{\bar{\rho}}
for the
k
-type TASEP which is unique under the conditions
(i)
and
(ii)
, and also under the conditions
(i)
and
(ii’)
below:

(i)

\widecheck{\pi}^{\bar{\rho}}\{\eta\in\{1,\ldots,k,\infty\}^{\mathbb{Z}}:\eta(x)=j\}=\rho_{j}
for each site
x\in\mathbb{Z}
and class
j\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
;

(ii)
under
\widecheck{\pi}^{\bar{\rho}}
, for each
\ell\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
, the distribution of the sequence
\{\mathbf{1}[\eta(x)\leq\ell]\}_{x\in\mathbb{Z}}
of indicators is the i.i.d. Bernoulli measure
\nu^{\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}\rho_{j}}
of intensity
\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}\rho_{j}
;

(ii’)

\widecheck{\pi}^{\bar{\rho}}
is ergodic under the translation of the lattice
\mathbb{Z}
.

Furthermore,
\widecheck{\pi}^{\bar{\rho}}
is extreme among translation-invariant stationary measures of the
k
-type dynamics with jumps to the right.

Theorem
5.1.2
is not stated exactly in this form in either reference. It can be proved with the techniques of Section VIII.3 of Liggett
[
104
]
.

Lemma 5.1.3

(
[
2
, Corollary 5.4]
)
.

Let
F:[-1,1]\rightarrow\{1,\dotsc,k,\infty\}
be a nondecreasing function and
\lambda_{j}=\frac{1}{2}{\rm Leb}\big(F^{-1}(j)\big)
, i.e., one-half the Lebesgue measure of the interval mapped to the value
j\in\{1,\dotsc,k,\infty\}
.
Then the distribution of the
\{1,\dotsc,k,\infty\}
-valued sequence
\{F(U_{i})\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
is the stationary measure
\widecheck{\pi}^{(\lambda_{1},\dotsc,\lambda_{k})}
described in Theorem
5.1.2
for the
k
-type TASEP with jumps to the right.

For example, the case
k=1
of Lemma
5.1.3
