tells us that to produce a particle configuration with Bernoulli distribution
\nu^{\rho}
from the TASEP speed process, assign a particle to each site
x
such that
U_{x}\leq 2\rho-1
. Lemma
5.1.3
follows readily from Theorems
5.1.1
and
5.1.2
because the nondecreasing projection
F
commutes with the pathwise dynamics.

Remark 5.1.4

(Direction of the jumps)
.

Throughout this section, jumps in TASEP go to the right. Later in Section
5.2
, we use the convention from
[
63
]
whereby TASEP jumps proceed left. This is convenient because then discrete time in the queuing setting agrees with the order on
\mathbb{Z}
. Notationally,
\widecheck{\pi}^{\bar{\rho}}
denotes the multiclass stationary measure under rightward jumps, as in Theorem
5.1.2
and Lemma
5.1.3
above, while

\pi^{\bar{\rho}}
will denote the stationary measure under leftward jumps. These measures are simply reflections of each other (see Theorem
5.2.1
).

5.1.2
Scaling limit of the speed process

The space
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{Z}}
of TASEP particle configurations
\eta
can be mapped bijectively onto the space of continuous interfaces
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
f(0)=0
,
|f(x)-f(x+1)|=1
for all
x\in\mathbb{Z}
, and
f(x)
interpolates linearly between integer points. Define
\mathcal{P}:\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{Z}}\rightarrow C(\mathbb{R})
by stipulating that on integers
i
the image function
\mathcal{P}[\eta]
is given by

\mathcal{P}[\eta](i)=\begin{cases}\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}(2\eta(j)-1),&i\in\mathbb{N}\\
0,&i=0\\
-\sum_{j={i}}^{-1}(2\eta(j)-1),&i\in-\mathbb{N}\end{cases}

(5.2a)

and then extend
\mathcal{P}[\eta]
to the reals by linear interpolation:

\text{for}\quad x\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Z},\quad\mathcal{P}[\eta](x)=(\lceil{x}\rceil-x)\mathcal{P}[\eta](\lfloor{x}\rfloor)+(x-\lfloor{x}\rfloor)\mathcal{P}[\eta](\lceil{x}\rceil).

(5.2b)

TASEP can therefore be thought of as dynamics on continuous interfaces
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
such that for all
x\in\mathbb{Z}
,
f(x)\in\mathbb{Z}
and
f(x\pm 1)\in\{f(x)-1,f(x)+1\}
. When a particle at location
x
lies immediately to the left of a hole at location
x+1
, the interface has a local maximum at location
x+1
. When the particle changes places with the hole, the local maximum becomes a local minimum.

Let
U=\{U_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}
be the TASEP speed process and for
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
\mathbf{1}_{U<s}=\{\mathbf{1}_{U_{j}<s}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}
a shorthand for the
\{0,1\}
-valued sequence of indicators. For each value of the centering
v\in(-1,1)
and a scaling parameter
N\in\mathbb{N}
, use
the mapping (
5.2
) to define from the speed process a
C(\mathbb{R})
-valued process indexed by
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
:

H^{N}_{\xi}(x)=H^{\sigma,v,N}_{\xi}(x)=N^{-1/2}\,\mathcal{P}\big[\mathbf{1}_{U\leq v+\xi(1-v^{2})N^{-1/2}}\big]\Bigl(\frac{\sigma^{2}x}{1-v^{2}}N\Bigr)-\frac{\sigma^{2}vx}{1-v^{2}}N^{1/2},\qquad\xi,x\in\mathbb{R}.

(5.3)

The main theorem of this chapter is the process-level weak limit of
H^{\sigma,v,N}=\{H^{\sigma,v,N}_{\xi}\}_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}}
. The path space of
\xi\mapsto H^{\sigma,v,N}_{\xi}
is the Skorokhod space
D(\mathbb{R},C(\mathbb{R}))
of
C(\mathbb{R})
-valued cadlag paths on
\mathbb{R}
, with its usual Polish topology. This topology is discussed in more detail in
[
34
]
.

Theorem 5.1.5
.

Let
\sigma>0
and
G^{\sigma}
be the stationary horizon. Then, for each
v\in(-1,1)
, as
N\rightarrow\infty
, the distributional limit
H^{\sigma,v,N}\Rightarrow G^{\sigma}
holds on the path space
D(\mathbb{R},C(\mathbb{R}))
.

Theorem
5.1.5
is taken from the author’s joint work with Busani and Seppäläinen
[
34
]
. In that work, we focus on the case
\sigma=\sqrt{2}
, but the extension to general
\sigma
follows the same proof (or alternatively from the scaling relations of Theorem
2.3.2
(ii)
). As is typical, the proof splits into two main steps:
(i) weak convergence of finite-dimensional distributions of
H^{N}
to the limiting object and (ii) tightness of
\{H^{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
on
D(\mathbb{R},C(\mathbb{R}))
. Both parts use the Ferrari-Martin queuing representation of the multitype stationary measures.
The first part shows that, in the limit, the queuing representation recovers the queuing structure that defines the SH
[
63
]
. In this dissertation, we only show the finite-dimensional convergence (Proposition
5.2.5
), and refer the reader to
[
34
]
for the proof of tightness.

5.2
Finite-dimensional convergence

5.2.1
The space
D(\mathbb{R},C(\mathbb{R}))

We observe here why the path
\xi\mapsto H^{N}_{\xi}
defined in (
5.3
) lies in
D(\mathbb{R},C(\mathbb{R}))
. Restriction of
x\mapsto H^{N}_{\xi}(x)
to a bounded interval
[-x_{0},x_{0}]
is denoted by
H^{N,x_{0}}_{\xi}=H^{N}_{\xi}|_{[-x_{0},x_{0}]}
. Then note that for
\xi<\rho
,
H^{N,x_{0}}_{\xi}\neq H^{N,x_{0}}_{\rho}
if and only if
U_{j}\in(v+\xi(1-v^{2})N^{-1/2},v+(1-v^{2})\rho N^{-1/2}]
for some

j\in\llbracket\,\lfloor{-\,\frac{\sigma^{2}x_{0}}{1-v^{2}}N}\rfloor,\lceil{\frac{\sigma^{2}x_{0}}{1-v^{2}}N}\rceil\,\rrbracket
. Since this range of indices is finite, for each
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
and
x_{0}>0
there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that
H^{N,x_{0}}_{\xi}=H^{N,x_{0}}_{\rho}
for
\rho\in[\xi,\xi+\varepsilon]
and
H^{N,x_{0}}_{\rho}=H^{N,x_{0}}_{\sigma}
for
\rho,\sigma\in[\xi-\varepsilon,\xi)
.

5.2.2
Ferrari-Martin representation of multiclass measures

This section describes the queuing construction of stationary multiclass measures from
[
63
]
. We use the convention of
[
63
]
that TASEP particles jump to the left rather than to the right, because this choice leads to the more natural queuing set-up where time flows on
\mathbb{Z}
from left to right. This switch is then accounted for when we apply the results of this section.

Queues with a single customer stream

Let
\mathcal{U}_{1}:=\{1,\infty\}^{\mathbb{Z}}

be the space of configurations of particles on
\mathbb{Z}
with the following interpretation: a configuration
\boldsymbol{x}=\{x(j)\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{U}_{1}
has a particle at time
j\in\mathbb{Z}
if
x(j)=1
, otherwise
\boldsymbol{x}
has a hole at time
j\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Let
\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{s}\in\mathcal{U}_{1}
. Think of
\boldsymbol{a}
as arrivals of customers to a queue, and of
\boldsymbol{s}
as the available services in the queue. For
i\leq j\in\mathbb{Z}
let
a^{\leq 1}[i,j]
be the number of customers, that is, the number of
1
’s in
\boldsymbol{a}
, that arrive to the queue during time interval
[i,j]
. Similarly, let
s[i,j]
be the number of services available during time interval
[i,j]
. The queue length at time
i
is then given by

Q_{i}=\sup_{j:j\leq i}\big(a^{\leq 1}[j,i]-s[j,i]\hskip 0.9pt\big)^{+}.

(5.4)

In principle this makes sense for arbitrary sequences
\boldsymbol{a}
and
\boldsymbol{s}
if one allows infinite queue lengths
Q_{i}=\infty
. However, in our treatment
\boldsymbol{a}
and
\boldsymbol{s}
are always such that
queue lengths are finite. We will not repeat this point in the sequel.

The departures from the queue come from the mapping
\boldsymbol{d}=D_{d}(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{s}):\mathcal{U}_{1}\times\mathcal{U}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{U}_{1}
, given by

d(i)=\begin{cases}1&\text{$s(i)=1$ and either $Q_{i-1}>0$ or $a(i)=1$},\\
\infty&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(5.5)

In other words, a customer leaves the queue at time
i
(and
d(i)=1
) if there is a service at time
i
and either the queue is not empty or a customer just arrived at time
i
.
The sequence
\boldsymbol{u}:=U_{d}(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{s})
of unused services is given by a mapping
U_{d}:\mathcal{U}_{1}\times\mathcal{U}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{U}_{1}
defined by

u(j)=\begin{cases}1&\text{if $s(j)=1$, $Q_{j-1}=0$, and $a(j)=\infty$},\\
\infty&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(5.6)

Last, we define the map
R_{d}:\mathcal{U}_{1}\times\mathcal{U}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{U}_{1}
as
\boldsymbol{r}=R_{d}(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{s})
with

r(j)=\begin{cases}1&\text{ if either $a(j)=1$ or $u(j)=1$},\\
\infty&\text{ if $a(j)=u(j)=\infty$}.\end{cases}.

(5.7)

Extend the departure operator
D_{d}
to queues in tandem. Let
D_{d}^{1}(\boldsymbol{x})=\boldsymbol{x}
be the identity, and for
n\geq 2
,

\displaystyle D_{d}^{(2)}(\boldsymbol{x}_{1},\boldsymbol{x}_{2})

\displaystyle=D_{d}(\boldsymbol{x}_{1},\boldsymbol{x}_{2})

(5.8)

\displaystyle D_{d}^{3}(\boldsymbol{x}_{1},\boldsymbol{x}_{2},\boldsymbol{x}_{3})

\displaystyle=D_{d}\big(D_{d}^{(2)}(\boldsymbol{x}_{1},\boldsymbol{x}_{2}),\boldsymbol{x}_{3}\big)

\displaystyle\vdots

\displaystyle D_{d}^{(n)}(\boldsymbol{x}_{1},\boldsymbol{x}_{2},\dotsc,\boldsymbol{x}_{n})

\displaystyle=D_{d}\big(D_{d}^{(n-1)}(\boldsymbol{x}_{1},\boldsymbol{x}_{2},\dotsc,\boldsymbol{x}_{n-1}),\boldsymbol{x}_{n}\big).

We use the notation
D_{d}
(with the subscript
d
) to denote discrete, in comparison to the operators on continuous functions in (
2.3
),(
2.5
).

Queues with priorities

Now consider queues with customers of different classes. For
m\in\mathbb{N}
, let

\mathcal{U}_{m}:=\{1,2,\dotsc,m,\infty\}^{\mathbb{Z}}
be the space of configurations of particles on
\mathbb{Z}
with classes in
\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket=\{1,2,\dotsc,m\}
. A lower label indicates higher class and, as before, the value
\infty
signifies an empty time slot. To illustrate the notation for an arrival sequence
\boldsymbol{a}\in\mathcal{U}_{m}
, the value
a(j)=k\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
means that a customer of class
k
arrives at time
j\in\mathbb{Z}
, while
a(j)=\infty
means no arrival at time
j
. Define

a^{\leq k}[j]=\begin{cases}1&\text{if $a(j)\leq k$},\\
0&\text{if $a(j)>k$}.\end{cases}

Consistently with earlier definitions,
a^{\leq k}[i,j]=\sum_{l=i}^{j}a^{\leq k}[l]
is the number of customers in classes
\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
that arrive to the queue in the time interval
[i,j]
. Let
\boldsymbol{s}\in\mathcal{U}_{1}
be the sequence of available services. The number of customers in classes
\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
in the queue at time
i
is then

Q^{\leq k}_{i}(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{s})=\sup_{j:j\leq i}\big(a^{\leq k}[j,i]-s[j,i]\big)^{+},\qquad i\in\mathbb{Z}.

The multiclass departure map
\boldsymbol{d}=F_{m}(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{s}):\mathcal{U}_{m}\times\mathcal{U}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{U}_{m+1}
is defined so that customers of higher class (lower label) are served first. These are the rules:

\begin{cases}d(i)\leq k&\text{for $k\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket$ if $s(i)=1$ and either $Q^{\leq k}_{i-1}>0$ or $a(i)\leq k$},\\
d(i)=m+1&\text{if $s(i)=1$, $Q^{\leq m}_{i-1}=0$, and $a(i)=\infty$},\\
d(i)=\infty&\text{if }s(i)=\infty.\end{cases}

(5.9)

The map
F_{m}
works as follows. The queue is fed with arrivals
\boldsymbol{a}\in\mathcal{U}_{m}
of customers in classes
1
to
m
. Suppose a service is available at time
i\in\mathbb{Z}
(
s(i)=1
). Then the customer of the highest class (lowest label in
\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
) in the queue at time
i
, or just arrived at time
i
, is served at time
i
, and its label becomes the value of
d(i)
. If no customer arrived at time
i
(
a(i)=\infty
) and the queue is empty (
Q^{\leq m}_{i-1}=0
), then the unused service
s(i)=1
is converted into a departing customer of class
m+1
:
d(i)=m+1
. If there is no service available at time
i\in\mathbb{Z}
(
s(i)=\infty
), then no customer leaves at time
i
and
d(i)=\infty
.

In particular, for
m=1
, the output
\boldsymbol{d}=F_{1}(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{s})
satisfies

d(i)=\begin{cases}1,&[D_{d}(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{s})]_{i}=1\\
2,&[U_{d}(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{s})]_{i}=1\\
\infty,&s(i)=\infty.\end{cases}

(5.10)

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
define the space
\mathcal{X}_{n}=\mathcal{U}_{1}^{n}=\{1,\infty\}^{\mathbb{Z}\times\{1,\dotsc,n\}}
of
n
-tuples of sequences. Let
\bar{\lambda}=(\lambda_{1},\dotsc,\lambda_{n})\in(0,1)^{n}
be a parameter vector such that
\sum_{r=1}^{n}\lambda_{r}\leq 1
. Define the product measure
\nu^{\bar{\lambda}}
on
\mathcal{X}_{n}
so that if
\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}=(\boldsymbol{x}_{1},\dotsc,\boldsymbol{x}_{n})\sim\nu^{\bar{\lambda}}
then the sequences
\boldsymbol{x}_{k}
are independent and each
\boldsymbol{x}_{k}
has the i.i.d. product Bernoulli distribution with intensity
\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}
. From this input we define a new process
\bar{\boldsymbol{v}}=(\boldsymbol{v}_{1},\dotsc,\boldsymbol{v}_{n})
such that each
\boldsymbol{v}_{m}\in\mathcal{U}_{m}
by the iterative formulas

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}_{1}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{x}_{1}\qquad\text{and}

(5.11)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}_{m}

\displaystyle=F_{m-1}(\boldsymbol{v}_{m-1},\boldsymbol{x}_{m})\qquad\text{for }\ m=2,\dotsc,n.

We denote this map by
\bar{\boldsymbol{v}}=\mathcal{V}(\bar{\boldsymbol{x}})=(\mathcal{V}_{1}(\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}),\dotsc,\mathcal{V}_{n}(\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}))
. For a vector
\bar{\lambda}=(\lambda_{1},\dotsc,\lambda_{n})
and
\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}\sim\nu^{\bar{\lambda}}
, define the distribution
\pi^{\bar{\lambda}}
as the image of
\nu^{\bar{\lambda}}
under this map:

\pi^{\bar{\lambda}}=\nu^{\bar{\lambda}}\circ\mathcal{V}_{n}^{-1}\ \iff\ \mathcal{V}_{n}(\bar{\boldsymbol{x}})\sim\pi^{\bar{\lambda}}.

(5.12)

Theorem 5.2.1
.

[
63
, Theorem 2.1]

For each
m\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket
,
the distribution of
\boldsymbol{v}_{m}
under
\pi^{\bar{\lambda}}
is
the unique translation-ergodic
stationary distribution of the
m
-type TASEP on
\mathbb{Z}
with leftward jumps and with density
\lambda_{r}
of particles of class
r\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
.
The distribution of the reversed configuration
\{\boldsymbol{v}_{m}(-i)\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
is the unique distribution
\widecheck{\pi}^{\bar{\lambda}}
described in Theorem
5.1.2
, in other words, the unique translation-ergodic stationary distribution of the
m
-type TASEP on
\mathbb{Z}
with rightward jumps, with density
\lambda_{r}
of particles of class
r\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
.

Remark 5.2.2
.

The statement about the TASEP with rightward jumps is not included in
[
63
]
, but its proof is straightforward: reflecting the index does not change the density of the particles, so the values
\lambda_{r}
are preserved. Consider an
m
-type TASEP with left jumps
\{\eta_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
defined by the Poisson clocks
\{\mathcal{N}_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
and started from initial profile
\eta_{0}\sim\boldsymbol{v}_{m}
. Let
\{\widecheck{\eta}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
be TASEP with right jumps defined by the Poisson clocks
\{\mathcal{N}_{-i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
and started from initial profile
\{\widecheck{\eta}_{0}(i)\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}:=\{\eta_{0}(-i)\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
, which has distribution
\{\boldsymbol{v}_{m}(-i)\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
. Then, in the process
\eta_{t}
, a particle jumps from site
i
to site
i-1
exactly when a particle in the process
\widecheck{\eta}_{t}
jumps from site
-i
to site
-i+1
. By the invariance of
\eta_{0}
under TASEP with left jumps,

\{\widecheck{\eta}_{t}(i)\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}=\{\eta_{t}(-i)\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\overset{d}{=}\{\eta_{0}(-i)\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}=\{\widecheck{\eta}_{0}(i)\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}},

so
\{\boldsymbol{v}_{m}(-i)\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
is the invariant measure for TASEP with right jumps and densities
\lambda_{r}
.

For
\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathcal{U}_{n}
, define

\Cls^{[i,j]}_{m}(\boldsymbol{x})=\#\{l\in[i,j]:{x}(l)\leq m\},\qquad m\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket.

\Cls^{[i,j]}_{m}(\boldsymbol{x})
records the number of customers in classes
\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
during time interval
[i,j]
in the sequence
\boldsymbol{x}
. Note that a customer of class
m
appears in
[i,j]
if and only if
\Cls^{[i,j]}_{m}(\boldsymbol{x})>\Cls^{[i,j]}_{m-1}(\boldsymbol{x})
, with the convention
\Cls_{0}\equiv 0
. The key technical lemma is that the iteration in (
5.11
) can be represented by tandem queues.

Lemma 5.2.3
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}=(\boldsymbol{x}_{1},\dotsc,\boldsymbol{x}_{n})\in\mathcal{X}_{n}
.
Let
\boldsymbol{v}_{n}=\mathcal{V}_{n}(\bar{\boldsymbol{x}})
, where
\mathcal{V}_{n}
is given in (
5.11
). Define

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{d}^{n,i}:=D_{d}^{(n-i+1)}(\boldsymbol{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{x}_{i+1},\dotsc,\boldsymbol{x}_{n})\quad\text{for }\ i=1,\dotsc,n-1,\quad\text{and}\quad\boldsymbol{d}^{n,n}:=\boldsymbol{x}_{n}.

Then for all time intervals
[i,j]
,

\big(\Cls^{[i,j]}_{1}(\boldsymbol{v}_{n}),\dotsc,\Cls^{[i,j]}_{n}(\boldsymbol{v}_{n})\big)=\big(d^{n,1}[i,j],\dotsc,d^{n,n}[i,j]\big).

(5.13)

Proof.

The proof goes by induction on
n
, with base case
n=2
. From (
5.10
),
\boldsymbol{d}^{2,1}=D_{d}(\boldsymbol{x}_{1},\boldsymbol{x}_{2})
registers the first class departures out of the queue
F_{1}(\boldsymbol{x}_{1},\boldsymbol{x}_{2})
while
\boldsymbol{d}^{2,2}=\boldsymbol{x}_{2}
is the combined number of first and second class customers coming out of the queue. The case
n=2
of (
5.13
) has been verified.

Assume (
5.13
) holds for some
n=k\geq 2
. This means that for each
m\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
,
\boldsymbol{d}^{k,m}
registers the customers in classes
\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
in
\boldsymbol{v}_{k}
. In the next step,
\boldsymbol{v}_{k+1}=F_{k}(\boldsymbol{v}_{k},\boldsymbol{x}_{k+1})
, and

\big(\boldsymbol{d}^{k+1,1},\dotsc,\boldsymbol{d}^{k+1,k+1}\big)=\big(D_{d}(\boldsymbol{d}^{k,1},\boldsymbol{x}_{k+1}),\dotsc,D_{d}(\boldsymbol{d}^{k,k},\boldsymbol{x}_{k+1}),\boldsymbol{x}_{k+1}\big).

Since the same service process
\boldsymbol{x}_{k+1}
acts in both queuing maps, the outputs match in the sense that for each
m\leq k
,
\boldsymbol{d}^{k+1,m}=D_{d}(\boldsymbol{d}^{k,m},\boldsymbol{x}_{k+1})
registers the customers in classes
\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
in
\boldsymbol{v}_{k+1}
.
Under
F_{k}(\boldsymbol{v}_{k},\boldsymbol{x}_{k+1})
, unused services become departures of class
k+1
. Hence, every service event of
\boldsymbol{x}_{k+1}
becomes a departure of some class in
\llbracket 1,k+1\rrbracket
. This verifies the equality
\Cls^{[i,j]}_{k+1}(\boldsymbol{v}_{k+1})={x}_{k+1}[i,j]=d^{k+1,k+1}[i,j]
of the last coordinate. Thereby, the validity of (
5.13
) has been extended from
k
to
k+1
.
∎

5.2.3
Convergence of queues

This section shows the finite-dimensional weak convergence of the TASEP speed process, using the representation of stationary distributions in terms of queuing mappings. To do this, we derive a convenient representation for the random walk defined by the departure mapping
D_{d}
(
5.14
). Consistently with the count notation
x[i,j]
introduced above for
\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathcal{U}_{1}
, abbreviate

x[i]=x[i,i]=\mathbf{1}_{x(i)=1}
. With this convention, configurations
\boldsymbol{x}
can also be thought of as members of the sequence space
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{Z}}
. Recall the operation
\mathcal{P}
from (
5.2
).

Lemma 5.2.4
.

For
i\in\mathbb{Z}
,

\mathcal{P}[D_{d}(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{s})](i)=\mathcal{P}[\boldsymbol{s}](i)+\sup_{-\infty<j\leq 0}[\mathcal{P}[\boldsymbol{s}](j)-\mathcal{P}[\boldsymbol{a}](j)]-\sup_{-\infty<j\leq i}[\mathcal{P}[\boldsymbol{s}](j)-\mathcal{P}[\boldsymbol{a}](j)].

(5.14)

Proof.

Recall the definition of
D
from (
5.5
). Observe that

D_{d}(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{s})[j,i]=Q_{j-1}-Q_{i}+a[j,i],

(5.15)

because any arrival that cannot be accounted for in
Q_{i}
must have left by time
i
.
Use also the empty interval convention
x[i+1,i]=0
. Then, from (
5.4
), we can equivalently write

Q_{i}=\sup_{j:j\leq i+1}\bigl(a^{\leq 1}[j,i]-s[j,i]\bigr).

(5.16)

Now, observe that for
\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathcal{U}_{1}
,

2x[j,i]-(i-j+1)=\sum_{k=j}^{i}(2x[k]-1)=\mathcal{P}[\boldsymbol{x}](i+1)-\mathcal{P}[\boldsymbol{x}](j)

(5.17)

Combining (
5.15
)–(
5.17
) and the definition
\mathcal{P}[\boldsymbol{x}](0)=0
, gives for
i>0
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}[D_{d}(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{s})](i)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{i-1}(2D_{d}(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{s})[k]-1)=2D_{d}(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{s})[0,i-1]-i

\displaystyle=2a[0,i-1]-i+2Q_{-1}-2Q_{i-1}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{xincs}}{=}\mathcal{P}[\boldsymbol{a}](i)-\mathcal{P}[\boldsymbol{a}](0)+2\sup_{-\infty<j\leq 0}[a^{\leq 1}[j,-1]-s[j,-1]]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-2\sup_{-\infty<j\leq i}[a^{\leq 1}[j,i-1]-s[j,i-1]]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}[\boldsymbol{a}](i)+\sup_{-\infty<j\leq 0}[2a^{\leq 1}[j,-1]+j-(2s[j,-1]+j)]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\sup_{-\infty<j\leq i}[2a^{\leq 1}[j,i-1]-(i-j)-(2s[j,i-1]-(i-j))]

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{xincs}}{=}\mathcal{P}[\boldsymbol{a}](i)+\sup_{-\infty<j\leq 0}[\mathcal{P}[\boldsymbol{a}](0)-\mathcal{P}[\boldsymbol{a}](j)-\mathcal{P}[\boldsymbol{s}](0)+\mathcal{P}[\boldsymbol{s}](j)]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad-\sup_{-\infty<j\leq i}[\mathcal{P}[\boldsymbol{a}](i)-\mathcal{P}[\boldsymbol{a}](j)-\mathcal{P}[\boldsymbol{s}](i)+\mathcal{P}[\boldsymbol{s}](j)]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}[\boldsymbol{s}](i)+\sup_{-\infty<j\leq 0}[\mathcal{P}[\boldsymbol{s}](j)-\mathcal{P}[\boldsymbol{a}](j)]-\sup_{-\infty<j\leq i}[\mathcal{P}[\boldsymbol{s}](j)-\mathcal{P}[\boldsymbol{a}](j)].

The case
i<0
follows an analogous proof.
∎

Proposition 5.2.5
.

Fix the diffusion coeffieient
\sigma>0
the centering
v\in(-1,1)
.
Then the scaled TASEP speed process
H^{N}
of (
5.3
) satisfies the weak convergence

(H_{\xi_{1}}^{N},\dotsc,H_{\xi_{k}}^{N})

\Rightarrow

(G^{\sigma}_{\xi_{1}},\dotsc,G^{\sigma}_{\xi_{k}})
on
C(\mathbb{R})^{k}
for any finite sequence

(\xi_{1},\dotsc,\xi_{k})\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, take
\xi_{1}<\xi_{2}<\cdots<\xi_{k}
.
For
N>|\xi_{1}|^{3}\vee|\xi_{k}|^{3}
, consider the following nondecreasing map
F:[-1,1]\to\{1,\ldots,k\}\cup\{\infty\}
:

\text{For }U\in[-1,1],\;F(U)=\begin{cases}1,&U\leq v+\xi_{1}(1-v^{2})N^{-1/2}\\
2,&v+\xi_{1}(1-v^{2})N^{-1/2}<U\leq v+\xi_{2}(1-v^{2})N^{-1/2}\\
\vdots&\\
k,&v+\xi_{k-1}(1-v^{2})N^{-1/2}<U\leq v+\xi_{k}(1-v^{2})N^{-1/2}\\
\infty,&U>v+\xi_{k}(1-v^{2})N^{-1/2}.\end{cases}

By considering the output of this map as classes, Lemma
5.1.3
implies that
\{F(U_{i})\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}

is distributed as the stationary distribution for
k
-type TASEP with right jumps and densities

\bar{\lambda}=\biggl(\frac{1+v+\xi_{1}(1-v^{2})N^{-1/2}}{2},\frac{(\xi_{2}-\xi_{1})(1-v^{2})N^{-1/2}}{2},\ldots,\frac{(\xi_{k}-\xi_{k-1})(1-v^{2})N^{-1/2}}{2}\biggr).

The reflection of Theorem
5.2.1
and translation invariance then imply that
\{F(U_{-i-1})\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}

has the stationary distribution
\pi^{\bar{\lambda}}
for TASEP with left jumps.
Lemma
5.2.3
implies that

\displaystyle\bigl(\mathbf{1}_{U_{-i-1}\leq v+\xi_{1}(1-v^{2})N^{-1/2}},\ldots,\mathbf{1}_{U_{-i-1}\leq v+\xi_{k-1}(1-v^{2})N^{-1/2}}\,,\mathbf{1}_{U_{-i-1}\leq v+\xi_{k}(1-v^{2})N^{-1/2}}\bigr)_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}

(5.18)

\displaystyle\overset{d}{=}\bigl(D_{d}^{(k)}(\boldsymbol{x}_{1}^{N},\ldots,\boldsymbol{x}_{k}^{N})[i]\,,\ldots,D_{d}^{(2)}(\boldsymbol{x}^{N}_{k-1},\boldsymbol{x}_{k}^{N})[i]\,,\boldsymbol{x}_{k}^{N}[i]\bigr)_{i\in\mathbb{Z}},

where
(\boldsymbol{x}_{1}^{N},\ldots,\boldsymbol{x}_{k}^{N})\sim\nu^{\bar{\lambda}}
.
Remark
5.2.7
at the end of the section gives an alternative way to justify the index reversal on the left-hand side above when
v=0
.

Before proceeding with the proof, we give a roadmap. First, by definition of
\mathcal{P}
, if
\widecheck{U}_{i}=U_{-i-1}
, then for
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle H_{\xi}^{N}(x)

\displaystyle=N^{-1/2}\mathcal{P}[\mathbf{1}_{U\leq v+\xi(1-v^{2})N^{-1/2}}]\Bigl(\frac{\sigma^{2}x}{1-v^{2}}N\Bigr)-\frac{\sigma^{2}vx}{1-v^{2}}N^{1/2}

(5.19)

\displaystyle=-N^{-1/2}\mathcal{P}[\mathbf{1}_{\widecheck{U}\leq v+\xi(1-v^{2})N^{-1/2}}]\Bigl(-\frac{\sigma^{2}x}{1-v^{2}}N\Bigr)-\frac{\sigma^{2}vx}{1-v^{2}}N^{1/2}.

Our goal is to show the weak limit

\displaystyle\biggl(-N^{-1/2}\mathcal{P}[\boldsymbol{x}_{k}^{N}]\Bigl(\frac{\sigma^{2}\hskip 0.5pt{\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt}}{1-v^{2}}N\Bigr)+\frac{\sigma^{2}v\hskip 0.5pt{\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt}}{1-v^{2}}N^{1/2}\,,

(5.20)

\displaystyle-N^{-1/2}\mathcal{P}[D_{d}(\boldsymbol{x}^{N}_{k-1},\boldsymbol{x}_{k}^{N})]\Bigl(\frac{\sigma^{2}\hskip 0.5pt{\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt}}{1-v^{2}}N\Bigr)+\frac{\sigma^{2}v\hskip 0.5pt{\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt}}{1-v^{2}}N^{1/2},\ldots,

\displaystyle-N^{-1/2}\mathcal{P}[D_{d}^{k}(\boldsymbol{x}_{1}^{N},\ldots,\boldsymbol{x}_{k}^{N})]\Bigl(\frac{2\hskip 0.5pt{\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt}}{1-v^{2}}N\Bigr)+\frac{2v\hskip 0.5pt{\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt}}{1-v^{2}}N^{1/2}\biggr)

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow(G^{\sigma}_{-\xi_{k}},\ldots,G^{\sigma}_{-\xi_{1}}).

Once we show (
5.20
), from (
5.20
), (
5.18
) and (
5.19
) it follows that
(H_{\xi_{1}}^{N},\ldots,H_{\xi_{k}}^{N})\Longrightarrow(G^{\sigma}_{-\xi_{1}}(-\hskip 0.5pt{\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt}),\ldots,G^{\sigma}_{-\xi_{k}}(-\hskip 0.5pt{\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt}))
. This limit has the same distribution as
(G^{\sigma}_{\xi_{1}},\ldots,G^{\sigma}_{\xi_{k}})
by Theorem
2.3.2
(iv)
. As mentioned previously, these reflections in the proof are a consequence of having time flow left to right in the queuing setting.

Now, we prove (
5.20
).
By construction, for
j\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
,
\{{x}^{N}_{j}[i]\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
is an i.i.d. Bernoulli sequence with intensity
\sum_{\ell\leq j}\lambda_{\ell}=\frac{1}{2}{(1+v+\xi_{j}(1-v^{2})N^{-1/2})}
. Hence, for
j\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
,

-N^{-1/2}\mathcal{P}[\boldsymbol{x}_{j}^{N}]\Bigl(\frac{\sigma^{2}{\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt}}{1-v^{2}}N\Bigr)+\frac{\sigma^{2}v\hskip 0.5pt{\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt}}{1-v^{2}}N^{1/2}

(5.21)

converges in distribution to a Brownian motion with diffusivity
\sigma
and drift
-\sigma^{2}\xi_{j}
.
To elevate this to the joint convergence of (
5.20
), we utilize the queuing mappings in (
5.18
) and the transformations
D^{(k)}
from (
2.5
) that construct the SH.

By Skorokhod representation (
[
54
, Thm. 11.7.2]
,
[
59
, Thm. 3.1.8]
), we may couple
\{\boldsymbol{x}_{j}^{N}\}_{j=1,\ldots,k}
and independent Brownian motions
\{Z^{j}\}_{j\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket}
with diffusivity
\sigma
and drift
-\sigma^{2}\xi_{j}
so that, with probability one, for
j\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
, (
5.21
)
converges uniformly on compact sets to
Z^{j}
. Let
\mathbb{P}
be the law of this coupling (To be precise, the sequences
\boldsymbol{x}_{j}^{N}
are functions of the converging processes (
5.21
), which Skorokhod representation couples with their limiting Brownian motions).

By Proposition
2.3.1
(ii)
and definition of
D^{(k)}
(
2.5
), for reals
\xi_{1}<\cdots<\xi_{k}
, the valued marginal
(G^{\sigma}_{-\xi_{k}},\dotsc,G^{\sigma}_{-\xi_{1}})
of the SH can be constructed as follows:

\displaystyle G^{\sigma}_{-\xi_{k}}

\displaystyle=D(Z^{k})=Z^{k},\qquad G^{\sigma}_{-\xi_{k-1}}=D^{(2)}(Z^{k},Z^{k-1})=D(Z^{k-1},Z^{k}),\ldots

\displaystyle G^{\sigma}_{-\xi_{1}}

\displaystyle=D^{(k)}(Z^{1},Z^{2},\dotsc,Z^{k})=D(D^{(k-1)}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{k-1}),Z^{k}).

We recall (
2.3
) that The map
D
is

D(Z,B)(y)=B(y)+\sup_{-\infty<x\leq y}\{Z(x)-B(x)\}-\sup_{-\infty<x\leq 0}\{Z(x)-B(x)\}.

By a union bound, it suffices to show that, under this coupling, for each
\varepsilon>0,a>0
, and each
j=0,\ldots,k-1
,

\displaystyle\limsup_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\biggl(\;\sup_{x\in[-a,a]}\Bigl|-N^{-1/2}\mathcal{P}[D_{d}^{(j+1)}(\boldsymbol{x}_{k-j}^{N},\ldots,\boldsymbol{x}_{k}^{N})]

\displaystyle\Bigl(\frac{2x}{1-v^{2}}N\Bigr)+\frac{2vx}{1-v^{2}}N^{1/2}

(5.22)

\displaystyle-D^{(j+1)}(Z^{k-j},\ldots,Z^{k})(x)\Bigr|>\varepsilon\biggr)=0,

where the argument of the discrete operator is understood via linear interpolation.
We prove (
5.22
) by induction on
j
. The base case
j=0
follows by the almost sure uniform convergence on compact sets of (
5.21
) to
Z^{j}
. Now, assume the statement holds for some
j-1\in\{0,\ldots,k-2\}
. The proof is completed by Lemma
5.2.6
below, once we recall (
5.8
) that

D_{d}^{(j+1)}(\boldsymbol{x}_{k-j}^{N},\ldots,\boldsymbol{x}_{k}^{N})=D_{d}(D_{d}^{(j)}(\boldsymbol{x}_{k-j}^{N},\ldots,\boldsymbol{x}_{k-1}^{N}),\boldsymbol{x}_{k}^{N}).\qed

Lemma 5.2.6
.

Let
v\in(-1,1)
. For each
N>0
, let
\boldsymbol{a}^{N}
and
\boldsymbol{s}^{N}
be
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{Z}}
-valued i.i.d. sequences such that the intensity of
\boldsymbol{s}^{N}
is strictly greater than the intensity of
\boldsymbol{a}^{N}
. Assume further that these sequences are coupled together with Brownian motions
Z^{1},Z^{2}
with diffusivity
\sigma
and drifts
-\sigma^{2}\xi_{1}>-\sigma^{2}\xi_{2}
so that, for each
\varepsilon>0
and
a>0
,

\displaystyle\limsup_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\Biggl(\sup_{y\in[-a,a]}\Bigl|-N^{-1/2}\mathcal{P}[\boldsymbol{a}^{N}]\Bigl(\frac{\sigma^{2}y}{1-v^{2}}N\Bigr)+\frac{\sigma^{2}vy}{1-v^{2}}N^{1/2}-Z^{1}(y)\Bigr|

(5.23)

\displaystyle\vee\Bigl|-N^{-1/2}\mathcal{P}[\boldsymbol{s}^{N}]\Bigl(\frac{\sigma^{2}y}{1-v^{2}}N\Bigr)+\frac{\sigma^{2}vy}{1-v^{2}}N^{1/2}-Z^{2}(y)\Bigr|>\varepsilon\Biggr)=0.

Then, for every
\varepsilon>0
and
a>0
,

\limsup_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\Biggl(\sup_{y\in[-a,a]}\Bigl|-N^{-1/2}\mathcal{P}[D_{d}(\mathbf{a}^{N},\mathbf{s}^{N})]\Bigl(\frac{\sigma^{2}y}{1-v^{2}}N\Bigr)+\frac{\sigma^{2}vy}{1-v^{2}}N^{1/2}\\
-D(Z^{1},Z^{2})(y)\Bigr|>\varepsilon\Biggr)=0.

(5.24)

Proof.

From (
5.14
), we have, for
\frac{\sigma^{2}Nx}{1-v^{2}}\in\mathbb{Z}
,

\displaystyle-N^{-1/2}\mathcal{P}[D_{d}(\boldsymbol{a}^{N},\boldsymbol{s}^{N})]\Bigl(\frac{\sigma^{2}x}{1-v^{2}}N\Bigr)+\frac{\sigma^{2}vx}{1-v^{2}}N^{1/2}

(5.25)

\displaystyle=-N^{-1/2}\mathcal{P}[\boldsymbol{s}^{N}]\Bigl(\frac{\sigma^{2}x}{1-v^{2}}N\Bigr)+\frac{\sigma^{2}vx}{1-v^{2}}N^{1/2}

\displaystyle+N^{-1/2}\sup_{-\infty<j\leq\sigma^{2}Nx/(1-v^{2})}[\mathcal{P}[\boldsymbol{s}^{N}](j)-\mathcal{P}[\boldsymbol{a}^{N}](j)]

\displaystyle-N^{-1/2}\sup_{-\infty<j\leq 0}[\mathcal{P}[\boldsymbol{s}^{N}](j)-\mathcal{P}[\boldsymbol{a}^{N}](j)]

\displaystyle=-N^{-1/2}\mathcal{P}[\boldsymbol{s}^{N}]\Bigl(\frac{\sigma^{2}x}{1-v^{2}}N\Bigr)+\frac{\sigma^{2}vx}{1-v^{2}}N^{1/2}

\displaystyle+\sup_{-\infty<j\leq\sigma^{2}Nx/(1-v^{2})}[-N^{-1/2}\mathcal{P}[\boldsymbol{a}^{N}](j)-(-N^{-1/2}\mathcal{P}[\boldsymbol{s}^{N}](j))]

\displaystyle-\sup_{-\infty<j\leq 0}[-N^{-1/2}\mathcal{P}[\boldsymbol{a}^{N}](j)-(-N^{-1/2}\mathcal{P}[\boldsymbol{s}^{N}](j))].

Hence, from (
5.25
) and the assumed convergence of
N^{-1/2}\mathcal{P}[\boldsymbol{s}^{N}](\frac{\sigma^{2}N}{1-v^{2}}\hskip 0.5pt{\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt})
in probability (
5.23
), to prove (
5.24
), it suffices to show that, for each
a>0
and
\varepsilon>0
,

\limsup_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\biggl(\,\sup_{y\in[-a,a]}\Bigl|\sup_{-\infty<x\leq[\sigma^{2}Ny/(1-v^{2})]}\bigl[-N^{-1/2}\mathcal{P}[\boldsymbol{a}^{N}](x)-(-N^{-1/2}\mathcal{P}[\boldsymbol{s}^{N}](x))\bigr]\\
-\sup_{-\infty<x\leq y}[Z^{1}(x)-Z^{2}(x)]\Bigr|>\varepsilon\biggr)=0.

(5.26)

For
x\in\mathbb{R}
, recall that
[x]
denotes the integer closest to
x
with
|[x]|\leq|x|
. Note that there is a drift term for both the walks
\mathcal{P}[\boldsymbol{a}^{N}]
and
\mathcal{P}[\boldsymbol{s}^{N}]
that cancels when they are subtracted.
For shorthand, let

X^{N}(x)=-N^{-1/2}\mathcal{P}[\boldsymbol{a}^{N}](x)-(-N^{-1/2}\mathcal{P}[\boldsymbol{s}^{N}](x))

For the
a
in the hypothesis of the lemma and arbitrary
S>a
, let
E_{N,a,S}
be the event where the following three conditions all hold:

(i)

\sup_{-\infty<x\leq[-\sigma^{2}Na/(1-v^{2})]}\bigl[X^{N}(x)\bigr]=\sup_{[-\sigma^{2}NS/(1-v^{2})]\leq x\leq[-\sigma^{2}Na/(1-v^{2})]}\bigl[X^{N}(x)\bigr].

(ii)

\sup_{-\infty<x\leq-a}[Z^{1}(x)-Z^{2}(x)]=\sup_{-S\leq x\leq-a}[Z^{1}(x)-Z^{2}(x)].

(iii)

\sup_{y\in[-a,a]}\Bigl|\sup_{[-\sigma^{2}NS/(1-v^{2})]\leq x\leq[\sigma^{2}Nx/(1-v^{2})]}X^{N}(y)-\sup_{-S\leq x\leq y}[Z^{1}(x)-Z^{2}(x)]\Bigr|\leq\varepsilon.

For every
S>a
, the event in (
5.26
) is contained in
E_{N,a,S}^{c}
. By assumption (
5.23
) and Lemma
A.3.4
(applied to the random walk
-\mathcal{P}[\boldsymbol{a}^{N}]+\mathcal{P}[\boldsymbol{s}^{N}]
with
m=\xi_{2}-\xi_{1}
,
\sigma=\sqrt{2\sigma^{2}}
,
\varphi(N)=N^{-1/2},\xi(N)=\sigma^{2}N/(1-v^{2})
, and
B=Z^{1}({\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt}/(2\sigma^{2}))-Z^{2}({\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt}/(2\sigma^{2}))
),
\lim_{S\to\infty}\limsup_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(E_{N,a,S}^{c})=0
, completing the proof.
∎

Remark 5.2.7
.

For
v=0
, one can alternatively arrive at (
5.18
) by considering the speed process for TASEP with left jumps. As in (
5.1
), let
X_{i}(t)
be the position of the right-going particle with label
i
that starts at
X_{i}(0)=i
and define the right-going speed process by
U_{i}=\lim_{t\to\infty}t^{-1}X_{i}(t)
. To flip the space direction, define left-going particles
\widetilde{X}_{i}(t)=-X_{-i}(t)
and the corresponding speed process
\widetilde{U}_{i}=\lim_{t\to\infty}t^{-1}\widetilde{X}_{i}(t)=-U_{-i}
. Reversing the lattice direction reversed the priorities of the labels (for the walks
\widetilde{X}
, lower label means lower priority), so the non-decreasing projection
F
to left-going multiclass stationary measures has to be applied to speeds
-\widetilde{U}_{i}=U_{-i}
.

The distributional equality
\{\widetilde{U}_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\overset{d}{=}\{U_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
from
[
2
, Proposition 5.2]
implies that both speed processes have the same SH limit. This can also be verified by replacing
U
with
\widetilde{U}
in (
5.3
), rearranging, taking the limit, and using the reflection property (Proposition
2.3.1
(iv)
) of the SH.

Appendix A
Auxiliary technical inputs

A.1
Maximizers of continuous functions

Recall the definitions of
f(x,y)
and
f\;{\leq}_{\rm inc}\;g
from Section
1.5
.

Lemma A.1.1
.

Let
f,g:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be continuous functions satisfying
f(x)\vee g(x)\to-\infty
as
x\to\pm\infty
and
f\;{\leq}_{\rm inc}\;g
. Let
x_{f}^{L}
and
x_{f}^{R}
be the leftmost and rightmost maximizers of
f
over
\mathbb{R}
, and similarly defined for
g
. Then,
x_{f}^{L}\leq x_{g}^{L}
and
x_{f}^{R}\leq x_{g}^{R}
.

Proof.

By the definition of
x_{g}^{R}
and by the assumption
f\;{\leq}_{\rm inc}\;g
, for all
x>x_{g}^{R}

f(x_{g}^{R},x)\leq g(x_{g}^{R},x)<0.

Hence, the rightmost maximizer of
f
must be less than or equal to
x_{g}^{R}
. We get the statement for leftmost maximizers by considering the functions
x\mapsto f(-x)
and
g\mapsto g(-x)
.
∎

Lemma A.1.2
.

Assume that
f,g:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfy
f\;{\leq}_{\rm inc}\;g
. Then, for
a\leq x\leq y\leq b
,

0\leq g(x,y)-f(x,y)\leq g(a,b)-f(a,b).

Proof.

The first inequality follows immediately from the assumption
f\;{\leq}_{\rm inc}\;g
. The second follows from the inequality

f(a,b)-f(x,y)=f(a,x)+f(y,b)\leq g(a,x)+g(y,b)=g(a,b)-g(x,y).\qed

Lemma A.1.3
.

Let
S\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and let
f_{n}:S\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be continuous functions that converge uniformly to
f:S\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
. Let
c_{n}
be a maximizer of
f_{n}
and assume
c_{n}\to c\in S
. Then
c
is a maximizer of
f
.

Proof.

f_{n}(c_{n})\geq f_{n}(x)
for all
x\in S
, so it suffices to show that
f_{n}(c_{n})\rightarrow f(c)
. This follows from the uniform convergence of
f_{n}
to
f
, the continuity of
f
, and

|f_{n}(c_{n})-f(c)|\leq|f_{n}(c_{n})-f(c_{n})|+|f(c_{n})-f(c)|.\qed

The following is a well-known fact, but is often stated without proof, so we include full justification for the sake of completeness. For example, it appears as Equation (1.4) in
[
124
]
and Equation (13) in
[
119
]
.

Lemma A.1.4
.

Let
f:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a continuous function such that

\lim_{x\rightarrow\pm\infty}f(x)=\pm\infty.

Set
F(y)=\sup_{-\infty<x\leq y}f(x)
. Then,

\inf_{y\leq x<\infty}(2F(x)-f(x))=F(y).

(A.1)

Proof.

The left-hand side of (
A.1
) is

\inf_{y\leq x<\infty}(2F(x)-f(x))=\inf_{y\leq x<\infty}\bigl(2\sup_{-\infty<u\leq x}f(u)-f(x)\bigr).

For all
x\geq y
,
\sup_{-\infty<u\leq x}f(u)
is greater than or equal to both
f(x)
and
\sup_{-\infty<u\leq y}f(u)
. Therefore,

2\sup_{-\infty<u\leq x}f(u)-f(x)\geq\sup_{-\infty<u\leq y}f(u)+f(x)-f(x)=F(y).

This establishes one direction of (
A.1
). To show the other direction, we show that there exists
x\geq y
such that

2F(x)-f(x)=F(y).

Note that
f(y)\leq F(y)
recall the assumption
\lim_{x\rightarrow\infty}f(x)=\infty
. By the intermediate value theorem,
f(x)=F(y)
for some
x\geq y
. Let

x^{\star}=\inf\{x\geq y:f(x)=F(y)\}.

Then, by continuity,
f(x^{\star})=F(x^{\star})=F(y)
, and

2F(x^{\star})-f(x^{\star})=2F(y)-F(y)=F(y).\qed

A.2
Miscellaneous probabilistic facts

Lemma A.2.1
.

For a nonnegative random variable
Z
on
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
and
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
, the following holds

\int_{\Omega}e^{-\alpha Z}\,d\mathbb{P}=1-\alpha\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\alpha z}\mathbb{P}(Z>z)\,dz.

Proof.

We use Tonelli’s Theorem below.

\displaystyle-\alpha\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\alpha z}\mathbb{P}(Z>z)\,dz=-\alpha\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\Omega}e^{-\alpha z}1(Z>z)\,d\mathbb{P}dz

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\alpha\int_{\Omega}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\alpha z}1(Z>z)\,dzd\mathbb{P}=-\alpha\int_{\Omega}\int_{0}^{Z}e^{-\alpha z}\,dzd\mathbb{P}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}(e^{-\alpha Z}-1)\,d\mathbb{P}=\int_{\Omega}e^{-\alpha Z}d\mathbb{P}-1.

Rearranging this equation completes the proof.
∎

The following is a classical result. We provide a proof for the specific formulation we use.

Theorem A.2.2

(The Cameron-Martin-Girsanov Theorem)
.

Let
X:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
be a standard Brownian motion with drift
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
on the space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
. Then, for
t>0
and
\rho\in\mathbb{R}
consider the measure on
(\Omega,\mathcal{F})
defined by

\widehat{\mathbb{P}}(A)=\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\exp\Bigl((\rho-\lambda)X(t)+\frac{\lambda^{2}-\rho^{2}}{2}t\Bigr)\mathbf{1}_{A}\Bigr].

Then, under
\widehat{\mathbb{P}}
,
\{X(s):0\leq s\leq t\}
is a standard Brownian motion with drift
\rho
.

Proof.

The measures
\mathbb{P}
and
\widehat{\mathbb{P}}
are mutually absolutely continuous, so continuity is preserved. It remains to show that, under
\widehat{\mathbb{P}}
, for
0=t_{0}<t_{1}<\cdots<t_{n-1}<t_{n}=t
,
X(t_{1}),X(t_{2})-X(t_{1}),\ldots,X(t_{n})-X(t_{n-1})
are independent Gaussian random variables with mean
\rho(t_{i}-t_{i-1})
and variance
t_{i}-t_{i-1}
. We prove this via moment generating functions: For
1\leq i\leq n
, set
s_{i}=t_{i}-t_{i-1}
and
Y_{i}=X(t_{i})-X(t_{i-1})
. Let
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}\in\mathbb{R}
. Then, using the fact that
t=s_{1}+\cdots+s_{n}
and
X(t)=Y_{1}+\cdots+Y_{n}
,

\displaystyle\quad\;\widehat{\mathbb{E}}\Bigl[\exp\Bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}Y_{i}\Bigr)\Bigr]=\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\exp\Bigl((\rho-\lambda)(Y_{1}+\cdots+Y_{n})+\frac{\lambda^{2}-\rho^{2}}{2}t+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}Y_{i}\Bigr)\Bigr)\Bigr]

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi s_{i}}}\exp\bigl(-\frac{(y_{i}-\lambda s_{i})^{2}}{2s_{i}}+(\rho-\lambda)y_{i}+\alpha_{i}y_{i}+\frac{\lambda^{2}-\rho^{2}}{2}s_{i}\bigr)\,dy_{i}

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi s_{i}}}\exp\bigl(-\frac{(y_{i}-\lambda s_{i})^{2}}{2s_{i}}+(\rho-\lambda)y_{i}+\alpha_{i}y_{i}+\frac{\lambda^{2}-\rho^{2}}{2}s_{i}\bigr)\,dy_{i}

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\exp\bigl(\lambda s_{i}(\rho-\lambda+\alpha_{i})+\frac{s_{i}(\rho-\lambda+\alpha_{i})^{2}}{2}+\frac{\lambda^{2}-\rho^{2}}{2}s_{i}\Bigr)=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\exp(\rho\alpha_{i}s_{i}+s_{i}\alpha_{i}^{2}/2).\;\;\qed

The following is derived from a classical result.

Lemma A.2.3
.

[
29
, Equation 1.1.4 (1) on pg 251 ]

For a standard Brownian motion
B
and
\xi>0
,

\sup_{-\infty<u\leq 0}\{\sqrt{2}B(u)+\xi u\}\sim\operatorname{Exp}(\xi).

Proof.

The statement given in
[
29
]
is

\sup_{0\leq u\leq\infty}\{B(u)-\xi u\}\sim\operatorname{Exp}(2\xi).

To get to the result we use, observe that

\sup_{-\infty<u\leq 0}\{\sqrt{2}B(u)+\xi u\}=\sqrt{2}\sup_{0\leq u<\infty}\bigl\{B(-u)-\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}u\bigr\}\sim\sqrt{2}\operatorname{Exp}(\sqrt{2}\xi)\sim\operatorname{Exp}(\xi).\qed

The following is also well-known (see, for example,
[
29
, Equation 1.2.4 on page 251]
; this result is stated for the running infimum, but can be modified by reflection invariance of Brownian motion). We provide a short proof for the specific choice of parameterization we use

Lemma A.2.4
.

Let
B
be a standard Brownian motion. Then, for all
x\geq 0
,

\mathbb{P}\bigl(\sup_{0\leq u\leq 1}\{\sqrt{2}B(u)+\xi u\}\leq x\bigr)=\Phi\Bigl(\frac{x-\xi}{\sqrt{2}}\Bigr)-e^{\xi x}\Phi\Bigl(\frac{-x-\xi}{\sqrt{2}}\Bigr).

Proof.

We prove that

\mathbb{P}\bigl(\sup_{0\leq u\leq 1}\{B(u)+\xi u\}\leq x\bigr)=\Phi(x-\xi)-e^{2\xi x}\Phi(-x-\xi),

(A.2)

and the statement given follows by replacing
x
with
x/\sqrt{2}
and
\xi
with
\xi/\sqrt{2}
. Let

W^{\xi}(u)=B(u)+\xi u,\quad M^{\xi}=\sup_{0\leq u\leq 1}\{W^{\xi}(u)\},

and denote
M=M^{0}
and
W=W^{0}
. A classical fact due to the reflection principle (see for example,
[
55
, Exercise 7.4.3]
), is that
M
and
W(1)
have joint density

f(m,w)=\frac{2(2m-w)}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-(2m-w)^{2}/2}\mathbf{1}(m\geq 0,-\infty<w\leq m).

By Theorem
A.2.2
,
M^{\xi}
and
W^{\xi}(1)
have joint density

f^{\xi}(m,w)=\frac{2(2m-w)}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-(2m-w)^{2}/2+\xi w-\xi^{2}/2}\mathbf{1}(m>0,-\infty<w<m).

Hence,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(M^{\xi}\leq x)

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{x}\int_{w}^{x}\frac{2(2m-w)}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-(2m-w)^{2}/2+\xi w-\xi^{2}/2}dm\,dw

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{x}\int_{w}^{2x-w}\frac{u}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-u^{2}/2+\xi w-\xi^{2}/2}\,du\,dw

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{x}\frac{e^{\xi w-\xi^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\Bigl[e^{-w^{2}/2}-e^{-(2x-w)^{2}/2}\Bigr]\,dw

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{x}\frac{e^{-(w-\xi)^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\,dw-e^{2x\xi}\int_{-\infty}^{x}\frac{e^{-(w-(2x+\xi))^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\,dx,

and this equals (
A.2
) after a simple change of variables.
∎

A.3
Maximum of random walk

We first state a random walk lemma that comes from p. 519–520 in
[
128
]
.
See also Chapter VIII, Section 6 in
[
4
]
.

Lemma A.3.1

(
[
128
]
, Proposition 6.9.4. See also Chapter VIII, Section 6 in
[
4
]
)
.

Let
\mu_{N}
be a sequence of strictly positive numbers with
\mu_{N}\to 0
. Let
\sigma_{N}
be a sequence satisfying
\sigma_{N}\to 1
. For each
N
, let
\{X_{N,i}:i\in\mathbb{Z}\}
be a collection of i.i.d. random variables with mean
\mu_{N}
and variance
\sigma_{N}^{2}
. Further, suppose that the sequence
\{X_{N,0}^{2}:N\geq 1\}
is uniformly integrable, meaning that

\lim_{b\to\infty}\sup_{N\geq 1}\mathbb{E}\bigl[X_{N,0}^{2}\mathbf{1}_{|X_{N,0}|>b}\bigr]=0.

Let
B
be a Brownian motion with diffusion coefficient
1
and zero drift. Then, the following convergence in distribution holds:

\sup_{-\infty<x\leq 0}\mu_{N}S^{N}(\lceil x\rceil)\underset{N\to\infty}{\Longrightarrow}\sup_{-\infty<x\leq 0}\{B(x)+x\}.

Next, we reformulate the statement slightly to suit our purposes.

Lemma A.3.2
.

Let
\mu_{N}
be a sequence of strictly positive numbers with
\mu_{N}\to 0
. Let
\sigma_{N}
be a sequence satisfying
\sigma_{N}\to\sigma>0
. Let
\varphi(N)
be a sequence satisfying
\mu_{N}/\varphi(N)\to m>0
. For each
N
, let
\{X_{N,i}:i\in\mathbb{Z}\}
be a collection of i.i.d. random variables with mean
\mu_{N}
and variance
\sigma_{N}^{2}
. Further, suppose that the sequence
\{X_{N,0}^{2}:N\geq 1\}
is uniformly integrable. Let
S^{N}(m)
be defined as

S^{N}(m)=\begin{cases}-\sum_{i=m}^{-1}X_{N,i}&m\leq 0\\
\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}X_{N,i}&m\geq 0\end{cases}

(A.3)

with
S^{N}(0)=0
. Let
B
be a Brownian motion with diffusion coefficient
1
and zero drift. Then, the following convergence in distribution holds:

\sup_{-\infty<x\leq 0}\varphi(N)S^{N}(\lceil{x}\rceil)\underset{N\to\infty}{\Longrightarrow}\sup_{-\infty<x\leq 0}\{\sigma B(x)+mx\}

(A.4)

Remark A.3.3
.

It is immediate that on the left-hand side of (
A.4
), one can replace
x
with
\lceil\xi(N)x\rceil
for any strictly positive sequence
\xi(N)
.

Proof.

The random variables
X_{N,i}/\sigma_{N}
satisfy the conditions of Lemma
A.3.1
with
\mu_{N}
replaced by
\mu_{N}/\sigma_{N}
. Then,

\displaystyle\,\sup_{-\infty<x\leq 0}\varphi(N)S^{N}(x)=\frac{\varphi(N)\sigma_{N}^{2}}{\mu_{N}}\sup_{-\infty<x\leq 0}\frac{\mu_{N}}{\sigma_{N}}\cdot\frac{S^{N}(x)}{\sigma_{N}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\varphi(N)\sigma_{N}^{2}}{\mu_{N}}\sup_{-\infty<x\leq 0}\frac{\mu_{N}}{\sigma_{N}}\cdot\frac{S^{N}(\lceil\sigma_{N}^{2}x/\mu_{N}^{2}\rceil)}{\sigma_{N}}\ \overset{\rm Lemma\ref{lem:resnick}}{\Longrightarrow}\ \frac{\sigma^{2}}{m}\sup_{-\infty<x\leq 0}\{B(x)+x\}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{m}\sup_{-\infty<x\leq 0}\Bigl\{B\Bigl(\frac{m^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}x\Bigr)+\frac{m^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}x\Bigr\}\overset{d}{=}\sup_{-\infty<x\leq 0}\{\sigma B(x)+mx\},

so we may now apply Lemma
A.3.1
.
∎

For
x\in\mathbb{R}
, recall that
[x]
denotes the integer closest to
x
with
|[x]|\leq|x|
.

Lemma A.3.4
.

Consider the setting of Lemma
A.3.2
. Let
\xi(N)
be a sequence satisfying
\varphi(N)^{2}\xi(N)\to R>0
. Then, for each
S<T\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\Bigl[\,\sup_{-\infty<x\leq[S\xi(N)]}\varphi(N)S^{N}(x)>\sup_{[S\xi(N)]\leq x\leq[T\xi(N)]}\varphi(N)S^{N}(x)\Bigr]

(A.5)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\bigl[\sup_{-\infty<x\leq S}\{\sigma B(Rx)+mRx\}>\sup_{S\leq x\leq T}\{\sigma B(Rx)+mRx\}\bigr].

Proof.

Observe that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigl[\,\sup_{-\infty<x\leq[S\xi(N)]}\varphi(N)S^{N}(x)>\sup_{[S\xi(N)]\leq x\leq[T\xi(N)]}\varphi(N)S^{N}(x)\Bigr]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\Bigl[\,\sup_{-\infty<x\leq[S\xi(N)]}\varphi(N)S^{N}([S\xi(N)],x)>\sup_{[S\xi(N)]\leq x\leq[T\xi(N)]}\varphi(N)S^{N}([S\xi(N)],x)\Bigr].

Now, note that

\sup_{-\infty<x\leq[S\xi(N)]}\varphi(N)S^{N}([S\xi(N)],x),\quad\text{and}\quad\sup_{[S\xi(N)]\leq x\leq[T\xi(N)]}\varphi(N)S^{N}([S\xi(N)],x)

are independent. By convergence of random walk to Brownian motion with drift (with respect to the topology of uniform convergence on compact sets) , we get that

\sup_{[S\xi(N)]\leq x\leq[T\xi(N)]}\varphi(N)S^{N}(RS,[S\xi(N)],x)\Longrightarrow\sup_{S\leq x\leq T}\{\sigma B(RS,Rx)+R(m-S)x\}.

By shift invariance of random walk and Lemma
A.3.2
,

\displaystyle\,\sup_{-\infty<x\leq[S\xi(N)]}\varphi(N)S^{N}([S\xi(N)],x)\overset{d}{=}\sup_{-\infty<x\leq 0}\varphi(N)S^{N}(x)

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\sup_{-\infty<x\leq 0}\{\sigma B(x)+mx\}=\sup_{-\infty<x\leq 0}\{\sigma B(Rx)+mRx\}

\displaystyle\overset{d}{=}\sup_{-\infty<x\leq S}\{\sigma B(RS,Rx)+m(R-S)x\}.

By independence, we have shown the following joint convergence:

\displaystyle\Bigl(\sup_{-\infty<x\leq[S\xi(N)]}\varphi(N)S^{N}([S\xi(N)],x),\sup_{[S\xi(N)]\leq x\leq[T\xi(N)]}\varphi(N)S^{N}([S\xi(N)],x)\Bigr)

(A.6)

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\Bigl(\sup_{-\infty<x\leq S}\{\sigma B(RS,Rx)+m(R-S)x\},\sup_{S\leq x\leq T}\{\sigma B(RS,Rx)+m(R-S)x\}\Bigr).

The right-hand side of (
A.6
) consists of two independent random variables with continuous distribution. Therefore,

\displaystyle\quad\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\Bigl[\,\sup_{-\infty<x\leq[S\xi(N)]}\varphi(N)S^{N}(x)>\sup_{[S\xi(N)]\leq x\leq[T\xi(N)]}\varphi(N)S^{N}(x)\Bigr]

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\Bigl[\,\sup_{-\infty<x\leq[S\xi(N)]}\varphi(N)S^{N}([S\xi(N)],x)>\sup_{[S\xi(N)]\leq x\leq[T\xi(N)]}\varphi(N)S^{N}([S\xi(N)],x)\Bigr]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\bigl[\sup_{-\infty<x\leq S}\{\sigma B(RS,Rx)+m(R-S)x\}>\sup_{S\leq x\leq T}\{\sigma B(Rx)+m(R-S)x\}\bigr]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\bigl[\sup_{-\infty<x\leq S}\{\sigma B(Rx)+mRx\}>\sup_{S\leq x\leq T}\{\sigma B(Rx)+mRx\}\bigr],

with the second equality holding because the event on the right-hand side is a continuity set for the joint vector on the right in (
A.6
).
∎

Appendix B
The directed landscape

B.1
Bounds and distributional invariances

The directed landscape satisfies the following symmetries.

Lemma B.1.1
.

[
47
, Lemma 10.2]
,
[
49
, Proposition 1.23]

As a random continuous function of
(x,s;y,t)\in\mathbb{R}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\uparrow$}}}}^{4}
, the directed landscape
\mathcal{L}
satisfies the following distributional symmetries, for all
r,c\in\mathbb{R}
and
q>0
.

(i)

(Space-time stationarity)

\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t)\overset{d}{=}\mathcal{L}(x+c,s+r;y+c,t+r).

(ii)

(Skew stationarity)

\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t)\overset{d}{=}\mathcal{L}(x+cs,s;y+ct,t)-2c(x-y)+(t-s)c^{2}.

(iii)

(Spatial and temporal reflections)

\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t)\overset{d}{=}\mathcal{L}(-x,s;-y,t)\overset{d}{=}\mathcal{L}(y,-t;x,-s).

(iv)

(Rescaling)

\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t)\overset{d}{=}q\mathcal{L}(q^{-2}x,q^{-3}s;q^{-2}y,q^{-3}t).

Lemma B.1.2
.

[
47
, Corollary 10.7]

There exists a

random constant
C
such that for all
v=(x,s;y,t)\in\mathbb{R}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\uparrow$}}}}^{4}
, we have

\Bigl|\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t)+\frac{(x-y)^{2}}{t-s}\Bigr|\leq C(t-s)^{1/3}\log^{4/3}\Bigl(\frac{2(\|v\|+2)}{t-s}\Bigr)\log^{2/3}(\|v\|+2),

where
\|v\|
is the Euclidean norm.

Lemma B.1.3
.

[
44
, Proposition 2.6]

For every
i=1,\ldots,k
and
\varepsilon>0
, let

K_{i,\varepsilon}=\{(x,s;y,t)\in\mathbb{R}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\uparrow$}}}}^{4}:s,t\in[0,\varepsilon],x,y\in[i-1/4,i+1/4]\}.

(B.1)

Then, there exists a coupling of
k+1
copies of the directed landscape
\mathcal{L}_{0},\mathcal{L}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{k}
so that
\mathcal{L}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{k}
are independent, and almost surely, there exists a random
\varepsilon>0
such that for
1\leq i\leq k
,
\mathcal{L}_{0}|_{K_{i,\varepsilon}}=\mathcal{L}_{i}|_{K_{i,\varepsilon}}
.

On a measure space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
, a measure-preserving transformation
T
satisfies
T^{-1}E\in\mathcal{F}
and
\mathbb{P}(T^{-1}E)=\mathbb{P}(E)
for all
E\in\mathcal{F}
. Such a transformation is said to be
ergodic
if
\mathbb{P}(E)\in\{0,1\}
whenever
T^{-1}E=E
. The transformation
T
is said to be
mixing
if, for all
A,B\in\mathcal{F}
,
\mathbb{P}(A\cap T^{-k}B)\to\mathbb{P}(A)\mathbb{P}(B)
as
k\to\infty
. By setting
A=B
, one sees that mixing implies ergodicity.

Lemma B.1.4
.

For
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
, not both
0
and
z>0
, consider the shift operator
T_{z;a,b}
acting on the directed landscape
\mathcal{L}
as

T_{z;a,b}\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t)=\mathcal{L}(x+az,s+bz;y+az;t+bz),

where both sides are understood as a process on
\mathbb{R}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\uparrow$}}}}^{4}
. Then,
\mathcal{L}
is mixing under this transformation. That is, for all Borel subsets
A,B
of the space
C(\mathbb{R}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\uparrow$}}}}^{4},\mathbb{R})
,

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}\in A,T_{z;a,b}\mathcal{L}\in B)\overset{z\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}\in A)\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}\in B).

In words, the directed landscape is mixing (and therefore ergodic) under space-time shifts in any planar direction.

Proof.

This key to the proof is Lemma
B.1.3
, and I thank Duncan Dauvergne for pointing this out to me. We prove the case
a\neq 0
, and the case
a=0
and
b\neq 0
will be proven separately. Further, since we send
z\to\infty
, it suffices to show the result for
a=1
, for then the result also holds for arbitrary
a
and
b=ab
. With this simplification, we use the shorthand notation
T_{z;b}=T{z:1,b}
. By Lemma
B.1.1
(i)
,
\mathcal{L}
is stationary under the shift
T_{z;b}
. By Dynkin’s
\pi
-
\lambda
theorem, it suffices to show that for a compact set
K\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\uparrow$}}}}^{4}
and Borel sets
A\subseteq C(K,\mathbb{R})
and
B\subseteq C(K,\mathbb{R})
,

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}|_{K}\in A,(T_{z;b}\mathcal{L})|_{K}\in B)\overset{z\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}|_{K}\in A)\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}|_{K}\in B).

Further, by temporal stationarity, it suffices to assume that
\inf\{s:(x,s;y,t)\in K\}\geq 0
. Consider the coupling
\mathcal{L}_{0},\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2}
of Lemma
B.1.3
with
k=2
. Then, using the rescaling and spatial stationarity of Lemma
B.1.1
,

\displaystyle\quad\;\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}|_{K}\in A,(T_{z;b}\mathcal{L})|_{K}\in B)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}_{0}(x,s;y,t)|_{K}\in A,\mathcal{L}_{0}(x+z,s+bz;y+z,t+bz)|_{K}\in B)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(z^{1/2}\mathcal{L}_{0}(z^{-1}x,z^{-3/2}s;z^{-1}y,z^{-3/2}t)|_{K}\in A,

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad z^{1/2}\mathcal{L}_{0}(z^{-1}x+1,z^{-3/2}s+z^{-1/2}b;z^{-1}y+1,z^{-3/2}t+z^{-1/2}b)|_{K}\in B)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(z^{1/2}\mathcal{L}_{0}(z^{-1}x+1,z^{-3/2}s;z^{-1}y+1,z^{-3/2}t)|_{K}\in A,

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad z^{1/2}\mathcal{L}_{0}(z^{-1}x+2,z^{-3/2}s+z^{-1/2}b;z^{-1}y+2,z^{-3/2}t+z^{-1/2}b)|_{K}\in B).

(B.2)

Specifically, we used the rescaling property with
q=z^{1/2}
in the second equality, and in the third equality, we shifted the entire process by
1
in the spatial direction.
Above, the restrictions
|_{K_{j}}
mean that
(x,s;y,t)\in K_{j}
.
Since
K
is compact and we assumed
s\geq 0
for all
(x,s;y,t)\in K
, for any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
Z>0
such that for
z>Z
,

\displaystyle\{(z^{-1}x+1,z^{-3/2}s;z^{-1}y+1,z^{-3/2}t):(x,s;y,t)\in K\}\subseteq K_{1,\varepsilon},\quad\text{and}

(B.3)

\displaystyle\{(z^{-1}x+2,z^{-3/2}s+z^{-1/2}b;z^{-1}y+2,z^{-3/2}t+z^{-1/2}b):(x,s;y,t)\in K\}\subseteq K_{2,\varepsilon},

where
K_{i,\varepsilon}
are defined in (
B.1
). Let
C_{z}
be the event where both these containments hold for the random
\varepsilon>0
in Lemma
B.1.3
, and let
D_{z}
be the event in (
B.2
). Then,
\mathbb{P}(C_{z})\to 1
as
z\to+\infty
. Then, for any
\delta>0
, whenever
z
is sufficiently large so that
1-\mathbb{P}(C_{z})=\delta>0
,

\displaystyle\quad\;\Big|\mathbb{P}(D_{z})-\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}|_{K}\in A)\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}|_{K}\in B)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\Big|\mathbb{P}(D_{z}\cap C_{z})-\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}|_{K}\in A)\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}|_{K}\in B)\Big|+\delta

\displaystyle=\Big|\mathbb{P}(z^{1/2}\mathcal{L}_{1}(z^{-1}x+1,z^{-3/2}s;z^{-1}y+1,z^{-3/2}t)|_{K}\in A,

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad z^{1/2}\mathcal{L}_{2}(z^{-1}x+2,z^{-3/2}s+z^{-1/2}b;z^{-1}y+2,z^{-3/2}t+z^{-1/2}b)|_{K}\in B,C_{z})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad-\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}_{1}|_{K}\in A)\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}_{1}|_{K}\in B)\Big|+\delta

\displaystyle\leq\Big|\mathbb{P}(z^{1/2}\mathcal{L}_{0}(z^{-1}x+1,z^{-3/2}s;z^{-1}y+1,z^{-3/2}t)|_{K}\in A,

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad z^{1/2}\mathcal{L}_{0}(z^{-1}x+2,z^{-3/2}s+z^{-1/2}b;z^{-1}y+2,z^{-3/2}t+z^{-1/2}b)|_{K}\in B)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad-\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}_{1}|_{K}\in A)\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}_{2}|_{K}\in B)\Big|+2\delta

\displaystyle=\Big|\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}_{1}|_{K}\in A,\mathcal{L}_{2}|_{K}\in B)-\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}_{1}|_{K}\in A)\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}_{2}|_{K}\in B)\Big|+2\delta=2\delta,

completing the proof since
\delta
is arbitrary. Specifically, in the two inequalities, we added and removed the event
C_{z}
at the cost of
\delta
. In the first equality above, we used the fact that the containments (
B.3
) hold on
C_{z}
and
\mathcal{L}_{0}|_{K_{i},\varepsilon}=\mathcal{L}_{i}|_{K_{i},\varepsilon}
for
i=1,2
. In the last line, we reversed the application of the rescaling and spatial stationarity, then used the independence of
\mathcal{L}_{1}
and
\mathcal{L}_{2}
from Lemma
B.1.3
.

The statement for the vertical shift operator when
a=0
is simpler. For a compact set
K
, the processes
\mathcal{L}|_{K}
and
T_{z;0,b}\mathcal{L}|_{K}
are independent for sufficiently large
b
by the independent increments property of the directed landscape, and the desired result follows.
∎

Recall the definition of the state space
\operatorname{UC}
(
3.5
) for the KPZ fixed point. Recall that the KPZ fixed point
h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}};s,\mathfrak{h})
with initial data
\mathfrak{h}
at time
s
can be represented as

h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,y;s,\mathfrak{h})=\sup_{x\in R}\{\mathfrak{h}(x)+\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t)\}\qquad\text{for }t>s.

(B.4)

Lemma B.1.5
.

[
47
,
20
,
68
,
120
]

Let
\mathcal{L}:\mathbb{R}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\uparrow$}}}}^{4}\to\mathbb{R}
be a continuous function satisfying the metric composition law (
3.3
) and such that maximizers in (
3.3
) exist. Then,

(i)

Whenever
s<t
,
x_{1}<x_{2}
,
y_{1}<y_{2}
,

\mathcal{L}(x_{2},s;y_{1},t)-\mathcal{L}(x_{1},s;y_{1},t)\leq\mathcal{L}(x_{2},s;y_{2},t)-\mathcal{L}(x_{1},s;y_{2},t).

Let
\mathfrak{h}^{1},\mathfrak{h}^{2}\in\operatorname{UC}
, and For
i=1,2
and
t>s
, define
h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,y;s,\mathfrak{h}^{i})
by (
B.4
).
Then, assuming that maximizers in (
B.4
) exist, the following hold.

(ii)

If
\mathfrak{h}^{1}\;{\leq}_{\rm inc}\;\mathfrak{h}^{2}
, then
h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,{\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt};s,\mathfrak{h}^{1})\;{\leq}_{\rm inc}\;h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,{\hskip 1.1pt\raisebox{1.5pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}\hskip 1.1pt};s,\mathfrak{h}^{2})
for all
t>s
.

(iii)

For
t>s
and
i=1,2
, set

Z_{\mathcal{L}}(t,y;s,\mathfrak{h}^{i})=\max\argmax_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\{\mathfrak{h}^{i}(x)+\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t)\}.

Then, if
x<y
and
Z_{\mathcal{L}}(t,y;s,\mathfrak{h}^{1})\leq Z_{\mathcal{L}}(t,x;s,\mathfrak{h}^{2})
,

h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,y;s,\mathfrak{h}^{1})-h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,x;s,\mathfrak{h}^{1})\leq h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,y;s,\mathfrak{h}^{2})-h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,x;s,\mathfrak{h}^{2}).

B.2
Geodesics in the directed landscape

We start by citing some results from
[
23
]
and
[
48
]
.

Lemma B.2.1
.

[
23
, Theorem 1.18]

There exists a single event of full probability on which, for any compact set
K\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\uparrow$}}}}^{4}
, there is a random
\varepsilon>0
such that the following holds. If
v_{1}=(x,s;y,u)\in K
and
v_{2}=(z,s;w,u)\in K
admit geodesics
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
satisfying
|\gamma_{1}(t)-\gamma_{2}(t)|\leq\varepsilon
for all
t\in[s,u]
, then
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
are not disjoint, i.e.,
\gamma_{1}(t)=\gamma_{2}(t)
for some
t\in[s,u]
.

Let
g
be a geodesic from
(x,s)
to
(y,u)
. Define the graph of this geodesic as

\mathcal{G}g:=\{(g(t),t):t\in[s,u]\}.

Lemma B.2.2
.

[
48
, Lemma 3.1]

The following holds on a single event of full probability. Let
(p_{n};q_{n})\to(p,q)=(x,s;y,t)\in\mathbb{R}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\uparrow$}}}}^{4}
, and let
g_{n}
be any sequence of geodesics from
p_{n}
to
q_{n}
. Then, the sequence of graphs
\mathcal{G}g_{n}
is precompact in the Hausdorff metric, and any subsequential limit of
\mathcal{G}g_{n}
is the graph of a geodesic from
p
to
q
.

Lemma B.2.3
.

[
48
, Lemma 3.3]

The following holds on a single event of full probability. Let
(p_{n};q_{n})=(x_{n},s_{n};y_{n},u_{n})\in\mathbb{R}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\uparrow$}}}}^{4}\to(p;q)=(x,s;y,u)\in\mathbb{R}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\uparrow$}}}}^{4}
, and let
g_{n}
be any sequence of geodesics from
p_{n}
to
q_{n}
. Suppose that either

(i)

For all
n
,
g_{n}
is the unique geodesic from
(x_{n},s_{n})
to
(y_{n},u_{n})
and
\mathcal{G}g_{n}\to\mathcal{G}g
for some geodesic
g
from
p
to
q
, or

(ii)

There is a unique geodesic
g
from
p
to
q
.

Then, the
overlap

O(g_{n},g):=\{t\in[s_{n},u_{n}]\cap[s,u]:g_{n}(t)=g(t)\}

is an interval for all
n
whose endpoints converge to
s
and
u
.

Remark B.2.4
.

We note that condition
(i)
is slightly different from that stated in
[
48
]
. There, it is assumed instead that
(x_{n},s_{n};y_{n},u_{n})\in\mathbb{Q}^{4}\cap\mathbb{R}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\uparrow$}}}}^{4}
for all
n
. The only use of this requirement in the proof is to ensure that there is a unique geodesic from
(x_{n},s_{n})
to
(y_{n},u_{n})
for all
n
, so there is no additional justification needed for the statement we use here.

Lemma B.2.5
.

On the intersection of the full probability events from Lemmas
B.2.2
and
B.2.3
, the following holds. For all ordered triples
s<t<u
and compact sets
K\subseteq\mathbb{R}
, the set

\{g(t):g\text{ is the unique geodesic between }(x,s)\text{ and }(y,u)\text{ for some }x\in K,y\in K\}

(B.5)

is finite.

Lemma
B.2.5
is known. Its derivation from Lemma
B.2.1
and some results of
[
48
]
are shown in
[
35
]
. Lemma 3.12 in
[
70
]
(posted after the first version of
[
35
]
) provides a stronger quantitative statement, but we do not need it for our purposes. This stronger estimate can be traced back to the work of
[
21
]
in exponential LPP using integrable methods.

Proof of Lemma
B.2.5
.

Assume, without loss of generality, that
K
is a closed interval
[a,b]
. We observe that by planarity, all geodesics from
(x,s)
to
(y,u)
for
x,y\in K
lie between the leftmost geodesic from
(a,s)
to
(a,u)
and the rightmost geodesic from
(b,s)
to
(b,u)
. Hence, the set (
B.5
) is contained in a compact set, and it suffices to show that the set has no limit points.

Assume, to the contrary, that there exists a point
(\hat{x},s;\hat{y},u)\in(K\times\{s\}\times K\times\{u\})\cap\mathbb{R}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\uparrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\uparrow$}}}}^{4}
with unique geodesic
\hat{g}
such that there exists a sequence of points
x_{n},y_{n}\in K
such that for all
n
, the geodesic
g_{n}
from
(x_{n},s)
to
(y_{n},t)
is unique and so that
g_{n}(t)\to\hat{g}(t)
but
g_{n}(t)\neq\hat{g}(t)
for all
n
.
By compactness, there exists a convergent subsequence
(x_{n_{k}},y_{n_{k}})\to(x,y)
. By Lemma
B.2.2
, there exists a further subsequence
(x_{n_{k_{\ell}}},y_{n_{k_{\ell}}})
such that the geodesic graphs
\mathcal{G}g_{n_{k_{\ell}}}
converge to the graph of some geodesic
\mathcal{G}g
from
(x,s)
to
(y,u)
in the Hausdorff metric. Since
g_{n}(t)\to\hat{g}(t)
, we have
g(t)=\hat{g}(t)
. By Lemma
B.2.3
, the overlap
O(g_{n_{k_{\ell}}},g)
is an interval whose endpoints converge to
s
and
u
, so
g_{n_{k_{\ell}}}(t)=g(t)=\hat{g}(t)
for sufficiently large
\ell
, contradicting the definition of the sequence
g_{n}
.
∎
